My godmother’s handsome, sunlit home stood on a quiet cobbled lane in Bretton: a spotless, centuries-old market town where her late husband’s line had resided for so many generations the family bore the very name of their birthplace—Bretton of Bretton, folks called them. Whether that odd quirk came down to random coincidence, or some far-flung, prominent ancestor grand enough to lend his name to the whole surrounding parish, I never learned for certain.

As a young girl, I visited Bretton twice a year, and I cherished every hour of those stays. The house and everyone in it were made perfectly for my quiet tastes: large peaceful rooms lined with well-polished furniture, clear wide windows framing the cobbled high street below, and a small balcony that looked down over a stretch of old stone homes where Sundays and holidays seemed to linger year-round, so still was the air, so spotless the paved paths. In a house full of grown-ups, one small child tends to be cosseted, and I was no exception. Mrs. Bretton had been left a widow young, with only her son to care for, long before I met her; her husband, a well-loved local physician, had passed when she was barely thirty, still in the full bloom of her youth and good looks. By the time I knew her, she was no longer young, but she remained striking: tall, sturdily built, dark-complexioned for an Englishwoman, with the bright glow of perfect health on her cheeks and a pair of warm, crinkling black eyes that seemed to hold a joke just for you. Everyone who knew the family thought it a crying shame her son had not inherited her dark coloring: John Graham Bretton’s eyes were bright sharp blue, even as a schoolboy, and the long soft fall of his hair was a shade no one could properly name save when the sun caught it, and they called it golden. Still, he got her strong, handsome features, her straight perfect teeth, her height (he was not yet fully grown, but he already promised to match her tall frame), and best of all, that unshakable, even-tempered good humor that is worth more to a person than any vast fortune.

It was the autumn of an unremarkable year when my godmother came in person to fetch me from the distant relatives I was living with permanently, and brought me back to Bretton. I think she already saw trouble brewing on the horizon then, shadows of events I could barely even guess at—though the faint, unnameable hunch that something was about to go very wrong hung over me with a soft, unsettling sadness, and I was gladder than I could say to trade my old home for her quiet warmth. Time at Mrs. Bretton’s side never rushed, never surged or raged: it moved soft and slow, like a full, deep river gliding smooth through flat, green meadows. My stays there felt like the stop Christian and Hopeful made beside that blessed stream in Pilgrim’s Progress, the banks lined with green trees and meadows full of lilies that bloomed all year round. There was no wild novelty, no exciting twist or unexpected incident—but I loved peace so deeply, and craved excitement so little, that when disruption did come, it felt like a rude, jarring disturbance I wished would stay far away.

One afternoon a letter arrived whose contents left Mrs. Bretton clearly surprised, a line of faint concern furrowing her brow. My heart leaped straight to my throat at first, certain it was bad news from my old home, waiting for some terrible announcement that would tear me away from all the calm I loved. But she never spoke of it to me, and after a day the small shadow seemed to pass. The next day, I came back from a long rambling country walk, pushed open my bedroom door, and stopped dead. Next to my own French bed in its shaded nook, there sat a tiny white-draped crib in the far corner, and beside my big mahogany chest of drawers stood a miniature rosewood case. I stood still and stared. A second guest is coming, I realized immediately. Mrs. Bretton is expecting someone new to join us.

At dinner, she explained it all. A little girl would be arriving very soon, she said: the daughter of a dear friend and distant relation of Dr. Bretton’s. She had just lost her mother, she went on, and though the loss was a heavy one, it was less tragic than it might have been: Mrs. Home, the little girl’s mother, had been pretty, giddy, and careless, who had neglected her child and left her gentle, quiet husband disappointed and heartsore. The pair had never been suited to one another at all, and had separated amicably by mutual agreement, no messy legal proceedings involved. Not long after, the young woman had danced too hard at a ball, caught a cold that turned to fever, and died very suddenly. Her husband, a man of soft, sensitive feelings, had been so shattered by the shock of the news that he had convinced himself his own lack of patience, his quiet coldness to his frivolous wife, had helped hasten her death. He brooded over the idea until his spirits were so broken his doctors ordered him to travel abroad to recover, and Mrs. Bretton had stepped in to offer a home to his little girl until he was well enough to come for her. “I only hope the child doesn’t take after her mother,” my godmother added, shaking her head. “Silliest, most frivolous little flirt that ever a sensible man was fool enough to marry. Mr. Home is a clever man, in his way, though he’s hopelessly impractical: spends half his life locked in his laboratory running science experiments, a thing that butterfly wife of his could never understand or bear. To tell the truth, I shouldn’t have liked it much myself.” She went on to tell me that Mr. Home was of mixed Scottish and French descent, his scientific streak inherited from a French savant uncle, and he had distant noble relatives in France who wrote “de” before their names.

That same evening at nine o’clock, our servant Warren was sent down to the coach stop to meet the little girl’s carriage. John Graham was away visiting a school friend who lived out in the country, so it was just me and Mrs. Bretton in the drawing room, she reading her evening paper, I plying my needle over a piece of sewing. It was a wild wet night, rain lashing hard against the windowpanes, the wind howling sharp and restless down the old streets. “Poor child,” Mrs. Bretton murmured every few minutes. “What a terrible night for a little one to travel. I wish she were safe here by the fire.”

Just before ten, the doorbell rang. I ran straight down to the hall before Warren could even shut the front door. There was her small trunk and bandboxes stacked by the wall, her nurse-girl huddled beside them, and Warren holding a tiny shawled bundle tight in his arms. “Is that the child?” I asked. “Yes, miss.” I tried to peek at her face, but she twisted it fast away from me, tucking it tight against Warren’s shoulder. “Put me down, please,” a tiny muffled voice said the second he stepped across the drawing room threshold. She fumbled with the pin fastening the big heavy shawl around her, yanked it out herself, and shrugged the clumsy wrapping off in a rush, then made a deft little attempt to fold it—though the fabric was far too big and heavy for her small hands to manage. “Give that to Harriet, please,” she directed, “and she can put it away.” Then she turned and fixed her big eyes straight on Mrs. Bretton.

“Come here, little dear,” my godmother said, opening her arms. “Come let me see if your hands are cold and damp, come warm yourself by the fire.” The child stepped forward promptly. Dressed in her neat little traveling gown, she was so small she looked no bigger than a porcelain doll: her neck as delicate as wax, her head full of silky light curls, making the resemblance even stronger. Seated on Mrs. Bretton’s ample lap, she was tiny enough to be held all in one arm. Mrs. Bretton cooed soft little fond phrases as she rubbed warmth back into the child’s cold hands and feet, and after a few quiet, wistful moments of staring, the little girl smiled back. Mrs. Bretton was never an overly sentimental, caressing woman—even with her beloved son John Graham, she rarely gushed or fussed—but when the small stranger smiled at her, she kissed her soft forehead, and asked what her name was. “Missy,” the child said, then added, “Papa calls me Polly.” “Will Polly be happy to live with me for a little while?” “Not always,” she said, shaking her head very seriously. “Till Papa comes home. Papa’s gone away. He will come back, or send for me.” Her eyes filled over before she could finish the sentence. She slipped off Mrs. Bretton’s lap before anyone could stop her, dragged a footstool over to the darkest corner of the room, and sat there curled up on it, only the tiny, almost silent occasional sniffle giving away that she was crying, no loud, unashamed wails like other grieving small children would make. Mrs. Bretton didn’t notice, which was for the best. After a little while, a small voice called out from the corner: “May we ring for Harriet?”

I rang the bell, and the nurse came. “Harriet, I must be put to bed,” her little mistress told her. “Ask where my bed is, please.” Harriet said she already knew. “Ask if you sleep with me, Harriet.” “No, Missy,” the nurse said. “You’ll be sharing a room with this young lady here,” she said, nodding at me. Polly didn’t move from her corner, but I saw her eyes dart over to study me, silent, for several long minutes. Then she stepped out of the shadows. “Good night, ma’am,” she said to Mrs. Bretton, then walked straight past me without a word. “Good night, Polly,” I said. “No need to say good night, since we sleep in the same chamber,” she replied, and vanished out of the drawing room. We heard Harriet offer to carry her up the stairs, but the little girl refused again and again, “No need, no need,” and we listened to her small, slow step trudge all the way up the staircase by herself.

An hour later, when I went up to bed, she was still wide awake, propped straight up on her pillows, her small hands folded one over the other on the sheet, with a strange old-fashioned calm no child her age should have. I didn’t speak to her for a long while, but just before I blew out the candle, I told her she ought to lie down before she caught cold. “By and by,” she said. She grabbed a tiny scrap of shawl off the chair by her crib and wrapped it tight around her shoulders, and I let her do as she pleased. When the room went dark, I listened for a long time, and heard her still crying, soft, holding the sobs in so no one would stumble on her grief unawares.

When I woke at dawn, I heard the soft trickle of water. She was already up, climbed on a stool by the washstand, struggling to tip the big heavy ewer she could not possibly lift high enough to pour water into the basin. She washed and dressed all by herself, fumbling with the tiny buttons, strings, and hooks, tackling every difficulty with a quiet stubborn persistence that was lovely to watch. She folded her nightgown perfectly, smoothed the sheets of her little crib till it looked untouched, then slipped behind the fall of the white curtain where I could not see her, and went still. I peeked over the edge of my bed, and found her on her knees, forehead pressed to her hands, praying very softly.

Harriet tapped at the door a moment later, and she jumped to her feet. “I am dressed, Harriet,” she whispered. “I dressed myself, but I don’t feel neat. Make me neat, please.” “Why did you dress yourself, Missy?” “Hush, speak very low,” she said. “You’ll wake the girl.” She nodded at me, where I was pretending to be fast asleep. “I dressed myself to learn, against the time you leave me.” “Do you want me to go?” “When you are cross, I have many times wanted you to go. But not now. Tie my sash straight. Make my hair smooth.” And so she fussed, correcting every crooked part of her dress, till Harriet laughed and said what a particular little thing she was. The pair walked to the door, and Polly stopped, pressing a small fist to her chest. “Oh Harriet, I wish this was papa’s house. I don’t know these people.” “Be a good child, Missy.” “I am good,” she said, her voice trembling, “but I ache here. Papa, papa.”

I roused myself then, and got up, to head the little crisis off before it could get worse. Harriet told her to say good morning to me, and she mumbled the words, then followed the nurse out of the room. Harriet left later that same day to visit her own family who lived nearby. When I went down to the breakfast parlor, I found Paulina Mary—they called her Polly for short—sitting quiet by Mrs. Bretton’s side, a mug of warm milk set in front of her, a piece of bread in her small hand that lay perfectly still on the tablecloth, untouched. She hadn’t eaten a bite, Mrs. Bretton said, and by the dark circles under her eyes, she hadn’t slept either. “I don’t know how on earth we’re going to win this little creature over,” she said to me. I told her time and a little kindness would work its magic. “She’ll settle right in the moment she takes a fancy to someone in this house,” Mrs. Bretton replied. “But not a minute before.”

Several days slipped by, and it became clear the small girl had no real fondness for any soul in Mrs. Bretton’s household. She was not wilful, not disobedient, not even strictly naughty—yet no one could glance at her and feel fully at ease, fully tranquil. She moped as no grown grieving exile could mope: her tiny, soft face bore lines of homesickness plainer than the face of a man pining for half-remembered Europe from the farthest antipodes. She looked, at times, half old, half unearthly.

I, Lucy Snowe, have never claimed an overheated, wandering imagination. Yet whenever I turned a doorknob to find her huddled in a far corner, her head propped on her own miniature fist, the room felt not lived in, but haunted. On moonlit nights, waking to see her small white nightgown figure kneeling upright in her bed, praying with the ferver of some devout precocious saint, I felt thoughts rise in me that were hardly more steady than the child’s own frayed, yearning mind. I rarely caught the words of her whispered prayers, many of them unspoken entirely—but the few that reached my ear always tangled around the same two phrases: *Papa. My dear papa*. This was a one-ideaed nature, that rare monomaniac streak I have always thought the cruellest curse a human soul can bear.

No one can guess how that quiet, gnawing grief might have ended if left unchecked—until it turned, all at once, one sunny afternoon. Mrs. Bretton coaxed her out of her usual corner, lifted her to the window seat, and told her to pass the time counting how many ladies walked down the street. She sat listless, hardly glancing out, making no effort to count—until I, my gaze fixed on her face, saw a startling shift in her dark eyes. The heavy, dull glaze cleared; her small overcast brow lifted; her drooping, sad features lit up with a sudden, sharp, burning eagerness.

“It *is*,” she breathed.

She was gone from the room before I could move, swift as a sparrow taking flight. I had no idea how she wrenched the front door open—perhaps it was ajar, perhaps the servant Warren obeyed her sharp, impulsive order without thinking. I watched from the window as her small black dress and braided pinafore (she hated proper frilled pinafores with all her might) darted halfway down the street. I was half-ready to turn and tell Mrs. Bretton the child had run mad, that someone must chase her at once, when a man’s tall figure swept her up off the pavement, wrapped his heavy dark cloak around her, and turned back to the house he had seen her dart from.

I expected he would leave her in a servant’s arms and go on his way—but he stepped inside, paused a moment in the hall, then climbed the stairs. Mrs. Bretton’s flustered, surprised greeting told me at once they were old acquaintances. She looked half ready to scold him for appearing unannounced, and he smiled gently, his deep northern Scots lilt softening his words: “I could not leave the country without seeing for myself how she settled here.”

“But you will unsettle her entirely,” Mrs. Bretton said.

“I hope not. How is papa’s little Polly?” he added, turning to the child, and set her gently on her feet before his chair.

“How is Polly’s papa?” she replied at once, leaning her whole weight on his knee, gazing up into his sharp, stern, knotty-browed face.

It was no loud, sobbing reunion, for which I was grateful—but the quiet, brimful weight of feeling hung in the air heavier than any noisy outburst could. Mr. Home was hard-featured, proud and unpolished all at once, but there was no mistaking the soft emotion behind his sharp eyes. He laid his hand on the top of her small head.

“Kiss Polly,” she said. He did.

I almost wished she would sob hysterically, just to release the tight, heavy feeling in my own chest. But she made no noise at all. She had gotten everything she wanted, and she moved through the rest of the afternoon like a girl in a trance of perfect contentment. She looked nothing like her father in feature, but every thread of her stubborn, single-minded spirit was his, as surely as water poured from a flagon into a cup.

“Polly,” he said, after a time, “go to the hall. You will find my great-coat on a chair. Reach into the pocket, bring me the handkerchief you find there.”

She obeyed, darted off, and returned deftly a moment later. He was still talking to Mrs. Bretton when she came back, and she waited silent at his knee, the handkerchief clutched in her small hand. When he did not seem to notice her, she lifted his unresisting hand, opened his fingers one by one, tucked the handkerchief into his palm, and curled his fingers closed over it. He still did not speak or look at her, but a moment later he lifted her to his knee, and she curled against his chest. For the next hour, neither spoke, neither looked at the other—and both were perfectly satisfied.

At tea that evening, the tiny girl’s behaviour held all of us watching. First she directed Warren to move the chairs, slow and firm: “Put papa’s chair here, and mine right next to it, between him and Mrs. Bretton. *I* will hand his tea.” She took her seat, beckoned her father closer, and when the teacup was passed down the table, she intercepted it, insisting she must stir the sugar, pour the cream, no one else could do it right. The sugar tongs were far too wide for her small hands, so she had to use both to wield them. The silver cream jug was almost too heavy for her to lift, the bread plates too weighty, but she never broke a single thing. A lesser girl would have fumbled and spilled everything—but she was as steady as a little old housewife, fussing over her father like he was her whole world. Her father, blind and soft as all parents are, smiled down at her. “She is my whole comfort,” he told Mrs. Bretton, and Mrs. Bretton, who doted on her own son as her own whole comfort, nodded in full sympathy.

That son, Graham, returned home partway through the evening. I had known all day he was due back, and Mrs. Bretton had waited for him every hour. When he burst in, sixteen years old, tall and fair, with a shock of wavy auburn hair and that easy, charming half-smile that made him look far more mischievous than he really was, the whole circle by the fire broke into bustle. He had met Mr. Home years before, so they greeted each other as old friends—and for a long while, he paid the tiny girl no mind. When Mr. Home stepped out of the room for a moment, Graham leaned against the hearth, his eyes fixed on the small figure perched on a high chair by the table, bent over a scrap of handkerchief she was pretending to hem, pricking her finger over and over with a needle that looked almost too big for her hand, leaving tiny red dots along the cambric, silent and focused, as proper as any grown lady.

“Mother,” he said, half laughing, “there is a young lady in this room I have not been introduced to.”

“Mr. Home’s little girl, you mean,” his mother said, warning in her voice. “Don’t you dare tease her, Graham. I will not have you making a butt of that child.”

“Little girl?” Graham laughed, ignoring her protest, and turned straight to the small figure. “Miss Home, might I have the honour of introducing myself? No one else seems willing to do the service for us. Your obedient servant, John Graham Bretton.”

She looked up at him, slowly set down her needle, her thimble, her scissors, climbed very carefully down from her high perch, and curtsied with a gravity so profound it could have graced a royal drawing room. “How do you do?” she said.

“I am very well, thank you, though a little fatigued from my long journey,” he replied, matching her formal tone. “I hope I find you in good health, ma’am?”

“To-rer-ably well,” she pronounced, slow and deliberate, and then tried to climb back up to her high chair. Realising it required clambering and scrambling, a terrible breach of decorum in front of a strange young gentleman, she gave up entirely and settled on a low stool instead. Graham pulled his own chair closer, right across from her.

“I hope you find my mother’s house a convenient place to stay?” he asked.

“Not par-tic-er-er-ly,” she said. “I want to go home.”

“A very laudable desire, ma’am, but I intend to oppose it. Mamma and Mistress Snowe there are dreadfully dull, you see, and I am counting on you to give me some amusement.”

“I will be leaving soon with papa,” she told him. “I will not stay here long at all.”

“Nonsense,” he said, grinning. “You will stay. I have a pony you can ride, and dozens of books full of pictures you have never seen.”

“Are you going to live here now?” she asked, her head tilting.

“I am. Does that please you? Do you like me?”

“No.”

“Why not?”

“You are queer. Your hair is long and red.”

“Auburn, if you please,” he said, fluffing his leonine tawny waves with great good humour, proud of the colour. “But even with my long red hair, I cannot possibly be queerer than you are, Miss Home.”

“I think I shall go to bed now,” she said, primly standing up.

“Little thing like you should have been in bed hours ago,” he teased. “I bet you stayed up on purpose just to see me arrive.”

“I stayed up for papa, not for you,” she said, sharp as a tack. “You are not nearly so important.”

“Very well,” he laughed. “I will be your favourite soon, you will see. You will like me better than papa before you know it.”

She said good night to Mrs. Bretton and then to me, hesitating a moment as if debating whether Graham deserved the same courtesy—before he reached down, caught her up with one hand, and lifted her straight up over his head, so she could see her own small startled face in the mirror above the mantel. The sudden, unceremonious disrespect of it sent her into a huff.

“For shame, Mr. Graham! Put me down at once!” she cried, indignant, and the moment her feet touched the floor she planted them firmly and said, “I wonder what you would do if I picked *you* up the way you picked me up—lifting you with one hand the same way Warren lifts the little stray cat.”

With that, she turned on her heel and marched off to bed, leaving Graham chuckling behind her at the hearth.

Mr. Home lingered at Mrs. Bretton’s hearth for two full days, and not all the cajolery in the world could draw him out of the warm, low-lit parlour. He sat slouched in his armchair by the fire from dawn till dusk, sometimes wrapped in heavy silence, sometimes listening and responding to Mrs. Bretton’s easy chatter—exactly the right tone for a man raw with quiet grief, never cloyingly sympathetic, never so detached he felt cast adrift, sensible, softened by a gentle, maternal warmth earned by the years she had over him.

As for little Paulina, the child was a strange blend of brightness and hush, half-busy, half-watchful, brimming with a quiet joy she dared not waste. Her father would lift her to rest on his knee for hours at a time, and she would stay perfectly still until the very second she sensed he might grow restless, then murmur soft and shy, “Papa, put me down; my weight will tire you.” That tiny “mighty burden” would slip light as a feather to the rug, settle on the carpet or a low stool right at his feet, and pull out her white work-box and that scarlet-speckled handkerchief she was stitching to give him as a keepsake before he left. It needed a score of stitches every half hour, and she worked at it with the utmost, unwavering gravity of a master craftsman on a royal commission.

When evening rolled around, Graham would trudge in the door home from school, and the whole house flooded with bright, crackling energy—energy that only spiked higher with the playful, half-hostile antics that always unfolded between him and little Miss Paulina.

Ever since he’d teased her that first night, she’d adopted a lofty, haughty reserve around him; whenever he spoke to her, her default answer was, “I can’t attend to you. I have other things to think about.” When he begged her to name those things, she’d lift her small chin and say, “Business.”

Graham set out to lure her attention anyway, flinging open his desk to show off its treasure trove: bright wax sticks, sharp penknives, fancy seals, and a stack of colourful engravings he’d hoarded for years. The temptation was far too strong to resist entirely; her eyes would dart up furtively from her stitching again and again, sneaking peeks at the pictures scattered across his writing table. One soft etching of a little child playing with a fluffy Blenheim spaniel fluttered right to her feet.

“Pretty little dog,” she breathed, delighted before she could catch herself.

Graham pretended not to hear. Before long, she’d slipped off her stool and crept over to get a closer look at the treasure, those great soft spaniel eyes and long floppy ears, the child’s feathered hat, too charming to pass up. “Nice picture,” she pronounced, magnanimous.

“You can have it,” Graham offered.

She hesitated. She ached to take it, but accepting would mean surrendering her hard-won dignity. No. She set it back down carefully and turned away.

“You won’t have it, Polly?”
“I would rather not, thank you.”
“Guess what I’ll do with it if you refuse.”
She half-turned, curious despite herself.
“Cut it into strips to light the taper.”
“No!”
“But I will.”
“Please—don’t.”

Graham acted inexorable the second he heard that soft pleading tone, grabbing a pair of scissors from his mother’s work basket. “Here goes!” he flourished the blades, menacing. “Right through Fido’s head, and splitting little Harry’s nose.”

“Then come to me. Come quickly, or it’s done.”

She hesitated, lingered, then complied, her small feet padding across the carpet to stand before him.
“Now will you have it?” he asked.
“Please.”
“But I want payment.”
“How much?”
“A kiss.”
“Give the picture to me first.”

Polly looked distinctly untrusting as she said that, so Graham handed it over. She grabbed it, darted away like a little thief who’d escaped the bailiffs, and scrambled to hide on her father’s knee. Graham rose in mock, dramatic wrath and chased after her. She buried her face in Mr. Home’s waistcoat, muffled. “Papa—papa—send him away!”

“I won’t be sent away,” Graham laughed.
Face still averted, she stuck out a small hand to fend him off.
“Then I’ll kiss the hand,” he said—only for that hand to curl into a tiny, tight fist, and thump him right on the mouth with a coin far from the kiss he’d asked for.

Graham, just as wily as his little playmate, retreated, acting thoroughly and dramatically wounded. He flopped onto the sofa, rested his head on the cushion, and lay there like he was in terrible pain. Polly, finding him strangely silent, soon peeked over at him. His hands were covering his eyes and his whole face. She twisted around on her father’s knee and gazed at her supposed “enemy” long and anxiously, until Graham let out a loud, dramatic groan.

“Papa, what’s the matter?” she whispered.
“You had better ask him, Polly.”
“Is he hurt?” (another groan.)
“He makes a noise as if he is,” said Mr. Home, smiling faintly.
“Mother,” Graham feebly moaned, “I think you’d better send for the doctor. Oh my eye!” (A long silence, broken only by his dramatic sighs.) “If I were to go blind—”

The little chastiser could not bear that thought for a second. She was at his side in an instant. “Let me see your eye! I didn’t mean to hit it, only your mouth, and I didn’t think I hit so very hard!”

Graham said nothing. Her face crumpled. “I’m sorry! I’m so sorry!” Then the tears came, thick and fast, her shoulders shaking as she sobbed.

“Stop tormenting that child, Graham,” Mrs. Bretton laughed.
“It’s all nonsense, my pet,” Mr. Home assured her.

Graham snatched her up off the floor before she could protest, and she punished him again, tugging at his thick, lion’s mane of hair while she called him “the naughtiest, rudest, worst, untruest person that ever lived.”

On the morning Mr. Home was set to leave, he drew his daughter aside in the window recess, and I caught snippets of their quiet conversation.
“Couldn’t I pack my box and go with you, papa?” she whispered, earnest as anything.
He shook his head.
“Am I a trouble to you?”
“Yes, Polly.”
“Because I’m little?”
“Because you’re little and tender. Only great, strong people should travel long distances. But don’t look sad, my little girl—you break my heart. Papa will come back to his Polly very soon.”
“Indeed, indeed I’m not sad, scarcely at all.”
“Polly would be sorry to give papa pain, wouldn’t she?”
“Sorrier than sorry.”
“Then Polly must be cheerful. No crying at parting, no fretting after I leave. Look forward to when we meet again, and try to be happy in the meantime. Can you do that?”
“She will try.”
“I see she will. Farewell, then. It’s time to go.”
“Now? Just now?”
“Just now.”

She lifted up her quivering lips for a kiss. Her father sobbed, but Polly did not shed a tear. When he set her down, he shook hands with the rest of us, and walked out the door. The second the street latch clicked shut behind him, she dropped to her knees by the nearest chair and cried out, soft and long: “Papa!” It was a cry so raw, so full of that quiet “why have you forsaken me?” agony that no child her age should have known. For long minutes she endured that pain, and in that short span of her young life, she felt emotions many people never experience in a lifetime—such was the depth of her nature, and I knew if she lived, there would be many more such moments for her. Nobody spoke. Mrs. Bretton, a mother, shed a tear or two. Graham, who had been writing, lifted his pen and stared at her. I, Lucy Snowe, was calm, and watched.

Left unharassed, the little creature fought that intolerable grief on her own, no coddling from others needed, and soon managed to push it down to a bearable ache. That day she would accept no comfort from anyone, nor the next. Slowly, after that, she grew more passive, more quiet.

On the third evening, she sat on the floor, worn thin and still, when Graham came in. He picked her up gently, not a word said. She did not resist. She nestled right into his arms, like she was exhausted from carrying all that weight alone. When he sat down, she rested her head on his chest, and within minutes she was fast asleep. He carried her upstairs to bed, and I was not at all surprised that the very next morning, the first thing she asked for when she woke was, “Where is Mr. Graham?”

Graham skipped breakfast that day, tied up writing exercises for his morning class, and asked his mother to send a cup of tea to his study. Polly volunteered to carry it—she had to be busy, had to look after someone, after all. Mrs. Bretton handed it to her, trusting her care even if she was restless. The study door faced the breakfast room across the passage, so my eye followed her every step.

“What are you doing?” she asked, pausing on the threshold.
“Writing,” Graham said.
“Why don’t you come have breakfast with your mamma?”
“Too busy.”
“Do you want any breakfast at all?”
“Of course I do.”
“Then here.” She set the tea cup down on the carpet like a jailer pushing a prisoner’s water pitcher through a cell door, then retreated. She was back a few minutes later. “What else do you want besides tea? Something to eat?”
“Anything good. Bring me something particularly nice, that’s a kind little woman.”

She trotted back to Mrs. Bretton. “Please ma’am, send your boy something good.”
“You choose for him, Polly. What should my boy have?”

She picked out every nicest thing on the table, then came back to whisper a request for marmalade that wasn’t already set out. Mrs. Bretton indulged every last one of them, and soon we heard Graham praising her to the skies, promising that when he had a house of his own, she could be his housekeeper, and maybe—if she showed enough culinary talent—his cook. When she didn’t come back, I went to check, and found them breakfasting tête-à-tête: she stood at his elbow, sharing his fare, except for the marmalade, which she refused to touch, no doubt determined no one would ever think she’d fetched it for her own benefit, not his. She was always full of those sharp, delicate, lovely little instincts.

Their friendship, once forged, never frayed. The more time passed, the tighter their bond grew, even though they were worlds apart in age, sex, and pursuits. Paulina’s true character only ever came out when Graham was around. With Mrs. Bretton, she was docile enough, sitting for hours on a stool at her feet learning her lessons, sewing, or scribbling figures on a slate, never once sparking to the wild, bright originality that made her special. I soon stopped watching her when she was quiet like that—she was no fun. But the second Graham’s knock sounded on the door in the evening, she was instantly at the top of the staircase, waiting, her first greeting usually a scolding or a threat.

“You didn’t wipe your shoes properly on the mat! I’m going to tell your mamma.”
“Little busybody! Are you up there?”
“Yes—and you can’t reach me. I’m higher up than you.” She peeked through the banister rails, too small to see over the top.
“Polly!”
“My dear boy!” she’d mimicked Mrs. Bretton, one of her favourite pet names for him.
“I’m fit to faint with fatigue,” Graham would declare, leaning against the passage wall, pretending to be all but dead. “Dr. Digby worked me half to death at school today. Come down and help me carry my books upstairs.”
“Ah! You’re cunning!”
“Not at all. I’m weak as a rush. Come down.”
“Your eyes are as quiet as a cat’s, but you’re going to spring on me.”
“Spring? I don’t have the strength. Come down.”
“Maybe I will—if you promise not to touch me, not to snatch me up, not to whirl me around.”
“Me? I couldn’t lift a finger!” He sank into a chair, dramatic.
“Then put the books on the first step, and go three yards away.”

Of course, the second she descended warily, never taking her eyes off him for a second, he’d spring to life, and the romping would start. Sometimes she’d pretend to be cross, sometimes she’d let it slide, leading him up the stairs by the hand, saying soft and motherly, “Now, my dear boy, come and have your tea. I know you must be starving.”

It was endlessly comical to watch her sit beside him while he ate. When he was gone, she was a quiet, reserved little thing, but with him, she was the most fussy, officious little caretaker in the whole house. I often wished she’d sit still and mind her own business, but no—she forgot herself entirely around him. He was treated better than the Grand Turk in her eyes. She’d assemble every plate within his reach, and just when you thought he had everything he could possibly want, she’d remember something else, whispering to Mrs. Bretton, “Ma’am, maybe your son would like a little sweet cake, you know the one in the sideboard cupboard.” Mrs. Bretton usually disapproved of sweet cake at tea, but Polly would beg, “Just one tiny piece, only for him. Girls like me and Miss Snowe don’t need treats, but he’d like it.”

Graham loved that cake, and he almost always got it. To do him justice, he’d try to share it with her, but she would never, ever accept. Even trying to force it on her would ruffle her mood for the whole evening. Standing by his knee, monopolizing his attention, was the only reward she wanted—not a bite of cake.

She adapted to every one of his interests faster than light. You’d think the child had no mind, no life of her own, after her father left—she nestled to Graham, felt his feelings, existed in his existence. She learned all his schoolfellows’ names in a trice, memorized every one of their personalities as he described them, never mixed a single one up. She could mimic the strict under-master Graham hated perfectly, after seeing him only once, and act him out for Graham’s amusement—till Mrs. Bretton found out and forbade it.

They almost never fought, but once, a sharp rupture left her feelings badly bruised. It was Graham’s birthday, and he’d invited a group of his school friends, boys his own age, over for dinner. Paulina had heard him talk about these friends for ages, and waited for their arrival with bated breath. After dinner, the boys shut themselves in the dining room, laughing loudly and making a racket. I found Polly sitting alone on the lowest step of the staircase, staring at the glossy dining room door panel, her little brow furrowed in deep thought.

“What are you thinking about, Polly?”
“Nothing much. I just wish that door was all clear glass, so I could see through it. They sound like they’re having so much fun. I want to go to them. I want to be with Graham, and meet his friends.”
“What’s stopping you?”
“I’m scared. But do you think I can try? Knock on the door and ask to be let in?”

I thought the boys wouldn’t mind her as a playmate, so I encouraged her. She knocked, softly at first, no one heard, then a little harder. The door swung open, Graham’s bright, flushed face peeking out, giddy and impatient.

“What do you want, you little monkey?”
“To come in with you.”
“Do you indeed? As if I’d be troubled by you! Go back to mamma and Miss Snowe, and tell them to put you to bed.” He vanished, and the door slammed shut hard. She stood there, stunned.

“Why did he talk like that? He never talked like that before,” she said, horrified. “What did I do?”
“Nothing, Polly. Graham is just busy with his friends.”
“And he likes them better than me! He’s turning me away now they’re here!”

I was about to comfort her, pull out a few of my usual philosophical maxims to soothe her, but she stuck her fingers in her ears the second I spoke, then laid face down on the hall mat, pressed against the cold stone flags. Neither the cook nor the maid could get her to move, so we left her there till she chose to get up on her own.

Graham forgot his impatience that same evening. When his friends left, he tried to greet her as usual, but she wrenched herself out of his hand, her eyes flashing, refused to say goodnight, refused to even look at his face. The next day he treated her with cold indifference, and she turned as still and hard as marble. The day after that, he teased her to tell him what was wrong, and her lips stayed sealed. He couldn’t stay angry for long, he soon softened and coaxed her. “Why are you so upset? What did I do?” Before long the tears came, he petted her, and they made up. But she never forgot that snub. I never saw her chase after him again, or follow him, or beg for his notice. Once I told her to take a book to Graham in his study. “I’ll wait till he comes out,” she said, proud as a little queen. “I won’t make him get up and open the door for me.”

Graham had a favourite pony he loved to ride, and Polly always watched him leave and come back from the window. She ached to be allowed a quick ride around the courtyard, but she would never, ever ask. One day she went down to

I left Bretton a few short weeks after little Paulina’s departure, never once guessing as I turned my back on its sun-warmed cobblestone lanes and the familiar glow of Mrs. Bretton’s parlour that I would never set foot there again. Six months away from my childhood home had stretched thin, and by the time I walked up the path to my family’s doorstep, the world I returned to was hollowed out, all the old certainties already washed away.

Readers might well picture me drifting through the next eight years wrapped in soft, unbroken calm: a small boat bobbing on glass-still harbour water, the steersman asleep on the deck, no sharp edges or storms to break the peace. So many young women are supposed to glide through life like that, untroubled, sheltered – why not me? I could let you imagine me lazing in the sun, warm, well-fed, safe, and no one would have any reason to contradict that pretty picture. But I cannot hide the truth forever. That quiet harbour was never mine. I still wake from nightmares gasping, choking on the briny salt of waves that drag me under, the cold, heavy pressure of water filling my lungs. The storm that wrecked my life raged for days and nights on end: no sun, no stars to steer by, every last bit of tackle thrown overboard to keep the ship afloat, until every shred of hope of rescue was gone. The ship sank. Everyone I loved went down with it.

I never spoke of that ruin to anyone. There was no one left to tell. I’d lost touch with Mrs. Bretton years before, obstacles other people had put between us cutting our correspondence clean off. I’d heard rumours, too: the fortune she’d held in trust for Graham, invested in a joint-stock venture, had all but vanished. Graham had qualified for his profession, and both he and his mother had left Bretton for London, no one knew exactly where. There was no one left I could lean on. Circumstances forced self-reliance out of me, same as it does thousands of people who have no other choice. So when Miss Marchmont, the wealthy, famously eccentric maiden lady who lived in the big house on the edge of our village, sent for me, I went at once, hoping she might have some work I could do.

Miss Marchmont had been a cripple from rheumatoid arthritis for twenty long years, her hands and feet all but useless, confined entirely to the upstairs floor where her sitting room opened straight onto her bedroom. She was furrowed-faced, grey-haired, sharp-edged from decades of solitude and constant unyielding pain, known far and wide for being irritable and exacting. Her lady’s companion of many years was about to marry, and she’d heard I was now orphaned and adrift, and thought I might fill that empty spot. She laid the offer out plain to me after tea, as we sat by her crackling fire, alone.
“It won’t be easy,” she said, no softening of the truth. “I need constant attention, you’ll hardly ever get to leave the house. But it might be better than the aimless life you’ve been leading lately.”
I hesitated. Logically, of course it should have been more than acceptable – but the thought of shutting myself up in those two small rooms, watching over a suffering woman for what felt like the rest of my youth, the years I’d already lost to grief weighing heavy on my chest for a heartbeat. Then I steadied myself. I’d never been one to romanticize hardship, to blow it all out of proportion, no matter how much it hurt.
“I’m only not sure I’m strong enough for it,” I told her.
“That’s my worry too,” she said bluntly. “You look half-dead, girl.”
She was right. I glanced in the mirror hung over her mantel, saw my own reflection in black mourning dress: pale, hollow-eyed, faded. But the blight was only on the surface, I thought. Deep down, I still felt the spark of life. When I told her I had no other clear plans yet, but might find work somewhere else, she was kinder than I expected. “Go try what you want,” she said. “I’ll keep this spot open for you for three months, just in case.”
Before I could thank her, a paroxysm of pain wracked her. I moved fast, following her directions to apply the right liniments, adjust her pillows, until the worst of it ebbed. In that small, urgent moment, a quiet sort of intimacy bloomed between us. I saw how firm and patient she was, even through agony, and she saw that my care for her was not just a paid duty, but genuine sympathy. She sent for me again the next morning, and the one after that, every day for a week. The more I knew her, the more her sharp edges softened into something I could respect: her outbursts of temper were never cruel, never humiliating, more like a grumpy, overprotective mother scolding a daughter than a harsh mistress lording over a servant. Even at her fiercest, she was always fair, always logical. By the end of that week, I’d agreed to stay.

My whole world narrowed down to those two warm, steam-fogged rooms. My days revolved entirely around her: her pain was my worry, her relief my small daily joy, her rare sharp scoldings my only small punishment, her quiet praise my greatest reward. I almost forgot there were fields outside the frosted windows, rivers, wide stretches of sea, a sky that changed colour with every dawn. I was almost happy to forget. I’d already been tamed by fate, softened by loss, and I found so much to love in her: the stubborn strength of her virtues, the depth of the passions she’d kept locked inside for thirty years, the raw honesty of every word she spoke. I would have stayed by her side, caring for her, for twenty more years if fate had let me. But fate had other plans. It wouldn’t let me hide away in that small, safe duty forever. The small, precious scrap of affection I held onto, that I valued like a solid pearl, was going to slip out of my fingers like melting hail.

It was a cold February night. After a winter that had been strangely still and mild, storms were howling to usher in spring. I’d helped Miss Marchmont into bed, and sat by the fire sewing, when the wind picked up outside, wailing at the windowpanes. All day it had moaned, but as midnight drew near, it gained a new, sharp, almost human voice – a plaint so raw and grief-stricken it made my nerves ache. I dropped my sewing, pressing my hands over my ears uselessly, because I’d heard that exact wind before. Three times in my life, that same keening east wind had blown before an outbreak of epidemic, before distant earthquakes, before rivers burst their banks and high tides swallowed the coastlines far away. I trembled, but Miss Marchmont slept peacefully.

Half an hour before midnight, the storm fell completely silent. The fire that had been smouldering flared bright, the air turned sharp and frosty. I pulled back the curtain, saw the stars glinting hard and bright over a world crusted with frost. When I turned back, Miss Marchmont was awake, propping herself up on one elbow, looking at me with a light I’d never seen in her eyes before.
“Is it clear out?” she asked. I nodded.
“I thought so,” she said, her voice soft, almost giddy. “I feel so strong tonight. So well. Raise me up, will you? I feel young again. Young, light, happy. What if this illness of mine is finally breaking? What if I get my health back? It would be a miracle.”
I said nothing. Miracles didn’t happen to people like us, I thought. She didn’t notice, though, she was already drifting back through her memories, her face soft with a glow I’d never seen there.
“Memory is my best friend tonight,” she said. “She’s bringing everything back to me, not as faded ghosts, but as real as they were thirty years ago. I can feel the joy of that one perfect year I had with Frank, the only love of my life. He was good, Lucy – noble, faithful, so much better than I was. We had one whole year of perfect, unclouded happiness. I loved him more than I’ve loved anything else in my life. Why did it get taken away? Why was I sentenced to thirty years of grief, after just twelve months of bliss?” She paused, her voice softening. “I don’t know why. But tonight, for the first time in thirty years, I can say: Thy will be done, God. And I truly believe I’ll see Frank again when I die. I never dared believe that before.”
“Frank is dead?” I whispered.
“It happened on Christmas Eve,” she said, and her voice was gentle, no sharp edge of pain left to it. “I was all dressed up, waiting for him to come visit me before our wedding. I sat by the fire, watching the snow fall outside the window, waiting to see him ride up the path. The moon was full, silver over the shrubs, over the snow on the lawn. I waited, a little impatient, but never a single doubt in my heart. I thought I heard his horse’s hooves thudding fast on the drive, and I laughed to myself, thinking you reckless thing, Frank, riding that hard in the snow, you’ll hurt yourself. Then I saw it: not Frank on his horse, but the horse itself, trembling, covered in sweat, being led up to the door. I ran out into the snow, and there he was, lying on the ground. He’d fallen, hit his head. He wasn’t dead yet. I dragged him into the house, wouldn’t let the servants push me away from him. The surgeon did everything he could, but there was nothing to be done. He had just enough strength to hold me, whisper my name, say he was dying in Paradise, with me. He breathed his last when the first pink light of Christmas morning broke over the snow. That was thirty years ago.”
She wiped a tear away, smiling a little. “I’ve been a bitter, selfish woman ever since. The pain just ate at me. But tonight, I think I’m finally ready. Tomorrow, I’ll start making things better for you, Lucy. I’ll leave something for you, so you’ll be taken care of long after I’m gone. My head aches now, talking so much. Go to bed, child. The clock’s striking two. Don’t worry about me. I’m going to sleep well tonight.”

I went to my small cot in the little closet connected to her bedroom. The night passed completely quiet. When I woke in the morning, she was still, no breath left in her, almost cold, her face soft and calm, no trace of the pain that had gnawed at her for thirty long years. That sudden flood of joy, that clear, soft peace, had been the last flicker of her life. One gentle stroke had cut the thread that had been fraying for decades, setting her free at last, to go meet her Frank.

My old mistress was gone, and once more I found myself entirely alone, with no choice but to hunt for a new position before the week ran out. I will not pretend my nerves were steady in those hollow, grief-soft days. I looked far from well: thin, haggard, my eyes hollow from all the late hours I had spent sitting at Miss Marchmont’s sickbed, so much so that a stranger might have taken me for an overwrought drudge or a desperate soul running from debt. But I owed no one a penny, nor was I fully destitute. Though Miss Marchmont had not lived long enough to bestow the generous legacy she had promised me on her final night, her heir—her pinched-nosed, narrow-templed, miserly second cousin—paid my wages in full after the funeral, leaving me with fifteen pounds tucked safe in my pocket, a body worn but not broken, and a spirit frayed but far from defeated. Beside so many who had far less, I was in a fortunate position; but it was also an unbearably awkward one, as I was all too keenly aware when I realized I had no new home lined up for the day I was due to leave my current lodgings.

In that tight dilemma, I turned to the only resource I had left: an old family servant, once my childhood nurse, who now worked as housekeeper at a grand mansion not far from Miss Marchmont’s home. I spent a few hours with her, and she comforted me as best she could, but she had no solid advice to offer. My mind still wrapped in unbroken darkness, I left her at twilight, faced with a two-mile walk home on a clear, frosty night. For all my solitude, my small purse, and my tangled worries, my heart—beating with the unspent vigour of twenty-two years, not yet even touching twenty-three—was light, not feeble. If my courage had faltered, I would have quailed on that lonely path, winding through silent fields with not a village, farmhouse, or cottage in sight, guided only by starlight with no moon to light my way. I would have been terrified by the strange, shifting mystery glowing in the northern sky: the Aurora Borealis. But that solemn, otherworldly stranger did not stir fear in me. It seemed to pour new strength straight into my bones, and I drew in that sharp, crisp breeze like it was made of pure energy. A bold thought landed soft and steady in my mind, and I found I had the strength to hold it. “Leave this narrow wilderness,” the thought whispered, “and go out into the wider world.” “Where?” I asked the dark, and before I could even wonder, I pictured it, vivid as a memory I had carried all my life even though I had never seen it with my own eyes: London. The great, sprawling, humming city, waiting fifty miles away.

The very next day I walked back to the grand hall to find Mrs. Barrett, and laid my plan before her. She was a grave, judicious woman, who knew barely more of the world than I did, but she did not call me mad. I had always carried myself with a quiet, staid composure that had long served me like a warm, unassuming cloak, letting me do things that would have made others brand me a silly dreamer if I had spoken of them with wild, excited airs. She was slowly pointing out possible hurdles as she prepared orange rind to make marmalade, when a small boy came bounding laughing through the window, running straight to me—we were not strangers, for his mother, the young married daughter of the house, had been my old schoolfellow. I lifted the pretty dark-eyed child onto my knee, and in walked his mother, Mrs. Leigh. The plain, dull girl I had known at school had bloomed into a soft, beautiful, kind-looking woman, transformed by wifehood and motherhood. She did not recognize me, changed as I was, and I made no move to remind her. She had come to fetch her son for a walk, with a foreign nurse carrying a new infant trailing behind her. I noticed she spoke to the nurse in French—very imperfect French, with a thick, unruly accent that took me straight back to our schooldays—and the little boy chattered away volubly in the same foreign tongue.

When they had left, Mrs. Barrett told me the foreign nurse had come back with the family two years prior from a trip to the Continent, was treated almost as well as a governess, and had no duties beyond walking with the children and speaking to them in French. “And she says there are dozens of young Englishwomen on the Continent placed just as well as she is, in foreign households,” Mrs. Barrett added. I tucked that little scrap of casual knowledge away in my mind, the way a careful housewife tucks away a seemingly useless scrap of fabric, knowing one day it might come in useful. Before I left, she gave me the address of a respectable, old-fashioned inn in the City of London, the very one my own uncles had stayed at in years gone by.

The trip to London was far less reckless than the reader might imagine. It was only fifty miles away. My fifteen pounds would pay for the coach fare, keep me a few days in the city, and even pay my way home if I found no reason to stay. I treated it like a tiny, rare holiday for my overworked, weary mind, not some life-or-death adventure. There is no better way to keep your mind and body calm than to take every step in moderation, rather than wrapping it up in grand, dramatic notions that only stir up feverish excitement. This was long before the railways made the journey swift: fifty miles took a full day of travel, and when I climbed down from the coach at nine o’clock on a wet February night, I had stepped straight into the vast, roaring wilderness of London, for the very first time.

I had no time for poetic first impressions, even if I had been inclined to cherish them. The cabmen’s rough, unfamiliar local accents sounded as strange to my country ears as a foreign language. Still, I managed to make myself understood, and got myself and my small trunk safely to the old inn. It felt overwhelming, oppressive, utterly disorienting: first time in the sprawling Babylon of London, first time staying in an inn, travel-weary, shivering with cold, with no experience to guide me and no choice but to figure out what to do next. I handed the problem over to my common sense, which was just as chilled and bewildered as the rest of my wits, but spurred on by sharp, unignorable necessity, it got to work. I paid the porter (and yes, he cheated me horribly, but I was too new and too tired to protest), asked the waiter for a room, called for the chambermaid, and bore the young lady’s supercilious, town-smart attitude without breaking down. She was all trim, crisp waist, neat cap, and mincing glib speech, her pretty, polished town manners making my plain country dress feel shabby and out of place. But I kept my quiet composure, and before long, even the snooty staff treated me with careful politeness, swinging between faint patronage and proper respect as they realized I was no common household servant.

I held myself together perfectly until I had eaten a little refreshment, warmed myself by the fire, and locked myself alone in my small room. But the second I sat down on the edge of the bed and rested my head on my arms against the pillow, a terrible, heavy oppression crashed over me. My whole situation rose up before me like a ghost: anomalous, desolate, almost bare of hope. What was I doing here, all alone in the greatest city in the land? What would I do tomorrow? What prospects did I even have? What friends did I have in the whole world? I soaked the pillow, my sleeves, and my hair with hot, rushing tears. That dark, bitter wave passed, though, and I never once regretted the step I had taken, never wanted to turn back. A strong, quiet certainty settled over me: it was far better to go forward than to turn back, that I could go forward, that some narrow, difficult path would eventually open up for me, no matter how impossible it seemed right now. That quiet hope hushed all my fears, until I was calm enough to say my prayers and climb into bed.

I had just blown out my candle and laid down to sleep, when a deep, low, mighty tone swung through the hush of the night. I did not know it at first, but it boomed twelve times, and by the twelfth deep, thrumming knell, I knew exactly where I lay: in the long, warm, waiting shadow of St. Paul’s Cathedral, with the whole of my unwritten new life stretched out before me.

The first of March dawned soft and smudged with fog, and when I woke, rose, and drew back my bed curtains, I found the risen sun fighting hard to pierce the mist. Above my head, above the gabled house-tops, nearly level with the low-hanging clouds, hung a solemn, rounded mass of dim dark blue: THE DOME of St Paul’s. As I stared, something long dormant in my chest shifted; the spirit that had dragged its fettered wings for years half-loosened them, and a sudden, bright feeling swept over me— that I, who had never truly lived a life of my own, was finally, finally about to taste it. My soul grew that morning as fast as Jonah’s gourd, green, sudden, and bursting with new possibility.

“I did well to come,” I murmured, dressing quickly and with more care than I had brought to my appearance in years. “I love the pulse of this great London thrumming all around me. What coward would waste their whole existence in tiny hamlets, letting their wits and wills rust away in obscurity?”

When I descended the stairs, I was not travel-worn or haggard, but tidy, rested, and light-footed. When the grey-haired elderly waiter brought my breakfast, I spoke to him calmly, cheerfully, and we talked for ten minutes that left us usefully well-acquainted. He had worked at the inn for twenty years, he told me, and when I mentioned the names of my uncles Charles and Wilmot, who had been frequent visitors fifteen years prior, he recalled them clearly and with great respect. Once I had shared that family connection, my standing in his eyes was settled and secure: he said I bore a strong likeness to Uncle Charles, a compliment I could not doubt, for Mrs. Barrett had often said the very same. His previously uncertain, distant manner softened into ready, obliging courtesy, and I knew I would never struggle to get a kind, prompt answer to any reasonable question I might ask.

The street outside my small sitting-room window was narrow, quiet, far from squalid, and the few passers-by moved at the same unhurried pace one might see in any provincial town. There was nothing daunting here: I felt sure I could venture out alone without fear.

After breakfast, I stepped out, elation bubbling light in my chest. To walk unescorted through London felt like a grand adventure all its own. Before long I found myself in Paternoster Row, that storied stretch of booksellers, and stepped into Mr. Jones’s shop. I splurged on a tiny volume, a little extravagance I could barely afford, but I thought I would send it one day to Mrs. Barrett as a small token of my affection. The dried, sharp man of business behind the desk felt like one of the most important people in the world, and I felt like one of the happiest.

That morning I lived more fully than I had in all the prior years of my quiet, staid life. I reached St. Paul’s, stepped inside, and climbed all the way up to the dome, where I looked out over the whole spread of London: the silver curve of the Thames, its bridges, its spired churches, old Westminster, and the green sun-dappled stretch of Temple Gardens, all laid out under a soft early spring blue sky veiled by thin, wispy haze.

I wandered aimlessly after that, still high on freedom and joy, until I found myself right in the thrumming heart of the city. I had found real London at last: I walked the Strand, climbed Cornhill, mingled with the crowds hurrying past, dared the chaos of busy street crossings all on my own. It was an irrational, unaccountable pleasure, but a sharp, real one. In later years I would see the West End, its parks and its grand squares, but I will always love the working city far better. It is earnest, alive: its rush, its roar, its industry are not idle amusements. The city is earning its living, while the West End only plays at its pleasure. You might be entertained in the grand squares, but in the heart of the city you feel alive, stung awake by excitement.

By two o’clock I was faint and hungry— a deep, healthy hunger I had not felt in years— and I returned to my dark, old, quiet inn. I dined on a plain roast joint and vegetables, both of which tasted far better than the tiny, dainty little meals Miss Marchmont’s cook used to send up for my kind dead mistress and me, neither of whom ever had half an appetite enough to finish them. Delightfully tired, I lay back across three hard chairs, for my small room had no sofa, and slept for an hour before waking to think long and deep for two more.

Every circumstance of my life right then pushed me to take a new, resolute, daring course— perhaps even a desperate one. I had nothing to lose. My utter loathing of the empty, desolate life I had left behind forbade me from ever turning back. If I failed in the plan I was about to form, who but myself would suffer? If I died far from England, the only land I had ever called anything close to home, who would weep for me?

I was well used to hardship, I told myself. Death held none of the terrible terrors for me that it holds for the gently reared and coddled. I had long looked on the idea of death with quiet, unflinching eyes. Prepared for whatever consequences might come, I laid my plans.

That very evening, my new friend the waiter told me all he knew about ships sailing for the continental port of Boue-Marine. There was no time to waste: I must take my berth that very night. I could have waited till morning to board, but I would not risk missing the ship.

“Best go claim your berth right now, ma’am,” he advised. I agreed, settled my bill, and tipped him a sum I now know was absurdly generous, so much so that he smiled a faint, knowing little smile as he pocketed the cash, quietly judging my lack of worldly sense. He called a coach, recommended me to the driver with strict instructions not to leave me to the mercy of the rough watermen, but the man broke his promise the second we reached the wharf: he set me down right in the middle of a huddle of jostling watermen, took his fare, and drove off at once.

It was an uncomfortable, dangerous moment. The night was dark, the river wharves reeked, and the watermen began fighting each other over my trunk and over me, their oaths loud and coarse enough to shake the steady calm I had held till then. One man seized my trunk, and I waited quiet, but when another laid a hand on my arm, I spoke up sharply, shook off his grip, stepped straight into the nearest empty boat, and told the man who owned it to set my trunk down beside me on the bench. He did it at once, for I was now his fare, and he pushed off into the black water before any of the other men could protest.

The river was as dark as a torrent of ink, lights glinting off its surface from the surrounding warehouses, ships rocking soft on its breast as we glided past. We rowed past *The Ocean*, *The Phoenix*, *The Consort*, *The Dolphin*, checking each name painted in great white letters on the dark hull under the lantern light. My ship, *The Vivid*, lay further down the sable flood.

I thought of the Styx, of Charon ferrying solitary souls to the land of shades, as the cold wind bit my face and a fine midnight rain began to fall, the two rude oarsmen beside me still swearing under their breath. But I was not wretched, not terrified. I had been far more miserable, far more afraid, in far safer, cozier rooms back in England. I was only alert, bright, alive.

At last *The Vivid* loomed up, white and bright against the black night. “Here you are,” the waterman said, and instantly demanded six shillings for the fare.

“You ask far too much,” I said. He pulled back from the ship’s side and swore he would not bring me aboard till I paid. A young man I would later learn was the steward leaned over the rail, grinning in anticipation of the fight, so I paid the money at once to disappoint him. I had overpaid three times that afternoon, handing out crowns where I ought to have given shillings, but I consoled myself: this is the price of experience.

“They’ve cheated you!” the steward cackled exultantly when I climbed aboard. I told him calmly that I knew that already, and made my way below.

A stout, handsome, showy woman stood in the ladies’ cabin, and when I asked to be shown my berth, she stared hard at me, muttering that it was most unusual for a passenger to turn up in the middle of the night, clearly inclined to be unkind. Her face was comely, yes, but stamped all over with insolence and selfishness.

“I am on board now, and I am certainly staying,” I said firmly. “Please show me my berth.”

She complied, sullenly. I took off my bonnet, arranged my few belongings, and lay down. I had won a small victory, pushed through a string of small difficulties, and my homeless, unsupported mind could rest for a little while. No further action would be required of me till we docked at Boue-Marine. I dared not think past that point, and exhausted, I drifted into a half-trance.

The stewardess talked the whole night through, not to me, but to the young steward, her son, who looked exactly like her. He wandered in and out of the cabin constantly, and the pair quarrelled, made up, and quarrelled again twenty times over the course of the dark hours. She said she was writing a letter home to her father, reading snippets out loud as if I were no more than a wooden post, recounting family secrets about her younger sister Charlotte, who was on the brink of making a romantic, imprudent match she was determined to stop. Her dutiful son laughed her whole letter to scorn, and she raved at him in response. She was a strange pair, nearly forty years old but buxom and blooming as a girl of twenty, loud, vain, vulgar, hard as brass. She talked as dawn neared about the Watsons, a wealthy family of passengers she adored for the generous fees they always gave her, saying a crossing with the Watsons was as good as a small fortune for her.

At dawn everyone stirred, and by full sunrise the rest of the passengers came aboard. The stewardess greeted the Watson family with boisterous excitement, making a great fuss over the four of them: two men, two women, both young women richly and gaily dressed in velvet cloaks, silk gowns, bonnets wreathed in bright flowers far better suited for a park promenade than a damp packet ship deck. The men were short, plain, fat, and vulgar, and I soon learned the oldest, greasiest, broadest man was the husband of the beautiful young girl. I was stunned to see that instead of looking miserable trapped in such a union, she was giddy to the point of recklessness, laughing loud and bright. I thought her laughter must be the frenzy of despair, but she tripped over to me, a total stranger, holding a camp stool, and offered it to me with a light, careless smile, showing off a perfect set of white teeth. I declined politely, and she danced off again, light as a feather. The man she had married looked as much like an oil barrel as a man.

The other passenger, escorted by a gentle, languid-looking gentleman, was a pretty, fair young girl dressed in the plainest of print dresses, untrimmed straw bonnet, and large soft shawl, simple enough to suit a Quaker, but graceful on her. Before the gentleman left her to return to shore, he scanned all the passengers carefully, clearly assessing the company his ward would be left in, looked askance at the flaunting Watsons, glanced at me, and murmured something to the girl, who curled her pretty short lip slightly in my direction. It could have been me, or my plain mourning dress, that earned her contempt— likely both. A bell rang, he kissed her goodbye, and the ship set sail.

Foreigners often marvel at the daring of English parents who trust their daughters to travel alone, calling their intrepidity unladylike and improper. Whether this particular girl was suited to unchaperoned travel I could not say then, but I soon learned the dignity of solitude did not suit her at all. She paced the deck twice, staring in disdain at the loud, laughing Watsons, before she came over to speak to me.

“Do you like sea voyages?” she asked.

I told her my fondness for the sea had yet to be tested, for I had never been on a voyage before.

“Oh, how charming!” she cried. “I envy you the novelty! First impressions are always so delightful. I have crossed the Channel ten times now, I can barely remember my first voyage— I am quite blasé about the whole thing.”

I smiled, and she huffed, half testy, half frank. “Why are you laughing at me?”

“Because you are far too young to be blasé about anything,” I said. “You barely look sixteen.”

“I am seventeen!” she said, piqued. “Do you like travelling alone?”

“Bah! I don’t mind it. I never stay alone long, I always make friends. You won’t make many friends on this voyage, will you?” I said, glancing at the loud, vulgar Watsons.

“Certainly not those horrid people! They ought to be in steerage. Are you going to school?”

“No.”

“Where are you going, then?”

“I have not the faintest idea past the port of Boue-Marine,” I told her. She stared, then rambled on, introducing herself as Ginevra Fanshawe. She was on her way to a school in Villette, the capital of Labassecour, listing all the schools she had attended across Europe, gushing about how many handsome students there had been in Bonn, where every nice girl in her school had an admirer who walked the promenade to pass them and murmur “schönes mädchen.” She confessed she was shockingly uneducated, could barely read or write French or German, knew almost no history, geography, or arithmetic, could not even tell the difference between Protestantism and Catholicism, and did not care to.

“Where are you going, really?” she asked, turning back to me.

“Wherever Fate leads. I intend to earn my living wherever I can find work.”

“Earn your living?” she gasped, horrified. “Are you poor then?”

“As poor as Job,” I said. She paused for a moment, then shrugged, saying she knew what poverty was like at home, with her half-pay captain father and her nine siblings, their only support her wealthy godfather M. de Bassompierre who paid for her schooling. Her older sister Augusta had already married a much older man who had survived yellow fever in India and was rich enough to give her a carriage and a fine house, and Ginevra fully expected to marry an elderly wealthy gentleman one day too, far better than “earning a living.”

She soon admitted she would be horribly seasick the second we cleared the harbour, and tripped below to bully the rude stewardess into waiting on her hand and foot. The deck was quiet after that, and I stayed out in the sea breeze, drinking in the sharp, sweet air, watching the waves heave, the gulls glide over their crests, the white sails far off on the dark blue distance. I daydreamed of the continent stretched far ahead of me, a wide dreamland of sunlit coast, clustered towns, snow-gleaming towers, deep woods and smooth pastures, with a great arch of rainbow hope striding across the solemn dark blue sky above it. I soon had to laugh at my own fancy: daydreams are delusions of the demon, after all, and I was suddenly seized with a violent fit of seasickness that made me stagger down to the cabin.

Ginevra’s berth was right next to mine, and she tormented me the whole rough crossing, her selfish impatience and peevishness making her far harder to bear than the Watsons, who were all violently sick but stoic in their misery. I finally snapped at her to hold her tongue, and the rebuke did her good: she stopped complaining, and even seemed to like me better for it. As full dark fell the sea roughened, great waves crashing against the hull, furniture tumbling across the cabin floor till it had to be lashed down, every passenger groaning in distress. Ginevra swore she was about to die, but the stewardess laughed and told her they were nearly in port. A quarter of an hour later, the ship went still, and the voyage ended at midnight.

I was sorry to dock. My little rest was over, my hard difficulties were starting again. The cold dark air hit my face when I stepped on deck, the lights of the foreign port glimmering across the harbour like a thousand strange, unknowing eyes. Crowds of friends rushed aboard to greet the Watsons, Ginevra’s whole large family swarmed up to carry her away, and I was left alone on the deck, no one to meet me, no one to welcome me. I dared not dwell on the sharp contrast between my position and theirs.

But I had to go somewhere, and necessity could not afford to be fussy. I gave the stewardess her fee, a sum that clearly shocked her far more generous than she had expected, and asked her to direct me to a quiet, respectable inn for the night. Not only did she give me clear directions, she called a commissionaire, and told him to take good care of me— though my trunk had already been sent off to the custom-house, to be claimed later.

I woke the next morning as if a heavy, damp shroud had been peeled from my bones. No more the fog of utter weariness that had dragged me under the night before – my mind was sharp, my limbs light, and courage hummed low and steady in my chest like a well-tended hearth fire. Just as I finished lacing my scuffed boots, there came a rough, sharp tap at the door. "Come in," I called, expecting the soft-spoken chambermaid. Instead, a burly, weathered man in a linen-smudged uniform trundled over my threshold, his accent thick as porridge. "Gif me your keys, Meess." "Why, sir?" I asked, confused. "Gif!" he snapped, half-snatching the key from my loose grip. "All right! Haf your tronc soon." Turned out he was from the custom house, come to clear my poor little portmanteau – no harm done, for all his gruffness.

I wandered down the staircase slow, noticing for the first time, when not half-dead with fatigue, that my tiny, closet-like chamber with its meager chipped basin was tucked away in the garret of a grand, sprawling hotel. The steps were uncarpeted, cool white marble streaked with grey veins, the walls hung with faded gilded murals, the ceiling so high the chandeliers dangled far overhead like distant stars. I paused on every step, musing half-amused, that the hotel staff had sussed out at a single glance the exact measure of my empty purse and my negligible social standing – no grand suites, no plush parlours for the unknown drifting English governess, of course. It was a strange little quirk of fate, but I refused to let it sink my new-found lightness of spirit.

When I finally reached the vast skylight-glowing great hall, I drifted to what I learned was the coffee room. My hands shook a little as I pushed the door open – every other patron there was a man, no woman in sight, and I felt small, solitary, almost ridiculous for daring to sit alone at a tiny table by the window. But none of them stared too long; the few that glanced my way murmured the word "Anglaise" to their companions, and turned back to their newspapers. I ate my roll and drank my bitter coffee in a daze, wondering where on earth I ought to go next.

A soft little voice nudged at the back of my head, pulling up a throwaway line Miss Fanshawe had tossed off in her hurry to run off with her friends the day I left: "Do go to Madame Beck’s in Villette! She’s got a passel of little marmots she needs someone to look after, she was advertising for an English gouvernante two months back." I knew nothing of Madame Beck, not her address, not even what she looked like, but it was the only thread I had to cling to, drifting as I was in that strange foreign city. Forty miles away, they said Villette was. But what did I have to lose? Nothing at all – the game I was playing could not make me worse off than I already was, and every step forward held the chance of winning something better. I booked a seat on the slow, lumbering diligence right then.

People might call me reckless, to chase a shadow of a job across forty miles of foreign countryside on no more than a half-remembered comment, but I drank in every minute of that ride, even when the rain lashed the windows and the grey sky hung low and heavy. The road was flat, lined with slimy green canals that coiled like half-asleep snakes, bordered by straight rows of pollard willows. My heart felt light, giddy almost, as if it was basking in secret sunshine, even while a quiet, niggling anxiety lurked at the edge of my thoughts, like a tiger crouched in the underbrush, waiting for dark.

We moved far too slow, delayed by long, unnecessary stops, thick fog, and fine, stinging drizzle. By the time we trundled through the garrisoned city gate, lamplight glowing through the mist, night had fallen thick and soft, you could almost touch it. The diligence rattled over rough flinty cobblestones and pulled up at the central bureau, all the passengers scrambling off to grab their luggage. I stood back, waiting for my little portmanteau, the one I'd tied a scrap of bright green ribbon to the handle of so I could spot it at once. One by one every bag, every tin case, every cloak and umbrella and hat box was hauled down from the roof. My portmanteau was not there. Not a wisp of green ribbon anywhere. All my clothes, my little pocket-book holding the last of my savings – gone. I could not speak a word of conversational French, I could not even ask where it had gone. I touched the conductor's arm, gestured wildly, but he misunderstood, and started hauling a stranger's trunk back towards the diligence roof.
