A clear, pleasant voice cut in, first in English, then in sharp, rapid French. I nearly wept with joy to hear my own native tongue. "Sir," I turned to him at once, no care for how desperate I must look, "I cannot speak French, would you please ask that man what he did with my trunk?" He was young, tall, his face open and kind, noble without being arrogant, chivalric without being pretentious, the sort of man who could have been a prince for all his natural grace. He quizzed the conductor for me, then turned back to explain my trunk had been left behind at the last stop, Boue-Marine, it would be forwarded to the bureau the day after next. My heart sank, but then he asked if I had friends in Villette, if all my money was in the trunk. Thank goodness I'd tucked twenty francs in my pocket, safe and sound. He gave me a scrap of paper torn from his pocket book, scribbled the address of a quiet, cheap inn that spoke English, and told me the park shortcut was too dark and dangerous for a woman alone. He walked with me through the drizzle, down the deserted boulevard, through the black fog-shrouded park, his step steady ahead of me, and I never felt a flicker of fear – I would have followed that tread to the ends of the earth, I think. When we reached the far side of the park, he pointed me towards the street that led to the inn, wished me good night, and vanished back into the dark. That memory of his bright, kind eyes stayed with me like a warm cordial long after he was gone.

I hurried on, but two rough, sneering men with cigars hanging out of their mouths followed me, jeering, till I met a patrol of guards and they slunk off. By then I was lost, turned around completely, I could not find the steps the gentleman had described. I wandered breathless, my heart thudding, till I spotted a warm lamplight over the door of a tall, solid house. It was no inn. A brass plaque was nailed to the great door: *Pensionnat de Demoiselles*. Madame Beck.

I nearly staggered. That was the name Miss Fanshawe had mentioned. Before I could think, before I could hesitate, I rang the bell. The maid who answered thought I was the English teacher Madame had been expecting, so she let me right in, no questions, and sat me in the cold gilded salon where the porcelain stove was unlit, the pendule on the mantel striking nine. Fifteen minutes later, a plump, motherly little woman in a large shawl and neat nightcap crept up beside me – that was Madame Beck. She could barely speak two words of English, I could barely speak a scrap of useful French, so she fetched a teacher who'd been educated in an Irish convent, who butchered the English language but could make herself understood. I told her my plain tale: I was an Englishwoman looking for honest work, I could look after children, do lady's maid work, even light housework, I had no references, no luggage yet, but I would work hard to earn my keep.

Madame Beck studied me, no trace of pity or softness on her sharp, practical face. She was about to send me away and tell me to come back the next day, when a quick footstep sounded in the vestibule. That was Monsieur Paul, her cousin, the gruff little man in spectacles who came to give readings to the older students. She called him in, asked him to read my face, to tell her if I could be trusted. He stared at me, brow furrowed behind his glasses, squinting hard like he was trying to see right through my skin. "Engage her," he pronounced at last. "If she is good, it will be good for all. If not – well, it will still be a good work, to help a stranger in need."

And that was how, by the grace of some kind fate, I was hired that very night. I did not have to step back out into the cold, dark, hostile streets of Villette. I was where I was meant to be, at last.

Handed over to the matron, I was led down a long, narrow passage into a foreign kitchen so spotless it felt almost alien. No obvious fireplace, no oven met my eye; I did not yet know that the great hulking black furnace crammed into one corner did the work of both. I told myself the quiet flicker of relief I felt when we did not stop there, as I’d half feared, had nothing to do with foolish pride. We pushed on to a tiny inner room called a “cabinet”, where a cook in a short petticoat and wooden sabots brought my supper: meat of an unknown sort served in a sharp, odd but pleasant sauce, savoury chopped potatoes I suspected were tossed with vinegar and sugar, a buttered slice of bread called a tartine, and a warm baked pear. I was ravenous, so I ate every bite, and felt small, unnameable gratitude.

After evening prayers, Madame herself came to look in on me once more, and bade me follow her up the stairs. We wound through a warren of peculiar little dormitories, rooms I later learned had once been nuns’ cells, for half the house dated back centuries, then through the oratory: a long, low, gloomy space where a pale crucifix hung on the wall and two tapers burned dim, keeping lonesome vigil. She led me to a room where three small children slept in three tiny beds. A overheated stove made the air thick and oppressive, and it was scented with an unexpected, heady fume: smoke tangled with the sharp tang of spirits, unmistakably the smell of whisky.

Beside a table where the last of a candle guttered down in its socket sat a coarse woman, fast asleep, clad in the strangest mismatched outfit: a showy, broad-striped silk gown over a plain linen apron. At her elbow, a bottle and empty glass completed the scene. Madame Beck regarded this tableau with perfect calm. No smile tugged at her lips, no scowl knotted her brow; no flicker of anger, disgust, or surprise ruffled the smooth, grave serenity of her face. She did not even wake the woman. Serenely she pointed to a fourth small bed, my assigned place, then blew out the dying candle, replaced it with a soft night-light, and glided through a half-ajar inner door that led to her own large, richly furnished bedchamber.

My prayers that night were all thanksgiving. I had been guided through strange turns since dawn, provided for in ways I had never dared hope for. It scarcely felt possible that less than forty-eight hours had passed since I left London, with no more protection than that which guards a lone migrating bird, no more promise than the faint, wispy tracery of hope in the clouds.

I was a light sleeper, and woke abruptly in the dead of night. The house was utterly still, but a white figure stood by my bed: Madame, in her nightgown, moving with no sound at all. She checked on each of her three sleeping children in turn, then turned to me. I feigned deep sleep, and she studied my face for a long, curious quarter of an hour, sitting on the edge of my mattress. She leaned closer, pushed back the edge of my nightcap to expose my hair, then picked up my hand where it lay on the bedclothes and turned it over, examining the lines of my palm. Next she turned to the chair at the foot of the bed where my clothes were laid. I opened my eyes very carefully, curious to see just how far her urge to research would take her. It took her quite far: she inspected every single article I owned, no doubt judging my station, my means, my habits of neatness from every thread. She turned my dress pocket completely inside out, counted the coins in my purse, opened my little memorandum book and read every page, and plucked the small plaited lock of Miss Marchmont’s grey hair from between its leaves. She paid special attention to my three keys, for my trunk, desk, and work-box, and stepped back into her own room for a moment with them. I raised my head softly to watch, and saw her press each key into a cake of wax on her dressing table to make a perfect impression. When she had finished every last detail of her search, she folded all my clothes back perfectly, tucked every item back where she found it. Not a single line on her stone face revealed what conclusions she had drawn.

Satisfied her duty was done — and it was clear, to her, this quiet intrusion was a duty — she rose as noiseless as a shadow, and turned back toward her own room. At the door, she paused to glance at the still snoring, whisky-soaked governess, and I read her quiet, immutable resolve at once. Madame did not rush to punish shortcomings, but her retribution was always sure. Nothing here was English, I thought to myself. I was truly in a foreign land.

The next day I learned the full story of Mrs Sweeny. She had presented herself to Madame as an English lady of reduced circumstances, a native of Middlesex who claimed to speak perfect metropolitan London English. Madame, unafraid to hire staff off the strength of her own well-tested tricks for digging up the truth in time, had taken her on as nursery governess to her three children. It soon turned out she was Irish, her native brogue peeking through her forced mincing cockney lilt, and while she claimed to have raised the son and daughter of a marquis, I suspected she had once been a nurse, foster-sister, or even a laundress in some grand Irish family. Her wardrobe was full of ill-fitting, expensive cast-off silk gowns and lace-trimmed caps, but the greatest prize of all, the thing that kept her from being dismissed immediately for a whole month, was a real Indian cashmere shawl — un véritable cachemire, as Madame called it, with unfeigned awe and reverence. Without that shawl, no one would have tolerated her for two days.

The second Mrs Sweeny realised I had been brought in to replace her, she erupted, raging at Madame first, then turning all her fury on me. Madame bore the outburst with such stoic composure that I could not help but match her calm. She stepped out of the room for just ten minutes, and returned with a policeman. Mrs Sweeny and all her belongings were marched straight out of the house. Madame’s brow had not once furrowed, not one sharp word had crossed her lips. The entire affair was settled before breakfast: the mutineer expelled, the nursery fumigated and scrubbed, windows thrown wide, every last trace of that fatal whisky scent scoured from the Rue Fossette house — all of it done in the small stretch of time between Madame emerging from her room like the dawn, and her sitting down to pour her first cup of coffee.

Around noon, I was called to help dress Madame. My post, it seemed, was a hybrid between nursery governess and lady’s maid. Till that hour, she had wandered the house in her wrapping gown, shawl, and noiseless soft slippers, a custom no strict English school principal would ever have countenanced. I fumbled awkwardly with her thick, auburn hair, untouched by grey even at forty. Seeing my embarrassment, she laughed gently, took the brush from my hand, and arranged it herself, half guiding, half helping me through the rest of her toilette without a spark of temper. That was the first and last time I was ever asked to dress her; that duty was passed off to Rosine the portress from then on.

Fully dressed, Madame Beck was a short, stout woman, yet graceful in her own way, the grace that comes from perfectly proportioned limbs. Her complexion was fresh and warm, not too ruddy, her blue eyes serene. Her dark silk dress fit her as no English seamstress could have managed, stitched to absolute perfection by a local dressmaker. She looked the very picture of a respectable French bourgeoise, a quiet harmony radiating from her whole person — yet her face held sharp contrasts. Her features were stern, her forehead high but narrow, speaking of capability and a quiet sort of benevolence, but no breadth of mind, no softness. Her peaceful, watchful eyes never held the fire that burns in the heart, nor the tenderness that flows from it. Her mouth was hard, thin-lipped, capable of a grim little line. I knew at once that for all the soft sentimentality and rash passion of the tender-hearted, Madame would make a perfect Minos in petticoats.

In time I learned she was far more complex than that. Her full name was Modeste Maria Beck, née Kint, but it might as well have been Ignacia, for all her unyielding purpose. She was genuinely charitable, and did a great deal of quiet good. No mistress ever ruled a school with a lighter apparent hand. She had never once remonstrated with the drunken, neglectful Mrs Sweeny, but the woman had been shown the door the very second her departure became convenient. No teacher or master was ever openly scolded, yet staff vanished quietly every few months, no one quite able to explain how or why. Her establishment held both boarders and day pupils: more than a hundred day girls, and roughly twenty boarders. Madame ran all of them, plus four teachers, eight masters, six servants, and her own three children, while managing every pupil’s parents and friends to absolute perfection — and she did it all with no visible effort, no bustle, no fatigue, no overexcitement. She was always occupied, but never rushed. Her system ran on two unspoken watchwords: surveillance, espionage. I had already seen a perfect example of that on the night she turned my pockets inside out and read my private notes.

Yet Madame knew what honesty was, and admired it — so long as its inconvenient scruples did not get in the way of her will or her interests. She held a deep respect for England, and for Englishwomen above all other nationalities, and would hire no one else to care for her own children if she could help it.

Often in the evenings, after a long day of plotting, counterplotting, and receiving reports from her network of spies, she would come up to the nursery, a faint trace of real weariness on her brow, and sit and watch while her children said their English prayers at my knee — the Lord’s Prayer, and the little hymn beginning “Gentle Jesus”, allowed even for these two small Catholic girls. When I had tucked them into bed, she would talk with me, and I soon gained enough French to follow and even answer her, about England, about the superior intelligence and genuine, reliable probity she swore all Englishwomen held. She made a great deal of sound, sensible points, even admitting that keeping girls locked in blind ignorance, unblinkingly monitored every second of their days with no private corner to call their own, was no way to raise honest, modest young women — but continental children, she insisted, had never known any other restraint. Give them the smallest measure of unmonitored freedom, and they would misunderstand it, push it too far, reap ruin for themselves. She claimed to hate the tricks she was forced to play, but she had no choice. Then she would slip away on her noiseless slippers, gliding like a ghost through the dark house, peeking through keyholes, listening behind every door.

For all that, her system was far from bad, and I will do her justice. Her arrangements for the physical wellbeing of her pupils were perfect. No minds were overtasked; lessons were evenly distributed, made wonderfully easy for the girls to master. They had endless time for play and exercise, so every girl was healthy and bright, no pale, puny faces anywhere to be seen in the Rue Fossette house. Food was abundant, fresh, and good. She never grudged a holiday, she allowed plenty of time for sleep, dressing, washing, and meals. Many an austere English schoolmistress would do very well to copy her methods, and I believe many would, if exacting English parents would only permit it.

She ruled through espionage, of course, and her team of spies were tools she used exactly as she saw fit: she did not hesitate to use the dirtiest, roughest instruments for the dirtiest jobs, tossing them aside like orange rind the second their use was spent. But for clean, honest work, she was fastidious about her staff, wrapping her most reliable, loyal workers in cotton wool to preserve them. Woe to anyone who trusted her even an inch past the point where their interests aligned with her own, though. Personal interest was the master key to every part of her nature, the mainspring of all her motives, the alpha and omega of her life. I saw people appeal again and again to her feelings, and I smiled in half-pity, half-scorn. No one ever won her favour that way. To try to touch her heart was to rouse her secret enmity at once, for it reminded her she had no heart to be touched, no soft spot, no vulnerable place. The line between charity and mercy was never more clearly drawn than in her. She was devoid of all personal sympathy, but full of rational, broad benevolence. She gave freely to funds for the poor at large, but kept her purse firmly shut against any individual man who stood before her in private sorrow. No private agony, no matter how sharp, could pierce her. Not the agony in Gethsemane, not the death on Calvary, could have wrung a single tear from her eyes.

She was a great, immensely capable woman. Running a girls’ school was far too small a sphere for her powers. She ought to have swayed a nation, led a turbulent legislative assembly. No one could have browbeaten her, no one fray her nerves, exhaust her patience, or outwit her shrewdness. She could have singlehandedly filled the roles of prime minister and head of police. Wise, firm, faithless; secret, crafty, passionless; watchful and inscrutable; acute and insensate — and perfectly decorous, at all times.

The sensible reader will not imagine I learned all this in a month, or even six months. At first, all I saw was the bright, thriving surface of the flourishing establishment. A great house full of healthy, lively girls, well-dressed, many of them handsome, learning without painful exertion, no useless waste of their spirits. They did not make blindingly fast progress at their lessons, but they were never oppressed, always occupied. The teachers and masters did the heavy head-work, rotated quickly through the hardest tasks so no one was burnt out. It was a foreign school, full of movement, variety, and charm, a world away from the strict, dour English institutions I had known.

Behind the house stretched a large garden, where in summer the pupils almost lived outside, wandering between rose bushes and fruit trees. On summer afternoons, Madame would sit under the vast vine-draped arbor, calling the classes to her in turns to sit around her and sew and read. Masters came and went, delivering short, lively lectures rather than dry lessons, and the girls could take notes or not, as they pleased, safe in the knowledge they could copy a friend’s work if they slacked. Beside the regular monthly permitted outings, Catholic feast days brought a constant string of holidays all year round, and on bright summer mornings or soft warm evenings, the boarders would be taken on long walks into the countryside, treated to warm waffles and light white wine, or fresh milk and wholemeal bread, or buttered rolls and coffee. It all seemed perfectly idyllic. Madame looked like the very embodiment of goodness, the teachers far better than I had any right to expect, the pupils a little loud and rough, but brimming over with health and glee.

That was how the view looked, seen through the soft haze of distance. But soon the haze would melt, I would be called down from my little nursery watchtower, and forced into far closer contact with this tiny world of the Rue Fossette.

One day I sat up in the nursery, hearing Madame’s children their English lessons, and turning a silk dress for her, when she wandered into the room, that distant, hard, thoughtful look on her face that made her seem far from genial. She dropped into a seat opposite me, silent for several minutes. Désirée, her eldest girl, was reading a little essay by Mrs Barbauld out loud to me, and I was making her translate every line from English to French to be sure she understood every word. Madame listened, intently.

Without any preamble, she spoke, almost like an accusation: “Miss, were you a governess in England?”

“No, Madame,” I smiled back, “you are mistaken.”

“Then this is your first try at teaching, this work with my children?”

I assured her it was. She fell silent again, and when I glanced up to take a pin from my cushion, I found myself the object of her full attention. She was turning me over in her thoughts, measuring me for some unspoken purpose, weighing my value for a plan I did not yet know about. She had long since scrutinised all my belongings, and I thought she knew a great deal about who I was — but from that day on, for a fortnight, she tested me in new ways. She listened outside the nursery door when I was shut in alone with the children, followed me at a cautious distance when I walked out with them, slipping within earshot whenever the trees of the park or boulevard gave her cover. Once that strict preliminary testing was done, she made her move.

One morning she came upon me abruptly, pretending to be harried, and told me she had run into a small dilemma. Mr Wilson, the English master, had not turned up at his usual hour, she feared he was ill, the sixty pupils of the second division were waiting for their lesson, and no one was there to give it. Would I object, just this once, to leading a short dictation exercise, so the girls would not complain they had missed their English class?

“In the classroom, Madame?” I asked.

“Yes, in the second division classroom.”

“Where there are sixty pupils,” I said, and my old cowardice rose up in me, shrinking like a snail into its shell. I claimed I was too inexperienced, too unfit, to face such a crowd. Left to my own devices, I would have let that chance slip forever. I was unadventurous, untouched by any ambition to make my way in the world, perfectly content to sit for twenty years teaching toddlers the alphabet, sewing silk dresses and children’s frocks. It was not true contentment, not really — the work held no charm for me, no hold on my interest — but it felt like a gift to live without heavy anxieties, without intimate, searing trials. The absence of sharp suffering was the closest thing to happiness I dared hope for. I had two separate lives, after all: the inner life of thought, and the outer life of reality, and so long as the former was fed with the

My days now brimmed over with purpose, every hour accounted for, every minute put to sharp, good use. Between leading my classes and poring over my own studies long after the lamps were lit, I scarcely had a spare moment to daydream. It was a pleasure I had not known for so long: no longer stagnant prey to mould and rust, I felt my faculties polish, my wits whet to a fine keen edge with constant, steady work. The world opening before me stretched far wider than the narrow bounds I had so far known. Villette was a cosmopolitan city, and Madame Beck’s school held girls from nearly every corner of Europe, of every rank imaginable. Labassecour wore no face of republicanism on its statutes, but its daily life breathed that same casual egalitarian spirit, so that at the long schoolroom desks, the young countess sat shoulder to shoulder with the daughter of a small shopkeeper, and often you could not tell noble from common by outward show at all. Indeed, the bourgeoise girls often carried themselves with a franker, more courteous grace, while the aristocracy bore away the prize for a delicate, perfectly balanced blend of insolence and deceit. Quick, hot French blood tangled in their northern marsh-phlegm, and that vivacity rarely showed itself in kindness: it only made their flattery glib, their lies flow smooth as oil, lending them a light, sparkling manner that was entirely hollow at the core.

To be fair, the honest native Labassecouriennes had their own brand of hypocrisy, coarse and unsubtle, fooling no one who cared to look. When a lie suited their ends, they tossed it out with careless, unblushing ease, no prickle of conscience to trouble them. From the scull scrubbing the kitchen floors to the directress herself, no soul in Madame Beck’s house thought twice about falsehood. To invent a fiction might not be a virtue, but it was the most trivial of faults. “I have lied many times,” was a regular line in every woman and girl’s monthly confession, and the priest heard it unshocked, absolved them without a second thought. But miss mass? Read a stray chapter of a novel? Those were grievous sins, sure to bring down sharp rebuke and stiff penance.

Half-blind to this strange code, untaught in its unwritten rules, I thrived at my new post after the first few harrowing weeks. The early lessons had felt like balancing on the lip of a moral volcano, rumbling under my feet, spitting sparks and hot acrid fumes into my eyes—but slowly, the fiery temper of my students subsided, at least where I was concerned. I was stubborn in my hunger to succeed: I would not be beaten by mere undisciplined disaffection, by wanton, willful disobedience, in my first real chance to make my way in the world. Many a night I lay awake long after the rest of the house was asleep, turning over what plan I might take to win a reliable hold on these mutineers, to bend this stiff-necked tribe to gentle, permanent influence. First of all, I saw clearly that no help would ever come from Madame. Her whole carefully cultivated system relied on staying endlessly popular with the pupils, no matter what injustice or discomfort her teachers had to bear. For a teacher to run to her for aid in a crisis of insubordination was little more than signing their own expulsion notice. She only ever claimed the pleasant, amiable, praiseworthy parts of interacting with the girls, and left all the annoying, firm, unpopular confrontations entirely to her underlings. I had no one to lean on but myself.

It was plain as day I could not drive this willful flock by force. They needed to be humoured, borne with endless patience; a quiet, courteous but unshaken manner impressed them far more than shouts. A rare, well-timed flash of raillery worked wonders, too. Severe, unbroken mental labour they would not, could not bear. Heavy demands on their memory, their reason, their attention, they rejected outright. Where an average, docile English girl would quietly take a difficult lesson, set herself to comprehend and master it, a Labassecourienne would laugh in your face and push the book back, exclaiming, “Good heavens, how difficult! I don’t want to do it. It bores me far too much.”
A clever teacher would take the book back at once, no hesitation, no squabble, no expostulation, smooth every difficulty away until the lesson shrank to fit the level of their understanding, and then lay the lash of hearty, unsparing sarcasm across their sloth. They would feel the sting, wince a little, but never bear malice, so long as the sneer was warm, not sour, and laid their ignorance and idleness bare in plain, unmistakeable terms. They would riot for three extra lines off a lesson they hated, but I never saw them rebel against a blow to their little store of self-respect: that quality had been trained down so hard for so long that it welcomed the weight of a firm, steady heel.

Bit by bit, as I gained fluency and ease in their language, learned to wield its sharp, vivid idioms to suit their case, the older, brighter girls began to like me, in their own way. I noticed that the moment I roused a spark of worthy emulation in their souls, or made them flush with honest shame, that girl was mine. If I could once make their ears burn under their thick glossy hair, all was well. Before long, little bouquets of wild violets and late roses began to appear on my desk each morning. To thank them for the sweet small attention, I sometimes walked with a select few along the alleyways during recreation. One afternoon, unguarded, I tried to correct their warped notions of right and wrong, and spoke plainly of how base and evil a lie was. I said, offhand, that of the two small failings, I thought falsehood far worse than the occasional lapse in church attendance. The poor girls had been trained to report every word their Protestant teacher said to the priest, and the consequences were immediate and unedifying. An unseen, nameless, indefinable shadow stole between me and my favourite pupils. The bouquets still appeared on my desk, but easy, open conversation was gone forever. Whenever I paced the gardens or sat in the rose arbour, a girl would drift to my right hand, and a teacher would appear by magic at my left. And Madame, in her silent soft-soled shoes, would glide to stand at my back as quick, noiseless, and unexpected as a wandering summer zephyr.

One day, a young pensionnaire to whom I had done a small favour spoke her naive opinion of my spiritual prospects outright, as we sat together. “Mademoiselle, what a great pity it is that you are a Protestant!”
“Why, Isabelle?”
“Because when you die, you will burn straight away in hell.”
“Do you truly believe that?”
“Of course I do! Everyone knows it, and the priest told me so himself.”
She was a blunt, odd little thing, and leaned in to whisper softly: “To make sure you get to heaven, we ought to burn you alive right here on earth, while you still can be saved.”
I laughed. There was nothing else to do.

Had the reader forgotten Miss Ginevra Fanshawe? I must reintroduce her now, thriving as a pupil at Madame Beck’s. She turned up on the Rue Fossette two or three days after I had settled in, and encountered me with barely a flicker of surprise. No duchess ever carried herself with such perfect, unforced nonchalance: a faint, passing amazement was the highest pitch of wonder she had ever known. All her other faculties were just as flimsy: her likes and dislikes, her loves and hates, were no sturdier than cobweb. The only thing strong and unshakable about her was her selfishness.
She was not proud, and seeing I was the “good old soul” type, she at once made me her confidante, pestering me for hours with vapid complaints about school quarrels and bad food. The Friday salt fish was too salty, the eggs too hard, the soup thin, the bread stale, the coffee weak. I bore it patiently for weeks, until I grew wearied of the same whine repeated over and over, and turned sharp, setting her firmly in her place. I ought to have done it from the start: a salutary dressing down always agreed with her.
I endured her demands on my labour far longer. All her fine outer gowns were elegant and well-made, but her undergarments were frayed, full of holes, and she hated mending so much she would heaps of her torn stockings and linen on my bed, begging me to repair them. When I finally put my foot down and told her she must mend her own clothes, she burst into tears, accused me of ceasing to be her friend, and threw a fit of hysterics that I let run its course without giving an inch.

For all her countless unrefined, unelevating foibles, she was so pretty. On a sunny Sunday morning, dressed in pale lilac silk, her long fair curls falling soft over her white shoulders, she looked positively enchanting. Sunday was her holiday, which she spent with friends in town, and she soon let slip that one of those friends was a man who burned with adoration for her, perhaps even real love. She called him Isidore, she told me—an alias, of course, because his real name was not nearly “pretty enough.” One day, after she had bragged for half an hour about the violence of Isidore’s attachment, I asked if she loved him in return.
“Sort of,” she shrugged. “He’s handsome, and he’s mad for me, so I’m very well amused. That’s enough.”
Finding she kept the game up far longer than her fickle nature had led me to expect, I one day asked seriously if he was the sort of man her parents, and especially her wealthy uncle who supported her, would ever approve. She admitted it was very doubtful, for she did not think Isidore had much money at all.
“Do you encourage him?” I asked.
“Furiously, sometimes.”
“Without any certainty you will ever be allowed to marry him?”
“Oh, how dreadfully dowdy you are! I don’t want to be married. I’m far too young.”
“But if he loves you as much as you say, and it all comes to nothing, he will be utterly miserable.”
“Of course he will break his heart. I should be shocked and disappointed if he didn’t.”
“I wonder if this Monsieur Isidore is a fool,” I said.
“He is a fool where I am concerned, but everyone says he is very wise and clever in other things. Mrs. Cholmondeley thinks he will go very far on his talents. All I know is, he sighs whenever he is near me, and I can wind him right around my little finger.”
I asked for a full description of the love-stricken Monsieur, but she could not paint a picture to save her life. She had hardly even properly looked at him, none of his features, none of the shifts of his expression had touched her heart or lingered in her memory. All she could say was that he was handsome, more of a fine striking man than a pretty boy. I would have lost all interest in her chatter long before, but every small hint she dropped, every stray detail, betrayed that Isidore offered her his homage with the utmost delicacy and respect. I told her plainly I thought he was far too good for her, and that she was nothing but a vain little coquette. She laughed, shook her curls out of her eyes, and danced away as if I had paid her the highest compliment.

Ginevra’s studies were little more than a formality. The only three things she practiced in earnest were music, singing, and dancing, plus embroidering fine cambric handkerchiefs that she could not afford to buy ready-made. History, geography, grammar, arithmetic—she left all of those undone, or bullied other girls into doing the work for her. Most of her time was spent out visiting. Madame knew her days at school were numbered, and would not be extended no matter how little she progressed, so she allowed her almost unlimited leave. Her chaperone, the gay fashionable Mrs. Cholmondeley, invited her whenever she had guests, and sometimes took her to evening parties across the city. Ginevra adored this arrangement, save for one catch: she needed a constant supply of new dresses, and she had no money to buy them. Her whole lazy, idle mind burned with schemes to solve that problem, spurring her to acts of wild daring that no one would have expected from her.
She begged boldly from Mrs. Cholmondeley, no trace of shy reluctance in her tone. “My darling Mrs. C, I have nothing fit to wear to your party next week. You must give me a book-muslin dress, and a sky-blue sash. Do, there’s an angel, won’t you?”
Darling Mrs. C yielded at first, but soon the demands grew so frequent that she was forced to put her foot down, like every other friend of Ginevra’s. After a while, I heard no more of presents from Mrs. Cholmondeley—but Ginevra still went out to parties, still had all the new dresses she needed, plus all the expensive little extras: gloves, bouquets, even small trinkets. She hid them all away, uncharacteristically secretive, until one evening before a grand ball that demanded extra fine costume, she could not resist coming to my chamber to show herself off in all her splendour.
She looked breathtaking. So young, so fresh, her delicate skin and lithe soft shape pure English charm, unlike any continental beauty I had seen. Her dress was new, costly, perfect, finished with every tiny expensive detail that lends an outfit that air of effortless, complete elegance. I looked her over from top to toe. She spun airily around so I could see every side, her small blue eyes sparkling with glee, about to bestow one of her giddy schoolgirl kisses on me, but I held her off at arm’s length.
“Steady,” I said. “Let us be steady, and work out what this magnificence is all about.”
“Will I do?” she asked.
“Do?” I said. “There are different ways of doing, and by my word I don’t understand yours.”
“But how do I look?”
“You look very well dressed.”
She thought my praise too cold, and began pointing out every part of her finery. “Look at this parure. The brooch, the earrings, the bracelets. No one in the whole school has a set like this, not even Madame.”
“I see them all.” I paused. “Did Monsieur de Bassompierre give you those jewels?”
“My uncle hasn’t the faintest idea they exist.”
“Were they presents from Mrs. Cholmondeley?”
“Certainly not. Mrs. Cholmondeley is a mean stingy creature, she never gives me anything now.”
I did not press her further, turning abruptly away.
“Now old Crusty, old Diogenes,” she said, those familiar teasing names she called me when we quarrelled, “what’s the matter now?”
“Go away. I have no pleasure looking at you or that parure.”
For a second she looked taken aback.
“Wait a minute, Mother Wisdom, I haven’t run anyone into debt for it. The jewels, the gloves, the bouquet, none of them are on credit. My new dress isn’t paid for yet, but my uncle will settle the bill. He never looks at the small items, just pays the total. And he’s so rich, he’ll never miss a few extra guineas.”
“Will you please go? I want to shut the door. Ginevra, people may tell you you look very handsome in that ball gown, but to my eyes you were a thousand times prettier in the plain gingham dress and straw bonnet you wore when I first met you.”
“Other people don’t have your puritanical tastes,” she snapped, angry. “And you have no right to sermonize me.”
“Of course I have little right, and you still less to flutter into my room like a jay strutting in borrowed plumes. I have no respect for your feathers, Miss Fanshawe, especially those peacock eyes you call a parure. They would be very pretty if you had bought them with your own money, money you could well spare—but they are not pretty at all under the circumstances.”
“A carriage is waiting for Miss Fanshawe!” the portress called from the hall, and she tripped away.

The mystery of her parure was solved two or three days later, when she came to me of her own accord to confess.
“You needn’t sulk at me, thinking I’m dragging papa or my uncle deep into debt,” she began. “I assure you nothing is unpaid for except those few new dresses I got lately. All the rest is settled.”
There it was, I thought. The secret. It wasn’t Mrs. Cholmondeley giving her gifts, her own allowance was barely a few shillings a month, which she hoarded like a miser.
“Listen!” she said, drawing close to me in her most coaxing confidential tone, for my sulkiness was inconvenient. She liked me to be in a listening mood, even if I only chided and railed at her. “Listen, dear old grumble-guts, I’ll tell you everything, and then you’ll see it’s all perfectly right, and very cleverly managed. First of all, I simply must go out into society. Papa himself said he wanted me to see a bit of the world, and he told Mrs. Cholmondeley that though I was a sweet creature, I had too much of a bread-and-butter schoolgirl air, and he wanted me to get rid of it here, before I make my formal debut in England. If I go out, I have to dress. Mrs. Cholmondeley is being mean now, and won’t give me anything new. It would be far too unfair to make my uncle pay for every single little thing I need—you can’t deny that, that agrees with all your own preachments, doesn’t it? Well, but someone overheard me complaining to Mrs. Cholmondeley, quite by chance I swear, about how hard up I was for a few ornaments. Someone who was not only not grudging about giving me a present, he was absolutely delighted at the chance to offer me some small trifle. You should have seen how shy he looked the first time he mentioned it, how he hesitated and blushed, positively trembling in case I turned him down.”
“That’s enough, Miss Fanshawe. I suppose I am meant to understand that Monsieur Isidore is your benef

Madame Beck was a study in unshakable consistency: lenient enough to the world at large to avoid friction, yet tender to no living soul within her orbit. Even her own children never cracked the smooth, unyielding surface of her stoic calm. She fussed over their futures, guarded their health, kept a sharp, unblinking eye on their interests, but she never once seemed to feel the urge to tuck a small warm body on her lap, press a kiss to a rosebud cheek, wrap them in a hug soft enough to melt the edge of a bad day, or murmur a word of unplanned, uncalculated affection.

I watched her often, sitting on the stone bench in the garden on mild afternoons, watching her three little ones trail far down the alley with their nurse Trinette, her face lined with prudence and quiet worry for what she called “leur avenir” — their future. But when the youngest, a puny, bright-eyed little thing, spotted her and broke free, chubby legs toddling fast, laughing so hard it panted, to wrap its arms around her knee? Madame would only extend one steady hand to stop the child from knocking itself off balance against her, say “Prends garde, mon enfant” in a voice no warmer than autumn rain, let it stand at her side for a few measured minutes, then rise without a smile, a kiss, or a single endearing turn of phrase to walk it back to its nurse.

Her treatment of the eldest girl, Désirée, was no less telling. The whole household — kitchen staff and schoolroom teachers alike — called her nothing short of a little pest, a walking poison. She had a rare, cruel gift for provocation that could drive the servants half wild: she’d sneak up to their attics, rip their best lace caps to shreds, stain their finest shawls, steal into the dining room buffet to smash porcelain plates and crystal glasses, break into the larder to guzzle sweet wine and spoon jam straight from the jar, then leave clues that framed the cook or the scullery maid for every mess. Every time Madame saw these tantrums, every time a servant came to her to complain, all she would say, perfectly calm, was “Désirée a besoin d’une surveillance toute particulière.” No scolding, no frank talk about the harm of her habits, no warning of where that theft and lying would lead. Just surveillance, as if constant watching could stamp out vice better than love or consequence.

Of course it didn’t work. Désirée only stopped tormenting the servants to torment her mother instead: she’d steal brooches from Madame’s dressing table, snip the threads of her embroidery silk, hide her silver scissors in the garden wall cracks. Madame saw every bit of it, but pretended not to — she had no honesty in her to confront her daughter about her flaws. Only when something valuable went missing would she pretend to think Désirée had taken it to play with, and coax her to give it back. Désirée was too sharp for that, though; she lied as smoothly as she stole, and swore she’d never touched the missing object. Madame would play along, pretend to believe her, then sneak after her the second she left the room, track her to her hiding spot in the garret or the old garden shed, and steal her things back while the child was out on a walk. Désirée, for her part, never so much as frowned when she found her hoard had been raided — the apple had not fallen far from the clever, unflappable tree.

The second child, Fifine, everyone said took after her dead father. She had Madame’s strong little frame, bright blue eyes, and rosy cheeks, but nothing of her cold, calculating spirit. Hers was an open, merry little soul: warm-tempered, impulsive, forever tripping into trouble. One day she misstepped and tumbled all the way down the steep flight of stone steps leading from the second floor to the vestibule. Madame, who heard every small noise in the house no matter how far away, was there in an instant to pick her up, and said, perfectly level, “Cet enfant a un os de cassé.”

We all hoped she was wrong. She wasn’t. The little girl’s plump arm hung limp at her side.
“Have Meess take her,” she said, meaning me, “and send for a fiacre right away.”
She left promptly, calm as ever, to fetch the family doctor, but found him out — not that it slowed her down. She searched the whole town until she found a substitute she trusted, and brought him home with her. By the time they arrived, I’d cut the sleeve off Fifine’s arm, undressed her, and tucked her into bed. The bonne, the cook, the portress and I all crowded into the small, stuffy nursery, all of us too wrapped up in soothing the screaming child to pay much mind to the new doctor when he stepped through the door.

Fifine’s wails redoubled when the stranger approached her bed. “Let alone!” she shrieked in her broken English, “I will not you! I want Dr. Pillule!”
“Dr. Pillule is a very good friend of mine,” he answered, in perfect, soft English, “but he’s three leagues away tending to a sick man, so I’ve come in his place. Once we’re a little calmer, we’ll get that poor little arm all bandaged and fixed right up, I promise.”
He called for a glass of sugared water, fed her teaspoon by teaspoon — Fifine was a little glutton, and anyone could win her heart through her sweet tooth — promised her more once the splint was on, and set to work. He asked the cook, a broad, strong-armed woman, to hold the child still, but she and the rest of the staff all fled the room in a panic. I didn’t want to touch that small, broken, hurting limb, but I was just about to step forward when Madame Beck’s hand beat mine to it. Hers was perfectly steady, while mine trembled so bad I could barely keep it still.
“Ca vaudra mieux,” the doctor said, turning to her instead of me, and he was right. My calm would have been forced, my courage feigned. Hers was as natural as breathing.
“Thank you, Madame — excellent, perfectly steady,” he said when he finished setting the bone. “That level-headedness is worth a thousand bursts of misplaced sentiment.”
He was charmed by her composure, she flattered by his praise — and it was no wonder, when you got a good look at him, and the lamp was lit to chase the dusk out of the small room. This young doctor was no ordinary man. He looked impossibly tall in that tiny nursery, surrounded by the stout, quiet women of the pensionnat, his profile sharp and clear, his eyes bright and quick to dart from face to face, but so warm you couldn’t mind the way he looked. His mouth was soft, his chin full and cleft, perfectly shaped, and his smile — it was hard to name what made it so special. It made you feel light, a little silly, like he could see every one of your small, silly flaws, and laugh at them kindly, not cruelly. Even Fifine, who he’d just hurt so bad, held out her hand to say good night when he was done, and he patted it gently.

As he turned to walk out, talking to Madame in his smooth, accented French that was still clearly second nature to him, I placed him. That voice, that tall frame — it was the very man who’d helped me at the customs bureau, who’d sorted out my lost trunk, who’d guided me through the dark, rain-soaked park on my first night in Villette. I could hear his familiar, firm, even stride down the vestibule to the street, and my chest gave a small, unnameable jolt.

I’d thought that would be his only visit. Dr. Pillule was due back the next day, there was no reason for the substitute to ever return. But the Fates had other plans. Dr. Pillule had been called out to the old university town of Bouquin-Moisi to see a wealthy, fussy hypochondriac, who’d liked him so much he’d hired him to accompany him on a weeks-long tour across the country. So Dr. John — the name Fifine had insisted on calling him, and the whole household picked up to use, till no one knew him by any other name — kept coming back to the Rue Fossette.

I saw him every time he visited, because Madame wouldn’t trust Fifine’s care to Trinette, and made me spend most of my days in the nursery. He was a skilled doctor, and Fifine’s arm healed fast, but even after she was running around the garden again, he kept coming. Destiny and Madame Beck seemed to be in cahoots, determined he’d get to know every inch of the pensionnat: the vestibule, the private staircase, the little nooks of the upper floors.

No sooner was Fifine fully recovered than Désirée announced she was ill. That sharp, mischievous child had a genius for pretending, and had decided the soft indulgences of a sickbed suited her perfectly, so she climbed in and refused to get up. She acted the part well, but Madame acted it better: she saw right through Désirée’s ruse, as clear as day, but played along with a perfectly serious, perfectly convincing show of concern. What surprised me most was that Dr. John played right along too. He gave a few quick, amused glances from the grinning, overexcited child who gorged on treats and threw her shoes at the nurse, to her perfectly unflappable mother, hesitated for a minute like he was debating calling them out, then gave in with a good-natured smile and took his place in their little farce. He wrote harmless, sugary prescriptions for the “patient”, and watched Madame with a sharp, sparkling glint in his eye that made her laugh instead of taking offense.

He was never obsequious, never trying to curry favor with Madame. He seemed drawn to the pensionnat for some unnameable reason, lingering longer than he needed to after every visit, wandering the halls like he was looking for something he couldn’t name, growing quiet and thoughtful when he thought he was alone. No one noticed me, of course — I was the quiet, unobtrusive governess, as invisible as a plain wooden chair, as unremarkable as the carpet under their feet. He’d muse to himself, smile at nothing, listen to the children’s laughter drift in from the garden, and never once remember I was sitting in the corner, needle and thread in hand, watching him.

One afternoon, he was sitting in the sun slanting through the window, the light gilding his fair hair and his whiskers so bright I found myself thinking, half amused, half caught off guard, of the golden idol Nebuchadnezzar set up in the old Bible stories. I was so wrapped up in the thought, staring, that I forgot myself entirely — until he turned, and his eyes met mine in the small oval mirror set in the window recess, the same mirror Madame used to spy on people walking in the garden below.

He turned around, his tone polite but dry, a little sharp with quiet annoyance. “Mademoiselle does not spare me. I am not vain enough to think my merits have drawn your eye — so it must be some defect. Dare I ask what it is?”

I was flustered, of course, but not mortified. I knew I hadn’t been staring out of silly admiration, or nosy curiosity. I could have cleared the air right then, but I didn’t. I said nothing, ducked my head back over my sewing, and kept it down until he left. There is a strange, quiet comfort in being misjudged, after all — in places where no one will ever really know you, there is a small, secret joy in being so thoroughly unseen, that even when you are caught staring, the mistake of their assumptions feels like a private little joke no one else is in on.

The summer heat hung thick and heavy over the pensionnat, settling in folds of warm linen and clinging to the back of every neck, the air still save for the drowsy hum of flies at the windowpanes. It was then that Georgette, Madame Beck’s smallest, most spoiled child, came down with a sharp, burning fever. Désirée, whose endless list of petty ailments had vanished as if by magic at the first hint of contagion, was bundled off alongside the youngest Fifine to their grandmother’s farmhouse in the country, every last door and window propped open behind them to chase the risk of sickness from the halls.

By then, no home remedy could do anymore, and Madame chose to overlook the fact that stuffy old local physician Dr. Pillule had returned home a week prior. Instead, she sent word straight to his young English rival, begging him to keep coming to attend her household. It was only a matter of days before a handful of the pensionnaires began complaining of splitting headaches, feeling faint and unwell enough to echo little Georgette’s symptoms. “Now,” I thought to myself, surely the cautious directress would recall Dr. Pillule—no prudent woman would let such a young, unmarried man move freely among her flock of sheltered teen girls.

But Madame Beck was not merely prudent. She could be daring, unshakably bold when it suited her ends. She walked Dr. John straight through the doors of the school wing, and set him to attend to proud, beautiful Blanche de Melcy and her vain, flighty friend Angélique, who were both laid low with migraine. Dr. John bore the mark of her unusual confidence with clear, quiet gratification, his bearing so gentle and discreet no one could fairly find fault with his presence. But this was a land of convents and whispered confessions, where a young man stepping foot unchaperoned in a girls’ boarding school was unheard of. The pupils gossiped behind their textbooks, the kitchen maids whispered over their soup pots, the story leaked out into the town, and soon angry parents were writing letters, turning up on the doorstep to remonstrate. A dozen rival schools were poised to seize on her misstep to ruin her reputation entirely.

But Madame did not break. I clapped my hands silently to myself, my heart cheering brava as I watched her move through the crisis, calm, good-humored, disarming every frowning parent with that folksy, easy warmth no other woman could mimic, a frank, motherly charm that won over even the most suspicious visitor when strict gravity and long-winded reasoning would have failed entirely. “Ce pauvre Docteur Jean!” she would chuckle, rubbing her plump little white hands together gaily, “That dear, sweet boy! The kindest creature alive!” She explained that her own children loved him so fiercely they would scream themselves into fits if forced to see any other doctor, and that if she trusted him with her own darlings, it was only natural she would trust him with everyone else’s. It was no more than a temporary measure, she added: he had written a prescription for the girls’ headaches, and that was the whole of it.

Every last parent was disarmed. Blanche and Angélique soon sang loud, giddy duets in their physician’s praise, all the other pupils echoed them, declaring they would have no one but Dr. John if they ever fell ill, and soon the parents were laughing too, declaring Madame had only acted out of a motherly sort of softness that no reasonable person could fault. She came out of the scandal more beloved as a directress than she had ever been before.

To this day I cannot fully say why she risked her entire livelihood for the sake of that young doctor. The whole house—pupils, teachers, servants alike—whispered openly that she intended to marry him. The fourteen years between their ages seemed no obstacle at all to anyone. The signs all lined up, too: she went out of her way to personally greet every one of his visits, was unfailingly bright and blithe in his company, and discarded all her frumpy morning house gowns and worn nightcaps entirely. Dr. John never arrived for his early visits without finding her auburn braids neatly coiffed, her silk gown fitted crisply to her frame, neat laced leather boots on her feet instead of scuffed slippers, her whole toilette as fresh and polished as a summer bloom. She was no great beauty, but she was never dull, never insipid, her temperate blue eyes and warm, fruit-blushed cheeks pleasant to look at for hours on end, and I suspected she only dressed so carefully to prove to that handsome young man that she was not at all plain.

Rumor said she dreamed of making him her husband, of settling him in her well-furnished home, endowing him with her carefully hoarded savings that were said to amount to a comfortable little fortune, and making his life soft and easy forever. I often wondered if Dr. John guessed at those quiet visions. More than once I caught him leaving her parlor with a half-mischievous, half-frustrated smile on his lips, a glint in his eyes that spoke of masculine vanity flattered and tickled. For all his good nature and kindness, he was not perfect. But he was more than that, too: there was a watchful, expectant, eager edge to his manner that never faded, that only grew sharper with every visit, as if he was searching for something he could not find, something far beyond my ability to see.

One morning little Georgette’s fever spiked higher than usual, her wails sharp and peevish, no toy or sweet able to soothe her. I suspected the draught the doctor had ordered was not agreeing with her, and waited impatiently for his arrival to ask him to adjust it. The door bell rang, I heard his familiar voice greeting the portress, and waited for him to bound up the stairs three at a time, as was his custom, his cheerful laugh preceding him like a surprise.

Five minutes passed. Ten. No sign of him, no sound of his step. What could he be doing, lingering in the corridor below? Georgette still whimpered “Minnie, Minnie, me very poorly,” and my heart ached so much I went down to find him. The corridor was empty. He could not be in Madame’s dining room— I had left her but moments before, dressing in her own private chamber. Three pupils were practicing piano in the three adjacent salons, and further off, a whole class of a dozen girls were singing a barcarole in the oratory, their voices ringing out about freshness, stormy seas, and Venice. And there, half-hidden behind the ajar door of the small little room built as a boudoir but now given over entirely to the portress’s use, I heard a giddy treble laugh. Then a man’s voice, low, deep, and pleading, cut through the music: “For God’s sake!”

After a beat of silence, Dr. John stepped out of the room. His eyes were bright, but not with joy or triumph. His fair English cheeks were flushed deep crimson, his face twisted with a look of bafflement, tortured anxiety, and strange soft tenderness all tangled together. The half-open door hid me from his sight, but even if I had stood directly in his path, I do not think he would have seen me. Some raw, sharp pain had hold of him— not hurt pride, but wounded affection, cruelly inflicted.

I peered around the door frame, bewildered. Madame was safe in her chamber, and the only other woman who used that little cabinet was Rosine Matou, the pretty, flighty young portress, vain and fond of fine dresses and coin. It could not be her, surely, who had left him looking so broken? Her voice trilled out a light, giddy French song from within, and I glanced in. She sat at the table in a fine pink sprigged muslin gown, trimming a tiny lace cap, no other living soul in the room save for goldfish swimming in a glass bowl, potted flowers on the windowsill, and a broad shaft of hot July sunlight spilling across the floor. I could not make sense of the mystery, so I turned and went back upstairs to ask about the medicine.

Dr. John was already seated by Georgette’s crib, Madame standing over him. The little girl was calm now, settled into her sheets. Madame was fretting over his own health, pointing out a faint change in his pallor, chiding him for overworking himself and insisting he take a holiday by the sea. He laughed it off good-naturedly, told her she was far too kind, that he felt perfectly well. She turned to appeal to me, and Dr. John’s eyes flicked my way slowly, a faint look of surprise on his face that she would bother asking so insignificant a person’s opinion. “What do you think, Miss Lucie?” she said. “Is he not paler and thinner?”

Ordinarily I spoke no more than a word or two in his presence, content to let him dismiss me as the quiet, unassuming foreign teacher. But that day I chose to speak clearly, meeting no one’s eye. “He looks unwell right now,” I said, “but I think it must be from some temporary trouble. Dr. John may have been vexed, or hurt.” He did not say a word, but I could feel his gaze fixed on me, like he was realizing for the first time that I was not just a background shadow. For the first time, he noticed I spoke his own English tongue, after weeks of speaking to me only in French. He looked about to make a remark, but thought better of it, and held his peace.

He bade them good morning soon after, courteous but with an air of someone who had been smothered by too much unasked-for attention, and left. No sooner had the door clicked shut behind him than Madame dropped heavily into the chair he had just vacated, resting her chin in her hand. All her bright, amiable warmth vanished from her face, leaving it stony, stern, pinched with quiet mortification. She sighed once, a deep, heavy, shuddering breath. The bell for morning school rang loud through the halls, and she stood to leave, pausing at the dressing table mirror to glance at her reflection. A single stark white hair streaked her rich auburn braids. She plucked it out with a little shudder. In the harsh bright summer light, I could see the bloom on her cheeks was still there, but the soft texture of youth was long gone, the round contours of her face hardened with age. For the first time I pitied her. Disappointment, that cold hag, had reached out to greet her, and she was the sort of practical, sharp woman who did not know how to welcome that kind of pain.

I puzzled over Rosine and that strange encounter all day, lingering by the portress’s cabinet five different times to try and make sense of the mystery. She was young, pretty, well-dressed, no doubt the kind of girl many a young man might dote on. But I could not quite wrap my head around the raw pain on Dr. John’s face, the desperation in his plea. Madame, for her part, did not wallow. She had a school to run, a business that filled her days, a good sense most women (and men) never possessed. She did not make a fool of herself, did not pine or mope, did not force her attentions on him any longer. She had fought the good fight against that foolish, soft little predilection, and she had won. Brava, once more, Madame Beck. But the secret hidden in that sunlit little cabinet, the secret that had left Dr. John looking as if his heart had been ripped open, stayed with me, lingering like the echo of that half-heard plea, long after the summer heat faded.

Behind Madame Beck’s house on Rue Fossette lay a garden—surprisingly large for the heart of a crowded continental city, and soft with remembered sweetness even now, across the stretch of all the years since. Time, like distance, wraps quiet, overlooked places in a tender haze, and set all about by blank stone walls and sun-baked pavement, one small shrub, one patch of planted, green earth, feels like a stolen miracle.

A very old tale clung to the house, too, that it had once been a convent, centuries back, when the land around was still tilled fields and shaded avenues, the kind of deep, leafy seclusion a religious order ought to hide within. Some horror had unfolded on this site, so the story went, something terrible enough to leave a ghostly legacy: a vague, half-forgotten rumour of a nun in black and white robes, seen wandering the grounds on certain dark nights. The ghost itself must have been built out generations ago, the city closing all around, but relics of that old convent orchard remained: gnarled, ancient fruit trees, their branches twisted like old knuckles. The oldest of them all was a patriarch of a pear tree, almost entirely dead, save for a few loyal boughs that every spring still burst into drifts of perfumed white blossom, and every autumn bore clusters of honey-sweet fruit. Scrape away the mossy earth between its half-exposed roots, and you could catch a glint of a smooth, hard black slab. The legend, never proven, never quite fading, said that slab was the lid of a vault, where a medieval conclave of monks had bricked a young girl alive beneath the grass and flowers for breaking her vows. It was her shadow the timid had feared for hundreds of years after her bones turned to dust; her black robe and white veil that moonlight and shifting shadow had teased the eye with, fluttering through the garden thickets on windy nights.

But far more than ghost stories, the old garden had its own quiet charms. Summer mornings I would rise early to wander it alone, summer evenings I would linger long after the pupils had been called indoors, to meet the rising moon, catch one soft brush of the evening breeze, or almost taste the fresh fall of dew before it fully settled. The turf was bright green, the gravelled walks pale and clean, and sun-bright nasturtiums clustered in glowing banks around the bases of the gnarled old orchard trees. There was a wide arbour draped in the shade of a tall acacia, and a smaller, far more secluded bower tucked into vines that ran all along a high grey wall, twisting their tendrils into a soft, tangled knot of green, hanging heavy with sweet clusters of fruit right where jasmine and ivy wove their branches together.

At midday, of course, when the whole of Madame Beck’s school poured out noisily to play, day students and boarders alike yelling and chasing, vying for volume with the boys from the college next door, the garden was nothing but a trampled, ordinary, unremarkable place. But at sunset, after vespers, when all the day pupils had gone home and the boarders were quiet over their books, it was a paradise to wander the empty alleys, and hear the bells of St. Jean Baptiste peal out soft and sweet over the rooftops.

One evening I stayed later than usual, held fast by the deepening hush, the mellow cool air, the faint scent of flowers that never saw direct sun, blooming only for the dew and the dark. I could see a light glowing in the oratory window, where the Catholic household were gathered for their evening prayers— a ritual I, as a Protestant, was sometimes exempt from attending. “Stay just a little longer,” the quiet and the thin young moon whispered to me, “no one will notice you’re gone for another quarter hour. The day’s heat and noise have worn you thin. Enjoy these precious, quiet minutes.”

The garden was walled in by the windowless backs of other houses, one full side bordered by the rear of the neighbouring boys’ college: all blank stone, save for a few high attic loopholes that opened out from the servants’ bedrooms, and one lower casement that was said to belong to a master’s study. The alley that ran parallel to that high stone wall was strictly forbidden to all pupils: l’allée défendue, they called it. Any girl who dared step foot there faced the harshest penalty Madame Beck’s gentle house rules allowed. Teachers could walk there with impunity, but the path was narrow, the neglected shrubs had grown so thick on either side they wove a roof of leaves overhead that sunlight only pierced in rare, dappled flecks. Even in the day it was almost empty, and after dark no one but me ever dared venture near.

I was drawn to it, from the first. The seclusion, the soft gloom, felt made for someone as quiet as I was. For a long while I held back, afraid of seeming strange, but slowly, as everyone got used to my quiet, peculiar little habits, I began to go there regularly. I tended to the pale little wild flowers that grew between the bushes, cleared out the dead autumn leaves that choked a weathered old rustic seat at the far end, borrowed a pail of water and a scrub brush from the cook Goton, and scrubbed every spot of mould and moss off the wood until it was clean. Madame Beck saw me at work and smiled her approval, sincere or not, I could never be sure, but she seemed kind enough. “See how neat she has made it, our Miss Lucy?” she said. “You like this little alley, then?” I told her yes, it was quiet and shady. “Quite right,” she agreed, all gentle goodwill, and said I was welcome to use it as much as I liked, since I had no duty to supervise the pupils. She only asked that I let her small children join me there sometimes to practice their English.

That night in question, I was sitting on that hidden seat, listening to the far-off hum of the city. It was not truly far, of course: the school sat in the very centre of the city, a five minute walk from the public park, ten from the grand, palatial avenues. Just beyond the walls, wide brightly lit streets teemed with life, carriages rolling past on their way to balls and the opera. The same hour that dimmed all our lamps and drew the curtains around every bed in our quiet pensionnat was the hour that summoned the rest of the city out to feast and celebrate. I barely thought of that contrast. I had few gay, worldly instincts: I had never seen a ball or an opera, and while I sometimes wondered what they looked like, it was no hungry, burning wish to shine in that bright world, only the soft, calm curiosity of someone who just wanted to peek at something new.

A thin crescent moon hung in the sky, visible through a gap in the leaves overhead. The stars beside her were no strangers, even in this foreign city: I had known them since childhood. I had seen that same golden crescent, with the dark faint outline of the full moon cradled in its curve, hanging over an old thorn bush on the edge of a field back in England, hundreds of miles and years away, just as it hung now over the spire of St. Jean Baptiste. Oh, my childhood. Passive as I lived, quiet as I was, cold as I seemed to most people, when I thought of those old days I could feel, sharp and bright, all the feelings I spent most of my time lulling to sleep. The present called for stoicism, the future I could imagine for myself called for complete stillness, and I held the quick, beating core of my nature carefully, almost buried, to keep it from waking and aching.

I remembered well how some things, thunderstorms for example, used to fill me with dread, because they woke that sleeping self and stirred a craving so deep I could never satisfy it. Once, a terrible thunderstorm had raged through the city, a hurricane that shook the dormitory windows while all the Catholic girls rose in panic to pray to their saints. The tempest seized me too, roused me roughly back to full, throbbing life, and I crept out of bed, climbed out the casement window onto the narrow ledge that jutted over the lower roof, sitting there in the wild dark and rain, watching the clouds split open to pour down white, blinding sheets of lightning. For 24 hours after that I ached so badly for something, anything, to pull me out of my small, narrow life and carry me forward, that I had to snuff that longing out like a flame— figuratively driving a nail through its head, like Jael did to Sisera. But those longings never truly die, they only lie stunned, and every so often they twist against the nail, and my whole being aches.

That night, though, there was no pain. My Sisera lay quiet, slumbering in his tent, and something soft, like an angel, knelt beside him, dropping balm on his temples, holding a magic glass that showed soft, sweet, hopeful visions. I was filled with a gentle, unnameable peace, no specific hope attached, only a quiet feeling of courage, of lightness in my chest. Such a soft, rare mood could only be a harbinger of something good, I thought. But I was wrong. The crude, rude real world burst in all too soon.

I heard a faint creak of a casement hinge somewhere above me, over the high wall. Before I could even look up to find where it had opened, something hit the tree branches over my head and dropped light at my feet. The clock of St. Jean Baptiste struck nine. It was not fully dark yet, the last gold of the sunset still glowing low in the sky, enough light that I could have read small print if I held it close. The thing at my feet was a small casket, carved from white and coloured ivory, its lid half open. Inside, a cluster of sweet violets lay wrapped around a closely folded sheet of pink paper, a note addressed in looping script: Pour la robe grise. To the grey dress. I was wearing my plain French grey dress that night, of course. A love letter? I had heard of such things, but never once dreamed of receiving one.

Not a moment did I believe it was meant for me. No man had ever looked at me that way. All the other teachers had their little romantic daydreams, every girl over fourteen knew of some future suitor most of them were already promised to by their parents as children. I had never once dared step foot in that world. All the other girls and teachers swore that Dr. John’s bright blue eyes had rested on them with an admiring glance at one time or another. But I knew, for me, those eyes were always calm, blank as the summer sky they matched. No one ever noticed me. So this could not be for me. I opened the note and read it, translated from the gushing French:
“Angel of my dreams! A thousand, thousand thanks for keeping your promise! I scarcely dared hope you would agree. You said the enterprise was so dangerous, the hour so late, this alley so strictly guarded by that terrible dragon, the English teacher— that Britannic prude you described, a brute as gruff as an old grenadier corporal, as sour as a cloistered nun! Little Gustave, sick with fever, was moved to a master’s chambers in the college, the very room whose window overlooks your little garden. I am his favourite uncle, you see, so I was allowed to visit. How my heart pounded when I leaned out the window and saw the glint of your straw hat, the sweep of your grey dress through the leaves! But why would you not look up, cruel one? One glance of your beautiful eyes would have revived me entirely. I write this in the greatest haste, while the doctor tends to Gustave, tucking it into this little casket with the sweetest violets that grow, none half so sweet as you, my Peri. Ever yours, you know who!”

“I wish I did know who,” I thought to myself, and even more than the writer, I wished I knew who the note was addressed to. Half the women in the house owned a grey dress that same season, it was the height of fashion: Madame Beck wore a grey day dress, another teacher, three of the older boarders, and even Rosine the portress. The straw hat was nothing special either, a dozen of us had identical plain ones for working in the garden.

I knew I had to go in soon. Lights were bobbing in the dormitory, the pupils getting ready for bed. Half an hour more and all the doors would be locked, all the lamps snuffed. The front door still stood open, letting the cool night air into the overheated house. Then a soft, cautious little bell tinkled, not loud at all, just a warning whisper. Rosine darted out of her lodge to open it, stood talking to whoever was on the doorstep for a minute or two, before she picked up her lamp and stepped out onto the garden steps, lifting it to look around. “What nonsense!” she laughed, coquettish. “There’s no one out here.”

“Let me pass,” I heard a familiar voice plead. “I only need five minutes.” And tall, broad-shouldered Dr. John, whom everyone in the pensionnat thought so handsome and imposing, stepped past her and strode out into the garden, his boots soft on the grass. A man in the garden after dark was almost sacrilege, but he moved like he knew he was privileged, trusting the night to hide him. He wandered the alleys, looking this way and that, trampling flowers, snapping branches in his search, until he pushed through the foliage and found me in l’allée défendue.

“Dr. John,” I said, holding up the ivory casket. “It’s found.”

He did not even ask who had it. His sharp eye had spotted it the second he rounded the bush. “Do not betray her,” he said, looking at me almost as if I really was that dragon the note had described.

“I cannot betray what I do not know,” I told him. “Read that note, you will see it gives away no names at all.”

He took it from my hand and read, and his face flushed bright red, hot with shame. “This is too much,” he said. “Cruel. Humiliating.” I agreed, seeing how upset he was.

“Will you tell Madame Beck what you found?” he asked. “Start a whole scandal, an esclandre?”

I said I had thought I ought to, that Madame was far too prudent to make a public fuss over something like this happening in her school. He stood meditating, far too proud and honourable to beg me to keep a secret that I thought was my duty to report. I wanted to do right, but I could not bear to see him grieve or get in trouble. Then I spotted Rosine standing in the doorway, her own grey dress glowing in the faint lamplight. Of course. No pupils were involved here.

So I held out the casket, the violets, the note, and said: “If you can assure me none of Madame Beck’s pupils are mixed up in this, I will happily forget the whole thing. Take it all. I will not breathe a word.”

“Look there,” he whispered suddenly, his hand closing over the casket as he pointed through the branches.

I looked. There was Madame Beck, wrapped in her shawl, her nightgown, soft slippers on her feet, creeping down the steps as quiet as a cat, about to round the corner and catch us both in two seconds. But Dr. John was as light on his feet as a leopard when he wanted to be. He waited until she turned her back, then bounded across the garden in two silent strides, Rosine yanking the front door shut behind him the very second he slipped inside, before Madame could even turn around again.

I did not run. I stayed right there, and met her openly. It was well known I liked to linger in the garden at twilight, but never once had I stayed this late. I expected a reprimand. But Madame was all soft sweetness, no surprise, no rebuke. With her legendary, perfect tact, she claimed she had only stepped out to “taste the evening breeze.” “What a beautiful night!” she cried, looking up at the stars. “How fresh the air is!” Instead of sending me inside immediately, she walked a few slow turns with me up the main alley, leaning gently on my shoulder for support up the steps when we finally went in, and kissed my cheek goodnight. “Sleep well, my dear good friend,” she said.

I lay in my bed later, awake and smiling, thinking of her. That overdone, over-sweet kindness was a dead giveaway. She had caught some glimpse through the branches, heard the low murmur of a man’s voice drifting through the quiet house, picked up some strange shadow or unusual footstep. She knew something odd had happened that night, even if she did not know what, and she had a new little tangled mystery to unpick. And there I was, plain quiet Miss Lucy, caught right in the middle of it, like a silly little fly stuck in a web I could not yet see all the threads of.

I found myself smiling—no, outright laughing—at Madame Beck again, not twenty-four hours after that little charged scene in the garden. Villette’s weather is as fickle as a coquette’s fancy: not as damp as London’s skies, but every bit as unpredictable. The soft, honeyed sunset we’d watched the night before gave way to a gale that howled through the hours of dark, and the whole next day blew dry and stormy: heavy clouds blotted out the sun, but not a drop of rain fell, the streets hazed fine with sand and dust torn up from the broad boulevards.

Even if the day had been bright and lovely, I never would have gone back to that garden nook I’d once thought of as my private, quiet refuge. My little flagged alley, every walk and shrub in the whole school grounds, had gained a new, unpleasant kind of notoriety. Their seclusion no longer felt safe, their calm felt fragile. That window that had rained down a little lead casket of billets had turned the soft, beloved spot vulgar, somehow. I half swore the rose bushes were peeking over their leaves, the gnarled knots in the old tree trunks leaning in like listening spies. I’d gotten up before dawn that day, before the household stirred, to prop up the flowerbeds Dr. John had trodden flat rushing after that fallen casket, to brush away the footprints he’d left sunk into the soft soil before anyone else could spot them, all while that strong gale was still whipping the bare branches.
