I settled down to my desk with my German textbook after that, soft, half-content and half-pensive, as the pupils filed in for evening lessons and the other teachers pulled out their needlework.

The *étude du soir* was always held in the small refectory, far smaller than any of the school classrooms, for only the boarding students were allowed there, a scant twenty of them. Two oil lamps hung from the ceiling over the long tables, lit as soon as dusk crept in. Lighting those lamps was the signal to close all the schoolbooks, hush all chatter, and begin *la lecture pieuse*—the pious reading. I soon learned that this reading was designed, it seemed, to do nothing but humiliate the human intellect, grind common sense down to dust under its heel. The book they used never changed: when they finished it, they just started it over again, a tattered old volume as grey and weathered as the spires of the Hôtel de Ville, stuffed full of wild, impossible saints’ legends. I would have paid two whole francs just to get my hands on it once, to turn its yellowed, crumbling pages and see the absurd tales I’d only ever listened to, wide-eyed, from my seat at the table.

The stories were outrageous: tales of priests who’d trampled high-born ladies and princesses into abject slavery under the name of piety, tales of cruelty and tyranny that made my ears burn hot, that made me stab my scissors point-first into the worm-eaten tabletop without noticing, snapping the tip clean off. I sat through those readings for as many nights as I could bear, until my temples throbbed so hard my head ached, until my nights were full of fevered, restless dreams. Then I decided I would slip away the very second that old tattered book was pulled out, vanishing in the small bustle before the enforced silence settled, before anyone could think to call me back. I’d flee to the unlit hall, or the empty classrooms, pacing fast to keep warm through the cold winter nights, and in the long summer twilight I’d slip up to my dormitory room, lean my head out the wide casement, listen to the band music drifting from the park in the square, and drift away into my own quiet, unshared thoughts.

That stormy evening, I fled the refectory as usual, and slipped up the stairs to the dormitory. Before I even saw a thing, I could feel the room was not empty, though no one stirred, no sound whispered. All the white *lits d’ange* stood empty, every one. I heard a soft, careful drawer slide open, stepped back behind the curtain, and there stood Madame Beck, neat as a pin in her shawl and her crisp white nightcap, rummaging through my toilet table. She’d opened my locked workbox, every drawer one by one, unfolding every scrap of paper, lifting every small trinket, tidying everything back into perfect, neat place, unhasting and unresting, like a star moving across the sky.

I stood there watching, half-amused, half-fascinated. It would have been so easy to step out then, to blow every pretense between us to smithereens, but I had no wish to leave this place. My life held no secret lover, no hidden assignation, nothing for her to find, and I had no desire to lose this easy, light employment. So I turned and fled, down the stairs as swift and silent as the spider that skittered down the banister right beside me. I laughed until my sides ached once I was safe in the empty classroom, tickled half to death at the sight of her so caught up in her own suspicions of a secret romance between me and Dr. John. When the laugh faded, though, a sharp little ache pricked at me, hot tears stung my eyes—not because I cared a farthing for her distrust, but because it reminded me just how far I was from ever having the luxury of a secret, soft romance to hide. The tumult settled after an hour, though, and by the time I climbed back up to my room, I was Lucy Snowe again, calm as ever. Every drawer was locked tight, not a single thing out of place. Even the little bunch of dried white violets a stranger had pressed into my hand once, that I’d tucked between the folds of my best dress, was untouched. I forgave her, right then and there.

The one question that still puzzled me was how Dr. John had known that casket had been thrown into the garden at all, to rush there so fast to look for it. I found myself daring to wonder if I could ask him, outright, the next time I saw him.

Little Georgette was almost fully recovered now, and Dr. John’s visits had grown few and far between. That evening, I’d just finished tucking her in after her lisped little bedtime prayer, when Madame walked into the nursery, took the child’s warm hand, and said Georgette still had a little fever. She glanced at me sharp and quick, said she was going out on errands in a fiacre, that she would call on Dr. John and send him over to see the child, that I should receive him while she was out. It was so obvious a ploy I could have laughed out loud: Georgette was only warm from the July heat, no fever at all, and Madame never went out on errands in the evening, never left the house when Dr. John was due to call. Her little pale green bonnet was perched at a very smart angle as she left, and I smiled to myself at how thoroughly her clever plot was misdirected.

Georgette curled up in my lap, wrapping her small warm arms around my neck, pressing her soft cheek to mine, and I felt tears prickle again at the sweet, uncomplicated affection, so rare in this cold, formal house. I promised her she could sleep as soon as she wanted, if the doctor didn’t turn up in ten more minutes.

Then the doorbell rang, and there was Dr. John, taking the stairs two at a time, Rosine the parlour maid trailing after him, bold as brass, hands stuffed in the pockets of her frilled apron. She chattered away to him without a scrap of shyness, asking right out if he’d found that dropped box the other night, if there was a secret romance the whole house was whispering about. Dr. John laughed, told her he’d been attending a patient in the college right across the street, had seen the box drop out a student’s window, so he’d run over to pick it up. No romance at all, just that. He tipped Rosine a gold coin, and she danced off to answer the door for the day students being picked up.

That whole mystery cleared up in ten seconds flat, and I felt my shoulders relax. But then, all at once, I saw that lattice window across the garden swing open, a white handkerchief waving out of it, and then a little folded white billet fluttered down, landing on a shrub branch right below the window.

I cried out before I could stop myself. Dr. John rushed to the window, told me to go pick it up—no one would notice me slipping out, but if he went, everyone would see. I ran, grabbed the note, brought it straight back, and he tore it to tiny pieces without even reading it. He told me it was no fault of the sweet, innocent girl he was trying to protect, that the man throwing the notes was a worthless, persistent fool, and that the girl was far too refined and unsuspecting to know how to fend off his impertinence. He looked at me, earnest, asking if I would help him watch over her, keep her safe from harm. I nodded, eager to return the small kindnesses he’d done for me before, even as I chuckled to myself, half-convinced he was talking about Madame Beck, the only woman in the whole house refined enough to qualify as his “spotless, unspeakably beautiful” paragon.

He leaned in, voice soft, about to tell me her name. “So spotless, so good, so unspeakably beautiful! Impossible that one house should contain two like her. I allude, of course—”

The door to Madame Beck’s private chamber, opening right off the nursery, clicked. Somebody had been leaning against the keyhole listening for ten minutes straight, and their attempt to hold back a sneeze had backfired spectacularly. Dr. John jumped, startled, and out stepped Madame, perfectly composed, pretending she’d just walked in the door, sneezing again and declaring she’d caught a cold on her errands. She rattled off a long story about the fiacre rides, and the prayer bell rang before Dr. John could ever finish that sentence. I left them there together, half-laughing to myself, that meddlesome, perfectly timed sneeze having spoiled the secret that was almost mine to hear.

No sooner was little Georgette fully recovered than Madame Beck sent her off to stay with relatives in the country, and I felt a quiet, hollow little loss settle in my chest where the child’s bright chatter had once been. I did not complain, though. The school hummed with robust, thrumming young life all around me; any of the other teachers might have been my companion, but I had long since chosen solitude over forced, half-hearted intimacy. I tried each of their proffered friendships in turn. The first was honest enough, but narrow of mind, coarse of feeling, and entirely wrapped up in her own small concerns. The second, Mademoiselle St. Pierre, the Parisienne, was polished and soft-spoken on the surface, but at her core corrupt: no creed, no fixed principle, no deep affection anchored her. Scratch through her thin crust of decorum, and you found a stagnant, greedy slough beneath. She had an insatiable hunger for gifts, and the third teacher — a quiet, unremarkable creature with little personality to mark her — shared that vice, plus one far stranger for a girl not yet twenty-five: a rabid avarice, a love of money for money’s own sake. The sight of a single gold coin would cast a strange green glint in her eyes, a sight I never forgot. Once, as a special mark of favour, she dragged me up to her garret room, unlocked a hidden cupboard, and showed me her hoard: a great pile of heavy five-franc pieces, some fifteen guineas in all. She loved that little stack of coin as a bird loves its nest of eggs, and would come to me day after day, chattering about her savings with a besotted, doting devotion that felt almost unearthly in one so young.

Mademoiselle St. Pierre, by contrast, was free-spending, reckless in her appetites if not her actions — that dark, secret snake side of hers only peeked out once, very cautiously, from between the pages of a tattered bad novel. I glimpsed just a sliver of its scaly hide, and my hasty flash of sharp wrath sent it recoiling, hissing, back into hiding, and she hated me from that hour forward. She was perpetually in debt, her salary spent months in advance on silken gowns, rare perfumes, fancy confections, and imported condiments. She was the coldest, most callous epicure I ever met: thin of face and figure, sallow of cheek, her features perfectly regular, her teeth white as pearls, her lips a thin sharp line, her chin prominent, and her wide, pale eyes open and frozen, craving pleasure but never grateful for the enjoyment of it. She loathed work, and worshipped what she called “pleasure”: a dull, heartless, brainless dissipation of hours that brought no real warmth to anyone.

Madame Beck knew her character perfectly, of course. She once spoke of her to me, with that odd mix of sharp discernment, total indifference, and quiet antipathy that was so uniquely hers. When I asked why she kept such a woman on staff, she answered plainly: “Because it suits my interests.” She pointed out what I had already observed: Mademoiselle St. Pierre possessed an almost unrivalled gift for keeping order among the most wild, undisciplined ranks of students. A sort of petrifying stillness hung about her; no shouting, no violence, no heated temper, and yet she held the whole crowd in check as a breathless frost stills a brawling, churning stream. She was of little use for teaching actual lessons, but for strict surveillance and upholding rules she was invaluable. “I know full well she has no principles, and perhaps no morals,” Madame admitted frankly, then shrugged philosophically. “But her conduct in the classroom is always proper, even marked with a certain dignity. That is all that matters. The pupils and the parents never look any deeper — and why should I?”

It was a strange, frolicsome, noisy little world, that school: great pains were taken to hide the iron chains of routine under garlands of flowers, a subtle undercurrent of Romanism threading every arrangement. Large, soft physical indulgences were permitted as deliberate counterweight to the jealous, tight spiritual restraints that bound every soul within its walls. Every young mind was being raised in quiet slavery, but to keep anyone from stopping to notice that fact, every possible pretext for games, walks, and recreation was seized and stretched to its fullest. The Church strove there, as elsewhere, to raise her children robust of body, feeble of soul: plump, ruddy, hale, giddy, ignorant, unthinking, and uncomplaining. *Eat, drink, and be merry*, she seemed to say. Tend to your bodies. Leave your souls to me. I hold their cure, I guide their course, I guarantee their final fate. It is a bargain every devout Catholic counts himself the gainer by. Lucifer offers the very same terms.

It was the peak of summer, the air thick and honeyed with golden light, when Madame Beck’s house grew as merry as a boarding school could ever be. All day long the broad folding doors and two-leaved casements stood propped wide open; steady, unbroken sunshine seemed to have settled permanently into the air, while clouds drifted far off across the sea, hanging over that dear, mist-kissed isle of my home, and entirely gone from the drier, brighter continent. We lived more in the garden than under roofs: classes were held out under the great leafy arbor, meals were spread there too, and there was a hum of holiday preparation that nearly tipped the usual rules into total liberty. The long autumn vacation was still two months off, but long before that a great, important day awaited celebration: Madame’s own annual fête.

All the arrangements for the fête fell to Mademoiselle St. Pierre, while Madame herself affected to stand aloof, disinterested and entirely unaware of the honour being planned for her. She never knew, or so the polite fiction went, that every year a small subscription was quietly levied across the whole school to buy her a handsome gift. The reader will be so kind as to pretend he never overhears the short, secret consultation that took place in Madame’s own private chamber one afternoon.

“What would you like this year?” her Parisian lieutenant asked her.

“Oh, never mind all that. Let it be. Let the poor children keep their francs,” Madame said, her face soft and benign, all modesty.

Mademoiselle St. Pierre stuck her chin out, rolling her eyes. She knew Madame’s little acts of “kindness” for the grimaces they were, and never for a moment pretended to respect them. “Quick,” she said, coldly. “Name what you want. Jewellery? Porcelain? Haberdashery? Silver?”

“Ah, very well. Two or three spoons, and the same number of forks, in solid silver.”

And the end result was a handsome engraved case, holding three hundred francs’ worth of fine polished plate.

The fête programme was full: the presentation of the silver, a lavish collation spread in the garden, a dramatic performance with pupils and teachers as the actors, then a dance and a late supper. It seemed to me, then, a dazzling, almost magical spectacle, and Mademoiselle St. Pierre managed every detail with perfect, polished skill.

The play was the centrepiece, and required a full month of prior drilling. Casting the roles demanded careful judgement, then lessons in elocution, posture, then the endless fatigue of dozens of rehearsals. Mademoiselle St. Pierre could not handle all that, of course; the flair and knowledge required belonged to another man entirely: M. Paul Emanuel, the fiery little professor of literature. I never once sat in on his dramatic lessons, but I saw him constantly striding across the square hall that linked the dwelling house to the school, and heard his voice ringing through the open doors on warm summer evenings. His name and endless anecdotes of his temper were on every lip. Most of all, our old acquaintance Ginevra Fanshawe, who had been cast in a prominent role in the play, spent nearly all her free time with me, larding every line of her chatter with tales of his odd sayings and wild doings. She swore he was hideously plain, and claimed she was nearly thrown into hysterics at the sound of his heavy step or sharp voice. He was certainly a small, dark man, pungent and austere: even to me he seemed a harsh, vivid apparition, with his close-shorn black hair, broad sallow brow, thin sharp cheek, wide quivering nostrils, piercing gaze, and quick, hurried bearing. Everyone knew he was irritable; you could hear it in his voice as he apostrophised the clumsy, slow-witted squad of amateur actors under his charge. Sometimes he would burst out at the raw young actresses, furious at their hollow understanding of the parts, their cold dead emotion, their feeble delivery. “Listen!” he would cry, his voice ringing through the whole building like a trumpet, and when Ginevra or Mathilde or Blanche mimicked his shout with their tiny piping voices, it was no wonder a groan of scorn or a fierce hiss of rage rewarded their tame little echo.

“You are nothing but dolls!” he thundered once, loud enough for me to hear clear across the garden. “You have no passions, you girls! You feel nothing at all! Your flesh is made of snow, your blood of ice! I want this whole thing to catch fire, to live, to have a soul!”

It was a hopeless ambition. When he finally accepted that the girls could never breathe life into the grand tragedy he had chosen, he tore the whole script to shreds, and showed up the next day with a short, compact little comic vaudeville farce. That they took to far more easily, and in no time at all he had hammered the lines and gestures straight into their smooth, round young heads.

Mademoiselle St. Pierre always sat in on M. Emanuel’s lessons, and everyone said her polished manners, apparent attention, and soft tact impressed him very favourably. She did have the art of pleasing anyone, for an hour or two — but that charm dried up like dew, vanished like gossamer, the moment her attention wandered.

The day before the fête was almost as much of a holiday as the fête itself, devoted entirely to scrubbing, cleaning, rearranging, and decorating the three main schoolrooms. There was no quiet corner left in the whole house, not upstairs nor down, for a solitary person to rest, so I fled out to the garden, and wandered or sat there alone the entire long day, basking in the warm sun, sheltering under the boughs of the old trees, finding gentle company in my own thoughts. I exchanged only two short sentences with another living soul all that day, and I did not feel lonely for a moment. It was enough to slip through the classrooms once or twice to observe the changes being wrought: the little green room and dressing room being fitted out, the tiny stage with its painted scenery being erected, M. Paul Emanuel and Mademoiselle St. Pierre directing the whole operation, and a crowd of giddy, eager pupils — Ginevra Fanshawe amongst them — working cheerfully under their orders.

The great day arrived at last. The sun rose hot and unclouded, and burned hot and unclouded from dawn till dusk. Every door and every window stood thrown wide open, lending a delicious sense of unbounded summer freedom, and freedom was indeed the order of the day. Teachers and pupils stumbled down to breakfast in their dressing-gowns and curl papers, revelling in the luxury of messy, unkempt ease for the morning, like aldermen fasting in preparation for a grand feast. Around nine o’clock, the most important functionary of the entire day arrived: the hairdresser. Sacrilegious as it sounds, he set up his station in the school oratory, right beside the holy water font, the burning candle, and the crucifix, performing the mysteries of his art right there in the sacred space. Each girl was summoned in turn to his chair, and emerged with her head as smooth as a polished shell, parted by perfect crisp white lines, and wreathed in Grecian plaits that shone as if lacquered. I took my turn with the rest, and could hardly believe what the mirror showed me when I went to look later: the garland of woven brown curls piled upon my head amazed me, I feared it could not all be my own hair, and it took several gentle, reassuring tugs to convince me otherwise. I acknowledged then that the hairdresser was a first-rate artist, who made the very most of even the most unpromising materials.

When the oratory was cleared, the dormitory became the scene of elaborate ablutions, layers of fancy underclothes, and endless bedizenment. It has always been an enigma to me how they contrived to spend three mortal hours putting on the gala uniform, so simple in the end: a clear white muslin dress, a bright blue sash (the colours of the Virgin), and a pair of white or pale straw kid gloves. For all that, the effect was perfect, immaculate in its fashion, fit, and freshness, every head dressed with exquisite neatness, a compact, formal taste that suited the full, soft, firm Labassecourien faces, even if it was too stiff for any more flowing, flexible style of beauty. The whole throng of girls looked like a field of bright, spotless snow, and I felt like a tiny, shadowy dark spot lost in that sea of light. I had not the courage to put on a sheer transparent white dress myself. The weather was far too hot for heavy fabric, so I had searched a dozen shops until I found a soft crape-like material of purple-grey, the exact colour of mist lying low over a moor in bloom. My dressmaker had sewn it as carefully as she could, noting wisely that since it was “so sad, so unnoticeable” the cut needed to be all the more neat and flattering. It was well she took such care, for I had no flowers, no jewellery to set it off, and worse: I had no natural rose of blush upon my pale cheeks.

You forget these small deficiencies in the dull routine of daily drudgery, but they force their unwelcome blankness upon you on bright days when everyone else’s beauty shines.

Yet in that shadowy grey gown I felt entirely at ease, a comfort I never would have found in any brighter, more striking garment. Madame Beck kept me in countenance, too: her own dress was almost as quiet as mine, save for a thin gold bracelet and a large bright brooch set with fine stones. We met on the stairs, and she gave me a nod and a smile of quiet approval. It was not that she thought I looked well — that point was unlikely ever to engage her interest — but that I was dressed properly, decently, and Propriety and Decency were the two calm deities of Madame’s worship. She even paused, laid her gloved hand on my shoulder, her embroidered perfumed handkerchief held in her palm, and whispered a sarcastic remark about the other teachers, whom she had just been complimenting to their faces. “Nothing could be more absurd,” she said, “than grown women dressing themselves like girls of fifteen. As for St. Pierre, she looks like an old coquette pretending to be an innocent young ingénue.”

Dressed a full two hours before anyone else, I slipped away not to the garden, where the servants were propping up long tables, setting out seats, and spreading cloths for the collation, but to the schoolrooms: now empty, quiet, cool, and clean, their walls newly painted, their planked floors freshly scoured and still damp, potted fresh blooms adorning every recess, bright new draperies hanging over the great windows.

I withdrew to the first class, a smaller, neater room than the rest, unlocked the glazed bookcase I kept the key to, pulled out a volume whose title promised a quiet few hours of reading, and sat down. The glass door of the classroom opened right out onto the great arbor; acacia boughs caressed the panes, stretching across to meet a rose bush blooming by the opposite lintel, and bees murmured busily and happily amongst its blossoms. I began to read, and soon the soft hum, the shaded green light, the warm lonely calm of my little retreat were stealing the meaning from the page, blurring the words in my vision, luring me down the slow gentle path of reverie into some deep soft dell of dreamland — when the sharpest peal of the street door bell that much-tried instrument had ever rung snatched me rudely back to full consciousness.

The bell had been ringing all morning, of course, as workmen, servants, hairdressers, and dressmakers came and went on their errands, and it was due to ring all afternoon too, as nearly a hundred external pupils arrived in carriages and hackney cabs, and it would not rest all evening when all the parents and friends thronged in to see the play. A sharp ring was nothing out of the ordinary, but this particular peal had a note all its own, that chased my dream away and sent my book tumbling from my knee.

I was just stooping to pick it up when a firm, fast, determined step came striding straight down the vestibule, along the corridor, across the great hall, through the first and second divisions, and into the main schoolroom. The closed door of my quiet sanctuary offered no resistance; it burst open, and a dark paletot and a Greek-style bonnet filled the doorway. Two dark eyes glanced over me vaguely at first, then dived hungrily right into the very core of me.

“That’s her!” a voice said. “I know her. That’s the Englishwoman. So much the worse. Any Englishwoman, prudish as she might be, will suit my purpose, or I shall know why.”

Then, with a sort of stern politeness (I suppose he thought I had not caught the drift of his uncivil mutterings) he spoke in the most mangled, execrable English I had ever heard. “Miss — you will play. I am placed here, no refusal.”

“What can I do for you, M. Paul Emanuel?” I asked, for of course it was he, in a state of great, heated excitement.

“You must play. I will not have you shrink, or frown, or play the prude. I read your character that night you first arrived; I see your capabilities. You can play. You must play.”

“But how, M. Paul? What do you mean?”

“There is no time to lose,” he went on, now switching to rapid French. “We will push all reluctance, all excuses, all silly coyness right up against the wall. You must take a role.”

“In the vaudeville?”

“In the vaudeville. You have said it.”

I gasped, horrified

After Madame Beck’s lavish fête—three slow, soft weeks of half-relaxed study leading up to it, twelve giddy hours of unrushed hilarity and glittering dissipation, and one subsequent day when every girl and teacher dragged through their tasks heavy-limbed and languid from late nights—an abrupt period of reaction settled over the school. Two months of unflinching, hard, focused study stretched ahead, the final stretch of the school year, and the only months of genuine, unbroken work the institution ever saw. Professors, mistresses, and pupils alike poured every scrap of their half-finished preparation for the end-of-year public examinations and prize distribution into those weeks, determined to pull off a showy, impressive exhibition for the crowd of visiting families and local dignitaries that would attend. No small effort was spared for that end.

I barely noticed how the other teachers went about their duties; I had more than enough work of my own to tend to. My task was no light one: I had to drill nearly ninety young Brussels girls in the tangled rules of a science they deemed nearly impossible—the English language—teaching their stubborn tongues to form the sharp lisping dentals and hissing sibilants that felt so unnatural to their native French mouths.

Examination day arrived at last. An awful, weighty day, prepared for with anxious care, dressed for in quiet, no-nonsense haste: no fluttering white gauze, no sky-blue streamers, only grave, neat, close-fitting practical clothes the order of the day. It felt to me as if the entire burden of the day fell on my shoulders alone, of all the female staff. None of the other women teachers were asked to oversee examinations for their own classes; that fiery little autocrat M. Paul Emanuel had claimed every last duty for himself. He gathered every possible rein of control into the palm of one small hand, irefully rejecting any offer of assistance, refusing all colleagues a place on the examiner’s platform. Even Madame Beck, who loved geography dearly and taught it exceedingly well, and had very clearly hoped to run that section of the exams herself, was forced to bow to her despotic kinsman’s will. He pushed every single instructor, male and female, aside, and stood on the examiner’s raised dais entirely alone. There was only one exception he was forced to make: he could not speak English. He had no choice but to leave that branch of the exams in my hands, and he did so with a flash of unmistakeable childish, naive jealousy.

This sharp, able, but fiery and grasping little man’s favourite pastime was waging a constant crusade against the vanity and pride of every human being alive—save himself. He adored public attention and grand displays of his own authority, but could not bear to see anyone else bask in even a fraction of that same spotlight. He quelled and suppressed others’ moments to shine whenever he could, and when he could not, he fumed like a storm bottled up in a glass jar.

The night before the exam, I was walking in the garden alongside the other teachers and all the boarders, when M. Emanuel stepped into the forbidden allée défendue to join me. A cigar smouldered between his lips, his shapeless, dark, distinctly characteristic paletôt hung around him like a menacing shadow, the tassel of his Greek cap cast a stern shadow over his left temple, and his black whiskers curled just like an angry cat’s. The usual bright glitter of his blue eyes was clouded over with storm.

“So,” he began, stopping short to face me abruptly, “you are going to sit enthroned like a queen tomorrow, right there at my side? No doubt you are already savouring all the delights of authority. I can almost see you glowing with it, little ambitious thing!”

The truth was, he could not have been more mistaken. I did not, and could not, value the admiration or good opinion of the next day’s audience anywhere near as highly as he did. Had that crowd held as many personal friends and acquaintances for me as it did for him, I cannot say how I might have felt; but as things stood, the glow of school triumphs cast only a cold, lifeless light on me. I had often wondered, and wondered still, how those same small wins could shine for him with the warmth of a hearth fire. He cared for them perhaps far too much, and I, for my part, cared far too little. Still, his jealousy lit him up, woke every corner of his sharp, odd nature, cast strange, dancing lights across his dark face and into those violet-tinged blue eyes he was so proud of, often claiming his black hair and bright blue irises were one of his finest features. There was a charm to his anger: it was artless, earnest, completely unreasonable, and never for a second hypocritical. I did not bother to deny the complacency he attributed to me. I simply asked where the English examination was slotted to fall, at the start or the end of the day.

“I cannot decide,” he said, “whether to place it at the very beginning, before most of the guests have arrived, so your ambitious little nature will not be gratified by a large audience, or at the very end, when everyone is exhausted, and only a tired, worn-out scrap of attention is left to give you.”

“How cruel you are, Monsieur!” I said, feigning dejection.

“One ought to be cruel to people like you. You are the sort that must be kept down. I know you! I know you far better than anyone else in this house does. Everyone else sees you pass by and thinks a colourless little shadow has just walked past. But I looked into your face once, and that was all I needed.”

“And you are satisfied you understand me perfectly?”

He ignored my question directly, and pressed on. “Were you not glowing with triumph when you pulled off that vaudeville performance? I watched you. I saw that passionate hunger for victory flash across your face. That was not just light in your eyes, that was fire. I have taken note, you see.”

“Whatever feeling I had that day, Monsieur—and I beg you to forgive me for saying so, but you wildly exaggerate both its strength and how much it mattered to me—was completely detached. I did not care for the vaudeville at all. I hated the small, ridiculous part you assigned me. I have not the faintest scrap of sympathy for that audience down below the stage. They are doubtless good people, but do I know them? Are they anything to me? Would I care to be paraded in front of them all again tomorrow? The examination will be nothing but a tedious chore to me, a chore I very much want to get over and done with as soon as possible.”

“Shall I take the whole thing out of your hands?”

“With all my heart, if you are not afraid of failing at it.”

“But I would fail. I only know three phrases of English, and a handful of words: for example, de sonn, de mone, de stare—have I said that correctly? In my opinion, it would be far better to scrap the English exam entirely, no?”

“If Madame agrees, I agree.”

“Truly, wholeheartedly?”

“Very heartily.”

He smoked his cigar in silence for a long minute, then turned to me suddenly.

“Give me your hand,” he said, and all the spite and jealousy melted right off his face, replaced by warm, generous kindness.

“Come, we will not be rivals. We will be friends,” he went on. “The examination will happen, and I will pick a good, prominent slot for you. Instead of vexing and hindering you, as I felt half-inclined to do ten minutes ago—for I have my malicious moods, I have had them ever since I was a small child—I will help you do it perfectly. After all, you are all alone, a stranger in a foreign country, you have your living to earn, your path to carve out for yourself. It would do you good to be known and respected. We will be friends. Do you agree?”

“With all my heart, Monsieur. I am glad of a friend. That means far more to me than any silly triumph could.”

“Pauvrette,” he murmured, and turned and left the alley.

The examinations passed off beautifully, just as he had promised. M. Paul kept his word, and did everything he could to make my part of the day easy and smooth. The next day came the prize distribution, which also went off without a hitch. Then the school broke up. All the pupils packed their trunks and hurried home to their families, and the long vacation began.

That vacation! I do not think I shall ever forget it. On the very first day of the holidays, Madame Beck left to join her own children at the seaside. All three of the other teachers went off to stay with parents or dear friends. Every professor left the city entirely: some travelled to Paris, others to the coastal watering-places, and M. Paul set off on a pilgrimage to Rome. The entire great schoolhouse was left almost empty, save for me, one elderly servant, and a poor, deformed, intellectually disabled little girl no one wanted, a quiet, gentle crétin whose stepmother in a far-off province had refused to let her return home for the holidays.

My heart felt like it was shrinking to nothing inside my chest. Miserable, sharp longings pulled at every string of it. How endless those September days were! How silent, how lifeless! The vast, empty premises felt huge and hollow, void of all warmth. The forsaken garden was grey with the fine dust of the long summer spent in the city. When I looked ahead to the full eight weeks of vacation, I barely knew how I would manage to survive to the end. My spirits had been sinking slowly for months, and now that the constant, steady distraction of work was gone, they plummeted fast. Even looking forward brought no hope: the dumb, blank future offered no comfort, no promise, no small joy to make the present loneliness worth bearing. A heavy, sorrowful indifference to the very fact of my own existence often pressed on me, a despairing resignation that made me long for the swift, quiet end of all earthly things. Alas, when I had all the leisure in the world to look at life as it must look to people like me, I saw nothing but a hopeless desert: tawny, endless sand, with no green fields, no palm trees, no well of fresh water anywhere in sight. All the bright, foolish hopes that sustain other young people, the hopes I did not know and dared not learn, if they ever knocked at the door of my heart, I would draw the heavy bolt shut against them. When they turned away, rejected, I would sometimes cry sad, burning tears—but I had no choice. I dared not give those fragile guests lodging. I was far too afraid of the sin and weakness of presuming I deserved that much joy.

You, my pious religious reader, will no doubt preach me a long, severe sermon about what I have just written. You too, moralist. You, stern stoic, will frown. You, cynic, will sneer. You, epicure, will laugh. Very well. I will accept the sermon, the frown, the sneer, the laugh. Perhaps you are all right. But if you had been placed in my exact circumstances, you might very well have felt and acted just as I did. That first month of vacation was, in truth, a long, black, heavy weight I had to drag through.

The little disabled girl did not seem unhappy at all. I fed her well, kept her warm, and she asked for nothing more than food and sunshine, or fire when the sun did not come out. Her weak, clouded faculties loved stillness and idleness: her brain, her eyes, her ears, her heart slept contentedly, and since they could not wake to work, that soft lethargy was paradise for her.

Three weeks of that vacation were hot, bright, and dry. But the fourth and fifth weeks brought tempests and endless, lashing rain. I cannot say why that shift in the weather settled so cruelly over me, why the raging storms and beating rain crushed me under a heavier paralysis than I had ever felt when the air was still and clear. But it was so, and my frayed nervous system could barely survive those long days and nights trapped alone in that huge, empty house. I prayed constantly to heaven for consolation and support, and that terrible, heavy conviction would grip me with cruel force: that fate was my permanent, unappeasable enemy. I did not, in my heart, rail against the mercy or justice of God for that. I accepted it as part of his great plan that some people are meant to suffer deeply all their lives, and I knew with a trembling certainty that I was one of those people.

It was a small relief when a kind old aunt of the little girl’s arrived one day, and took my strange, quiet deformed companion away. The hapless child had been a heavy charge at times. I could not take her out past the garden gates, and I could not leave her alone for even a single minute: her poor warped mind had a vague, unguided impulse to mischief, a random, aimless streak of small cruelty that made constant vigilance non-negotiable. She almost never spoke, and would sit for hours on end moping and pulling the most indescribable, grotesque grimaces with her face, so that keeping her company felt less like spending time with a fellow human, and more like being locked up with some strange, untamed wild animal. There were personal care tasks that required the steady nerve of a hospital nurse, and many a time my resolve would falter, fall utterly sick at the unpleasantness of the work. Those duties should never have fallen to me in the first place. A different servant had tended to her before, but that woman had left in the mad rush of everyone departing for the holidays, and no replacement had been arranged. That unlooked-for burden was not the least of the many trials I had borne in my life. But for all that it was menial and distasteful, my mental pain was far more wasting, far more wearing. Tending to the little girl often left me too exhausted to eat, sent me faint out to the fresh air by the well in the courtyard—but those duties never wrung my heart, never brimmed my eyes with tears scalding hot as molten metal.

Once the little girl was gone, I was free to walk out whenever I pleased. At first I lacked the courage to wander far from the Rue Fossette, but little by little I made my way to the city gates, passed through them, and began wandering for miles down paved country roads, through fields, past both Catholic and Protestant cemeteries, past quiet farmsteads, out to narrow lanes and tiny woods, I knew not where. An invisible goad pushed me onward, a quiet fever in my bones forbade me to rest, and the ravenous, unmet hunger for any kind of companionship gnawed at my soul. I often walked all day long, through the burning noon, the dry golden afternoon, the soft dusky evening, and returned to the school just as the moon was rising.

As I wandered all alone, I would picture the lives of all my acquaintances, living their happy holidays far away. Madame Beck was at a cheerful seaside resort with her children, her mother, and a whole crowd of friends who had all travelled there for relaxation. Zélie St. Pierre was in Paris with her relatives. All the other teachers were home with their families. Ginevra Fanshawe, though, had been taken on a lovely tour south by her wealthy relations. Ginevra seemed to me the happiest of all. She was travelling through country stitched with beautiful scenery, those soft September suns shining down on fertile plains where the last of the harvest and the ripening vines basked in their golden light. Those crystal harvest moons rose over her view, hanging low above blue, rolling distant hills.

But none of that was mine. I felt those same autumn suns on my face, saw those same harvest moons hanging in the sky, and I almost wished I could sink down deep under the earth and the turf, hidden away from their light forever, because I could not make that beauty belong to me, could not make a friend of the sun or the moon. But Ginevra had a soft, constant guardian spirit at her side, someone who gave her unending strength and comfort, who brightened her days and made even her dark nights gentle. The kindest of all the good spirits that watch over humanity was shielding her with his wings, holding a soft canopy over her head. True Love followed Ginevra everywhere. She was never alone. It seemed impossible to me that she could ever be unaware of that warm, loving presence. I imagined her secretly grateful, loving her adoring hero now with quiet shyness, but vowing one day to show him just how deeply she cared. I pictured her faithful hero half aware of that coy fondness, sustained and comforted by the knowledge of it. I wove a thin, electric thread of sympathy between them, a fine chain of unspoken mutual understanding that kept their bond strong even through a hundred leagues of separation, carrying their prayers and quiet wishes back and forth across every hill and hollow. Little by little, Ginevra turned into a kind of quiet heroine in my overwrought mind. One day, noticing that strange illusion taking root, I thought to myself, My nerves are fraying too thin. My mind has suffered too long, an illness is taking hold in it—what am I to do? How am I to keep well?

There was no possible way to stay well under those circumstances. At last, a day and night of almost unendurable, agonizing depression gave way to physical fever, and I was forced to take to my bed. Around that time, the soft Indian summer drew to a close, and the rough equinoctial storms began. For nine dark, wet days, the hours rushing by loud with hurricane wind and drumming rain, I lay in a strange fever of the nerves and blood. Sleep vanished entirely. I would rise in the dead of night, look all around my dark room for her, beg her earnestly to come back to me. Only the rattle of the window panes and the cry of the howling blast answered me. Sleep would not come.

I was wrong, though. She did come once, but in anger. Impatient of my constant begging, she brought a cruel, vengeful dream with her. By the clock of the Church of St. Jean Baptiste, that dream lasted no more than fifteen short minutes—barely any time at all, but enough to wring my whole frame with a kind of anguish I had never known before, to leave me with a nameless experience that had the hue, the look, the terror, the very tone of a visitation from eternity. Between twelve and one that night, a cup was forced to my lips, black, strong, strange, drawn from no ordinary well, but filled seething hot from a bottomless, boundless sea

Where my soul wandered in that long, dark swoon I can never say. Whatever sights it drank in, whatever far shores it drifted to on that wild, storm-lashed night, it kept its secrets sealed tight: no whisper drifting back to memory, no trace left for imagination to chase. Perhaps it climbed upward, caught sight of its eternal home, and dared to hope it might rest at last, free of the aching, heavy weight of the frail mortal frame it had grown so tired of dragging around. Just as it lingered on heaven’s threshold, daring to dream of release, an angel may have touched its shoulder, wept to turn it away, and guided it back, all shuddering and unwilling, to that cold, wasted body it had almost escaped.

I know it re-entered that prison of flesh with a sharp, pained gasp, with a long, trembling shiver, dragged back against its will. Spirit and substance, those two parted mates, fought to rejoin rather than embraced: the first glimmer of returning sight bled red, as if my eyes swam in thick blood; sound rushed back in a roaring thunderclap, every distant creak and patter of rain loud enough to make me flinch; consciousness returned wrapped in raw, unnameable fear. I sat bolt upright, appalled, wondering what strange, unearthly realm I had woken into, what alien beings surrounded me. At first nothing I saw made sense: a wall was no wall, a lamp no lamp. I would have understood a ghost just as well as I understood the most ordinary household object—every shape my eyes fell on seemed spectral, something not quite of this world. But slowly, my frayed faculties settled back into their places, and the old tired human clock of my body began ticking at its usual, steady pace once more.

Even then, I had no clue where I was. Only slowly did I realize I had been carried far from the church steps where I had fainted: no portico stone pressed into my back now, the raging night and tempest shut out solidly by walls, windows, a ceiling overhead. Someone had brought me inside, to a house—but whose?

My first half-delirious thought was the Rue Fossette pensionnat. Still foggy with faintness, I tried to place which room they had laid me in—the great shared dormitory, or one of the tiny little single chambers? I was utterly puzzled, for none of the furniture peeking through my haze lined up with any room I knew there. No empty white iron beds, no long row of tall bare windows greeted me. “Surely they did not carry me to Madame Beck’s own private chamber,” I thought, and then my gaze fell on an easy chair draped in rich blue damask. Other matching cushioned seats swam into view, until I could make out the whole shape of a cozy parlour, a crackling wood fire glowing on a polished hearth, a carpet stitched with bright blue arabesques over a soft fawn ground. Pale walls were strung with an endless, tangled garland of azure forget-me-nots woven through thousands of tiny gold leaves and tendrils. A gilded mirror hung between two windows draped in the same blue damask, and in its glass I saw myself stretched out not in a bed, but on a sofa. I looked like a ghost: eyes too large and hollow, dark hair stark against my thin, ashen face. It was plain, from every piece of furniture, every placement of doors, windows and hearth, that I was in a stranger’s house, in a room I had never stepped foot in before.

Or—was it? That blue armchair looked familiar, the scroll-shaped couch, the round centre table covered in blue cloth bordered with autumn-hued foliage, and most of all, two little stools with embroidered covers, that tiny ebony chair whose seat and back were stitched with bright flower clusters on a dark background.

A jolt ran through me. Old, beloved acquaintances smiled out of every nook, echoes of auld lang syne rising soft and warm from every surface. Over the mantel hung two oval miniatures: I could recite every detail of their subjects by heart, the pearls strung in their high powdered wigs, the velvet ribbons circling their white throats, the soft swell of their full muslin neckerchiefs, the delicate pattern of lace ruffs at their cuffs. On the mantelshelf sat two china vases, the tiny fragments of a miniature tea set as smooth as enamel and thin as eggshell, a white alabaster classic group under a glass dome. I could name every tiny flaw, every faint crack on every last one of them, as clearly as any clairvoyant. Above all, there was a pair of hand-screens, covered in fine pencil drawings as sharp as line engravings. My eyes ached looking at them, recalling long hours of my girlhood, when I had guided that tedious, fussy schoolgirl pencil stroke by stroke, my thin young fingers moving over the page.

Where on earth was I? Not just in what corner of the world, but what year? All these objects belonged to a distant country, to days long past. I had said goodbye to them ten years prior. I had not set eyes on a single one since I was fourteen years old. I gasped out loud, “Where am I?”

A figure I had not noticed before stirred, rose, and stepped forward, a shape that clashed with the familiar old decor and tangled the riddle even further: a local bonne, in a plain print dress and common work cap. She spoke no French I knew, no English at all, and I could make no sense of her thick patois. But she bathed my forehead and temples with cool scented water, propped my cushions higher, gestured for me not to speak, then went back to her post at the foot of the sofa, knitting.

Too weak to unpick the mystery, I told myself it was a mistake, a fever dream, a hallucination—even as I knew I was not asleep, not delirious, not mistaken. All these things, the blue damask, the little hand-screens, the miniatures, were exact in every last detail to the drawing room in my godmother’s house in Bretton. It felt as if I had been spirited away like Bedreddin Hassan from the Arabian Nights, snatched from my doorstep in Villette and dropped thousands of miles away in the drawing room of the home I had loved as a girl, the home I had thought long gone. But that hearth had gone cold years ago, I knew, the household scattered to the winds.

The bonne noticed my wide, perturbed gaze, set down her knitting, poured water and measured drops from a small phial, and held the glass to my lips. I drank the strange sweet draught passively, and at once a soft tide of calm washed over my raw brain. The ache of weakness drained from my limbs, my muscles went loose and warm, and I drifted off into deep, dreamless sleep.

When I woke, everything was changed again. Bright autumn daylight filled the room—soft, leaden light of a raw, blustery October day, not warm summer sun. I thought for a moment I was back in the pensionnat, hearing rain lash the casement and wind howl through the garden trees, but when I pushed back the dimity curtains of the bed, I found myself in a tiny cabinet room, walls painted soft sea-green. The familiar furniture was all here too, the little green chintz armchair, the black carved foliated frame of the toilet mirror, the pale green china washbasin, the pincushion frilled with lace, stitched with my own hands. I turned it over, and there was the gold bead cipher: L.L.B. — Louisa Lucy Bretton, my godmother’s initials.

My heart hammered. Was I in England? At Bretton? I hauled myself up to yank up the blind, half expecting to see the old familiar grey stone buildings of St. Ann’s Street, the minster towers rising in the distance. Instead, I looked out over a grassy lawn terrace, tall old beech trees groaning in the gale, yellow leaves swirling across the empty avenue. The place was beautiful, and utterly unknown to me.

I sank back on the bed, turning my face away from the bewildering sights, until my gaze fell on a gilded framed portrait hanging on the wall: a watercolour sketch of a boy of sixteen, fair cheeked, hair glowing gold in the light, laughing, bright-eyed. It was the portrait of Graham Bretton, I realized at once, the one that used to hang over the breakfast room mantel. I remembered climbing up on a music stool as a girl to take it down, little Polly Home in my arms, both of us staring up at that bright face, until the little girl had begged me to put her down, too stirred to speak. “Graham,” I breathed the name out loud, half to myself.

“Graham?” echoed a soft, surprised voice at my bedside. “Do you want Graham?”

I turned, and my breath caught. There she stood, my godmother Mrs. Bretton, tall, stately, dressed in quiet widow’s silk, the same motherly warm face I remembered from my childhood, hardly aged a day. My pulse fluttered, blood draining from my cheeks, cold under the skin. “Madam, where am I?” I asked.

“In a safe, quiet house, my dear. You fainted on the church steps last night in the storm, and my son brought you back here. You must rest easy now, until you are well again,” she said, in that steady, no-nonsense English voice I knew so well. She told me she had just arrived in Villette from England, that her son, Dr. John, had recognized me as the English teacher from the Rue Fossette pensionnat.

I ate a morsel of dry toast and a sip of tea that morning, the first solid food I had tasted in nine days, and strength seeped slowly back into my bones. By evening, I was restless with confinement, sick of the tiny room, longing for firelight. I dressed myself, and Mrs. Bretton offered me her arm to walk downstairs, to the blue damask parlour I had first woken in. It was warm there, amber lamplight spilling over the rug, the fire glowing red, the familiar silver tea urn, the spiced seed cake Graham had always loved sitting on the table. I waited, watching Mrs. Bretton glance at her watch, listening for the sound of her son’s return through the storm. Soon enough, the front gate clanged shut, boots crunched on the gravel path, and Dr. John strode into the room, tall, broad-shouldered, the bright boy from the portrait all grown into a handsome, kind-faced man.

He rose courteously to greet me, smiling. “You must be feeling much better, then,” he said. “I was so alarmed when I found you last night, but I had to rush off to a dying patient, I am sorry I left you. Tell me, what brought on that faint in the storm?”

I met his eyes, steady and calm, and smiled softly.

“Much better,” I said. “Much better, I thank you, Dr. John.”

To fight the unruly leanings of your own heart, to bend your wild, untamed natural bent to the steady governance of reason, might seem like a useless, futile struggle at first—but it does good, in the end. It turns your steps, however gently, down the path that calm judgement approves, the path that raw, heedless feeling so often pushes you to abandon. It smooths the tenor of your days, makes life more even, quieter on its surface: and that is the only surface the eyes of the world, your fellow weak, fallible mortals, will ever stop to see. What lies beneath? Leave that to God. No equal of yours, no man as frail as you, is fit to judge the secrets of the spirit He gave. Bring all your hidden pains to your Maker, kneel in the dark and beg Him for light when you are blinded, for strength when you are too weak to stand, for patience when every last thread of it is frayed thin.
Surely, one day, though it may not be the hour you beg for, the stagnant waters of the healing pool will stir. Some herald will descend, in a shape you never dreamed of, never dared to bleed for, to lead the maimed, the blind, the heartbroken down to bathe. Herald, come quickly! Thousands huddle round the edges of that pool, weeping for years on end, watching it lie still. Heaven’s hours stretch long to mortal eyes; the paths of its messengers are so wide they can circle whole ages, whole generations, before they turn back. How many millions of mourning, wounded souls will ever meet only Azrael, the angel of death, as their first and only visitor?

I dragged myself out of bed the next morning, my whole frame trembling so hard I could barely fasten my gown. I sipped cold water from the carafe on my washstand, trying to brace that trembling weakness, when Mrs. Bretton burst through the door.
“Now this is absurd!” she announced, in that bright, brusque, no-nonsense tone I had always loved, the same sharp energy she used to turn on her son when he was a boy and tried to dodge her orders. Before I could protest, she had me tucked back under the covers of the soft French bed, my clothes smoothed out of sight. “You stay right there until afternoon,” she said firmly. “My boy left strict orders before he went out this morning, and you will obey them—he is master of this house, and no one here crosses him. I will bring you your breakfast presently.”
She did, carrying the tray herself, no servant’s hand touching it, and perched on the edge of the bed while I ate. Not every respected friend can be that kind of comfort, you know: the kind whose very presence in a sick room feels like light, whose hands make bread taste sweeter, whose step alone chases the gloom out of a corner. Mrs. Bretton had always been that for me. Her lively dark eyes, her warm quick smile, her brisk, self-assured way of moving, felt like stepping out into bright, clean sea air. Even now, when she was past middle age, she moved with the quickness of a five-and-twenty-year-old.
“I would bring my needlework and sit with you the whole day,” she said, taking my empty teacup when I was done, “but that overbearing John Graham put his foot down. ‘Mamma,’ he told me as he left, ‘don’t chatter my god-daughter to exhaustion, you keep to your own rooms and spare her your company.’ He thinks you’ve had a nervous fever, Lucy—was that close to the mark?”
I told her I could not name exactly what had ailed me, only that I had suffered terribly, most of all in my mind. I did not dare say more. That hale, serene, unbroken woman had never known the dark, storm-wracked corners of loneliness where I had lately wandered. She was a stately full-rigged ship, sailing always on smooth, sunlit seas, with a brave crew and a sure captain, while I was a scuffed little lifeboat, kept dry and forgotten in a dark shed, only dragged out when the waves were mountain-high and the sky was black. I had no tales of that storm to tell her.

She left me then, and I lay content. The quiet day stretched soft around me, my small pale room all white and pale green, like a cave sunk under the sea. The cornice was edged with shell carvings, the angles of the ceiling had white mouldings that looked like dolphins, the little red satin pincushion on the dresser glowed like a piece of coral. When I closed my eyes, the wind sounded like a slow, receding tide, lapping far away against a rock base, and every footstep of the housemaid on the stairs was muffled, soft, as if it came from far above the surface of the water.
Evening crept in, and Martha brought a lamp. Dressed now, and far steadier on my feet than I had been at dawn, I walked down the stairs unassisted to the blue saloon. Dr. John was already there, standing in the window recess, reading the fine print of a newspaper by the fading dusk light. The fire glowed bright, the lamp on the table was still unlit, and Mrs. Bretton was curled half-asleep in her deep cushioned armchair, actually napping.
He saw me at once, and stepped forward on tiptoe, so as not to wake his mother, his low mellow voice soft as a lullaby.
“This little place is called La Terrasse,” he murmured, gesturing out the window. “You can’t see it from the main road, the chaussée—you turn down a lane a mile past the Porte de Crécy, that opens out into an avenue of trees, leading right here. It’s not a modern house, built in the old style of the Basse-Ville, more a small manor than a château. They named it for that wide turfed walk out front, that runs down grassy steps to the avenue. Look—there’s the moon.”
I followed his gaze. The moon was rising rosy and fiery over the bank of trees, rising fast until the red faded to pure gold, and she floated, stainless, into a soft calm sky. I saw him sigh, quiet to himself, as he watched her. I knew that sigh was not for the moon—it was for Ginevra Fanshawe. I was just about to speak her name, to give him the chance to pour out all the thoughts he had been holding back, when he spoke first.
“I went straight to Rue Fossette first thing this morning,” he said, turning away from the moon to sit down. “Told the cook you were safe, and do you know? She hadn’t even noticed you were gone. Goton had only been bringing you tisane and crusts of bread, and when you turned them away a dozen times, she got tired of the walk and only came once a day to make your bed. No one had checked on you.”
I laughed a little, and explained that Madame Beck could not have foreseen I would fall ill, that no one at the pensionnat was to blame for my loneliness. He pressed me, then, about my religion, asking if I was a Catholic, and I stared, confused. That was when he told me the full story of how he had found me: his way home through the dark, wet Basse-Ville the night before, seeing Père Silas the good old priest holding my fainting body by the gate of the Béguinage. The priest had watched me leave his church after that desperate confession I had made, I told him, when I was so lonely I had poured all my dreary, desperate complaints out to a stranger, because I had not a single other soul to speak to. I had told him nothing of my home, no names, no addresses, so Père Silas still did not know where I stayed at the pensionnat.
By the time he finished telling me the whole tale, Mrs. Bretton was waking up, squaring her shoulders and indignantly denying she had been napping at all. Graham teased her, calling her Titania, and she snapped back that he looked like Bottom, growing stout and beefy like a little John Bull, and threatened to disown him if he got too fat. The two of them bantered, laughing, across the fire, and I sat quiet, watching them, warm and soft inside. I knew he still loved Ginevra, that his heart was given over to that vain, silly girl, but for that quiet evening, at least, I was safe here, in this warm house, with the two people who cared to look after me. That night, I fell asleep not crying, not trembling, but on a bed of soft, pleasant thoughts, no storm to disturb my rest.

The first bright, soft-edged days of my stay at the Terrace stretched out in a hush of held breath. Every time Graham drifted to lean against the arm of my sofa, every time his long, pacing steps slowed near the corner of the parlor where I sat at my needlework, every time his brow furrowed into that preoccupied, uncharacteristic gravity, my mind flew straight to Miss Ginevra Fanshawe. I kept my ears pricked, my sympathy stored brim-full like a honey jar set out ready to pour, my patience armed and waiting for the tender, fumbling confession I was sure would soon fall from his lips.

At last, after a small, visible inward struggle I had the grace not to name aloud, he broached the subject gently, almost anonymously.
“Your friend is travelling through her vacation, I hear?”
“Friend, forsooth!” I thought to myself, but I held my tongue. There was no use contradicting him; I would bear the soft impeachment. Still, I could not help teasing a little, and asked which friend he meant.

He dragged a chair over to my work-table, reached out, and seized a full reel of silk thread, which he proceeded to unwind recklessly into a tangled, loose heap across the linen I was mending.
“Ginevra—Miss Fanshawe—left with the Cholmondeleys for a tour of the south of France, did she not?”
“She did.”
“Do the two of you correspond?”
“It will surprise you to hear I never once thought to beg that privilege.”
“You have seen samples of her writing, then?”
“Yes, several letters she sent to her uncle, M. de Bassompierre.”
“They cannot be lacking in wit and naïveté, surely? Her soul is all sparkle, no guile at all.”
“Her letters are plain enough for even the hasty reader to parse,” I told him. The truth was, Ginevra’s epistles to her wealthy kinsman were little more than blunt, unvarnished requests for more pocket money, no subtlety or sentiment about them.
“And her handwriting? It must be pretty, light, so ladylike, I should think.”
It was, and I said as much.
“I verily believe everything she does is done perfectly,” Dr. John said, and when I did not immediately leap to agree, he pressed, “You know her better than most. Can you name a single point in which she falls short?”
“She does a great many things very well,” I allowed, adding under my breath in my own head: Flirtation chief among them.
“When do you suppose she will return to town?” he asked next.

“Pardon me, Dr. John, I must set this straight. You do me too much honor to imagine I hold any intimate friendship with Miss Fanshawe. I have never been the keeper of her secrets or her plans. Her particular friends move in a sphere far above mine—among the Cholmondeleys, for example.”

The fool actually mistook my disclaimer for the pique of a jealous, discarded confidante! “Do not judge her too harshly,” he said, patting my hand lightly. “The glitter of fashion misleads her for now, but soon she will see how hollow those people are, and come back to you all the fonder, all the more trusting. I know the Cholmondeleys—superficial, showy, selfish. Rest assured, at heart Ginevra values you more than a dozen of her fine new friends.”

I only said, “You are very kind.” The urge to deny that I was pining for Ginevra’s attention burned hot on my tongue, but I swallowed it down. I submitted to being seen as the humiliated, cast-aside former favorite of the distinguished Miss Fanshawe—but I will own to you, it was a bitter, heavy submission.

“And you see,” Graham went on, “while I can comfort you, I cannot give myself that same solace. I cannot hope she will ever see how sincere my regard is. That worthless De Hamal, I fear he amuses her. What a wretched, foolish delusion she is caught in!”

That was the last straw. My patience, worn thin as it was by recent illness and weeks of held tongue, snapped all at once, without warning.
“Dr. Bretton,” I broke out sharply, “there is no delusion half so great as your own. On every subject but one you are frank, clear-sighted, good and true. On this one, you are no better than a slave. I swear, where Miss Fanshawe is concerned, you do not deserve respect. And you have none from me.”

I stood up so fast my sewing basket tipped, and I left the room trembling with anger, before he could speak a single word in reply.

That scene played out in the morning. When I had to meet him again that evening by the light of the parlor lamp, I saw at once I had done more harm than I meant. Graham was no common man, no vulgar, thin-skinned creature. His nature was broad, vigorous, shaped on generous lines, but laced through with a fine, almost feminine delicacy of feeling far keener than anyone who had not jostled his sharp, sensitive nerves would ever guess. He was not the sort to parade his hurts, nor was he unkind—if you laid your need before him, his purse was open, if you spoke your grief aloud, he never turned a deaf ear. But he was no mind-reader, no seer of unspoken feeling, and that night he carried the weight of my words quiet in his bones.

He entered the room, his face grave, almost cold even to his mother, but there was no malice, no petty resentment in the set of his handsome features. He was turning my accusation over in his head, wondering if I was right, if his foolish passion really was the root of all the disappointment that had gnawed at his peace for months. I scrambled to pull his chair out for him before the servant could reach it, handed him his tea with shaking, over-careful hands. “Thank you, Lucy,” he said, in that same warm, full, pleasant tone of his that my ear had always loved so well.

I knew there was only one course to take: I had to make amends for my hasty, cruel outburst, or I would never sleep a wink that night. The thought of being at odds with Dr. John, of that quiet rift stretching on between us, was worse than the cold silence of the school, worse than the stagnant stillness of the convent pensionnat. I would never again cross him over Ginevra, I swore to myself—let her fly as high as his fondest fancies could carry her, let her be set as a star among the constellations of his imagination, I would never argue the point again.

I tried again and again to catch his eye across the tea table, but every time our glances met he looked away, quiet, reading his book. I longed to go sit beside him, but feared if I did he would meet me with all the indignation I knew I deserved. When his mother left the room at last, I could bear the regret no longer, and murmured, soft as a breath, “Dr. Bretton.”

He looked up at once, his eyes not cold, not sharp, his mouth no cynical twist. His nature was too mellow, too generous to sour over one angry word.
“Dr. Bretton, forgive my hasty words. Do, please forgive them.”
He smiled, the soft, familiar smile I knew so well. “Perhaps I deserved them, Lucy. If you do not respect me, it must be because I am not respectable. I fear I am a clumsy fool—whatever I do, it seems I cannot make the one I want to please care for me at all.”
“That is not something you can be sure of, and even if it were, it is not a flaw in your character, it is only a flaw in someone else’s perception. Let me unsay every angry word I spoke. In all things, great and small, I respect you deeply. If you think too little of yourself and too much of others? That is no flaw—it is a virtue.”
“Can I think too much of Ginevra?”
“I believe you can. You believe you cannot. Let us agree to differ. Only please say you pardon me. That is all I ask.”
“Do you think I would hold ill will against you for one honest, heated word?” he laughed softly. “I see you are not angry. But there is no need to nurse that heartache—you wounded me a little, yes, but the pain is gone now. I do not only forgive you, Lucy. I am grateful to you. You are the only sincere well-wisher I have, who will not lie to me to spare my feelings.”
“I am your sincere well-wisher,” I told him, and my voice was softer than I meant it to be.

And that was the end of our quarrel. If you find my opinion of Dr. John shifts as this story goes on, I beg you pardon the seeming inconsistency: I write only what I felt in the moment, what I saw of him as it revealed itself to me, day by day.

He proved the fineness of his nature by being kinder to me after that misunderstanding than he had ever been before. The thin, cold, translucent screen of ice that had glazed over all our interactions since we were children, that quiet reserve we had never quite pierced, dissolved in the heat of those angry, honest words. From that day on, he never spoke to me in ceremony, never stood on polite pretense. He knew I would always listen, always care, no matter what he chose to tell me.

Which meant, of course, that I heard a great many things about “Ginevra.” He spoke of her so fondly, so full of praise for her beauty, her sweetness, her supposed innocence, that even I, who knew exactly how vain and money-hungry she was, began to see her through the glow of his devotion, a soft, reflected light settling over even my plain, unvarnished impression of her. I knew he often spoke complete nonsense about her, but I made myself endlessly, unfailingly patient. I had learned my lesson: the pain of hurting him, of grieving him even a little, was worse than any irritation at his foolish fondness. I grew selfish, in a soft, new way: I could not deny myself the quiet pleasure of indulging his mood, of bending to his will, of being the only person he could speak freely to.

He still fretted, still despaired that he would never win her, and it made me stubbornly certain that Ginevra was only coquetting to tease him, that at heart she treasured every word and every look he gave her. One day, to soothe his fretting, I told him flat that I was positive she would accept him in the end.
“Positive? That is easy enough to say—but what grounds do you have for that certainty?” he pressed.
“The very best grounds.”
“Tell me what they are, Lucy. Tell me the full explanation, no evasions.”
“I will. You are a lavish, generous man. If Père Silas ever converted you, you would give alms to all his poor, hang tapers on every altar, and fill the shrine of your favorite saint with offerings fit for a king. Ginevra, Dr. John—”
“Hush,” he said, holding up a hand.
“I will not hush! I will speak plain. Ginevra’s hands have been filled with your gifts more times than I can count. You have tracked down the costliest, rarest flowers for her, thought up delicate, thoughtful presents that any woman would have been charmed by. She owns a whole set of fine jewelry, too, that you paid for—your generosity verged on outright extravagance to buy those for her.”

The bright flush that Ginevra herself had never once bothered to show when accepting his gifts, burned hot across his face. “Nonsense,” he said, snipping violently through a skein of my silk with my sewing scissors. “I offered those things to please myself. I felt she did me a favor just to accept them.”
“She did far more than a favor. She pledged her honor, almost, to return that regard. If she will not pay you back in affection, she ought to hand you back every last penny’s worth of those jewels in gold.”
“You do not understand her,” he protested. “She is far too disinterested to care for my gifts, too simple to even guess their value.”

I laughed out loud. I had heard Ginevra sit for hours, naming the exact price of every trinket she owned, scheming and plotting how to wheedle more money out of her uncle or her admirers—money had been the primary preoccupation of her thoughts for years, young as she was.
“Whenever I laid a small, trivial gift in her lap,” he went on, “she was always so cool, so unmoved. No eagerness to take it, no visible pleasure in looking at it. She would only keep the bouquet out of gentle reluctance to grieve me, or let me fasten a bracelet on her ivory arm without even glancing at the trinket, no matter how pretty it was. The glitter never dazzled her bright eyes. How can I take that as a sign she favors me? If I gave her every penny I owned and she took it, I still would not dare think it brought me a single step closer—she is far above sordid little considerations like that.”

“Dr. John,” I began, “Love is blind—”
And then a sharp, subtle, amused blue glint sped sideways from his eye. It reminded me of his old childhood portrait, of the boy I had known long ago. For a second, I half suspected that all his professed belief in Ginevra’s naivete was at least a little bit of an act. For all his passion for her beauty, maybe he was not nearly as fooled as he pretended, maybe he saw every one of her foibles clear as day, and loved her anyway.
Chance glance, or intentional, real or imagined—it was enough. The conversation ended there.

My stay at La Terrasse stretched two full weeks past the end of vacation, a small, soft reprieve Mrs. Bretton had wangled for me out of pure kindness. Her son had casually declared one afternoon that “Lucy is still far too weak to go back to that den of a boarding school,” and no sooner had the words left his mouth than the good lady had driven straight to Rue Fossette to speak to Madame Beck, pleading that extra rest and gentle change were critical to my full recovery.

What followed was a favour I could very happily have done without: a polite, entirely uninvited call from Madame Beck herself.
One fine, sun-dappled day, she rode all the way out to the chateau in a hired fiacre, no doubt determined to see exactly what sort of household Dr. John inhabited. The pleasant grounds and crisp, cheerful interiors far outstripped her expectations; she praised every corner of the house, pronounced the blue salon “une pièce magnifique,” heaped me with gushing compliments on my good fortune to have secured friends “so worthy, so kind, so respectable.” When Dr. John walked in the door, she flew to him with all the buoyancy of a sparrow, chattering a mile a minute in bright, florid French, fawning over his “château,” his “dignified chatelaine of a mother,” and his own flourishing good looks. He smiled through it all, warm and amused, used to her endless flow of words. That day, Madame Beck was at her very best: a living Catherine wheel spinning sparks of compliments, delight, and easy affability. Half on purpose, half to ask a quick question about school business, I followed her out to the fiacre, and leaned in to speak to her just as the door clicked shut. In that single heartbeat, the brightness vanished. Where a moment before there had been nothing but jests and sparkles, she sat sterner than a judge, graver than any sage. Strange, sharp little woman.

I went back inside and teased Dr. John mercilessly about her obvious fondness for him, and he laughed until his eyes crinkled, mimicking her rapid, voluble delivery as he quoted back some of her more over-the-top lines. He had a sharp, perfect sense of humour, and when he could manage to forget Miss Fanshawe for an hour, he was the very best company in the world.

They say sitting in calm, sweet sunshine restores strength to the weak, and I knew that truth firsthand. When little Georgette Beck had been ill, I would carry her in my arms and walk the garden paths for hours under a wall draped with ripening grapes, the southern sun warming her pale small frame as it swelled the fruit on the vines. There are human natures just as warm, just as generous: the kind whose company nourishes the poor in spirit as well as noontide sun nourishes the feeble in body. Both Dr. John and his mother had that rare gift. They spread happiness as instinctively as some people spread misery: no fuss, no fanfare, hardly even conscious of how much good they did, little small pleasant plans rising to their lips unbidden every single day I stayed with them. For all his packed schedule, Dr. John always made time to join me and his mother on our short little excursions. I never could figure how he managed it, with so many patients to see, but by dint of quiet, steady system, he carved out an hour of free time for himself every single day. I saw him work himself to the bone often, but never overstretched, never irritable, never confused. Everything he did came with the easy, unforced grace of someone who knew his own strength, brimming with unbroken, bright good humour. In those two happy weeks, I saw more of Villette, its surrounding countryside, and its people than I had in the full eight months I’d lived there before. He took me to hidden little corners of the town I’d never even heard of, told me story after story of their history, and never once made me feel like I was a burden for listening. He never droned on with dry generalizations or borrowed trite phrases from books; he spoke from his own sharp, observant experience, full of little vivid details that made every story feel fresh and bright.

His mother was a kind woman, but Dr. John’s benevolence ran far deeper. When I accompanied him on his rounds in the crowded, poor Basse-Ville quarter, I saw his errands were as much the work of a philanthropist as a doctor.
