He was quietly, habitually, unthinkingly doing endless small acts of good for the city’s most wretched residents, never pausing to pat himself on the back for it. The lower classes adored him; his hospital patients greeted him with the kind of bright enthusiasm reserved for a beloved old friend.

But I will not fall into the trap of becoming his partial eulogist. Dr. John was no more perfect than I am. Human fallibility ran through every part of him. No god could have had his sharp, cruel vanity, or his giddy, thoughtless levity. No immortal could have been so completely caught up in the pleasure of the present, so selfish in extracting every scrap of gratification to feed his masculine self-love, never stopping to count the cost of keeping that ravenous sentiment sleek and overfed.

The two portraits of him I have drawn – the public man, generous and unselfconscious in his good works, and the private man, vain of the homage he drew, pleased when people went out of their way to serve him – are both entirely true. You could never do him a quiet favour in secret. If you thought you’d finished some small, thoughtful trifle for him unnoticed, he would surprise you later with a soft, smiling comment that made clear he’d watched the whole process from the first stitch to the last, noticed every detail, and glowed with quiet pleasure at the effort. And he always paid those small kindnesses back, never letting you do a favour for him without receiving some gentle little treat or pleasant outing in return.

He knew Villette like the back of his hand, every hidden gallery, every private hall of art and science, every door that led to something worth seeing, as if he possessed the magic “Open Sesame” to them all. I never had a head for science, but I harboured an ignorant, soft fondness for art. I loved visiting the picture galleries, and loved most of all to be left there entirely alone. I had a wretched quirk of temperament that made it impossible for me to see or feel anything profound in company; if I had to make polite conversation about every painting in front of strangers, I would wear myself out completely in half an hour, too tired to think or feel anything at all.

Dr. John was the perfect cicerone for me. He would bring me to the galleries early, before the crowds rolled in, leave me there for two or three hours, and come back to collect me when his appointments were done. I was perfectly happy there, not just admiring, but examining, questioning, making up my own mind about every piece. At first I fought myself, straining to force admiration for all the supposedly great works everyone said I should love, until I grew tired of the effort, and let myself sink into soft, calm quiet before ninety-nine out of a hundred frames. I found an original, good painting was as rare as an original, good book, and I stopped trembling to admit to myself that most of the famous “masterpieces” were nothing like nature at all – their daylight was the wrong muddy shade, their skies of indigo were not real ether, their dark blobby weeds were not real trees. Many of the plump, complacent painted ladies struck me as very far from the goddesses they clearly thought they were, and dozens of painstaking little Flemish pictures felt no more meaningful than fashion plates for fancy fabric costumes. But every so often there was a fragment of truth, a gleam of light that felt like a friend: a snowstorm over a mountain that breathed real cold air, a portrait with a look in the sitter’s eyes that showed the painter truly knew her heart.

One quiet early morning, I found myself nearly alone in a gallery that held one particular monstrous, enormous painting, hung in the very best light, strung with a protective cordon, with a plush bench set right in front for the connoisseurs who wanted to sit down to finish worshipping it. It had clearly anointed itself the queen of the whole collection.
The portrait was of a woman, far larger than life, I judged – easily fourteen to sixteen stone, so broad and heavy that she must have eaten an enormous quantity of butcher’s meat to attain that wealth of muscle and soft flesh. She half-reclined on a couch for no discernible reason, in broad, bright daylight, strong enough to do the work of two plain cooks, no business lounging away noon on a sofa. Her clothes were absurd, seven and twenty yards of drapery that somehow still managed to be barely decent, and the foreground around her was a total mess of toppled vases, strewn flowers, and lumpy curtain fabric heaped over the couch and across the floor. When I checked the catalogue, the whole ridiculous production was named *Cleopatra*.

I sat down on the bench to rest, thinking that while a few small details – the roses, the gilded cups, the jewels – were prettily painted, the whole thing was nothing more than overblown claptrap. The gallery, nearly empty when I walked in, began slowly to fill, but I barely noticed, far more interested in the tiny, exquisite little still lifes hung humbly underneath that absurd giant canvas: wild flowers, wild berries, mossy nests holding eggs that looked like pearls seen through clear green seawater.
A light tap on my shoulder made me jump. I turned, to meet a face bent close to mine, frowning, almost shocked.
“Que faites-vous ici?” the voice said.
“Mais, Monsieur, je m’amuse.”
“Vous vous amusez! And at what, if you please? First of all, do me the favour to stand, take my arm, and come with me to the other side of the room.”
I obeyed at once. It was M. Paul Emanuel, newly returned from Rome, and no travel in the world could have made that fiery little professor any more tolerant of what he saw as insubordination.
“Allow me to take you to your party,” he said as we crossed the room.
“I have no party.”
“You are not alone?”
“Yes, Monsieur.”
“Did you come here unescorted?”
“No. Dr. Bretton brought me.”
“Dr. Bretton and his mother, of course?”
“No. Only Dr. Bretton.”
“And he told you to go look at *that* picture?”
“Not at all. I found it out for myself.”
His short raven-dark hair would have bristled straight up if it had been long enough. I could see exactly what was bothering him, and I found a quiet, mischievous pleasure in staying perfectly calm and winding him up.
“Astounding insular audacity!” he cried. “Singulières femmes que ces Anglaises!”
“What is the matter, Monsieur?”
“Matter! How dare you, a young unmarried person, sit there as cool and self-possessed as a boy, staring at *that* picture?”
“It is a very ugly picture, but I cannot for the life of me see why I should not look at it.”
“Enough, enough! Speak no more of it. But you ought not to be here alone.”
“But I have no party, as you say. And what difference does it make if I am alone or accompanied? No one bothers me.”
“Taisez-vous, and sit down there – there!” He plonked a chair down with great emphasis in the dullest corner of the room, right in front of a row of exceptionally dreary frames.
“Mais, Monsieur?”
“Mademoiselle, sit down, and do not move – do you hear? – until someone comes to collect you, or I give you permission to leave.”
“What a sad little corner!” I protested. “And what hideously ugly paintings!”
They were ugly, indeed: four panels catalogued as *The Life of a Woman*, painted in a flat, dead, pale, formal style. The first was a Young Girl stepping out of a church door, missal in hand, dress starched and prim, eyes cast down, mouth pursed tight, the very picture of a hypocritical little she-preacher. The second was a Bride, long white veil trailing, kneeling at her prie-dieu in her bedroom, hands pressed flat palm to palm, showing the whites of her eyes in the most exasperating way. The third was a Young Mother, hanging disconsolate over a lumpy, puffy baby with a face like an unwholesome full moon. The fourth was a Widow, all in black, holding a little black girl’s hand, both of them staring stonily at an elegant French tomb in a corner of the Père Lachaise cemetery. All four “angels” were grim as burglars, cold and lifeless as ghosts. As insipid, as absurd in their own way, as the lazy giantess Cleopatra over on the other side of the room.

I could not keep my eyes fixed on those dreadful paintings for long, and soon I turned to survey the rest of the gallery. A whole crowd of people was now clustered around the Cleopatra I’d been banished from, half of them ladies. M. Paul later informed me those ladies were all married “des dames,” so it was perfectly proper for them to look on what no unmarried “demoiselle” ought to glance at. I told him plainly I thought that doctrine was utter nonsense, and he shushed me at once, as was his wont, calling me both rash and ignorant. No man who ever filled a professor’s chair was more despotic than fiery little M. Paul. I noticed he himself stood looking at the Cleopatra for a very long while, perfectly at his ease, only glancing my way every few minutes to make sure I was obeying orders and not sneaking a peek back.
Presently he came back over to me. He asked if I had not been ill, as he had heard, and I told him I was fully recovered now. He asked where I had spent the vacation, and I told him mostly in Rue Fossette, partly at the Brettons’ house. He had heard I was left all alone in the pensionnat during the break, and he raised his eyebrows when I told him I’d only had simple, slow-witted Marie Broc for company. He knew Marie well, the poor girl, who was the source of constant inner conflict for him: he would grow irritable at her unkempt manners and stubborn moods, then immediately soften at the thought of all her hardships, pity winning out over impatience almost every time.
“How did you get on with Marie Broc?” he asked after a long silence.
“I did my best, Monsieur, but it was terrible to be alone with her.”
“Then you are soft-hearted in the worst way! You lack courage, and perhaps charity. You would never make a proper Sister of Mercy.”
“I washed her, kept her clean, fed her, tried to amuse her,” I told him. “But she made faces at me instead of speaking. When her aunt finally came to take her away, I was so relieved.”
“Ah, you are an egotist. There are women who nurse whole hospitals full of such unfortunates.”
“Could Monsieur do it himself?”
“Women worthy of the name ought to be far more capable of that kind of self-sacrifice than our coarse, fallible, self-indulgent sex.”
“Then my powers are limited, I suppose,” I said, “for tending that one poor girl left me ill with a nervous fever, not my body but my mind worn thin.”
“Vraiment! You are made of far too weak stuff. You will never have the courage to bear long solitude – all your rashness only goes so far as to sit and stare calmly at paintings of Cleopatra.”
It would have been easy to get angry at his sharp, teasing tone, but I had never been angry with him before, and I had no wish to start now.
“Cleopatra,” I repeated, very quiet. “But Monsieur was looking at Cleopatra just now, too. What does he think of her?”
“Cela ne vaut rien,” he said dismissively. “A superb woman – an empress’s height, Juno’s form – but the sort of person I would never wish for a wife, a daughter, or a sister. And you will never so much as glance in her direction again.”
“But I have looked at her many times while Monsieur has been talking,” I told him. “I can see her perfectly well from this corner.”
“Turn to the wall and study your four paintings of a woman’s life.”
“Excuse me, M. Paul, they are far too hideous. But if you admire them, let me vacate my seat and leave you to contemplate them in peace.”
He gave me a half-smile, a grim, hurried little flicker of amusement. “You nurslings of Protestantism astonish me. You unguarded Englishwomen walk calmly across red-hot ploughshares and escape burning. I am convinced if you were tossed into Nebuchadnezzar’s hottest furnace, you would walk out without even smelling of fire.”
“Will Monsieur be so good as to move an inch to the side?”
“What? What are you staring at now? You are not recognizing an acquaintance among that group of young men over there?”
“I think so,” I said. “Yes. I see someone I know.”

One crisp sunlit morning, Mrs. Bretton swept into my room without preamble, requesting I open my drawers and show her my full collection of dresses. I obeyed, no questions, no protest, letting her run a decisive, experienced hand over every hem and fold.

“That will do,” she declared, shutting the last drawer with a firm click. “You must have a new one.”

She was gone before I could frame a reply, and returned ten minutes later with a dressmaker on her arm, who measured me for fabric before I had quite processed what was happening. “I’m following my own taste here,” my godmother said, a glint of unshakable amusement in her eye. “No arguments, Lucy, I’m getting my way on this little matter.”

Two days later, the finished dress arrived: soft, rose-pink, the color of wild apple blossom in May.

“That cannot be for me,” I blurted, stumbling back a step. I felt as though she had handed me the elaborate embroidered robes of a high-ranking Chinese court lady, a garment entirely too bright, too foreign, too un-Lucy for my quiet, shadowed life.

“We shall see about that,” she smiled, unassailable, as she added with that tone that never brooked pushback: “Mark my words. You will wear it this very evening.”

I was certain I would not. I thought no force on earth could make me slip that bright fabric over my shoulders. Pink was a color that had never known me, that I had never dared to claim. But my godmother had already settled our plans: we were to attend a grand concert that same night, hosted in the great gilded hall of Villette’s most prestigious musical society. Advanced students from the city Conservatoire would perform, followed by a charity lottery for the poor, and as the final, glittering surprise, the King, Queen, and young Prince of Labassecour were all to be in attendance. Graham had sent the tickets over, urging us to dress with proper respect for royalty, and to be fully ready and waiting by seven o’clock.

At six, I was ushered up to the dressing chamber, and without the slightest show of force, I found my will gently overruled, my protests melting away under their cheerful, unrelenting care. The pink dress slipped on, softened around the neck and sleeves by delicate draperies of black lace, and they declared me properly en grande tenue, pushing me to face the mirror. I met my own reflection with trembling nerves, and turned away almost immediately, too flustered to linger.

Seven o’clock struck. Dr. John Bretton arrived, and we trooped down the stairs. Mrs. Bretton wore a deep brown velvet gown, its heavy, dignified folds the very picture of quiet grace. I walked in her shadow, searingly envious of that dark, unassuming majesty. Graham stood in the drawing room doorway, and my heart twisted with unease, certain he would think I had decked myself out in bright colors deliberately to draw his eye.

“Lucy, these are for you,” he said, pressing a small, sweet-smelling bouquet into my hands. He said no word about the dress, only gave me a soft, kind smile and a small, satisfied nod, and in that single gesture all my shame and fear of ridicule melted away. The dress, I soon noticed, was cut with perfect simplicity, no frills, no over-the-top furbelows: it was only the light, bright tint that had scared me, and with Graham’s quiet approval, my own eye soon grew happy with it.

People who go to public amusements every night can never know the giddy, gilded thrill of a first gala for someone who has barely ever left the quiet walls of a schoolroom. I had no grand expectations for the music, but the drive to the hall was pure magic: the snug warmth of our closed carriage on the crisp, clear night, the joy of riding out with two companions so cheerful, so unreservedly kind, as if I were their own blood kin. Stars glinted fitfully through the tree boughs over the avenue, and when we rolled out onto the open high road, the whole vast night sky stretched above us, dark and spangled. We passed through the city gates, past the posted guards who made a playful show of inspecting our carriage, a small silly formality that made us all laugh.

We drove through Villette’s brightest streets, shops glowing with gaslight, the pavements thronged with far more life than you would ever see at high noon. As I stared, the quiet shadow of Rue Fossette crossed my mind: the walled school garden, the great empty classrooms where, at this very hour, I would usually wander alone, watching the stars through the high uncurtained windows, listening to the monotonous reader intone the evening pious lecture in the refectory. I would be back to that quiet solitary life soon, I knew, and the thought laid a soft, sobering weight across all the bright joy of the present.

By then we were part of a long stream of carriages all heading the same direction, and soon the great illuminated facade of the concert hall blazed up before us. I had never stepped foot in any public place of entertainment before, so every new sight felt like a fairy tale come to life. We alighted under a bustling crowded portico, and climbed a wide, shallow staircase carpeted entirely in deep crimson, leading to tall solemn doors draped in the same rich fabric.

Graham worked some small bit of magic to make those doors roll silently open, and the great hall revealed itself to me: wide, high, domed, its sweeping circular walls finished in what looked like burnished dead gold, trimmed with snow-white alabaster garlands and gilded leaf wreaths. Every drapery, every carpet, every cushion was that same rich, warm crimson. Hanging from the center of the dome was a great mass of crystal, flashing like scattered rainbows, so dazzling I half expected a dark genie’s hand to hover in the scented glowing air, guarding a treasure plundered from some eastern sultan’s palace.

We moved forward, half lost in the crowd, until we rounded a pillar and nearly ran headlong into what looked like a small group of strangers: a handsome middle-aged lady in dark velvet, a tall gentleman with the finest face I had ever seen, and a quiet third woman in a pink dress and black lace mantle. I took them all in for a split second before I realized we were looking at ourselves in a great full-length mirror set between two pillars. For the first and only time in my life, I saw myself exactly as others saw me. It was not a perfectly flattering view, but it was not nearly as bad as I had feared, and I turned away quietly grateful.

We reached our seats, which gave us a perfect view of the vast, warm, cheerful hall, already nearly full of a splendid, elegant crowd. The local women were not all classically beautiful, but their gowns were immaculate, and every one of them carried herself with that easy, practiced public grace that makes even the most ordinary person look regal on a gala night. Scattered here and there were specimens of that distinct, sculptural Labassecour beauty I had never seen at home in England: solid, rounded, no sharp angles, still and stately as marble caryatids, their faces calm and passionless as a polar snowfield. Right in our direct line of sight sat the most striking of these beauties, a Juno-like blonde in pale blue satin, so cold and self-contained she did not even flicker an eyelash at the hundreds of gazes turned her way.

I noticed Graham’s attention linger on her, and I leaned over to whisper in his ear. “For heaven’s sake, guard your heart well. You could die at that lady’s feet, and she would never care for you in return.”

“Fair enough,” he laughed. “But what if that very grand insensibility is the thing that makes me want to worship her all the more? You know nothing of these romantic impulses, Lucy. Mamma! I’m in terrible danger of falling in love.”

“As if that’s any of my concern,” Mrs. Bretton sniffed, not looking up from adjusting her gloves.

“Cruelty!” he mock-lamented. “No man ever had a more unsentimental mother. She never even considers the possibility that I might bring home a daughter-in-law one day.”

“That’s only because you’ve been threatening to do it for ten years,” she shot back. “You were yelling ‘Mamma, I’m getting married soon!’ before you were even done wearing children’s jackets.”

“One day it will happen,” he grinned. “I’ll head off like Jacob or Esau, and bring home a wife from the local girls here, just to shock you.”

“At your own peril, John Graham,” she said, but her eyes were crinkling with amusement. “That little cottage of ours will never hold two mistresses, especially not a great big statuesque doll in satin and wax.”

The good-natured bickering went on while the hall filled to bursting, every last seat packed tight, and the great temporary platform at the front began to swarm with life. Around two grand pianos at its center, a flock of young Conservatoire girls in white muslin gathered, marshalled by two men I recognized. One was M. Josef Emanuel, the long-haired, artistic piano teacher who visited Madame Beck’s school twice a week to give lessons to the wealthiest pupils. The other, strutting about bossing all hundred girls as if they were his personal army, was his fiery little half-brother M. Paul Emmanuel.

I bit back a smile watching him. He was so perfectly in his element, ordering everyone around with absolute, unapologetic authority, even though I knew full well he could barely tell one musical note from another. It was only his naive love of being in charge that had dragged him here, and soon it was clear he even had his famous pianist brother wrapped around his little finger. He was a tiny hawk of a man, and the moment the actual famous professional performers stepped onto the platform, he vanished out of sight — he could not stand being anywhere he was not the center of attention.

A signal was given. The great crimson doors at the back of the hall rolled open, the whole assembly stood to their feet, and the orchestra burst into a rousing welcome fanfare. The King, the Queen, and the entire court of Labassecour filed in.

I had never seen a real living monarch before, so I strained my eyes to take in every detail, half-disappointed that they did not sit permanently on thrones wearing crowns and clutching sceptres. The King was a man of fifty, slightly bowed, with lines carved deep into his face as if by an iron stylus. I could read at once he was a quiet lifelong sufferer, a man haunted by the dark, constant ghost of hypochondria that follows some people their whole lives, freezing their blood and clouding their sight in the middle of even the brightest celebrations. The Queen beside him was mild, thoughtful, graceful, her face soft with gentle pity for her husband. Again and again I saw her nudge his arm, point out their little son the young Prince sitting on her knee, try to draw him out of his dark abstraction. He would start, smile for a moment at their boy’s chatter, and then slip right back into his somber quiet. No one else in the whole crowd seemed to notice the quiet tragedy playing out right before their eyes.

Right beside the Queen, on the benches reserved for the noblest young girls of Villette, I spotted Ginevra Fanshawe, her hair curled and flowing instead of braided tight like the local girls, chattering away to the daughter of an English peer beside her. I did not need to look at Graham to know he had seen her too. He went suddenly quiet, his answers to his mother growing short, every now and then he suppressed a small, soft sigh. Soon Ginevra’s roving gaze landed on our little group. She looked steadily at Graham, lifted her lorgnette to examine Mrs. Bretton, and then laughed and whispered a joke to her friend, turning her attention to the platform as the concert began.

The music itself was imperfect, charming in its flaws: the nervous young Conservatoire girls played a little tremulously on the grand pianos, M. Josef not having half the firm hand his half-brother would have used to bully them into courage. A great dramatic soprano in white satin ran through a series of incredible vocal tricks that felt more like a conjurer’s show than real music, and a dramatic tenor wailed for ten full minutes about his lost “false Isabelle” until I was quite relieved to see him leave the stage. The best part of the whole night, by far, was the rousing, hearty choruses sung by stolid, barrel-shaped provincial singers from all across Labassecour, their voices loud and unapologetic, ringing through the great gilded hall with a raw, unmistakeable warmth.

For one long perfect night, all the quiet loneliness of my usual life felt very far away, suspended in the soft golden glow of that evening I would remember for years after.

Three last days stretched out before me, and then I must return to the Rue Fossette pensionnat. I counted every tick of the clock like it was a bead strung on grief, slow to pass but slipping through my fingers all the same before I was ready, gone before I dared to stop fearing their end.
“Lucy won’t leave us today,” Mrs. Bretton coaxed over breakfast, her hand brushing mine across the table. “We can easily arrange a second respite, you know.”
“I would not beg for another day even if it could be mine for a single word,” I told her, my voice sharper than I meant it to be. “I want to get the goodbye over with, to settle back into the rhythm of the school at once. My trunk is already packed and corded, I must leave this very morning.”
As it turned out, my departure was not mine to schedule: Graham had promised he would walk and ride with me to the door, and he was tied up with patients from dawn until dusk, only stumbling home at twilight. Then a gentle tussle broke out, mother and son pressing me to stay just one more night, their soft, warm kindness pricking at me so sharply I could have cried. I ached to leave the way a man on the scaffold aches for the axe to fall: not because I wanted the pain, but because I wanted it to be over, no more drawn-out lingering in a paradise I had no right to call my own. They could not guess the shape of that restlessness, such a state of mind was entirely outside their soft, sheltered experience.
Dr. John handed me down from the carriage at Madame Beck’s door when the dark was thick. The lamp above the threshold was lit, and a thin November drizzle fell just as it had all day, gilding the wet pavement underfoot with wavering gold. It was the exact same kind of night as the one, less than a year prior, where I had first stood on this very step, alone, heart hammering, a stranger begging for a roof and a crust of bread. I had met him, too, that night, the man who stood at my side now. I had never told him of that old brief encounter, never felt the urge to: it was a little soft, private treasure, stowed away in the dark corners of my heart, too sweet to share.
Graham rang the bell, and the door flew open at once, for it was the exact hour the half-boarders took their leave, and Rosine was hovering on the threshold.
“Don’t come in,” I said to him, but he stepped a moment into the bright vestibule anyway. I did not want him to see the tears brimming over my lashes, for his was too tender a nature not to rush to mend what could not be mended, to soothe a hurt no doctor’s remedy could reach.
“Keep up your courage, Lucy,” he said, squeezing my hand tight. “My mother and I are your true friends, we will not forget you.”
“Nor I you, Dr. John.”
My trunk was hauled inside, we shook hands, he turned to go, but his generous, kind heart would not let him rest. He stepped back after me, voice soft.
“Lucy… will you feel very solitary here at first?”
“I will.”
“Well, my mother will come to call on you soon. And until then? I will write to you — nothing but silly, cheerful nonsense, whatever pops into my head. Would you like that?”
Good, gallant heart, I thought to myself, but I shook my head, smiling. “Never trouble yourself with such a task. You write to me? You will never have the spare time.”
“Oh, I will make the time. Good-by.”
He was gone. The heavy oak door crashed shut behind him. The axe had fallen, the first sharp pang was mine to bear.
I let myself stop for nothing, swallowing my tears like bitter wine, and went straight to Madame Beck’s parlour to pay the required courtesy call. She greeted me with perfectly rehearsed warmth, even a brief show of affection, before dismissing me ten minutes later. I moved on to the dining hall where the rest of the pupils and teachers were gathered for evening study, and their welcome felt far more genuine than hers, soft around the edges, no sharp hidden motive behind the smiles. Then I was free to slip up to the dormitory, sinking tired onto the edge of my narrow bed.
Will Graham really write? I wondered.
That cold, sharp voice of Reason crept up to me through the twilight of the long, dim room, whispering frostily in my ear: He may write once, out of that innate kindness of his. But he cannot keep it up, he will not repeat the effort. Only a fool would build a whole future on that single promise, only a madwoman would mistake one small temporary rain puddle, holding but a single sip of water, for an eternal spring that feeds the land through every season.
I bowed my head, sitting silent for an hour more, while Reason laid her cold, withered hand on my shoulder, nipping at my ear with blue, frosty lips. “If he does write?” she muttered. “What then? Do you dare think you will find pleasure in writing back? Fool, I warn you. Be brief, let yourself hope for no delight, no soft unfurling of your heart, no sweet friendly exchange. Let no feeling in you stretch, not one inch.”
“But I spoke to Graham for hours and you never chided me,” I pleaded.
“Talk is good discipline for you,” she said. “You stammer, you know your place, you never forget how far below him you stand, there is no room for delusion there. But writing?”
“Where speech is faltering and clumsy, can it be wrong to let written words say all the soft, true things my lips cannot stumble through?”
“Cherish that idea at your own peril,” she snapped.
“May I never, never express what I feel?”
“Never.”
I groaned under her stern, bitter weight. Never — what a hard, cruel word! That hag Reason would not let me look up, or smile, or hope, until I was crushed, broken, completely subdued. According to her, I was born only to work for a crust of bread, to wait for death, to despond through every day of my life. She might be right, but is it any wonder we sometimes defy her, run out from under her rod for an hour of truancy, to meet our soft, bright friend Imagination? That sweet, divine hope, the only kindness that never fails us.
Reason had thrown me out into the midwinter snow so many nights before, tossing me a gnawed bone that even dogs had abandoned, vowing she had nothing more to give me — but then Imagination would come, soft as starlight, bringing with her the scent of unfading flowers, the cool breeze of a world that needs no sun to light it. She fed my hunger on sweet, strange manna, soothed my unspeakable grief, gave hope back to my paralyzed despair. That night, she leaned over my bed, and whispered, “Sleep, Lucy. I will gild your dreams.”
She kept her word, watching over me through a full night of rest — but at dawn, Reason took guard again. I woke with a jolt, rain lashing the windowpanes, wind whimpering around the eaves, the night lamp guttering on its stand in the middle of the dormitory. The sharp pain of waking hauled me out of bed like a giant’s hand curled around my collar, and I dressed fast in the raw cold of November dawn, drinking deep of the ice-cold water in my carafe, my usual cordial whenever chagrin left me unsteady.
The school bell soon rang, calling the whole house to rise. I was the first down to the refectory, where the big iron stove burned bright, the only warm spot in the whole drafty building, the rest of the rooms nipping with the sharp bite of an early continental winter. I sat huddled before that dark, comforting hunk of metal, making firm, unbreakable rules for myself: no more lingering over happy memories that were gone forever, walk the hard wilderness of the present patiently, put my faith in the pillar of cloud that guides even as it awes me, don’t let myself long for a far-off promised land I will likely never reach, save for in the dreams of my dying days.
A feeling half strength, half sharp pain coiled tight around my heart, steadying me, holding my wild, unruly throbbings in check, until I was ready for the day’s work. I lifted my head.
Right beside the stove, set into the same wall, was a small window looking out onto the school courtyard. Through that pane, I suddenly saw a tassel of a cap, a furrowed brow, two sharp dark eyes, fixed straight on mine. They had been watching me for who knows how long. I had not known tears were on my cheeks until that moment, but I felt them now, cold and salt against my skin.
This was a strange house, where no corner was sacred, where not a tear could fall, not a private thought could stir, without a spy waiting to note and parse it. And this new, out-of-door, male spy, what was he doing here at such an ungodly early hour? No other professor would dare cross the courtyard before the class bell rang. But Monsieur Paul Emanuel never cared for hours, or rules, or other people’s boundaries. He had come to the library to fetch a reference book, and on his way had spotted me through the little window. He opened the refectory door, and stepped inside.
“Mademoiselle, vous êtes triste.”
“Monsieur, I have every right to be.”
“You are sick at heart and angry,” he went on. “You are half mournful, half mutinous. I see two tears on your cheek, hot as sparks, salt as sea crystals. You look at me like a wild newly-caught creature, the moment the animal trainer steps through the door, half fire half fear.”
It was a rude, unwarrantable thing to say, to a fellow teacher no less. He loved provoking the hot-tempered pupils into outbursts, and he expected me to snap back. But I sat silent, denying him the satisfaction.
“You look like the sort of person who would snatch a draught of sweet poison, and push away wholesome bitter medicine with disgust,” he said.
“I never liked bitters,” I answered, low. “And I do not believe they are always wholesome. What is sweet, even if it is poison, cannot be denied its own sweetness. It is better to die a quick, pleasant death, than drag out a long, charmless one.”
“Then if I had my way, I would force you to swallow your bitter medicine every day,” he said. “And I would break that cup of sweet poison before you could lift it to your lips.”
I turned my head sharply away, his presence chafing at my raw nerves, and I fought to keep my self-command from slipping.
“Come,” he said, his voice softening. “Tell me the truth. You grieve to be parted from your friends, is that not all it is?”
That feigned softness was no more welcome than his prying curiosity. I said nothing, and he sat down on the bench two yards from me, patient for once, trying for ages to draw me into conversation. I could not speak, not a single word, and at last I begged him to leave me alone. My voice faltered, my head sank onto my arms on the table, and I wept quietly, bitterly. He sat a while longer, until I heard the door click shut, and his footsteps retreat down the hall. Those tears were a relief.
I had time to bathe my face before breakfast, and I looked as calm as any other pupil at the table — though not nearly as bright and bubbly as Ginevra Fanshawe, who plopped down opposite me, her small eyes twinkling gaily, and stretched a white hand across the table to shake mine. Her holidays, parties, and flirtations had done her a world of good; she was plumper now, her cheeks round as apples, her golden curls bright against the dark blue, faintly plaided school wrapper that made her fair skin glow all the more.
“I’m so glad you’re back, Timon,” she said. “You have no idea how often I wanted you around in this dismal hole.”
“Oh? Then you must have some task for me, I suppose. Stockings to mend, most likely.” I never gave Ginevra credit for disinterested kindness.
“Still as crabby and sharp as ever!” she laughed. “I expected no less, it wouldn’t be you if you didn’t snub me. Now come, grandmother, I hope you still hate the school coffee and love the rolls: want to barter?”
Ginevra hated the weak, oversweet breakfast coffee the school served, but had a ravenous appetite for the fresh baked morning rolls. I never finished mine, so I always gave her half, and she would slip me a few sips of her own, stronger coffee, the two of us sharing cups as we had on so many long country walks, me always letting her take the lion’s share of whatever treat we found. We bickered every day, but we never grew alienated.
After breakfast, I withdrew to the empty first class to sit and think, as was my custom, before the nine o’clock bell rang and the horde of day pupils and half-boarders poured in, filling the house with noise and bustle until five o’clock. Everyone thought I was some solemn studious bluestocking, so wrapped up in my books I would overwork myself to illness, much to Madame Beck’s frequent mock concern. Only M. Emanuel had somehow guessed that I was not half as learned as the rest imagined, and he would chuckle quietly in my ear, delighted by how little I actually knew. I did not mind, though: I loved nothing better than to sit and think my own quiet thoughts, reading only the books that bore the clear stamp of the writer’s own heart, no matter how unremarked they were.
A soft tap at the door: a young pupil slipped in to fetch a book from her desk, and tiptoed out, murmuring that Mademoiselle looked so very hard at work. No sooner was she gone than the door flew open without a knock, and Ginevra burst in, a huge music book tucked under her arm.
“Go practice at the piano,” I told her at once. “Off to the little salon with you.”
“Not until I talk to you, chérie,” she grinned. “I saw you at the concert the other night, all dressed up like a proper belle! Who is your dressmaker, Lucy?”
“Tittle-tattle, Ginevra, leave me be.”
“Not a chance, wild swan. We all know you’re keeping company with Isidore — you know, our Dr John Bretton. Do tell me how he is. Was he terribly upset after the concert? Wasn’t I cruel to him, flirting so openly with de Hamal?”
“Cruel? I barely noticed you,” I said, lying through my teeth to tease her.
“Oh he noticed! I saw him standing off in the distance, all sulking and dying of jealousy! And his dear old mother, my future mamma-in-law! I do hope Lady Sara and I weren’t too rude, making fun of her.”
“Mrs. Bretton will survive your sneers, never fear,” I told her.
“But that poor son of hers! Tell me what he did after the concert!” she begged, eyes sparkling.
“He said you looked very much like a woman who was already half married to Monsieur de Hamal,” I said.
“Did he?” she squealed, delighted. “He noticed? That’s perfect! I thought he would be mad with jealousy.”
“Ginevra, are you truly done with Dr. Bretton? Do you really want him to give you up entirely?”
“He can’t give me up!” she laughed. “But tell me, how did you get him home that night? Was he absolutely furious?”
“Raving mad,” I assured her, leaning in to spin the tale. “Furious as a March hare. We had to hold him down in the carriage, him fighting the whole time, the coachman got so distracted we lost our way, and he tried to climb out the window to ride on the roof. And once we got home? He refused to touch his supper, the chicken and sweetbread his mother had set out all cold on the table. He would not lie still in his bed, no matter how tight Mrs. Bretton tucked the sheets in. Not even when he was a little boy did she have half as much trouble taming him.”
Ginevra cackled, throwing her head back, and the bell for nine o’clock class rang, cutting off her next request for more details.

The whole pensionnat had fallen soft and still at last. Dinner plates were cleared away, the noisy hour of recess where the pupils ran screaming through the halls had faded to quiet, and dusk bled purple over the slate roofs. The dim study lamp glowed low in the refectory, gilding the edges of open textbooks, as the day pupils trooped out the heavy brass front door, its latch clanging shut behind them, the shrill evening bell falling silent at last. Madame Beck was settled safe in her private dining parlour, laughing low over coffee with her mother and a small group of friends, and that was my cue.

I slipped to the kitchen, cheeks warm with a tender secret, and begged my friend Goton for a single tallow candle, just half an hour’s light for something that mattered very much. “Mais certainement, chou-chou,” she laughed, pressing two fat bougies into my palm, “you shall have two, if you like.” Light in hand, I crept noiselessly up the stairs to the dormitory, only to sink into utter chagrin when I spotted a pupil curled up in one of the beds, indisposed. Worse still, I recognized the ruffled muslin edges of Ginevra Fanshawe’s nightcap, her messy, pretty face half-buried in the pillow. She looked fast asleep—but I knew better. A tiny twitch of her eyelid told me she was feigning rest, watching slyly for my movements, ready to burst into a flood of giddy, chattering questions the moment I sat down to read. I could not trust her, not at all, and I had waited all day for this one hour of peace to read my precious letter.

I slipped back down the stairs, prize tucked safe in my pocket, to try the classrooms next. But ill luck hounded me: it was the weekly cleaning day, and the maids were sweeping every room by candlelight. Benches were piled haphazard on top of desks, dust motes swirled thick in the dim air, and damp coffee-grounds (the Labassecour housekeepers’ trick for trapping dust) were strewn dark across the floor. There was no quiet here, no spot to hide. Baffled but unbroken, I turned away, resolved to find my solitude somewhere—anywhere.

I fetched a long-forgotten key I knew was tucked behind the shelf in the coat closet, climbed three flights of creaking stairs one after another, until I reached a narrow, dark, silent landing. I unlocked the worm-eaten wooden door, and stepped into the garret: cold, black, so quiet I could hear the wind whistle through the cracks in the slates. No one would follow me here. No one would interrupt. Not even Madame Beck. I shut the door behind me, set my candle on a dented, mouldy old chest of drawers, wrapped my thin wool shawl tight around my shoulders to fend off the ice-cold air, and drew out my letter. My hands trembled with that sweet, sharp impatience, and I broke the seal.

Would it be long? Would it be short? I brushed a stray strand of hair from my eyes, still damp from the soft southern rain that had fallen that afternoon. It was long. Would it be cool? Would it be kind? It was kind. To all the checked, disciplined, starved parts of me that had learned never to hope for much, it felt so much kinder than I had ever dared imagine. I had hoped so little, feared so much, that the full, honeyed delight of that moment was something many people go their whole lives never to taste. The poor English teacher, huddled in that frosty garret, reading by a guttering candle in the wintry draught, holding a letter that was nothing more than gentle, warm good-nature, was far happier than any queen sitting on a gilded throne in a palace.

Dr. John had written to me at length, with real pleasure, his mood soft and sunny as he dwelt on all the small, shared moments we had lately lived: the walks we had taken along the city ramparts, the silly conversations we had shared over tea, the little quiet afternoons that had felt so bright and warm. The very best part, the part that made my chest feel fit to burst with joy, was the certainty that he had not written those lines just to please me—he had written them because it had pleased him, too. That joy might never come again, I knew, but that was a worry for another day. This moment was perfect: no pain, no shadow, no lack. A passing seraph might have rested right there beside me, leaning his soft, healing wing against my throbbing heart. I forgave every sharp thing he had ever said to me, every slight, freely, for the sake of this one perfect, dear memory.

And then something moved.

There was a strange, soft sound in the vast silent garret, the quiet scrape of a stealthy foot on the bare wooden floor, gliding out of the dark recess where the pupils’ old winter cloaks hung. I turned fast. My candle had burned low, the room stretched long and shadowed, and there, plain as day in the middle of that ghostly space, stood a figure all stark black and white: narrow, straight black skirts, a head wrapped tight in a bandage, veiled all in white.

Say what you will—call me nervous, over-excited by the joy of the letter, say I dreamed—this I swear: I saw it. An image like a Nun.

I cried out, my throat tight with sickness. If the shape had stepped closer I would have fainted dead away. But it receded, and I ran for the door. I do not remember how I stumbled down all those flights of stairs. By instinct I avoided the refectory, and ran straight for Madame Beck’s private parlour, bursting right in.

“There is something in the garret!” I gasped, pale as death, cold and shaking all over. “I was up there, I saw something. All of you, come and look!”

The room was not as full as it felt to my frayed senses: only Madame Beck, her ailing mother Madame Kint, her brother Victor Kint, and one other gentleman, who had his back turned to the door, talking to the old lady. They all jumped up in alarm, surrounding me, and the sight of the men in the room gave me courage. I beckoned them after me, back up the stairs, and only then remembered my letter, left sitting on that chest of drawers next to the candle. My precious letter! I would defy any ghost or thief, flesh or spirit, to get it back. I ran up the stairs faster, hearing them tramping behind me, forced to follow.

When I wrenched open the garret door, the whole room was dark as a pit. My candle had been snuffed out. Happily Madame Beck had had the good sense to grab a parlour lamp before we left, and its soft ray cut through the thick blackness a moment later. The snuffed candle sat on the drawers, exactly where I left it—but my letter was gone.

I did not care about the nun anymore. “My letter! My letter!” I sobbed, scrambling on the floor, wringing my hands. It was cruel, cruel, to have that little bit of comfort snatched away before I had even half read it all. The others searched every corner, chattering about disturbed cloaks, a cracked pane in the skylight, but I paid them no mind, frantic for my lost letter.

“Lucy? My dear girl, what letter is this?” A soft, familiar voice spoke right by my ear. I looked up, and my breath caught. It was him. Dr. John Graham Bretton himself. He had been called to the pensionnat that very evening to check on Madame Kint when her illness flared up—he was that second gentleman I had seen in the parlour.

“It’s yours,” I wept. “The letter you wrote to me. I came up here to read it quiet, because there was nowhere else I could be alone. I saved it all day, never opened it until tonight, I only read it once. I cannot bear to lose it.”

“Hush, don’t distress yourself so,” he said, his warm hand curling around my cold icy fingers, pulling me to my feet. “Come out of this cold room. They can send for the police to search more, we don’t need to stay.” He led me down to a tiny unused parlour where a low fire still glowed in the stove, sat me down beside it, and soothed me, promising he would write me twenty letters to replace the one I lost. His kindness was so soft, so tender, that I carried the echo of it in my heart for the rest of my life, warm even in the darkest days.

Later, when I was calmer, he smiled at me, drawing the crumpled letter out of his waistcoat pocket. His quick eye had spotted it fall on the floor when I scrambled, and he had snatched it up, teasing, but he could not bear to see me grieve so long over it. “Here is your letter,” he laughed. “Why is it worth all these tears, Lucy?”

I did not tell him I prized it like the blood in my veins. I only said I had so very few letters worth caring for.

He asked me then, gentle now, no trace of teasing left, what exactly I had seen up in the garret. I told him the whole truth, about the veiled nun, the old legend of the ghost that haunted the pensionnat, the story I had told him that soft October afternoon when we rode together through the Bois l’Etang. He sat quiet, thinking, and told me it was no ghost, just my overworked, tired nerves, my mind playing tricks on me after so much long-held stress. He told me not to tell anyone else, not to fear, that if the nun ever came back he would come fight her himself. He laughed, light and warm, and bade her give him his compliments if she haunted my garret again.

When he was gone, and all the fuss had died down, Madame Beck told me there was nothing to be found up in that garret: the cloaks hung as they always did, the cracked skylight had been broken for months, no intruder could have gotten in. She made me promise not to tell any of the pupils or staff about what I had seen, and the whole matter was soon forgotten by everyone but me.

I sat long that night by my bed, holding my crumpled, precious letter close to my chest, half glowing with the warm, soft joy of the lines Dr. John had written, half shivering, wondering deep in my heart: had that nun been a ghost from beyond the grave, or had the long, lonely weight of my days made her real, all spun out of my own overtaxed mind?

I did not linger in sad wondering, as I half thought I might. A new, bright influence had crept into the hollow of my life, and for a little while, sorrow was held entirely at bay. Imagine a dell, sunken deep in the secret heart of an old forest, wrapped forever in mist and dimness: its turf is dank, its grass pale and perpetually damp. Then a storm, or a woodsman’s axe, tears a wide gap in the oaks. The breeze rushes in, the sun leans down, and that cold, sad dell becomes a deep cup brimming with light. High summer pours her blue glory and golden radiance down from a sky the starved hollow had never once gotten to see.

That was the new creed I carried now: a belief that happiness was not a dream reserved for other people.

Three weeks had passed since the garret encounter with the nun, and tucked in a locked casket in my upstairs box drawer, next to that first kind letter from Dr. Graham Bretton, rested four more just like it. All traced in his same firm, steady hand, sealed with the same clear wax, brimming with that quiet, vital comfort that had felt like a lifeline to me back then. I have read them in later years, of course; they are gentle, pleasant notes, written by a man who was pleased to be kind. The last two even had a few closing lines, half playful, half soft, “touched by feeling, but never overwhelmed by it.” Time mellowed them to that mild, warm quality, but when I first drank their nectar, fresh from that honoured source, it tasted like the wine of the gods — a draught Hebe might have poured, one the very immortals would have approved.

I think the reader might wonder how I answered those letters: whether I held myself back under Reason’s dry, strict check, or let the full, free rush of Feeling carry me away.

To tell the truth, I compromised. I bowed at the altar of Rimmon, and lifted my heart to a different, truer shrine. I wrote two replies for every one of his letters: one for my own heart to pour itself out on, the other short, neat, and suitable for Graham’s eyes.

First, I turned Reason right out of doors, barred the door behind her, and sat down with eager pen to spread my paper. I spilled every sincere, true feeling on those two full sheets: the deep, clinging affection, the rooted, burning gratitude I carried for him — and let me say here once and for all, with utter scorn for any cheap suspicion of cheap “warmer feelings”: no woman ever indulges those soft, silly illusions for a man she has known from the very start, and never once mistaken for someone who could ever return that kind of love. No one ever falls in love unless they have seen, or dreamed, the star of Hope rising over love’s troubled waters. What I felt was a devotion that wanted to take every last pain from his fate onto my own shoulders, that would have absorbed every storm and lightning bolt aimed at his life if it could, out of a passion of care so fierce it bordered on reverence.

And just when I had finished writing all of that, the bolts on my heart’s door rattled, Reason burst in, sharp and revengeful, snatched the full sheets, sneered at every line, tore them all to shreds, and wrote a terse, curt missive barely a page long, before folding, sealing, and sending it off. She was right, of course.

I did not only live on letters, though. He came to visit me, he checked on me constantly, took me out to his mother’s home at La Terrasse once every week, and made a point of making much of me. Dr. Bretton never failed to tell me exactly why he was so kind: “To keep that nun away,” he would say, laughing. “I’m determined to fight her for you, I can’t stand that ghost — not with that pale face cloth and those cold grey eyes. The second I heard those horrible details, I decided I was going to oppose her at every turn. I want her to show up while I’m here just once, so we can see which of us is cleverer.” But the nun never did appear. He treated me half as a doctor would treat a nervous patient, and half as a man naturally full of benevolence, tending to my frayed nerves with constant, gentle care.

One evening, the first of December, I was walking alone in the school’s paved carré at six o’clock. The classroom doors were closed, and inside the pupils were running wild, loud and chaotic in their evening recreation. The carré was almost entirely dark, save for a faint red glow seeping out from under the stove. The big glass doors and long windows were frosted over, and scattered sparkles of starlight spangled the white winter veil, proving it was a clear, moonless night. That I dared to linger there alone in the dark proved my nerves were growing strong and healthy again. I thought of the nun, but I barely feared her — even with the staircase behind me, leading up through blind, pitch-black dark landing after landing to that haunted garret. Even so, my heart jumped hard and my pulse spiked when I heard breathing and rustling, and turned to see a deeper shadow in the dark of the steps: a shape moving, descending. It paused for a moment at the classroom door, then glided right past me. Just then, the distant front door bell clanged loud. Real, ordinary sounds chase away ghostly fears: this shape was too short and round to be my gaunt, tall nun. It was only Madame Beck, making her rounds.

“Mademoiselle Lucy!” Rosine burst into the carré, lamp in hand, from the corridor. “There is someone waiting for you in the salon!”

Madame saw me, I saw Madame, Rosine saw us both, but none of us stopped to exchange pleasantries. I walked straight to the salon, and found exactly who I had half-expected: Dr. Bretton, all dressed in fine evening wear.

“The carriage is at the door,” he said. “My mother sent it to take you to the theatre. She was going herself, but a last-minute visitor stopped her. She said immediately, ‘Send Lucy in my place.’ Will you come?”

“Right now? I’m not dressed!” I glanced down despairingly at my plain dark merino frock.

“You have half an hour to change. I would have sent word earlier, but I only decided to come at five o’clock, when I heard we would get to see a genuine treat: the great actress Vashti is performing tonight.”

He spoke a name that sent a thrill right through me — a name that could make all of Europe hold its breath back then. It is quiet now, its once-roaring echoes long stilled, the woman who bore it long dead and gone, her name buried under night and oblivion. But back then, her day blazed at its full height, hot and bright as the star Sirius.

“I’ll go! I’ll be ready in ten minutes,” I promised, and ran. I never once hesitated with that silly, prudish thought that might cross your mind now, reader — that going anywhere with Graham without his mother as chaperone was improper. I could not have even imagined, let alone said that thought out loud, without being eaten up by shame so hot I think it would have burned the life right out of my veins. Besides, my godmother knew her son, and she knew me. She would no more have fussed over our unchaperoned outings than she would have felt the need to chaperone a brother and sister.

I did not need any fancy dress for the occasion. My soft dun mist crape frock would be perfect, so I went to find it in the big oak wardrobe in the dormitory, where no less than forty pupil dresses hung. But someone had recently cleaned out the wardrobe, and sent piles of old clothes up to the garret — my crape was amongst them. I got the garret key, and went up, fearless, almost thoughtless. I unlocked the door, and stepped inside. You can believe this or not, but the garret was not entirely dark the moment I walked in. A solemn light shone from one far corner, broad and steady, brighter than a star. It lit up the deep alcove, and a tattered scrap of tarnished scarlet curtain half drawn across it. Then, instantly, silently, before my very eyes, the light vanished. The curtain and the alcove vanished too, and that whole far end of the garret turned pitch black. I did not stop to investigate. I had no time, and no will. I grabbed my dress, which hung conveniently on the wall right near the door, rushed out, locked the door behind me in a frantic hurry, and darted back down the stairs to the dormitory.

But I was shaking so hard I could not dress myself. My fingers trembled too much to pin my hair, or fasten hooks and eyes, so I called Rosine and bribed her to help me. Rosine loved small gifts, so she did her work beautifully: she smoothed and plaited my hair as neatly as any professional stylist, adjusted my lace collar perfectly straight, tied my neck ribbon exactly even. She handed me my handkerchief and gloves, then lit the candle to walk me down the stairs — only I realized I had forgotten my shawl. She ran back to fetch it, and I stood with Dr. John in the vestibule, waiting.

“What is this, Lucy?” He looked down at me closely, frowning. “You’re all excited again. Ha — the nun came to see you, didn’t she?”

I denied it completely. I was vexed he would suspect me of seeing another ghostly illusion. He was still sceptical.

“She was here, as sure as I live,” he said. “That look in your eyes, that bright, wide stare, no one mistakes that.”

“She was not,” I insisted — and I could say it truthfully, for I had not seen the nun’s apparition.

“I can see the old symptoms on your face,” he argued. “You’re pale, and you have that dazed, high look the Scots call ‘raised’.”

He was so stubborn I decided I might as well tell him exactly what I had seen. Of course, he waved it off as another optical illusion, another symptom of overtaxed nerves. I did not believe him for a second, but I did not argue back. Doctors are all so stubborn, so immovable in their dry, materialist ways.

Rosine came back with my shawl, and I was ushered into the carriage.

The theatre was packed to the rafters — royalty and noble families had emptied their palaces and hotels into every tier, the whole crowd quiet, waiting reverently for the show to start. I felt so privileged to have a seat before that stage. I had heard so many wild tales of this actress’s power, I waited with a strange, severe, completely riveted curiosity, wondering if she would live up to her legendary name. She was a new, unstudied world I had never encountered before: a great, strange new planet, rising over the horizon.

She rose at nine o’clock that December night. She shone with a pale grandeur and steady might, but that star was already nearing its end. Seen up close, it was a chaos: half hollowed out, half consumed, a dying orb made half of lava, half of glowing light.

I had heard people call her plain, and I expected bony sharpness, grimness, something large, angular, sallow. What I saw was the shadow of a royal Vashti: a queen, once as fair as dawn, now turned pale as twilight, wasted like wax melting in a flame.

For a long while, I thought she was only a very unique woman, moving through the tragedy with grace and terrible power over the crowd. Then I realized I was wrong. There was something in her that was neither man nor woman: two devils sat in her eyes. Those dark forces carried her through the performance, propped up her frail, weak body, and as the action rose and the crowd’s excitement deepened, those devils shook her with all the passions of hell. They wrote HELL across her straight, haughty brow. They tuned her voice to the note of raw torment. They twisted her regal face into a demon’s mask. She stood there, Hate and Murder and Madness made flesh.

It was a marvellous sight, a mighty revelation. It was also low, horrible, immoral. Gladiators dying in the sand of the arena, bulls goring disembowelled horses, would have been a softer, gentler spectacle for that audience than Vashti torn apart by seven devils, devils that howled and ripped at the human body they haunted, refusing to be cast out.

Suffering had struck that stage empress, and she did not yield to it, or endure it, or even merely resent it. She stood locked in struggle, rigid in resistance. She was draped in pale antique folds of fabric, long and regular like marble sculpture. A background of deepest crimson made her stand out white as alabaster, white as silver — white as Death, most of all.

Where was the artist who had painted that soft, fleshy Cleopatra? He should have sat down there and studied this vision, searched for the thick brawn, the abundance of blood and overfed flesh he worshipped. All materialists should have come and looked on her.

Pain for her never softened into something good. Tears never watered a harvest of wisdom. She looked on sickness, on death itself, with the eyes of a rebel. She was wicked, perhaps, but she was strong. Her strength had conquered Beauty and Grace both, and bound them to her side as captives, peerlessly fair and docile. Even in the wildest frenzy of her performance, every movement was royal, imperially grand. Her hair flying loose in the throes of the tragedy was still an angel’s hair, glorious under a halo. Fallen, insurgent, banished, she still remembered the heaven she had rebelled against. The light of heaven pierced the dark of her exile, showing her just how far she was from it.

Vashti was not a good woman, I was told. I could see that myself: she was a spirit from the depths of Tophet. But if so much unholy power could rise up from below, surely an equal force of sacred grace might one day descend from above?

I soon forgot to wonder what Dr. Graham thought of her. The powerful magnetism of her genius pulled my heart right out of its usual quiet orbit. My attention turned from the soft, gentle light I usually followed, to this fierce, red, cometary light, hot and burning on my vision. I had seen acting before, but nothing like this. Nothing that had ever silenced Desire and astonished Hope, outstripped Impulse and paled every thought I had ever conceived. It was a power like a swollen winter river, thundering over a cataract, carrying the soul along like a leaf on the steep, steel sweep of its descent.

Miss Fanshawe once called Dr. Bretton a serious, impassioned man, too grave and too easily moved. I never saw him that way. He was not a meditative man, not a sentimental man. He was as impressionable as rippling water — but almost as unchangeable as water, too. Breeze and sunlight might move him, but no metal could engrave him, no fire could leave a permanent brand on him. He could think, and think very well, but he was a man of action before he was a man of thought. He could feel, vividly, in his own way, but his heart had no chord for wild, all-consuming enthusiasm. For bright, soft, gentle joys, his face and his smile lit up as beautifully as summer clouds dyed rose and silver, pearl and purple. But for anything stormy, wild, intense, dangerous and flaming, he had no sympathy at all. When I finally looked over at him, I saw he was watching that sinister, sovereign Vashti not with wonder, or worship, or even dismay — just with intense, detached curiosity. Her agony did not pain him. Her wild, strangled moan did not move him much. Her fury made him recoil a little, but never to the point of horror. Cool, steady young Briton! The pale cliffs of his native England never look down on the tides of the English Channel more calmly than he watched that Pythian, possessed performance that night.

I leaned over and asked him what he thought of her. He woke as if from a little daydream, he had been so deep in his own quiet thoughts. “How do you like Vashti?” I asked.

“Hm-m-m,” he said, that first vague, expressive sound, and then a strange, critical, almost callous smile spread across his lips. For natures as steady as his, of course, wild passions felt alien. In a few short, terse phrases, he gave me his opinion: he judged her as a woman, not as an artist. It was a harsh, branding judgment.

That night was already marked in my book of life, not with a white line, but with a deep red cross. But we were far from done with it, and far more unforgettable moments were about to be written down in ink that would never fade.

Towards midnight, as the tragedy darkened to its final death scene, the whole theatre held its breath. Even Graham bit his lower lip, knitted his brow, and sat transfixed. All eyes were fixed on the stage, all ears strained to listen. Nothing was visible but that white, pale form sunk in a seat, still fighting her last, worst foe, Death. Nothing was heard but her gasps, her mutinous breaths, her defiance even as she died. It seemed as if that overabundant will, convulsing her perishing mortal frame, was fighting every inch of ground, selling every drop of blood, resisting to the very last the theft of every last one of her senses — determined to see, to hear, to breathe, to live, right up to the very edge of the line Death draws and no one can cross.

Just then, a ominous rustle ran backstage. Feet ran, voices called. A murmur of “Fire!” rippled through the gallery, and then it was yelled, echoed, screamed at the top of every voice. Panic exploded, faster than pen can ever describe it: people rushing, shoving, crushing, blind selfish cruel chaos.

But Dr. John? I can see him still, his face calm with that quiet, comely courage he always carried.

“Lucy, sit still, I know you will,” he said, glancing down at me with that same serene, firm kindness I had seen at his mother’s safe, peaceful hearth. Adjured like that, I think I would have sat still even under a toppling crag. And I would never, at the cost of my life, have moved to cause him trouble, or get in his way, or make him stop to

Those who live walled away from the rush of the wider world—schoolgirls tucked behind high iron gates, recluses of quiet ordered lives—can slip unawares right out of the memory of their friends who move in freer, faster lanes outside. Without warning, often right after a stretch of bright, frequent meetings, a string of small exciting moments that you thought would only draw you closer, a hush falls. A wordless, unaccountable silence settles, a long blank stretch of nothing. No letters come, no unexpected knocks at the door, no little books, pressed flowers or small tokens that used to mark someone was thinking of you. The gap never makes itself clear, never offers an explanation.

If the quiet, solitary soul waiting there is wise, they tuck their restlessness out of sight, lock their aching hopes away through these weeks of inner winter. They learn to be like the dormouse, tucking themselves neatly into a small warm nook, letting the drift of life pile up over them, holding still through the cold season. It will pass, they tell themselves, or if it does not, if the frost seeps so deep it never thaws, that is no more than mortal life promised anyway.

The seven weeks that followed that bright, fateful night at the theatre were as bare and blank as seven unmarked sheets of paper, not a single word or visit or small gift scrawled across any of them. Halfway through, my fraying nerves began to spin dark fancies: what if something terrible had happened to the Brettons, to the man whose memory had lit up my quiet days? The long strain of waiting turned nights cruel, sleep slipping away from me, chased by bad dreams full of calamity, the sick cold dread that I had been abandoned entirely. I clung on as best I could, but I grew pale, and thin, and worn.

By the end of those seven long weeks, I finally accepted the silence as fate, not a wrong done to me. I busied my hands with elaborate lacework, pored over German textbooks, waded through the driest, heaviest tomes the school library held, but all of it felt like gnawing on a file to fill my hunger, drinking brine to quench my thirst. The hour the postman knocked was my worst torture: I would wait for that familiar ring, my chest tight with painful expectation, then slump in hollow disappointment when no letter came. I was no better than a caged creature, half-starved, waiting for the scrap of comfort that never came. Those seven weeks, despair breathed cold and sharp right through me, so close it made my heart stutter under its weight. The letter I ached for, the one I thought held all the sweetness left in my life, never arrived.

In the depths of that longing, I returned again and again to the small packet tucked in my case: the five letters I had hoarded, the five little stars that had lit up one perfect month. I only dared read them after dark, too shy to beg the kitchen for extra candles, so I bought my own little wax taper and matches, slipping up to the dormitory after the study hour to feast on that thin, ghostly bread. It never nourished me. I pined on it, growing thin as shadow, though I was never properly ill.

One night, I sat up late poring over those over-read letters, their ink fading to nothing in my sight, my gold crumbling to dead leaves right before my eyes, when I heard quick, tripping footsteps running up the stairs. It was Ginevra Fanshawe, back from a dinner in town, coming to stow her shawl in the wardrobe. I barely had time to tuck my precious letters back into their case and lock it before she was at my side, her bright silk dress askew, her curls drooping damp with the night rain, her mood sour.

“Stupidest evening I ever spent,” she huffed, dropping onto my bed without invitation. “Those people are dreadful.”
“Not Mrs. Cholmondeley’s, then? I thought you loved her parties.”
“I didn’t go there. My uncle de Bassompierre has arrived.”
“Your uncle? I thought you were fond of him.”
“Fond of him? He’s horrid, a great surly bear. He’s English really, you know, just changed his name a few years back when his foreign mother’s family left him a title and estates. Mamma says he killed my aunt Ginevra with his cruelty. Fancy walking into a room all alone, and this great fifty year old man turns his back on me right after we say hello and storms out. They all say I look just like the aunt he treated so badly, I suppose his conscience pricks him.”
“Were there no other visitors?”
“Only his silly spoiled little daughter, missy. She was nearly crushed to jelly in that silly theatre hubbub weeks ago, you know.”
“Ah. Do they live in the big hotel on Rue Crécy?”
“Wait—how do you know that? Oh, right, you’ve been there. The Brettons, of course, Mother Bretton and her son John have free run of the place. That’s who was there tonight, too. John Bretton tended the little girl after her accident, and now they’re all thick as thieves over old times. The little girl got to play hostess, and all the attention that should have been on me was lavished on her. And John—great lout—was being all attentive to her, frowning at excitement and making her lie on the couch. Ugh, it was sickening.”

My temper snapped. “You are lying about him. I’ve had quite enough of your nonsense. Get off my bed and leave this room at once.”

Ginevra cackled, teasing me about how red my face had gone, how cross I got over “gros Jean”, the big oaf. Too proud to rise to her petty taunts, I blew out my taper, locked my bureau, and walked out, leaving her to her sour little vapours.

The next day was Thursday, our half-holiday. The dreaded post hour drew near, and I waited in the first classroom, feeling as tense as someone waiting to see a ghost. The east winter wind howled outside, sharp enough to make every ache sharper, every sorrow heavier. I could not bear to hear the postman’s ring only to face the hollow silence of no letter for me, so I slipped out of the room without my bonnet, ran down to the end of the bare winter garden, and hid among the stripped shrubs, muffling my ears in my apron to block out that cruel clang.

When I dared slip back inside, the classroom was still empty, no pupils yet arrived. On my black desk sat a small flat white letter. The post had come and gone, and I had not heard a thing. Rosine had slipped in and left it there. My heart soared—at last, the letter I had waited seven long weeks for! I stumbled close, only for my hope to crumble: the handwriting was not the firm, bold masculine script I ached for, but a pale, looping female scrawl. The disappointment was so sharp I spoke out loud, unthinking: “This is cruel.”

But I picked it up anyway, and soon recognised the familiar hand of Mrs. Bretton. The letter was dated La Terrasse, and it ran:
> DEAR LUCY,—I’ve been wondering what on earth you’ve been keeping busy with these last few months! I am sure you’ve been as happy and occupied as we have here. Graham’s medical practice is growing by leaps and bounds, everyone in Villette is begging to see him, though I tease him constantly to keep his head from getting too big. He is such a good, dear boy, though—after rushing all day from patient to patient, dealing with every sort of temper and grief, he comes home to me every night as warm and gentle as ever.
> I’ve been busy with old business, trying to win back some of the fortune his father left him for him, though he laughs at my fuss, teases me about wanting sky-blue turbans and diamond jewelry and a grand hotel of my own. Speaking of that turban, you missed the funniest sight last week! Graham came home so tired he fell asleep in my chair, and I snuck that great blue turban of mine onto his head, and draped my best cashmere shawl over his shoulders. He looked like the handsomest pasha you ever saw! He woke up and caught a glimpse of himself in the mirror above the fireplace, and now he swears he will take revenge on me for the prank.
> That brings me to the point of my note: I know you have a half-holiday today. Be ready by five o’clock, and my carriage will come to fetch you out to La Terrasse. Be sure to come—you might meet an old acquaintance you haven’t seen in years. Good-by, my dear, quiet little god-daughter.
> Yours truly,

That letter softened every sharp edge of my grief. My friends were well, they had not forgotten me, the seven weeks of silence had been nothing but the rush of their busy lives. By late afternoon, the sky had turned thick and heavy with snow, a great wild white tempest blowing in from the north, and I was sure no carriage could make it through. But Mrs. Bretton never broke a promise. At six o’clock, the carriage pulled through the drifts, and I was lifted over the snow-blocked steps of the chateau, and carried inside to La Terrasse.

The drawing room was so warm and bright, it felt like stepping into summer. Mrs. Bretton’s warm kiss, the great crimson fire blazing in the hearth, the soft plush carpets and velvet curtains made the cold school rooms I lived in seem like a distant, grey dream. She fussed over me, chiding me for growing too thin, then declared the snow wind had messed my hair, and sent me up to my old little sea-green bedroom to fix myself.

My room had a bright fire burning too, tall wax candles lit on either side of the great mirror. Between those two flames, before the looking glass, something was dressing itself: a small, slight, airy figure, all in white, dotted with tiny scarlet drops, a red sash at its waist, a little wreath of glossy evergreen in its dark curls. For a second I thought of Graham’s old spectral illusions, but there was nothing frightening here. The figure turned, and a pair of large dark eyes under long, soft lashes fixed on me.

“Ah! You are come,” she breathed, in a soft quiet voice, smiling slow and warm.

I stared, recognising that fine, delicate face. “Miss de Bassompierre,” I said, at once.

“No,” she said, shaking her head, “not Miss de Bassompierre to you. You have forgotten that once, long ago at Bretton, I sat on your knee, cried to you when I was a lonely little girl, and you let me share your pillow? You don’t remember little Polly Home?”

All the pieces clicked into place. It was her. The tiny, serious child I had known all those years ago, grown into this soft, lovely young woman.

“I am Paulina Mary Home de Bassompierre,” she said, and her smile lit up the whole warm candlelit room.

For all my godmother’s natural cheer, for all the effort she put into keeping us entertained that wintry evening at La Terrasse, no real warmth settled in our bones until we heard the distant jingle of harness through the gale, the thud of hooves crunching through snowdrifts that signaled the riders’ return. How often do women sit wrapped in firelight, soft and sheltered, while their hearts tear themselves away from that coziness? We wander out into the dark, brave the blasts, linger at lonely gates, watching and listening for the father, the son, the lover coming home through the storm.

At last they arrived. The Count de Bassompierre had ridden back alongside Dr. John Bretton that night, and none of us could say who spotted the horses first. The gale howled so fierce we all ran down to the hall to meet them the second they stepped through the door—only to be waved sharply back. They were caked head to toe in snow, two walking mounds of winter white, and Mrs. Bretton herded them straight for the huge, sunlit old Dutch kitchen at the back, forbidding them to step one foot on her carpeted staircase until they’d stripped off every last flake of that wintry mask. We trailed after them, of course, no one wanting to miss the fun, and Paulina Mary danced in giddy circles around her father, clapping her hands and crying that he looked exactly like a giant polar bear fresh off the ice.

The Count shook himself hard, sending a shower of snow flying, and the little sprite darted away laughing, only to come bouncing right back to help him tug off his heavy frozen coat. When he finally stepped free of his thick dreadnought, he swept it up over his head and threatened to bury her under it like an avalanche. “Come on then!” she laughed, bending to invite the “fall” before bounding out of reach as nimbly as a wild chamois, her movements soft and supple as a kitten’s, her laugh ringing clearer than struck silver and crystal. She rubbed his cold stiff hands between her own warm ones, stood on tiptoe to press a kiss to his lips, and a glow of loving delight seemed to wrap around her like a little halo. The grave, stately Count looked down at her the way men only look at the thing that is the very apple of their eye.
