“Mrs. Bretton,” he said, smiling, “what am I to do with this daughter of mine? She doesn’t grow in wisdom, or even an inch in height. Don’t you find her still nearly as much of a child as she was ten years ago?”

“Not half as much of a child as my great oaf of a son,” Mrs. Bretton retorted, already caught up in a playful tussle with Graham over the thick snow-soaked coat he refused to change out of. He leaned against the polished old Dutch dresser, laughing and holding her at arm’s length. “Come on, mamma,” he said, striking a compromise, “let’s mix a Christmas wassail bowl, toast Old England right here on this hearth, so we can be warm inside and out.”

While the Count stood close by the fire, and Paulina danced back and forth across the wide, hall-like kitchen, Mrs. Bretton directed Martha to spice and heat the wassail, pouring the steaming brew into the old Bretton family flagon to be passed around, served hot by the tiny silver cup that had been Graham’s christening gift.

“Here’s to Auld Lang Syne!” the Count called, lifting the little glowing cup high, and recited the old Scottish verse out loud, his voice soft with nostalgia. Paulina clapped her hands, grinning that the old Home family Scottish blood was showing through his fine count’s title, that they were half Caledonian, half Gallic. “That’s a Scottish reel you’re dancing, isn’t it, my little Highland fairy?” her father teased. “Mrs. Bretton, I wouldn’t be surprised if a fairy ring of green grass pops up in the middle of your kitchen floor any second. That one’s no ordinary little mortal.”

“Tell Lucy to dance with me, papa!” she said, spotting me across the room. The Count held out his hand, greeting me warmly, saying he remembered me well, that his daughter had talked about me so often I felt like an old friend already. No one had thought to interrupt her giddy dance to offer her a sip of the spiced ale, but she was not about to be left out of the fun. When Graham set the christening cup on the high shelf of the dresser just out of her reach, she looked up and asked for a taste.

Graham had been watching her, charmed by the ease of her, the way she moved through his mother’s home like she still belonged there, the same old little Polly he’d known as a boy. “Your ladyship wishes for the tankard?” he teased, falling back into their old familiar banter, and pretended to refuse, telling her it was strong old October ale, brewed the year he was born, far too strong for her. She pouted, looking up at him like a child begging for a forbidden sweet, until he finally relented, holding the cup so she could only sip it drop by drop. His eyes were bright, smiling, openly warm with the pleasure of indulging her, until she took a proper sip and made a face, stepping back at once. “It’s not sweet at all!” she laughed. “It’s bitter and hot, it takes my breath away. The ale was only good when it was forbidden. No more, thank you.” She slipped away from him, graceful as a shadow, to rejoin her father, and Graham was left standing there, baffled for a minute, staring after her. That girl who’d just teased him over the ale was still the seven-year-old Polly he remembered, but the dignified little countess standing by her father’s side was a stranger he was only just meeting.

We moved to the drawing room for tea soon after, and Paulina slipped her arm through her father’s, staying close at his side, listening intently to every story he and Mrs. Bretton told, prompting them to share old adventures they’d half forgotten. The giddy, childlike sparkle was gone for the night; she was soft, thoughtful, gentle. When she said goodnight to Graham, her smile was quiet, her bow dignified, the perfect little Countess, and Graham could only bow back, grave and a little flustered, struggling to reconcile the dancing, playful fairy from the kitchen with this poised, graceful young lady.

By breakfast the next morning, the snow drifts had risen high enough to darken the lower panes of the windows, the gale tearing fallen snow up from the ground and whirling it into wild, fantastic shapes. Mrs. Bretton decreed no one would leave the house that day unless it was a matter of life and death, and Paulina wholeheartedly agreed, tucking a chair right next to her father’s armchair and announcing she would keep him from venturing out. The Count teased her back, pretending he would leave if they didn’t “pet” him properly over breakfast, so Paulina rushed to fetch him buttered rolls and the same marmalade they’d eaten at the old Bretton house years before, and Mrs. Bretton laughed, reminding everyone how tiny little Polly used to tug at her sleeve and beg for extra marmalade for Graham. Graham blushed bright red, laughing and protesting he’d never been that fond of sweet things, and Paulina backed up her old memory without hesitation.

Then the Count turned serious, and said he wanted to send Paulina to a good school, to finish her education properly. Mrs. Bretton pointed him to Madame Beck’s establishment, where I worked, and I took the chance to say plainly that I was a teacher there, no guest of leisure. For a second, I worried the pair might treat me differently now, but the Count only nodded, his voice gentle, saying it was an arduous calling, and wished me health and success. Paulina stared at me, wide-eyed with surprise, saying she’d never thought to ask what I did, that to her I’d always just been Lucy Snowe, no title, no job, just my friend. When I explained I worked to keep a roof over my own head, so I’d never be a burden to anyone, the Count told Paulina that if she ever found herself alone in the world, he hoped she would do the exact same.

When he asked if Madame Beck would take her, I told him the school was always happy to take English pupils, and the Count decided they would drive over that very afternoon to arrange it. Paulina hesitated, then laughed, and told them all the story of how he’d signed her up for a school in Switzerland five years prior, and then had come to visit her every other day, until they’d both been politely asked to leave, and warned him he’d almost certainly end up moving into Madame Beck’s school right along with her.

The two elders slipped away after breakfast to talk over old business, leaving Graham, Paulina, and I alone in the drawing room. The last trace of childish glee left Paulina’s face at once; the round, dimpled expression she wore with her father softened into something sharper, more thoughtful, lines of young womanhood settling soft across her features. Graham, who’d been staring out at the snow, wandered over to the hearth, and tried to make small talk about Villette’s sights, awkward for the first time in my memory. Paulina answered him with quiet grace, quick-witted, well-spoken, though every now and then a little lisp would slip out when she spoke fast, making her flush as she corrected the word slowly, carefully. Every time she did it, Graham smiled. The awkwardness melted fast; they were soon talking easily, the way they’d done as children, no hesitation, no stiffness, the old easy familiarity rising to the surface after ten years apart. Graham’s whole face softened, his voice warm and low, no trace of his usual playful levity, just quiet, unmistakeable pleasure at being with her again.

But his patients called, and he had to leave. He walked out, then came back only a minute later, claiming he’d forgotten a paper in his desk—though we all knew he’d only come back for one more look at her, to make sure he hadn’t imagined the soft, warm glow between them. He took one final parting glance from her, shy and soft, as sweet and innocent as a fawn peeking out from a fern thicket, and left for good.

Paulina and I sat in silence for a long time after that, both working at our needlework. Her old tiny wooden workbox from Bretton days was replaced now with one inlaid with mosaic, fitted with gold needles, but her little mannerisms were exactly the same: the small furrow of concentration between her brows, the quick little movement to tuck a stray lock of hair back, the tiny shake of her skirt to brush off a stray thread. Outside, the winter storm was still raging, the gale howling itself hoarse, and Paulina, for all her urge to talk, saw I was in no mood to chatter, and respected the quiet: no pushy questions, no giddy teasing about the doctor, just soft, easy silence shared between old friends.

From that week forward, my life no longer dragged in unbroken sameness. Madame Beck gave me full, ungrudging leave to go out whenever I pleased—she had always treated me with unwavering respect from the day I joined her establishment, and when she saw I was receiving regular invitations to the grand château outside town and the fashionable hotel where the de Bassompiere party stayed, that respect softened into deliberate, gracious favour.

She was never fawning, of course. For all her worldly pragmatism, Madame had no weakness for toadying, no clumsy eagerness that would have earned my contempt. She pursued her self-interest with measured sense, held tight to her gains with calm consideration, and made it clear without ever saying so outright that she was pleased one of her teachers moved in circles that elevated, rather than dragged down, the reputation of her pensionnat. She never praised my character or my friends to my face, only once, when she sat sunning herself in the garden with a cup of coffee at her elbow and the Gazette spread open on the bench, did she speak warmly when I asked for leave to go out for the evening:
“Yes, yes, my good friend, I give you permission with all my heart. Your work in my house has always been admirable, full of zeal and discretion, and you have earned every right to amusement. Go out as often as you like. As for your choice of acquaintances? I am perfectly content with it. It is sensible, dignified, laudable.”
She snapped her lips shut and returned to her paper.

It was around that time that my small, triple-wrapped packet of five letters went missing from my bureau drawer. My first instinct was blank dismay, but I steadied myself almost immediately.
“Patience,” I whispered to myself. “Say nothing, wait peacefully, and they will come back.”
And come back they did. They had only taken a short trip to Madame’s private chamber, passed her quiet inspection, and were returned to their proper place untouched the very next day. I found myself wondering what she had made of them: of Dr. John Bretton’s sharp, pithy thoughts, his sound, unpretentious opinions, his warm half-humorous tone that had always made my chest light up, the scattered, sparse kind words that felt rarer than diamonds in a mine. For a few days afterward I caught her gazing at me, steady and contemplative, a little puzzled but never malicious, and she offered only one stray comment before trotting off to attend to the pupils like the neat, determined little pony she resembled: “There is something very remarkable about English character. I cannot quite explain how, but you all have your own ideas about friendship, love, everything. And at the very least, you never need to be monitored.”
I breathed a quiet, private hope she would leave my letters alone after that.

But the thought crashed over me then, so sharp it blurred the sun, the schoolroom, the garden all out of focus: those letters were the last of their kind. I would never receive another one. That broad, bright river of Dr. John’s kind attention that I had lingered beside, sipping its few reviving drops, was bending to a new course, leaving my little lonely patch of ground dry and desolate, pouring all its rich, warm waters somewhere far away. The change was right, just, natural—there was not a single word I could say to argue it. But I had loved that river, my Rhine, my Nile, my almost-worshipped Ganges, and I grieved to see its grand tide roll away estranged, vanishing like a mirage. I was stoical, but not a stone: hot tears dripped fast onto my hands and my desk, a brief, heavy, sultry storm of sorrow, before I steadied myself again.
I told myself the hope I was mourning had brought me, and made me endure, far too much lingering pain. Its death had come at the right time. I closed the eyes of that dead hope gently, covered its face, composed its limbs with perfect calm. But mementos of a grief so private could not be left out where a stray glance could stab me fresh every hour. I had to put the letters somewhere completely, irrevocably out of reach.

When I went to fetch them one empty Thursday holiday afternoon to arrange their final rest, a hot spark of displeasure flared in my chest. The packet was still in my drawer, but the blue ribbon I had tied around it had been untied and retied. Someone had rummaged through my things again.
This was too much. I could almost forgive Madame Beck, sharp and discreet as she was, for reading my private notes—she would see them for what they were, innocent friendly letters, and judge them fairly. But the cold, dreadful thought struck me that she might have shared what she found with someone else, that she had laughed over my most sacred things with a confidant. I knew exactly who that confidant was: her sharp-tongued kinsman M. Paul Emanuel, who had dined with her the night before, and whose angry, lurid blue glance had struck me like a flash of lightning that morning in class, a look I had not understood until that moment. He had never judged me fairly, never treated me with tolerance. The thought of those gentle, harmless letters falling into his suspicious, unkind hands made my very soul jar.

I searched the whole pensionnat for a hiding place, and found none. The attic? No, its drawers were all mouldering, broken, full of rats and mice that would gnaw the paper to pulp, damp that would blur the ink beyond reading. There was no lock here strong enough to guard something that mattered. Then, sitting on the dormitory window seat, watching the pale winter sun set over the forbidden walk’s gnarled old pear tree—the nun’s pear tree, its bare branches standing like a grey, gaunt dryad skeleton—a strange, solitary thought struck me. I pulled on my bonnet, cloak, and furs, and walked out into the city.

I wandered to the oldest, quietest, most shadowed part of town I always sought when my heart was heavy, and found my way to a dusty old broker’s shop crammed full of ancient discarded things. I bought the thickest, sturdiest glass jar I could find, brought my letters home, wrapped them tight in oiled silk, bound them with twine, and paid the old frost-whiskered Jewish broker to stopper, seal, and solder the jar completely airtight. He glanced at me suspiciously the whole time, no doubt thinking I was hiding some dark secret, but for me it was not pleasure, it was a sad, lonely, solemn satisfaction, the same quiet compulsion that had once driven me to the confessional. I slipped back into the pensionnat at full dark just in time for dinner.

At seven the moon rose. By half past seven, all the pupils were at their evening study, Madame Beck was with her mother and children in the dining room, all the half-boarders had gone home, and the maid Rosine had left the vestibule empty. I wrapped a shawl tight around my shoulders, slipped the sealed jar under my arm, and stole out through the first classroom door, through the arbour, and into the forbidden walk.
The old pear tree stood at the far end, dim and grey, its thick ivy hiding a deep hollow near its roots that I had known about for months. I cleared away the ivy, pushed the jar deep inside the hollow, fetched a slate and a trowel of leftover mortar from the masons’ tool shed at the end of the garden, sealed the slate over the hole, covered it all with soft black garden mould, and tucked the ivy back into place to hide every trace. Then I leaned against the trunk, lingering like any mourner beside a fresh new grave.

The night air was perfectly still, veiled in a luminous mist that turned the moonlight to soft glowing haze. There was something electric in that mist, something that hummed in my bones, making me feel as strong as I had the night a year before in England, when I had been lost in lonely fields watching the aurora borealis stream across the sky. I was not happy, far from it, but I felt reinforced, steady, ready to fight my own battles alone, no one leaning on me and no one to lean on. I was already planning how I would leave this pensionnat when my resources ran out, ready to face whatever pitched battle fortune threw my way next.

Then the dim moon suddenly brightened. A sharp, distinct white ray gleamed before me, a dark shadow carved itself out of the haze, and I was standing no more than three yards away from a tall figure in a black sable robe, its face entirely covered by a white veil. Five minutes passed. I did not flee, I did not scream.
“Who are you?” I called out. “Why have you come to me?”
It stood silent. It had no features, no face beneath the veil, but its eyes were fixed straight on mine. I stepped forward, desperate enough to be brave, and stretched out my hand to touch it. It receded, fast and silent, slipping behind a thick mass of evergreen laurel and yew. When I pushed past the shrubs, it was gone. No amount of waiting or calling brought it back. This time, there was no Dr. John Bretton I could run to, no one in the world I dared whisper to that I had seen the nun again.

Paulina Mary de Bassompierre insisted I visit her at their house on Rue Crécy as often as possible. Back in the old Bretton household, she had never openly admitted she liked my company, but it had soon become an unconscious necessity for her—she would trot after me to my room, peep around the door, and order me in her sharp little tone to come back down and sit with her. Now she did everything short of dragging me through her front door.
“Leave Madame Beck’s pensionnat,” she begged. “Come live with us. Papa will pay you three times what she does to be my companion.” Mr. Home himself made the offer, generous and sincere, but I turned it down at once. Even if I had been poorer, with fewer prospects to my name, I would still have said no. I could teach, I could give lessons, but being someone’s paid shadow, a lady’s companion, was something I could never do. I would rather scrub floors for my independence, sew shirts for starvation wages, than be a pretty girl’s silent accessory.
I was no gem’s foil, no great person’s appendage. Madame Beck and I never tried to be friends, but we understood each other perfectly. She gave me total freedom, no strings tied to her or her interests, no petty demands. When she came back from a fortnight away tending her sick relative and found the school running perfectly, no signs of neglect, she handed out presents to all the other teachers, and came to my bedside at midnight to tell me she had no gift for me. “If I pay you more, we will misunderstand each other, and you will leave me. But the one thing I can give you that you want is your full liberty.” She kept that promise, and every small shackle she had ever laid on me vanished after that. I was happy to work twice as hard for her, no pressure needed.

My visits to Paulina soon made it clear my company would not be needed much longer. M. de Bassompierre, for all his sharp scientific mind, was still completely convinced his “little Polly” was still a child, totally blind to the way his daughter was growing up, completely unaware of the soft, tender little romance that was blooming between her and Graham Bretton, right under his nose.
With her father, Paulina was still playful, affectionate, the little girl he remembered. With me, she was serious, thoughtful, fully a woman. With Graham? She was painfully shy. His step would make her start, his entrance hush her, she could barely string a sentence together to answer him, and after he left she would fume at herself for being so awkward. Even her father noticed.
“Polly, you are far too shy,” he told her one day. “You treat Dr. Bretton like a total stranger. Don’t you remember how fond you were of him when you were small?”
“Rather fond, papa,” she echoed, in that soft, gentle little tone of hers.
“Then talk to him, get rid of that silly strangeness. He won’t bite you, I promise.”
“He barely talks to me,” she said. “Do you think he is afraid of me?”
“Any man would be afraid of such a quiet little lady!” he laughed.
The next day, I watched her make a brave, graceful effort to talk to him of ordinary, everyday things. The glow on Graham’s face was bright and soft, he spoke to her in his gentlest tones, like he was breathing around a wisp of gossamer happiness he dared not break with a sharp breath. It was the most exquisite, fairy-like thing I had ever seen. After he left, Paulina ran to her father.
“Did I do well, papa? Did I behave better?”
“My Polly behaved like a queen,” he said, grinning. “Soon we will have to polish up our own manners so we don’t get outshone.”

Since I refused to be her paid companion, Paulina talked me into taking weekly German lessons with her from the same tutor, so we would have a regular, consistent excuse to spend hours together every week. M. de Bassompierre was delighted, perfectly pleased that the quiet, discreet model governess Miss Snowe would spend her free time with his precious daughter.
I smiled to myself, thinking of how very differently everyone saw me. Madame Beck thought I was learned, a bluestocking. Miss Fanshawe thought I was sharp, cynical, ironic. Mr. Home thought I was the very model of sedate, proper governess correctness. And M. Paul Emanuel lost no chance to hint he thought I was fiery, rash, unruly, and audacious. None of them were entirely right. The only person who saw the real me, I thought, was small, quiet Paulina Mary.

The dawn that followed unfolded far brighter, busier, and more full of unexpected turns than I had dared to anticipate. It was the birthday of the eldest young prince of Labassecour, the Duc de Dindonneau, and every school across the city had been granted a public holiday in his honor—none more bustling than the principal Athénée college, where the students had drafted a formal loyal address to present before the royal party. The ceremony was to be held in the great public hall where yearly examinations and prize distributions took place: after the address was read, one of the college’s most renowned professors would deliver a grand public oration.

Many of M. de Bassompierre’s learned savant friends were closely affiliated with the Athénée, and he had been invited to join their party alongside the burgomaster, the city council, and every parent and relative of the college’s students. His beloved daughter Paulina would of course attend too, and she sent us a little handwritten note bidding Ginevra Fanshawe and me come early to join her group.

As we dressed side by side in the Rue Fossette dormitory, Ginevra suddenly paused mid-brushstroke and burst into peals of laughter.
“What’s got into you now?” I asked.
“It seems absurd, doesn’t it?” she said, with her usual unapologetic mix of frankness and cheeky insolence. “That you and I are now on equal footing, moving in the exact same social circle, counting the same people as friends.”
“I don’t disagree,” I told her. “Though I never had much patience for the crowd you used to run with—Mrs. Cholmondeley and her set would never have suited me at all.”
“Who even are you, Lucy Snowe?” she asked, her curiosity so unguarded and unvarnished that it was my turn to laugh. “You used to call yourself a nursery governess! When you first came to this house I saw you carry little Georgette in your arms like a common bonne—hardly any governess would have stooped that low. Now Madame Beck treats you with more courtesy than she gives that fancy Parisian teacher St. Pierre, and my proud little cousin Paulina acts like you’re her dearest bosom friend!”
“It is quite the marvel,” I agreed, highly entertained by her utter confusion. “Who am I, indeed? Maybe I’m some grand personage in disguise. Shame I don’t look the part, isn’t it?”
“I’m surprised you aren’t more flustered by all this attention,” she went on. “You take it all so weirdly calmly. If you really are the nobody I once thought you were, you’ve got nerves of steel.”
“The nobody you once thought me!” My cheeks flushed a little, but I refused to take offense. What did a schoolgirl’s silly crude labels of “nobody” and “somebody” matter at all? I only told her I had simply been met with basic kindness, and asked what about that kindness was supposed to throw me into a fever of embarrassment.
“Some things are just impossible not to wonder at,” she insisted.
“Wonder at myths you’ve made up in your own head. Are you finally ready to leave?”
“Yes. Let me take your arm.”
“I’d rather we walked side by side.” Whenever she took my arm, she leaned her entire weight on me, and since I was neither a gentleman nor her suitor, I found the habit irksome.
“There you go again!” she cried. “I was trying to pay you a compliment! I wanted to say I approve of how you look today, that I’m proud to be seen with you.”
“Ah. You meant you weren’t ashamed to walk down the Rue Crécy on my arm, in case Mrs. Cholmondeley was cooing over her lapdog in some window, or Colonel de Hamal picking his teeth on a balcony, and spotted us together?”
“Exactly,” she said, with that blunt honesty that was her only redeeming quality—the trait that even made her outright lies feel unfeigned, the only thing keeping her flighty, shallow character from rotting through entirely.

I let my amused, half-scornful smile answer for me, and she grumbled all the way across the great square and into the quiet, pleasant park that was our shortest route to the Rue Crécy. “No one in the whole world is such a tyrant to me as you are!” she complained.
“You bring it on yourself. Leave me alone, be quiet, and I’ll leave you alone too.”
“How can I leave you alone when you’re so weird and mysterious?”
“All that mystery is maggots in your own brain. Be good enough to leave them out of my sight.”
“But are you actually someone important?” She pushed her hand under my arm anyway, and I pressed my arm tight to my side to try to shake her off, but she clung on tight, squeezing until I had to stop in the middle of the path and laugh. The whole walk home she rambled on about her silly theory that I was hiding some grand secret identity, proving over and over that she could not fathom how a person without noble birth, family wealth, or a famous name could possibly hold their head high and live with unassuming integrity. For my part, I cared nothing for pedigree or social rank: the parts of my life that truly mattered were the people who knew me well, the rest I could take or leave. I had learned long ago the world places far more value on status, and I do not blame it. There are people whose social position is the very guardrail that keeps them morally steady, people who would lose all their self-respect if their humble origins were laid bare. Who are we to judge them for clinging to those small defenses that keep them from debasement?

We reached the Hôtel Crécy to find Paulina already fully dressed and waiting, with Mrs. Bretton at her side. Under the chaperonage of Mrs. Bretton and M. de Bassompierre, we were soon escorted to the ceremony hall, and shown to excellent seats well within view of the orator’s Tribune. The Athénée students were lined up before us, the city council and burgomaster sat in their places of honor, the young princes and their tutors occupied a prominent raised platform, and every corner of the hall was packed with Villette’s aristocracy and wealthiest burghers.

I had not cared at all who would be delivering the scheduled oration. I vaguely assumed some stuffy old savant would stand up and drone through half dogmatic lectures to the students, half fawning flattery of the royal princes. The Tribune stood empty when we first entered, but ten minutes later, a head, broad chest, and pair of broad shoulders popped up suddenly above the crimson desk.

I knew that head immediately. The tight black curls, the wide pale brow, the sharp bright blue fire in his eyes—every single detail was so familiar, tied to a hundred silly, chaotic little memories of the Rue Fossette classroom, that I burst out laughing so hard I grew warm, hiding my face behind my handkerchief and lowered veil so no one could see my unseemly mirth.

It was M. Paul Emanuel. I realized I was glad to see him. It felt far more pleasant than awkward to watch him stand up there, fierce and frank, sharp and unapologetic, testy and fearless, exactly the same as he was when he ruled over his classroom estrade. He filled the role so perfectly I’d never once thought to expect him, even though I knew he held the Belles Lettres chair at the Athénée. With him at the Tribune, I knew we would be spared all the hollow formality and empty flattery—but nothing could have prepared me for what he actually said.

He spoke to princes, nobles, magistrates, and ordinary burghers with the exact same unflinching ease, the same sharp, fiery earnestness he used to lecture his three unruly classroom divisions. He did not talk down to the college students like schoolboys—he spoke to them as future citizens, as patriots in the making. His words rang with a political courage I had never expected to sprout from the flat, complacent soil of Labassecour. He trampled over empty Utopian fantasies underheel, dismissed foolish whims with scorn, and when he turned to speak of tyranny, a light sparked in his eyes that was almost breathtaking. When he spoke of injustice, his voice was no soft murmur—it blared like a military trumpet echoing across the park at twilight. His whole audience might not have caught the full fire of his convictions, but the young students ate his words up, and gave him a long, thundering, ringing cheer when he finished. He was their undisputed favorite professor, sharp edges and all.

When our party left the hall, he was standing by the entrance. He spotted me at once, lifted his hat, and pressed my hand as we passed, uttering that typical, impulsive little question: “Qu’en dites vous?” What do you think? Even in this moment of his greatest public triumph, he could not help that restless, inquisitive streak that sometimes made him lack the calm self-control he should have cultivated. He should not have cared what any random person thought of his speech at that moment—but he did, and he was too natural, too impulsive to hide it. If I found his over-eagerness a little silly, I loved his total unpretentious naiveté. My heart was full of praise for him, but the words would not come—who ever finds the right words at the exact right moment? I stammered through a few awkward, lame phrases, and was deeply relieved when other well-wishers swarmed around him with gushing congratulations to cover for my floundering.

Someone introduced him to M. de Bassompierre, and the Count, who had been equally moved by the speech, invited him to join their party and dine with them at the Hôtel Crécy. He declined the dinner—he was always a little shy of wealthy people’s overtures, carrying a quiet, unobtrusive, rock-solid strain of independent pride that was all the more charming for never being flaunted. He did promise, however, to drop by later that evening with his friend the French Academician M. A—.

At dinner that day, Ginevra and Paulina were each beautiful in their own entirely distinct way. Ginevra might have had the edge in raw, obvious physical charm: her deep crimson dress set off her pale curls perfectly, matching the rose glow of her cheeks. But Paulina shone with a far more subtle, spiritual grace: her eyes were bright and eloquent, her bearing soft and graceful, her expression shifting with a thousand delicate winning nuances. Her dress was simple, close-fitting, and made of pure, clear white that made her soft complexion glow, her hair darker than Ginevra’s Saxon blonde, her brows and long lashes rich dark brown, every feature finished with a delicate, careful hand that had only sketched faint, careless outlines on Ginevra’s face.

Paulina was a little awed by the room full of learned savants, but she was not struck completely mute. She spoke softly, modestly, a little shyly, but with such sweet warmth and sharp, gentle good sense that her father paused his own conversations more than once just to look at her, his face glowing with proud delight. It was the polite, courtly learned M. Z— who drew her into talk, and her French was perfect: every sentence structure correct, every idiom natural, her accent pure as a native’s. Even Ginevra, who had spent half her life living on the Continent, could never match that flawless fluency, and M. de Bassompierre, who was very particular about the language, could not have been more pleased.

There was one more observer at the table: Dr. John Bretton, who had arrived late, detained by last-minute work at his practice. He glanced quietly at both young women the moment he sat down, and returned that careful, lingering survey more than once. His arrival perked Ginevra right up: she stopped looking listless, began smiling and chattering nonstop, though almost nothing she said was relevant to the intellectual conversation around the table. I could not help noticing that while Graham’s eyes were on her and his ears were listening, his sharp, lively, intelligent taste was not being fully engaged. Still, he was perfectly courteous, gave her almost all his attention through the whole meal, and she left the table for the drawing room in the highest of spirits.

No sooner had we stepped into the drawing room than Ginevra grew bored again, throwing herself on a couch to complain that the oration and the entire dinner party were dreadfully stupid, and asking Paulina how she could stand to spend time around such a pack of stuffy, prosaic old fogeys. But the second we heard the gentlemen’s footsteps heading our way, she stopped her complaints cold, jumped up, and dashed over to the piano to play with a great show of spirit. Dr. Bretton was the first to enter the room, and he went straight to stand by her side. I had expected he would soon drift over to the hearth where Paulina sat, but he only glanced that way, and it was the learned French savants who clustered around her, charmed by her quiet grace, her soft courtesy, her sharp, quiet tact. They did not bore her with talk of dry science—they spoke of literature, art, daily life, and soon it became clear she had read widely and thought deeply on all of it. I knew Graham was listening, even from across the room: his senses were sharp, and he was absorbing every word of that gentle, clever conversation, looking so pleased it almost hurt. Under that soft, delicate exterior, Paulina held far more strength of feeling and character than anyone guessed—strength that would keep her grace and beauty steady through any storm, long after shallow, unrooted prettiness faded.

While he waited for a gap in the group around Paulina, his wandering glance fell on me, sitting in a quiet nook not far from Mrs. Bretton and M. de Bassompierre, who were as usual deep in their easy, intimate tête-à-tête. Graham smiled in recognition, crossed the room to ask how I was, and told me I looked pale. I smiled to myself: it had been almost three whole months since he had spoken to me one on one, a lapse he had not even noticed. He sat down beside me, quiet, content to look around the room more than talk. He could gaze at Ginevra and Paulina both, study every detail of both their faces, as much as he liked.

I had caught glimpses of M. Paul hovering in the back inner saloon earlier, too, dark and severe, his sharp professor’s profile half-hidden by the doorway. He knew most of the gentlemen in attendance, but almost none of the ladies except me, and he started to walk over to say hello—until he spotted Dr. Bretton sitting beside me. He changed his mind, hung back, puckered his brows, stuck his lip out in that familiar grumpy scowl, and looked so absurdly cross that I averted my eyes to hide my smile. His brother Joseph Emanuel was there too, and he was just then taking over the piano from Ginevra, filling the room with grand, rich, masterful notes that made the old piano sing far better than it ever had under Ginevra’s schoolgirl fingers.

“Lucy,” Graham said, breaking the silence with a smile, as Ginevra glided past us and threw him a quick glance, “Miss Fanshawe is undeniably a remarkably fine girl.”
I nodded in agreement.
“Is there anyone else in the room as lovely as she is?”
“I don’t think there’s anyone here quite so classically handsome.”
“I agree with you. You and I so often share the same opinions, the same taste, I think.”
“Do we?” I said, a little doubtful.
“I believe if you had been a boy, Lucy, instead of a girl—mamma’s godson instead of her goddaughter—we would have been the very best of friends. Our opinions would have fit together perfectly.” He was half bantering, half gentle, half ironic, that familiar warm light glinting in his eye. I could not help the small, sad little ache in my chest: if I had been born wealthy, high born, pretty in the way other women were, would he have ever dismissed me as a quiet, inoffensive shadow the way he so often did? I pushed the feeling away, and asked him calmly what points we were so perfectly aligned on.
“Both of us are sharp observers. You probably don’t think I notice anything, but I do.”
“But we might see the exact same thing and judge it completely differently, mightn’t we?”
“Let’s test it. Of course you admire Miss Fanshawe. What do you think of the other people here? My mother, for example, or those two learned lions Messieurs A— and Z—, or that little pale lady, Miss de Bassompierre?”
“You know how much I think of your mother. I haven’t spared any thought for Messieurs A and Z.”
“And the pale little lady?”
“I think she is exactly what you say: a pale little lady, especially right now, fatigued from all the excitement.”
“Don’t you remember her as a child?”
“I wonder sometimes if you do.”
“I had completely forgotten her. But it’s strange, the way old people, old moments, old words that had slipped your mind can rise up again, under the right circumstances.”
“That’s certainly true.”
“But those old memories are fuzzy, half like a dream, half like a fantasy. You need a witness to confirm they’re real. Weren’t you staying at Bretton house ten years ago, when Mr. Home brought his little daughter, little Polly, to stay with mamma?”
“I was there the night she arrived, and the morning she left again.”
“Such a strange little child, wasn’t she? I wonder how I treated her. Was I even kind to children back then, big reckless schoolboy that I was? But you don’t remember me back then, do you?”
“You’ve seen your own portrait at La Terrasse. It looks exactly like you. Your manners are almost exactly the same now as they were then.”
“That only makes me more curious. What am I like today? What was I like ten years ago?”
“Gracious to everything that pleased you, never intentionally cruel to anything.”
“You’re wrong. I think I was almost a brute to you, for instance.”
“A brute? No, Graham. I would never have patiently put up with brutality.”
“But I do remember this: quiet Lucy Snowe never got any of that kindness from me.”
“Nor any of your cruelty, either.”
“Even Nero himself could not have tormented someone as harmless, as inoffensive as a shadow.”
I smiled, but I had to muffle a small, quiet groan. I only wished he

M. Paul Emanuel was notoriously, acutely sensitive to interruption during his lessons, no matter the cause. To cross his classroom mid-lecture was a prospect every teacher and pupil at the pensionnat considered a risk to life and limb.

Even Madame Beck herself, when forced to brave it, would scurry across with skirts tucked tight, edging around his raised teaching platform like a ship skirting jagged breakers. Poor Rosine the portress, whose half-hourly job it was to fish pupils out of his class to take music lessons scattered across the building, regularly grew nearly tongue-tied with terror after two or three trips in a row, struck dumb by the searing, darting glares he sent her way through his spectacles.

One morning, I sat in the adjacent workroom, stitching embroidery a pupil had left half-finished, my ears drinking in the rising, stormy crescendo of his voice from the next room, every note more unquiet, more ominously sharp than the last. A thick stone wall separated me from his gathering temper, and the glass door leading to the courtyard stood open for easy escape if the storm drifted my way, so I fear I found more amusement than alarm in his brewing irritation. Rosine, though, had no such shelter: five times already that blessed morning she had braved his class, and now a sixth summons came, to fetch yet another pupil out from under his very nose.

“Mon Dieu, what am I to do?” she whimpered. “He will kill me, I swear it, he is in such a rage!”
Nerved by pure desperation, she wrenched the door open.
“Mademoiselle La Malle for her piano lesson!” she called.
Before she could slip back out or even pull the door shut, his voice boomed out: “From this moment, this class is forbidden territory. The first person who opens this door, or even steps across this threshold, will be hanged—if it is Madame Beck herself.”

Barely ten minutes had passed since that edict was issued when I heard Rosine’s soft slippers shuffling down the corridor again.
“Mademoiselle,” she said, “I would not take five francs to step into that class right now. Monsieur’s spectacles are truly terrifying, and there’s a messenger from the Athénée waiting with an urgent note. I told Madame Beck I dared not deliver it, and she says you have to go.”
“Me? That is hardly my job. Come, Rosine, bear your own burden. Be brave, go just once more.”
“I? Impossible! I have crossed him five times already today. Madame ought to hire a gendarme for this work. I am quite done in!”
“Coward,” I teased, though I was half smiling. “What is the message, then?”
“Precisely the kind of summons he hates most. He is to report to the Athénée at once, an official inspector has arrived and he *must* meet him. You know how he rebels against anything that tries to command him.”

I knew well enough. The stubborn, fiery little man detested being prodded or ruled, and would bristle at any order, no matter how reasonable. Still, I accepted the task—not without a flicker of fear, but tangled up in equal parts curiosity, and that strange, quiet thrill I always felt when I dared cross paths with his temper. I turned the handle slowly, slipped inside, and shut the door behind me as softly and quickly as my slightly unsteady hand would allow: to rattle a latch, or leave the door gaping, was a crime far more likely to enrage him than the intrusion itself.

There he sat, hunched over his desk, his mood clearly nearly at its worst. He was teaching arithmetic, a subject he despised more than any of his pupils feared it, and every last girl in the room was trembling at the sound of his sharp, staccato voice listing off sums. He made no move to look up, too proud to acknowledge that someone had dared defy his explicit order, and that gave me time to walk the length of the long classroom. I had never minded facing his anger close up, far less than I feared its distant, menacing threats.

I paused at the foot of his raised platform, just out of his immediate line of sight. I was not tall enough to see over the edge of his desk, and after two quick, unobserved peeks around the corner to catch a glimpse of his face—dark, sharp, almost tiger-like with suppressed irritation—my third glance was caught, pinned straight through the pupil of my eye, by the sharp, unblinking lenses of his spectacles. Rosine was right: those glasses held a blank, unyielding terror that outstripped even the fury of his bare eyes.

Luckily for me, those same short-sighted spectacles were useless for inspecting a trespasser standing right under his nose. He took them off at once, and suddenly we were on far more equal footing.

I was not really afraid of him, not close up, and when he grumbled about finding rope and a gallows to carry out his sentence, I had the presence of mind to pass him a threaded needle from my embroidery work, the thread already looped into a noose, tucked discreetly over the back of his chair where no one but he could see.

“Que me voulez-vous?” he growled, the sound rumbling deep in his chest, his teeth still clenched so tight it seemed impossible a smile could ever break free.
“Monsieur,” I said, unflinching, “I have come to ask for the impossible, the unheard of.” I wasted no time beating around the bush, and in a low, quick voice I delivered the summons from the Athénée, exaggerating its urgency just a little to push him over the edge into complying.

Of course, he immediately protested that he would not go, that he would not leave his class for any inspector, any king, any government body in Villette. But I knew duty and his own professional interests would force him to comply, so I stood silent, waiting, as if he had not spoken a word at all. When he snapped at me to say what else I wanted, I only answered that I needed his answer to give the waiting messenger.

I reached out, before he could stop me, for his battered Greek cap resting on the windowsill. He watched the daring movement with a look of half-amused, half-incredulous pity, that I would ever presume to touch his things.
“Ah,” he muttered, “if Miss Lucy is going that far, she might as well put the cap on herself, turn newsboy, and go to the Athénée in my place.”
I set the cap gently on his desk, where its tassel seemed to nod at me like a tiny, solemn judge.
“I will write a note of apology,” he said, still trying to evade the task. “That will be enough.”

I knew very well it would not. I gave the cap a soft nudge, pushing it towards his hand. It slid down the smooth, unlined slope of his polished desk, knocked his discarded spectacles right over the edge, and they clattered onto the wooden platform. A dozen times before I had seen them fall unharmed—this time, by some cruel twist of my bad luck, both lenses shattered into a thousand glittering, shapeless pieces.

Dismay flooded me, sharp and hot. I knew how precious those glasses were: M. Paul’s sight was strange, hard to fit, and this pair had been perfectly adjusted to his eyes. He often called them his most treasured possession. My hands shook as I picked up the broken fragments, and for several seconds I dared not look him in the face. I was far more sorry than I was afraid.

“Là,” he said, and to my shock there was no anger in his voice at all. “Now I am a widower, robbed of my spectacles. I think Mademoiselle Lucy will admit she has more than earned the rope and the gallows. You tremble, waiting for your doom. Ah, traitress! Traitress! You are determined to leave me blind and helpless in your hands.”

I lifted my eyes, and his face was not furrowed with rage at all. It was soft, lit up by that bright, warm bloom of a smile I had seen on him that night at the Hotel Crécy. He was not angry, not even grieved. The accident I had thought would ruin any chance of convincing him to go had turned out to be my greatest help. Stubborn and unmanageable as long as I had done nothing to cross him, he turned gentle, pliant, almost indulgent the second I stood before him a guilty, contrite offender.

He kept teasing me gently, calling me a terrible Englishwoman, a reckless little breaker of things, saying no one could dare refuse a woman bold enough to smash his spectacles to get her way, just like the great Emperor smashing a vase to cow his enemies. At last he settled his Greek cap on his head, took the broken glasses from my hand with a little squeeze of pardon and encouragement, bowed, and left for the Athénée in the highest of good spirits.

And yet, for all that unexpected amiability, I found myself quarrelling with him again before the sun had fully set on that day—and I could not help it.

It was his frequent, charming custom to arrive unannounced in the evenings, bursting into the quiet hour of study to take over our occupations, order all the pious, droning readings led by half-asleep pupils put away, and pull out a thick volume of drama or a stack of new Parisian feuilletons to read aloud. He would pour his own fiery, bright verve into every line, cutting out every unsuitable phrase for the young girls in the room, and whenever a passage would be left gaping and meaningless after his cuts, he would improvise whole new paragraphs far more vivid and proper than the parts he had struck out.

That evening, we were all sitting silent as nuns in retreat, pupils bent over their books, teachers over their work. I was stitching a small, intricate piece I meant as a gift, and the deadline to give it was close, so my fingers moved fast, entirely focused on the work.

We heard his familiar sharp ring at the bell, then his quick, familiar step down the corridor. The moment every girl whispered *“Voilà Monsieur!”* the double doors flew open, and he stood in the middle of the room.

Zélie St. Pierre, the senior teacher, jumped up at once to offer him her seat by the lamp, smiling that wide, sharp fake grin that stretched all across her face but never reached her eyes. But M. Paul, as capricious as they said women could be, never even seemed to see her. He walked straight past her, stopped right beside me, and whispered *“Ne bougez pas”* before I could stand up to make space, settling himself right between me and Ginevra Fanshawe, who had been crowding my side all winter, prodding me with her elbow every few minutes for warmth. I had even taken to tucking a small pin in my sash to deter her.

It was all very well for him to tell me not to move, but I had to make space for him. I pulled my embroidery basket back, shifted myself a full yard down the bench to leave him plenty of room, the sort of polite, respectful gap any reasonable man would have welcomed. But M. Paul Emanuel was never reasonable. Flint and tinder, he caught fire the instant he saw the empty space I had left between us.

“You do not want me as your neighbour,” he growled, scowling. “You put on airs of rank, you treat me like a pariah. Very well! I will fix this.”
He ordered all the girls to stand, file over to the other long table, then carefully carried my work basket, my silk, my scissors, every last one of my tools, over to the far end of my bench, and planted himself at the completely opposite end, a full ten feet away from me.

No one dared laugh at the absurd, dramatic arrangement. I sat completely calm, stitching away, quiet and not at all unhappy, even cut off from everyone else in the room.
“Is this distance enough for you?” he demanded.
“You are the judge of what is enough, Monsieur,” I said.
“You know very well it is not. You created this enormous empty gap, I had no part in it,” he insisted, and opened his book to begin reading.

He had chosen a terrible French translation of what he called “a drama by Williams Shakespeare, that false god of those silly pagan Englishmen”—a title he never would have used, I knew, if his temper had not already been ruffled. The translation was clunky, full of absurd mistranslations, and I did not bother hiding the little flickers of amused contempt that crossed my face. His spectacles were sharp, and he caught every single one of my tiny eye rolls. Soon he was fuming so hard, exiled at his end of the bench, that he was hotter than he would ever have been sitting right under the full glow of the overhead lamp.

When the reading ended, no one was sure if he would leave without saying a word, or let his anger spill over. I had not spoken a single sound, so he had no real grounds to scold me. The plates of bread and tepid diluted milk for supper were brought in, and everyone ate, chatting softly across the other table, while I stayed right on my end of the bench, working as I munched my bread, perfectly calm. The strange, restless, thorny energy of him across the room seemed to draw all the jittery, unsettled feelings out of me like a magnet, leaving nothing but quiet, steady peace.

He stood up, turned for the door. I thought he would leave without a word. But he turned back, fumbled for his forgotten pencil case, snapped the point of his pencil against the table, re-sharpened it, and then walked straight over to me.

He stood behind my chair. He asked what I was making.
“A watchguard,” I said.
“For whom?” he demanded.
“For a gentleman friend of mine,” I answered.

He leaned down, and hissed in my ear, voice sharp with jealous, tender irritation, that I was the most exasperating woman he had ever met, that no one could be on friendly terms with me for five minutes without me turning all harmony to discord. He said he wished me no harm, he had always meant well by me, but I treated him as if he was a nuisance, full of stubborn mutiny against every gentle thing he tried to do for me.

“Vivacities? Mutiny?” I said, surprised enough to interrupt him. “I never—”
“Chut! There you go again, as fiery as gunpowder,” he fumed, but there was no real bite in it. He said he grieved for my stubbornness, that I was wasting my good qualities on being too fond of vain, pretty, tall people who cared more about their looks than anything of substance, that I ought to be more sober, less flighty, less silly. He chided me for my recent clothes, too: the flowers in my bonnet, the embroidered collars, the time he had seen me in a bright scarlet gown.

“It was not scarlet!” I protested, half indignant half laughing. “It was pale pink, and covered over with black lace.”
“Pink, scarlet, all the same, all those giddy, flaunting colours,” he sighed, mourning my supposed degeneracy. When I asked him what possible fashion he could find in my plain winter merino dress and simple white collar, he said there was a ribbon bow tied at my neck, far too frivolous. I held up the half-finished silk and gold watchguard in my hand, teasing him if he disapproved of a ribbon bow on a woman, he would surely disapprove of a watchguard for a man too. He only groaned, as if my levity pained him deeply.

He sat in silence for a few minutes, watching my fingers fly over the stitching of the chain, before he spoke again, soft, almost quiet.
“Tell me,” he said. “After all the scolding I have just done—will you utterly detest me now?”

I rose an hour before the first streak of dawn seeped through the dormitory windows, and knelt on the cold bare floor beside the centre lamp stand to finish my work, working by the last glimmer of the sputtering night-lamp as it guttered to its end. Every bead, every skein of coloured silk I owned was exhausted before the guard I wove reached the length and richness I had dreamed of: I stitched it double over, knowing full well that to catch the eye of the sharp, exacting man I designed it for, nothing short of a lush, intentional finish would do. For a final touch I pried the small gold clasp from my only necklace, fastened it neatly to the end of the chain, coiled the finished piece soft and compact, and tucked it into a tiny polished box I had bought for its rare nacarat-pink sheen, carved from a tropical shell and crowned with a circlet of tiny sparkling blue stones. With the sharp point of my scissors I carefully scored his full initials deep into the inside of the lid: P. C. D. E., for Paul Carl David Emanuel, that long string of baptismal names every continental gentleman seemed to carry.

You will remember the lavish annual fête held for Madame Beck, no doubt, and how every pupil contributed a small sum to buy her a grand gift. That honour was reserved only for her, save for the spontaneous, unscripted tributes offered each year to her kinsman, the fiery, adored Professor Emanuel. No one planned or pressured the girls to participate for his day: it rose of its own accord, proof of the quiet fondness everyone held for him, even in spite of his quick temper, stubborn prejudices, and bouts of unsparing sharpness. He would not accept costly plate or jewellery, he had made that perfectly clear—no pompous gift of gold or diamonds would please him half so much as a single wild flower, or a simple sketch, offered honestly and without fanfare. That was his nature: far from the worldly, calculating men around him, there was a tender, unguarded spot deep in his chest that leaned toward soft, unvarnished goodness.

Monsieur Paul’s fête fell on the first of March, a Thursday, and the day broke bright and sun-warmed. It was a day set aside for morning mass, with a half-holiday promised for the afternoon to walk the streets, shop, or call on friends, and the whole school buzzed with uncharacteristic excitement. Clean new collars appeared on every girl’s uniform, the usual dingy workday wool dresses were swapped out for lighter, brighter fabrics. Mademoiselle Zélie St. Pierre went so far as to wear a full silk gown, a luxury almost unheard of in thrifty Labassecour, and sent for a professional coiffeur to arrange her hair that morning; sharp-eyed pupils spotted her dabbing expensive new perfume on her handkerchief and wrists. Poor Zélie! She had complained for months that she was sick to death of seclusion and drudgery, that she dreamed of a husband who would pay off her mounting debts, stock her wardrobe, and set her free to “taste a little of life’s pleasures.” Everyone had long suspected she set her cap for Monsieur Emanuel. And it was true, his eyes often lingered on her, for minutes at a stretch, while the class worked silent at their desks and he sat idle on his raised platform. She would squirm under that unblinking gaze, half-flattered, half-confused, while he read right through every petty lie and hidden desire she tried to veil, that terrible, unerring instinct of his peeling back every soft, flowery pretense to lay bare the warped, empty corners of the soul no one else ever saw. If you owned your flaws to him openly, he would pity and forgive you anything—but if he met with denial and deceit? He could be cruel, brutally so, snatching the screen from cowering pretenders and dragging them out for all to see their own falsity, convinced he was doing righteous justice. More than once I had cried for his victims, and snapped back at him in anger, and he never once understood why I would side with the liars he thought he had a right to unmask.

When breakfast was over and mass attended, the school bell rang and the classroom filled, every pupil and teacher neatly dressed, holding a fresh bouquet of spring blooms that perfumed the whole air. Only my hands were empty. I love to see flowers growing in the earth, but plucked roots are nothing to me: they are rootless, perishable, too much a reminder of something already dying, and they make me sad. I never offer cut flowers to those I love, and I never wish to receive them from dear hands. Zélie spotted my empty hands instantly, and her eyes darted around me in greedy triumph, certain I must have hidden at least a small knot of violets somewhere. When she saw I truly had nothing, no bloom, no leaf at all, as bare as a winter tree, she smiled, well pleased.
“How wise of you to save your money, Miss Lucy!” she trilled. “Foolish me, I threw two whole francs away on this hothouse bouquet!” She held it aloft, preening at the size and richness of it.

Hush. There was that step, the familiar quick, sharp footfall that belonged only to him. It was as prompt as ever, but it carried a lightness we all liked to think was born of happy anticipation, not his usual nervous vehemence. He stepped through the door in a mood that was brighter than the morning sun spilling over the classroom walls. He had dressed for the occasion, no shapeless, soot-dark conspirator’s overcoat hiding his frame, no defiant old Greek cap perched on his head. Instead he wore a neat, well-cut coat and a soft silk waistcoat that looked almost pretty, bare-headed, holding his formal hat in gloved hands. The little man looked unexpectedly fine: there was a warm glow of good humour in his bright blue eyes, a flush of gentle feeling on his dark cheeks that outshone conventional beauty, and no one cared to notice his slightly lopsided nose, thin cheeks, square marked brow, or un-rosebud mouth. He was himself, and his presence was warm, alive, impossible to ignore. He set his hat and gloves on his desk.
“Bon jour, mes amies,” he said, in a soft, rough tone that made amends for every sharp snap and savage snarl we had endured from him before. It was his voice, the one he only used when his heart was speaking before his lips, the tender spot deep in his chest coming to the surface, the part that was softer to women and little children than most men ever dared to be.
“We all wish Monsieur a happy fête day, and offer him our warmest congratulations,” Zélie announced, stepping forward uninvited as spokeswoman for the whole room, laying her lavish two-franc bouquet on his desk. He bowed over it, and the line of pupils filed past after her, leaving their small tributes behind, until the pile of blooms rose into a tall, lush pyramid, so tall it completely hid the man behind it. We all sat silent, waiting for his speech.

Five minutes passed. Ten. No sound. He was completely concealed behind the flowers, still as a statue. At last a deep, hollow voice rumbled out from behind the petals:
“Est-ce là tout?” (Is that all?)
Zélie glanced around, asked all the girls if they had given their bouquets. Every last one, from the eldest senior to the tiniest seven year old, nodded. “Est-ce là tout?” he repeated, his voice dropping several notes lower, heavier.
“Monsieur,” Zélie purred, rising with her sweetest, most affected smile, “I have the honour to tell you every single person in class has offered their bouquet, save one. Miss Lucy, as a foreigner, probably does not know our customs, and thinks this silly little ceremony too frivolous to honour with her presence.” I bit back a snort of amusement. Famous, Zélie, you are a very fine orator when you want to be.
From behind the flower pile, a hand waved dismissively, ordering silence. A moment later he stepped out from behind the petals, planted himself on the edge of his platform, and stared straight ahead at the giant wall map of the world, his tone tragic, sharp with hurt, as he asked a third time:
“Est-ce là tout?”
I still could have stepped forward, slipped my little pink shell box into his hand, and made everything right. That was what I had planned all along. But the comedy of his dramatic show had made me delay, and now Zélie’s petty, lying jab pricked my stubborn streak. I would not defend myself. I would not give him my gift just yet, not when he was being so dramatic, not when he so clearly thought I had snubbed him. Let him be vexed a little. I sat still, face blank as stone, clutching the shell box tight in my lap.

“C’est bien,” he said at last. A shadow of wrath, of bitter disappointment, crossed his brow, rippled over his lips, lined his cheeks. He swallowed down all the words he wanted to snap, and launched into his expected speech. I did not listen. The force of him gulping back his hurt sent a strange little jolt through me, half-laughable, half tender. Toward the end of his speech, I fumbled my thimble, dropped it on the floor, and hit my head hard on the sharp corner of my desk when I stooped to grab it. The small clatter was all it took to break his fragile forced equanimity. He threw all his careful dignity to the wind, and suddenly found himself ranting across the English Channel, furious about “les Anglaises.”
I have never heard English women handled with such vicious, spiteful glee. He spared nothing: our height, our long necks, our thin arms, our slovenly dress, our pedantic education, our impious scepticism, our unendurable pride, our self-righteous pretence. He bared his teeth and snarled, repeating every ridiculous, slanderous false continental myth about our greatest men and our national history, dragging the Union Jack through the mud. Zélie and the whole class grinned, delighted, for all of Labassecour secretly harboured a silly, petty hatred for England. I could not bear it. I slammed my fist down sharp on my desk, and yelled at the top of my voice:
“Vive l’Angleterre, l’Histoire et les Héros! A bas la France, la Fiction et les Faquins!”

The whole room froze, as if I had gone mad. Monsieur Paul hid his face in his handkerchief, and smiled a fiendish, triumphant little smile. He had won. He had provoked me out of my stubborn silence, and now his whole sour mood vanished. He turned soft and bland again, singing poetic praises of the flowers’ sweetness and purity, comparing each pupil to the blossoms on his desk, paying Zélie a grand, fulsome compliment on her bouquet, and announcing that the first warm mild spring morning, he would take the whole class out for breakfast in the country. “That is,” he added, sharply, “only those of you I can count among my friends.”
“Then I will not be coming,” I blurted out, before I could stop myself.
“Soit!” (So be it.) he snapped back. He gathered his whole armful of flowers, and stormed out of the classroom. I stuffed my neglected shell box back into my desk, grabbed my things, and ran up the stairs. I was hot, angry, my cheeks burning. And yet, an hour later, as I sat on the edge of my bed replaying the whole ridiculous scene over and over in my head, I found myself smiling. Only one small, soft pang of regret pricked me: I had meant to give him my gift, to make him happy, and fate had tangled everything up.

Later that afternoon, I remembered the classroom desks were never perfectly secure. I had better go retrieve the pink shell box, before someone found it and saw his initials scored into the lid. The school was quiet, fast asleep in its holiday repose: all day pupils had gone home, all boarders were out walking, all other teachers were off shopping or calling on friends. The great salle was empty, the grand piano closed and silent, the giant globe hanging still in the middle of the room. I was surprised to find the first classe door ajar—usually it was locked when empty, and only Madame Beck and I had keys to it. I heard movement inside: a step, a chair scraping, the soft click of a desk lid opening.
I peeked through the crack. It was not Madame Beck in her shawl and clean cap. It was Monsieur Paul, back in his familiar ink-stained old overcoat, that silly Greek cap of his lying discarded on the floor at his feet. His olive hand was propped in my open desk, his face buried in my papers, his back turned to me. I had known for ages he was my secret brownie, sneaking in when no one was around to go through my things. I would leave exercises full of mistakes on my desk overnight, and wake to find them carefully corrected. Tucked between a tattered sallow dictionary and worn grammar, I would find a brand new novel, or a well-loved classic, smelling strongly of his cigar smoke. Once I had tried to air one of those books out the window, and he had caught me, snatched it out of my hand, and almost threw it into the glowing stove before I could fight it back from him. I never fussed about the cigar smell after that.

And now I had caught him red-handed, the little friendly phantom, wreathed in pale blue cigar smoke, carefully tucking two beautiful, brand new volumes I had been dying to read into the back of my desk, his hands moving over my papers gently, so he would not bend or tear a single page. All the anger from that morning melted right out of me. I did not dislike him at all.
He heard my quiet breath, turned sharp as a flash. His nerves were frayed, but he never started, never paled. “I thought you were off to town with the other teachers,” he said, gripping hard to his slipping composure. “It is just as well you are not. I do not care if you catch me. I visit your desk all the time.”
“I know that, Monsieur.”
“You find a new book sometimes, I suppose. But you do not read them, not when they smell of this?” He tapped the cigar between his fingers.
“They do, and they are no worse for it. I read every one.”
“Without pleasure?”
“I would never dare contradict Monsieur.”
“Do you like them? Are they a welcome little amusement for you? You have seen me read them a hundred times. You know I have so few small recreations, I would never undervalue anything you give me.”
“I mean well,” he said, his voice softening. “If you can see that I mean well, if my little gifts do make you smile, why can we not be friends?”
“Any fatalist would say—because we cannot.”
“This morning,” he went on, his voice dropping, wounded, “I woke up in such a bright, happy mood. I came to class full of joy, and you spoiled my whole day.”
“No Monsieur, only an hour or two. And not on purpose.”
“Not on purpose! It was my fête day. Every last person wished me happiness, but you. Even the smallest little girls in the third division came up to me, lisped their congratulations, gave me a tiny knot of violets. You? Nothing. Not a bud, not a leaf, not even a kind glance. And that was unintentional?”
“I meant no harm.”
“Then you truly did not know our custom? You were not prepared? You would willingly have spent a few centimes on a flower to give me pleasure, if you had known it was expected? Say that, and all the hurt is gone, all is forgiven.”
“I did know it was expected. I was prepared. And I spent no centimes on flowers.”

Do not rush to easy, generous conclusions, dear reader—do not assume out of hasty charity that from our last gentle shared moment, M. Paul Emanuel softened at once into an easy, placid man, free of the sharp, unruly sparks that could flare into discomfort, even quiet hurt, at a moment’s notice. That would be to misjudge him entirely. He was made of moods as unreasonable and sudden as summer thunderstorms: overworked as he so often was, he curdled into acute irritation at the smallest provocation, and threaded through the very blood in his veins ran the bitter, heady tincture of belladonna—no mere soft, tender jealousy of the heart, but a sterner, narrower, unforgiving sentiment that rooted itself in his very thoughts.

Sitting across from him at his desk, watching him knit his brow or thrust out his lip over an exercise of mine that bore far fewer errors than he’d hoped to find (for he loved a handful of my blunders as a child loves a cluster of sweet shelled nuts), I often thought he bore striking points of resemblance to Napoleon Bonaparte. I think so still. In his shameless disregard for performative magnanimity, he matched the great Emperor step for step. M. Paul would have quarrelled with twenty learned women, would have carried on a whole system of petty bickering and sniping with an entire capital’s worth of literary coteries, never once troubling himself over lost dignity or what others thought of him. He would have exiled fifty Madame de Staëls if they annoyed, offended, outrivalled, or dared oppose him.

I well remember one blazing, ridiculous episode with a certain Madame Panache, a lady Madame Beck had temporarily employed to teach history. She was clever—knew a great deal, in fact, and had perfected the art of making every scrap of her knowledge shine; she had unlimited command of words and easy, unshakable confidence. Her looks were far from unappealing; most would have called her a “fine woman,” yet there was something in her robust, ample charms and her bustling, demonstrative presence that the nice, capricious tastes of M. Paul could not abide. The echo of her voice through the common hall would fluster him into a strange, fidgety state; her long, free stride down the corridor would often make him snatch up his papers and decamp on the instant.

One day, moved by pure mischief, he barged unannounced into her classroom. In a flash he picked apart her teaching method, saw it conflicted with a pet system of his own, and pointed out what he called her “errors” with barely a scrap of ceremony, let alone courtesy. Whether he expected her to bow to his authority and listen, I know not—what he got was sharp, bitter opposition, and a full, round reprimand for his entirely unjustifiable interference. Instead of withdrawing with the dignity he still could have claimed, he threw down the gauntlet of defiance. Madame Panache, as warlike as the Amazon Penthesilea, picked it up at once. She snapped her fingers in his face, and unleashed a storm of words. M. Emanuel was eloquent, but Madame Panache was unstoppably voluble. A state of fierce, unrelenting antagonism followed. Instead of laughing at his prickly, loud, proud foe, M. Paul detested her with complete, deadly seriousness; he honoured her with his full, earnest fury, pursuing her vindictively and implacably, refusing to rest peacefully in his bed, enjoy his meals, or even smoke his cigar serenely until she was rooted out of the boarding house entirely.

The Professor won, but I cannot say the laurels of that victory sat gracefully on his temples. Once I dared hint as much. To my great surprise, he admitted I might be right, but swore that when he came face to face with people of Madame Panache’s coarse, self-satisfied breed, he had no control over his own passions; an unspeakable, driving aversion impelled him to wage a war of total extermination. Three months later, when he heard his vanquished foe had fallen on hard times and was at real risk of destitution for lack of work, he forgot all his hatred. Equal parts ferocious in goodness and ferocity in temper, he moved heaven and earth to find her a new post. When she came to visit him to make amends and thank him for his kindness, that old slightly loud voice, that old slightly forward manner, acted on him so strongly that within ten minutes he started up and bowed her—quite as good as chased himself—out of the room, in a fit of nervous irritation.

To press that bold Napoleon parallel a little further: M. Emanuel loved power, hungered for supremacy, just as the Emperor did. He was never a man to be submitted to unthinkingly. Sometimes resistance was necessary; it was right to stand your ground, look straight up into his eyes, and tell him his demands went past all reason—that his absolutism verged on tyranny.

The first glimmers, the first faint stirrings of unique talent that fell under his watchful eye never failed to excite him, even disturb him. He would watch that talent fight to be born, brow dark with a scowl, holding back his hand as if to say, “Come on if you have the strength,” but refusing to help the labour along. When the pang and peril of that first struggle were over, when the new talent drew its first breath, when he saw its metaphorical lungs expand and contract, felt its heart beat and discovered life glinting in its eye, he still refused to coddle it. “Prove you are true before I cherish you,” was his unyielding rule—and what a trial he made that proof to be! He strewed thorns and briars, sharp flints, across the path of feet unused to rough, unforgiving travel. He watched tearlessly as he exacted ordeals that had to be faced fearlessly, following footprints that grew sometimes faint, sometimes streaked with blood, as they neared their goal—following grimly, keeping the strictest, most unforgiving watch over the pain-wearied pilgrim. And when he finally granted them rest, before they could let slumber close their eyelids, he would pry those lids wide open with pitiless thumb and finger, gazing deep past the pupil and iris into the brain, into the heart, hunting to see if Vanity, or Pride, or Falsehood in any of its subtlest forms could be hidden in the furthest, darkest recess of their being. Only when every last severest test had been endured, when the most corrosive nitric acid had been used and failed to tarnish the ore, did he admit it was genuine—and, still wrapped in clouded silence, stamp it with his deep, unmistakeable brand of approval.

I do not speak of these trials from hearsay. Up to the close of our last chapter, M. Paul had never been my teacher, never given me lessons—but around that time, he heard me admit one day to an ignorance of arithmetic that, as he very truly pointed out, would have shamed a boy from the poorest charity school. He took me in hand at once, examined me thoroughly, found me abundantly deficient as you might guess, pressed some books into my hands, and set me my first tasks.

He did this at first with open, unconcealed exultation, condescending to say he believed I was “bonne et pas trop faible” — well enough disposed, and not wholly destitute of wit, but, he supposed thanks to all the hard, adverse circumstances of my life, trapped in a state of wretchedly imperfect mental development. The start of every new effort in my life has been marked by that strange, preternatural dim-wittedness. I never could, even when making the most ordinary new acquaintance, lay claim to average quickness. A depressing, difficult passage has prefaced every new page I have turned in life.

So long as that heavy, overcast passage lasted, M. Paul was so kind, so gentle, so forbearing. He saw the sharp pain of my own humiliation at my incapacity, and words can barely do justice to how tender and helpful he was. His own eyes would moisten when tears of shame and effort clouded mine; burdened as he was with endless work, he would steal half his own short recreation time to spend on me.

But strange, bitter grief! When that heavy, overcast dawn finally began to yield to sunlight, when my faculties began to struggle free and wake, when my time of energy and fulfilment came, when I voluntarily doubled, trebled, quadrupled the tasks he had set to please him, as I thought—his kindness curdled into sternness. The soft beam in his eyes turned to a sharp, restless spark. He fretted, he opposed me, he curbed me imperiously; the more I did, the harder I worked, the less content he seemed. Sarcasms whose severity amazed and puzzled me harassed my ears; then flowed out the bitterest insinuations against the “pride of intellect.” I was vaguely threatened with who knows what doom if I ever trespassed the proper limits of my sex, and conceived a contraband appetite for unfeminine knowledge. Alas, I had no such grand, abstract appetite! What I loved, it joyed me to work as hard as I could to please—but the noble hunger for science for its own sake, the godlike thirst after discovery, those feelings only touched me in the briefest, faintest flashes. Even so, when M. Paul sneered at me, I wanted to know them more fully; his injustice stirred ambitious wishes in me, gave my aspirations wings. At first, before I understood his strange motives, that uncomprehended sneer made my heart ache—but little by little, it only warmed the blood in my veins, and sent new, quick action to my pulses. Whatever powers God had given me, feminine or otherwise, I felt resolute not to be ashamed of any faculty He had bestowed on me.

The combat was sharp for a time. It felt as if I had lost M. Paul’s affection entirely; he treated me so strangely. In his most unjust moments, he would insinuate I had deceived him when I pretended, as he called it, “faible” — incompetent. He said I had feigned false stupidity on purpose. Then he would turn around and accuse me of the most far-fetched, impossible plagiarisms, swearing I had extracted all the pith from books I had never even heard of, and would have fallen asleep over as deep as Eutychus if I’d ever tried to read them.

Once, after he levelled that accusation, I turned on him, rose up against him.
