I gathered an armful of his books out of my desk, piled them in my apron, and emptied the whole heap in a pile at his feet on his little raised platform. “Take them away, M. Paul,” I said, “and teach me no more. I never asked to be made learned, and you compel me to feel very profoundly that learning is not happiness.” I went back to my desk, laid my head on my arms, and would not speak to him for two days afterwards. He had pained and chagrined me. His affection had been so sweet and dear, a new, incomparable pleasure—now that it seemed withdrawn, I did not care for his lessons at all.

He did not take the books away, though. He picked them all up carefully and restored them to their places, and came to teach me the next day as usual. He made his peace somehow—too readily, perhaps: I ought to have held out longer, but when he looked kind and good, and held out his hand in amity, my memory refused to hold the weight of his oppressive moments long enough to stay cross. And reconcilement, of course, is always sweet.

One particular morning, a message arrived from my godmother, inviting me to attend a notable lecture at the public rooms we had visited before. Dr. John had brought the message himself, and delivered it verbally to Rosine, who did not scruple to follow M. Emanuel down the hall as he walked to the first class, stand brazen-faced right before my desk with her hand in her apron pocket, and recite the whole announcement out loud, saucy as could be, finishing with: “Qu’il est vraiment beau, Mademoiselle, ce jeune docteur! Quels yeux—quel regard! Tenez! J’en ai le cœur tout ému!”

When she was gone, my professor demanded to know why I suffered “that impudent girl, that creature without an ounce of modesty” to speak to me in such terms. I had no pacifying answer to give. The terms were exactly what Rosine—whose skull held very little room for the organs of reverence and reserve—spoke every day. Besides, what she said about the young doctor was true enough. Graham was handsome; he had fine eyes and a thrilling, unforgettable glance. “Elle ne dit que la vérité,” I said. “Ah! Vous trouvez?” “Mais, sans doute.”

The lesson we had to sit through that day was so tense, we were all half-trembling and half-exultant with relief when it was over, and the rest of the pupils rushed out. I was heading for the door too, when a mandate to remain arrested me. I muttered that I desperately needed fresh air—the stove was glowing hot, the whole room overheated. An inexorable voice merely recommended silence. This salamander of a man, for whom no room ever seemed too hot, sat himself right between my desk and the stove—a spot where anyone else would have roasted alive, but which did not bother him a bit—and proceeded to confront me with a Greek quotation.

Deep in M. Emanuel’s soul festered a chronic suspicion that I already knew both Greek and Latin. Just as people say monkeys could speak if they dared, and hide the gift for fear it will be used against them, so I was accused of hoarding a fund of knowledge I was criminally, craftily concealing. It was insinuated I had enjoyed all the privileges of a classical education, revelled in the honey of Hymettus, and had a whole golden store of learning hived in my memory to secretly nourish my wits.

A hundred tricks M. Paul used to try to surprise my supposed “secret”: he wheedled, he threatened, he tried to startle it out of me. Sometimes he left Greek and Latin books lying in my path, then watched me the way Joan of Arc’s jailors tempted her with warrior’s armour, lying in wait for a reaction. Other times he would quote lines from who knows what ancient authors, rolling their sweet, sonorous verses off his tongue—for he had a wonderful voice, remarkable for its range, modulation, and matchless expression—while he fixed on me a vigilant, piercing, often malicious stare. He clearly expected grand reactions sometimes, but none ever came: not comprehending a single word, of course I could be neither charmed nor annoyed. Baffled, almost angry, he clung stubbornly to his fixed idea, pronounced my susceptibilities marble, my face a mask. It was as if he could not bring himself to accept the plain, homely truth, and take me for exactly what I was: men, and women too, must have some kind of delusion to hold to, and if none is ready to hand, they will invent exaggerations for themselves.

At moments I *did* wish his suspicions had been well-founded. There were times I would have given my right hand to possess all the treasures he ascribed to me. He deserved condign punishment for his testy crotchets. I could have gloried in proving his worst fears true, bursting upon his vision in a blaze of acquired erudition that would have confronted and confounded his little spectacles once and for all. Oh, why had no one tried to make me clever when I was young enough to learn, that I might by one grand, sudden, inhuman revelation—one cold, cruel, overwhelming triumph—crush that mocking spirit right out of Paul Carl David Emanuel!

Alas, no such feat was in my power. That day, as usual, his quotations fell completely flat. He soon shifted his ground. His next favourite theme was “women of intellect,” and here he was entirely in his element. A “woman of intellect,” it appeared, was a sort of lusus naturae, a luckless freak of nature, a thing with no place or use in creation, wanted neither as wife nor worker. Beauty far outstripped her in the first role. He believed in his very soul that lovely, placid, passive feminine mediocrity was the only pillow on which manly thought and sense could find rest for its aching temples; and as for work, male mind alone could produce any good practical result—hein?

That “hein?” was a sharp little interrogative intended to draw contradiction or objection out of me. But I only said, “Cela ne me regarde pas: je ne m’en soucie pas,” and presently added, “May I go, Monsieur? They have rung the bell for the second déjeuner, luncheon.”

“What of that? You are not hungry?”

“Indeed I am,” I said. “I have had nothing since breakfast at seven, and I will get nothing till dinner at five if I miss this bell.”

“Well, I am in the same plight,” he said, “but you may share with me.” He broke the brioche he had set aside for his own snack right in two, and gave me half. Truly his bark was far worse than his bite, but the really formidable attack was yet to come.

While eating my cake, I could not forbear expressing my secret wish that I really knew all the learning he accused me of hoarding. “Did I sincerely feel myself to be a total ignoramus?” he asked, in a softened tone. If I had replied meekly with an unqualified yes, I believe he would have stretched out his hand then and there, and we would have been friends on the spot—but instead I answered, “Not exactly. I am ignorant, Monsieur, of the knowledge you ascribe to me, but I sometimes, not always, feel a knowledge of my own.”

“What did I mean?” he asked, sharply.

Unable to answer that question in a breath, I evaded it by changing the subject. He had now finished his half of the brioche: certain that such a tiny fragment could not have satisfied his appetite, any more than mine had been sated, and breathing in the warm, sweet fragrance of baked apples drifting from the refectory far away, I dared ask if he did not smell that agreeable odour too. He confessed he did. I said if he would let me out by the garden door and let me run across the courtyard, I would fetch him a plateful; I added that I knew they were excellent, because Goton baked (or rather, stewed) the fruit perfectly, with a little spice, sugar, and a glass or two of white wine—might I go?

“Petite gourmande!” he said, smiling. “I have not forgotten how

Spring crept fast over Villette, and the weather burst into sudden, unseasonable warmth. For me, as for so many frail, overworked souls, that soft heat leached my strength thin: the smallest task left me drained, sleepless restless nights bled into heavy languid days where even lifting a pile of exercise books felt like a labour. One Sunday afternoon, I tramped half a league to the distant Protestant church, and by the time I stumbled back through the school gates, my legs were shaking. I fled straight to my quiet sanctuary, the first classe, and sank into my chair, resting my arms and head on the hard wooden desk, too exhausted to even light a candle.

For a while I listened to the bees droning low in the arbour outside, and watched through the glass door, past the tender new spring leaves, as Madame Beck paraded her dinner guests through the central orchard alley. The boughs were heavy with blossom that day, white and flushed the exact colour of mountain snow streaked with sunrise pink. My eyes kept drifting back to one figure in that party: a bright, handsome young girl I’d seen visiting before, Mademoiselle Sauveur, they said she was M. Emanuel’s goddaughter, that her mother had been a dear old friend of the professor’s. I’d seen her with him once before, laughing, looping her arm through his, leaning her weight on him like a spoiled adored child, and a strange sharp unwelcome little twinge had lanced through me then, some odd premonition I’d refused to name, refused to unpack. M. Paul was nowhere in that holiday party today, and I followed the flash of her rich silk gown winding through the flowers until my eyes grew heavy, the hum of bees and warm air lulling me fast asleep.

Two hours slipped by before I woke. The sun had sunk behind the tall city houses, the garden and the classroom draped in soft grey dusk, the bees all gone back to their hives, the flowers folded shut for the night, the last of Madame Beck’s guests vanished, every alley empty. But I was not cold, not stiff, not numb from pressing my cheek to the hard desk for two whole hours. Where the bare wood had been, there was a thick folded wool shawl tucked under my arms and face, and another soft shawl wrapped tight around my shoulders. Someone had crept in quiet as a shadow while I slept, and tucked me in.

I puzzled over it. Not Ginevra, that reckless bright thing would have shaken me awake laughing for a joke. Not Zélie St. Pierre, who had never hidden her dislike for me. Not the other teachers, none of them had that soft unthinking tenderness. It must be Madame Beck, I told myself pragmatically – she values her useful English teacher, would not have me fall ill and disrupt the classes. But the thought did not sit quite right.

I stepped out into the fresh cool evening air then, and wandered down my favourite alley. I had not forgotten the strange spectral illusion I’d seen there months before, the night I’d buried my little glass jar under the old patriarch tree, but a last slant of sunset still gilded the stone statue of Jean Baptiste at the far end, and the birds were still chattering in the ivy on the walls. I walked slow, turning over that old half-plan I’d nurtured for months: when I saved a thousand francs, I would rent a small flat, set up a little school of my own, start with day pupils, work my way up, just as Madame Beck had done. Independence, that steady safe prize. But then another thought crept in, soft and unruly: what then? Was that all? No true home, no one dearer to me than my own careful plans, no one at whose feet I could lay down all that hard-won self-reliance, and labour just to make them happy? I laughed at myself, quiet, in the dusk. Lucy Snowe, you ask too much. Most of us walk through life on the crescent, never the full moon. You will make do. I said my quiet goodnight to Dr. John then, out loud, the kind gentle man whose warm regard I’d mistaken for something meant only for me. “Goodnight, Dr. John. You are good, you are beautiful. But you are not mine. God bless you.”

A voice answered me, not three feet away, low and rough and familiar. “Good-night, Mademoiselle. Or rather, good evening – the sun is scarcely set. I hope you slept well?”

I started, my heart jumping, but I knew that voice instantly. M. Paul Emanuel stood half in the shadow of the arbour, his dark eyes glinting, his expression half teasing, half soft. He was the one who had left the shawls, he admitted when I told him I’d woken warm and coddled, and he brushed off my thanks like they were trivial. He told me he watched all of them from a little rented room in the boarding house across the way, with a telescope, his own little observation post for studying human nature. He laughed as he told me he’d seen through Zélie St. Pierre’s honeyed flattery months before, that he’d once almost married her, if he hadn’t spied her cruel petty rages through that window, seen her true face. He confessed gruff and half-embarrassed that for all his boldness in front of crowds and crowned heads, he could be shy, that he blushed like a schoolgirl at the thought of asking a woman to marry him. Then he told me he’d watched me, long before we’d ever exchanged more than a formal greeting – that he’d seen me wander this alley every evening, that he’d snuck up to me once when we were strangers, and pressed a little knot of white violets into my palm. I told him I still had those violets, pressed flat in the back of my prayer book, and his face lit up like I’d given him a gift.

The air turned cold then, clouds blotting out the last of the sunset, fat raindrops starting to patter through the leaves. He asked me if I believed in the supernatural, and I admitted I’d seen strange things in this very garden, months before. He said he’d seen them too – the ghost nun, the legend of the woman bricked up alive under the roots of the old patriarch tree hundreds of years before. He said he’d seen that nun’s black and white habit gliding through the halls at night, more than once.

And then, without warning, the great old tree behind us convulsed. There was no breeze, not a breath of wind to stir the feathery lilac bushes, but its great heavy boughs heaved and creaked, like something was struggling to be born out of its shadow, something dark and solid writhing free from the trunk. The prayer bell rang out sharp inside the house.

And out of the writhing foliage burst the nun herself, all stark black robes and stark white wimple, tall, fierce, rushing past us so close her habit brushed my sleeve, the wind screaming after her, the rain lashing wild and cold, the whole dark night seeming to shiver in her wake.

It is high time, now, to ask: where was Paulina Mary, and what had become of my quiet, intermittent visits to the grand, gilded Hôtel Crécy? Our little pattern of meetings had been broken for weeks by absence: M. de Bassompierre and his daughter had been travelling, splitting their time between sunlit French provincial towns and the glitter of the Parisian season, and I had not known the exact day of their return until chance brought me face to face with them.

I was wandering a quiet, tree-lined boulevard one mild April afternoon, slow under the soft honeyed sun, the air sharp with the faint green scent of new linden leaves and the first bloom of lilacs tucked in garden borders, when I spotted a small group of riders halted mid-path, as if they had just stumbled on one another, laughing as they exchanged greetings. On one side was a middle-aged gentleman and a young lady, on the other, a tall, striking young man who sat his horse as lightly as a bird. The lady held herself with a grace so rare, her riding habit stitched with such quiet, costly care, that even from a distance I knew I did not gaze on a stranger. Drawing a little closer, I recognised them all at once: the Count Home de Bassompierre, his daughter Paulina, and Dr. Graham Bretton.

Graham’s face was lit so bright it might have caught and held the very April sun. The joy on it was warm, true, and yet edged with a soft shyness, the kind of reverence only a man who has finally found the exact treasure he has spent years unknowingly searching for can feel. This was the exact state of things to bind Dr. John heart and soul: the pearl he worshipped was pure, rare, priceless in itself, but he was never a man who could admire a fine gem blind to the beauty of its setting. Had he met Paulina as a pretty, unguarded working girl, on foot in a simple cotton gown, he would have thought her a lovely, charming creature, admired the lightness of her step and the softness of her smile, but he would never have fallen as utterly, as completely vanquished as he was now. Graham was a man of the world through and through: his own private pleasure was never enough. The world he moved in must approve, society must admire the choice he made, or he would count his own actions foolish and unworthy. In the woman who would rule his heart, he required all that stood before him now: the mark of gentle, careful upbringing, the quiet protection of a father who would never let a harsh thing touch her, the little graces that fashion decrees, that wealth buys and good taste refines. Every single one of his unspoken conditions was met here, perfectly, and now he stood proud, passionate, and half afraid, doing open, soft homage to Paulina as his queen. As for her, the smile on her lips was not the smile of someone who knew she held power, but the soft, secret smile of a girl who felt joy bloom deep in her bones.

They parted not long after. Graham spurred his horse and passed me at a gallop, skimming over the paving stones so lightly he hardly seemed to touch the earth, his eyes fixed on nothing ahead but the fading shape of Paulina on her horse, so wrapped up in his own happiness he did not even see me standing at the path’s edge. He looked magnificent: all his old quiet purpose was roused, his whole frame thrumming with bright, eager life.

“Papa, there is Lucy!” called a clear, musical voice I would have known anywhere. “Lucy, dear Lucy, do come over here!”

I hurried to her side. She pushed back her riding veil, leaned down from her saddle to press a warm, soft kiss to my cheek. “I was coming to call on you tomorrow,” she said, laughing, “but now you shall come to me instead. Come at seven o’clock, won’t you? I will have no other guests.” I promised I would be there.

The next evening found me shut away with her in her private bedchamber, the fire burning low and ruddy in the hearth, the curtains drawn against the soft spring dusk. I had not seen her since that evening at the pensionnat, when her quiet, gentle charm had outshone the bright, shallow Ginevra Fanshawe so completely, and she had no end of stories to tell me of her travels. She spoke fast and vividly, painting little pictures of village festivals and Parisian galleries, and yet her voice was so soft, her diction so artless, that she never seemed to speak too quickly or say too much. Even my attention, long used to being easily wearied by idle chatter, did not flag. But after a little while, she seemed eager to wrap up her tales quickly. Silence fell then: a restless, dreamy silence, full of little signs that her thoughts were far away.

“Lucy,” she said, turning to me, her voice half timid, half pleading.

“I am right here,” I told her.

“Is my cousin Ginevra still staying at Madame Beck’s?”

“Still there. I thought you would be longing to see her.”

“No, not much,” she said, twisting the edge of her silk gown between her fingers. “I suppose she still talks about being married?”

“Not to anyone you care for,” I assured her.

“But she still thinks of Dr. Bretton, of course? She couldn’t have changed her mind, not when she was so set on it two months ago.”

“It does not matter, truly,” I said. “You saw for yourself what little there ever was between them. She is not unhappy, rest assured.”

She paused, then, a little flush rising in her cheeks. “During our absence, did you ever hear or see anything of Graham?”

“Papa got letters from him now and then, about business,” she said. “He offered to manage some legal matter for us that needed tending while we were out of the country. Dr. Bretton seems to respect papa very much, and goes out of his way to do him favours.”

“I saw you two meet on the boulevard yesterday,” I said. “You could tell from his face that his friends never need worry over his health, could you not?”

“Papa said the same thing, when he thought no one was listening,” she smiled. “Papa is never very observant, he is always lost in his own thoughts, but as Graham rode away he muttered ‘It does a man good to see that boy’s spirit and energy.’ He still calls him a boy, you see, just as he still thinks I am his little Polly, not a grown woman at all.” She left her armchair then, and came to sink onto the low little stool at my feet. I stroked her soft hair back from her forehead. It is not often I say I truly like someone I meet, but I liked her deeply. Long acquaintance and close insight had never revealed anything in Paulina but delicacy, intelligence, and perfect sincerity. My regard for her was quiet, but rooted deep.

“What is it you want to ask me, little one?” I said. “Be brave, speak out.”

But her eyes dropped, and the flush on her cheek burned deeper, a warm, inward excitement that had nothing to do with a passing surface blush. “Lucy, I want to know what you truly think of Dr. Bretton. Tell me your real, honest opinion of his character, his disposition. You know him well.”

“His character is sterling, and everyone who knows him respects it, and they are right to.”

“And his disposition? Tell me what he is like as a son, you have seen him with his mother.”

“He is the kindest, most devoted son in the world. He is his mother’s whole comfort, her pride, her greatest joy.”

She held my hand between both of hers, and stroked my fingers gently, one soft caress for every favourable word I spoke. “What else is he good at? Tell me more.”

“Dr. Bretton is gentle, benevolent to every living soul. He would show kindness even to the most savage stranger, or the worst criminal in the world.”

“I heard some of papa’s friends saying that exact same thing not long ago,” she said, her eyes shining. “They say the poorest patients at the hospital, who tremble before all the other cold, selfish surgeons, run to meet him when he walks the wards.”

“They are right,” I said. “He once took me on a tour of that very hospital, and I saw how they welcomed him.”

She lifted her eyes for a second, bright with soft, quiet gratitude. She had more to say, but she hesitated, as if waiting for the room to grow darker, the hour later. The dusk was pooling soft in the corners now, the firelight gilding only the edges of the furniture. “It is so quiet and secluded here,” I said, to reassure her.

“Yes. No one will call us down for tea. Papa is dining out this evening.” She kept playing with my fingers unconsciously, slipping her rings one by one onto my digits, then winding a strand of her own beautiful golden hair around my wrist, before she pressed my palm to her warm cheek. When she spoke at last, her voice was as clear and soft as a lark’s song, only a little unsteady. “You must think it very strange that I talk so much about Dr. Bretton, ask all these questions, care this much. But—”

“Not strange at all,” I said. “Perfectly natural. You like him.”

“And if I did?” she said, with a little flash of quick petulance, just like the little girl Polly I had known at Bretton all those years ago. “Is that any reason I should gush about it? You must think I am silly and weak, just like my cousin Ginevra.”

“If I thought you were one bit like Ginevra, I would not be sitting here waiting to hear what you have to say. I would stand up, walk straight across the room, and lecture you until you were tired.”

“I am going to tell you anyway,” she retorted, the old sensitive, stubborn spark back. “If I loved Dr. John so much I felt fit to die of it, that would never give me leave to be noisy about it, to whine and weep about a crush that was only one sided, would it? You would despise me, Lucy Snowe, if I lost all self-control that way.”

“It is true I have no patience for girls who brag about their little romantic triumphs, or wail about their silly little heartbreaks. But you, Paulina? Speak as much as you like. I want to hear every word. Whatever you tell me will be a relief to you, and a pleasure to me.”

“Do you truly care for me, Lucy?”

“Truly I do, child.”

“And I love you. Even when I was a little naughty, stubborn girl at Bretton, I always felt most at peace when I was with you. Now I trust you more than anyone. Listen, then.” She leaned her weight gently against my arm, no greedy, heavy weight like Ginevra’s, light as a bird. “A few minutes ago you asked if we had heard from Graham during our travels, and I said there were two business letters for papa. That was true, but I did not tell you all of it. I evaded.” She smiled, a little guilty. “Now I will tell you the whole truth. It is dark enough now, no one will see my face. Papa always lets me sort through the post bag and hand him his letters. One morning three weeks ago, what do you think I found? Amidst a dozen letters all addressed to M. de Bassompierre, there was a note addressed to Miss de Bassompierre. I spotted the handwriting instantly. It was Graham’s. I was just about to say ‘Papa, here is another letter from Dr. Bretton’ when the ‘Miss’ on the envelope stopped my tongue. I had never received a letter from a gentleman in my whole life. Should I have handed it straight to papa, let him open it and read it first? I could not, Lucy. I know how papa sees me: he forgets I am grown. He still thinks I am a little schoolgirl. So I handed him all twelve of his letters, his whole little pile of post, and kept back my one, my very own letter. It lay in my lap all through breakfast, like a secret alive, looking up at me. I felt like I was two people at once: still a little girl to my dear papa, but not a child to myself any more. After breakfast I carried it straight up to my room, turned the key in the lock behind me, and sat there staring at the outside of my treasure for ten whole minutes. I could not bring myself to break the seal right away. It was like storming a fortress, you do not rush right in. Graham’s handwriting is just like him, you know, clear, firm, rounded, no messy slants, no careless ink blots. His seal was just as perfect: a full solid drop of wax, a clean, sharp impress, no harsh point pricking at your eye. It is exactly like his face, the same clean, chiselled lines. I cut the paper around the seal with my embroidery scissors, so I did not break the wax at all. Then I pulled back again, one more time. I did not want to drink that sweet draught all at once, the sparkle in the cup was too beautiful, I wanted to look at it a little longer. Then I realised I had not said my prayers that morning. I had woken late, seen papa go down to breakfast earlier than usual, and run down straight after dressing, thinking I could put prayers off later. But a little voice in my head said I ought to pray first, before I dared read what I ached to read. I knelt down, and I prayed that no matter what happened, I would never hurt papa, never make him sad, never put anyone above him. The very thought of making him unhappy made me cry. But I knew, too, that one day he would have to be told, that he would have to understand his little girl was grown.

“I read the letter then. Lucy, people say life is full of nothing but disappointments. But I was not disappointed. My heart did not just throb, it trembled fast, every beat like a thirsty creature that has just found a well, drinking deep. The well was full, the water clear as glass, no mote, no moss, no tiny insect floating in it, the sun shining right through the golden ripples. Life is supposed to be full of pain, people say. I have read biographies where people wander from one sorrow to the next, where hope flies just out of their reach, where they sow in tears and their harvest gets blighted before it can ripen, and they end the winter with empty shelves, starving in the cold dark. I am not a particularly good person. But God let me grow up safe, warm, sheltered by my dear father, and now… now another person has come. Graham loves me.”

We sat quiet for a long minute, the fire popping soft in the hearth. “Does your father know?” I asked, at last, in a low voice.

“Graham wrote of papa with such deep respect, but said he dared not speak to him yet, not until he had proved he was worthy, not until he knew what my feelings were. I replied to him, I did not turn him away. But I wrote the letter three times over, Lucy! Every time I rewrote it, I made it a little calmer, a little more restrained, until it was like a block of cold ice with just the faintest hint of sugar and fruit flavour in it, so he would not think me too eager. Then I sent it.”

“That was perfect,” I said. “Your instinct is so fine. You understand Graham perfectly.”

“But what will I do about papa? I am so afraid to tell him.”

“Do nothing at all, for now. Wait. Only promise me you will not write another letter to him, not until your father knows everything and gives you his full permission.”

“Do you think he will ever agree?”

“Wait, and time will tell. You already did the right thing, didn’t you?”

“I wrote him one letter back, telling him that while my feelings stayed the same, I could not write to him any more without papa’s knowledge. He wrote once more after that, so grateful for my calm little note.”

“Dr. Bretton will respect that more than any gushing, eager note you could have sent. It will make him prouder of you, fonder of you, if it is even possible for him to be fonder.”

“Lucy, I know how delicate he is,” she said. “I know no amount of care is too much for how I treat him.”

“And you will be just as truthful, just as open, just as gentle with your father, when the time comes.”

“Oh it will hurt so much to wake him up from his dream, to tell him I am not his little girl any more!” she whispered.

“Do not rush it,” I told her, smoothing her hair. “Leave that to time, to fate. She has been so gentle to you all your life, she will arrange the perfect hour. I knew from the very first day I saw you two together at La Terrasse that you two were meant for one another. Everything about you and Graham fits perfectly. Your youth will not end in storm and strife. You will get to live the quiet happy life so few mortals ever get, the kind of life that feels like a leftover trace of Eden. Not everyone gets that, I know. Some people walk through nothing but storm and gale, get lost in the early winter dark. But that path is not yours. You are safe, and you are loved.”

Outside, the last of the April sun had faded entirely, and the soft spring dark wrapped around the room like a blanket. Somewhere far off, a church bell rang soft, for evening prayers, and we sat on in that golden hush, holding hands, no more words needed.

On the first of May, all twenty boarders and four teachers of Rue Fossette woke to a notice taped to every dormitory door: rise at five, dress and be ready by six, to place ourselves under the full command of M. le Professeur Emanuel. The day he had promised us weeks before, the country breakfast excursion, had finally arrived.

I, for my part, had never thought I’d be invited. When he first floated the plan, I’d been pointedly excluded, and when I teased him gently about that, wondering how it was I’d ended up on the guest list after all, he gave my ear a playful, sharp little tug, and threatened the other with the same if I dared raise a single further complaint. “I’d advise you not to make people beg for the pleasure of your company,” he said, imperious as a little Napoleon, and one of his characteristic half-gruff, half-gentle gestures was enough to make me resolve not to risk being left behind.

The morning broke as soft and warm as high summer, blackbirds singing loud in the garden, a thin dew mist curling low over the flowerbeds that promised a glowing, hot afternoon. We all folded away our thick winter wool gowns with giddy relief, pulling on the crisp, new print dresses and neat straw bonnets the Parisian seamstress had stitched for us that spring—modest, unshowy, and perfectly flattering, no faded cast-off silks or shabby hand-me-downs in sight.

At six, the bell pealed merrily, and we all poured down the staircase, through the central common room, down the corridor and out into the vestibule. There stood M. Paul, not wrapped in his usual severe dark overcoat and flat Greek cap, but wearing a light belted linen blouse and a jaunty straw hat, looking ten years younger than he did in the classroom. He greeted every last one of us with a warm good-morning, and most of us smiled back, bright and eager, as he marshalled us into line to march out of Villette.

The streets were still quiet, the boulevards smelling of fresh cut grass, as peaceful as open fields. None of us could stop smiling. Our professor had a rare gift for stoking joy in the people around him, the very same gift that could send a shiver of fearful attention down the row of students in his lecture hall when he was cross. He didn’t lead the procession, or follow it—he walked alongside the line, stopping to say a word to every single girl, teasing his favourites, even sparing a kind glance for the students he found dull or tiresome.

I had no wish to draw his eye, though. I was paired with Ginevra Fanshawe, whose arm was heavy and warm on mine, chattering nonstop about how the walk would ruin her new boots. I used her as a deliberate human shield, shifting my position every time I heard M. Paul’s step draw near to my right or my left. The reason for my silly little manoeuvre? My new print dress was pale pink, and I felt exactly as I had once when I’d had to cross a meadow full of grazing bulls wearing a shawl with a bright red border. For a little while, hiding behind Ginevra and fanning out the wide fringe of my black silk scarf worked, but M. Paul soon noticed that no matter which side of the line he walked, Ginevra was right there, prattling away in her sharp, affected English accent. The two of them had never gotten along: he thought her shallow and vain, she thought him rude, meddlesome and unbearably gruff.

On the sixth time he switched sides, he leaned forward, fixed his dark eyes on mine, and demanded, half laughing half annoyed: “What is this? Are you playing tricks on me?”

He caught on before I could even stammer a reply. I shook my scarf out wider, but he laughed, and said, “Ah! It’s the pink dress!” The sound was just as startling as a bull’s low, but it held no anger.

“It’s only cotton,” I hurried to explain, flustered. “It’s cheap, and washes far better than any darker colour.”

“And Mademoiselle Lucy is more vain than ten Parisiennes,” he teased. “Who ever saw such a thing from an English girl? Look at her bonnet, her gloves, her little boots!” Those things were no fancier than any of my classmates’—if anything, mine were plainer—but he’d found his excuse to tease me, and I’d braced for a lecture. It never came. All I got was one quick, bright, bantering smile, like a flash of summer lightning, and he softened: “Courage. I’m not cross at all. In fact, I’m very pleased you made yourself look so nice for my little party.”

“But my dress isn’t nice at all, monsieur,” I protested. “It’s just clean.”

“I love cleanliness,” he said, and his good mood held, bright and warm as the rising sun, chasing away every last wisp of the tiny, silly cloud of tension.

Soon we were out in the country, winding down the soft green lanes locals called les bois et les petits sentiers, so fresh and lush in their May greenness, before the summer dust would settle over them and turn them dry and dull. We stopped at an old stone well ringed with neat lime trees, and M. Paul ordered us all to sit on the soft grass around it, settling himself right in our midst. The little girls who loved him more than they feared crowded close; the ones who feared him more than they loved hung back a little; the few of us who felt both affection and a soft, giddy thrill of fear of him kept the farthest distance of all.

Then he told us a story. He narrated it masterfully, simple in its strength, vivid and full of tender little touches, the kind of twilight scene he painted so clearly in words that I can still see it in my mind’s eye, more real than any painting I have ever seen. He was not a man to sit still and write books, but he scattered such sharp, bright, unconsciously generous gems of thought that day that I thought, if someone who loved him got to hoard every one of those gold-dust fragments, they would have a treasure far greater than any printed volume. His mind was my favourite library, and every time he opened it to me, I felt like I was stepping into bliss.

When the story ended, he walked over to the little knoll where Ginevra and I sat apart. He never had the patience to wait for someone to volunteer an opinion, so he asked directly: “Were you interested?”

I answered, quiet as I always was: “Yes.”

“Was it good?”

“Very good.”

“I could never write it down,” he said. “I hate the drudgery of sitting still to write. But I could dictate it, happily, to an amanuensis who suited me. Would Mademoiselle Lucy write for me, if I asked her?”

“Monsieur would speak too fast,” I told him. “You’d get impatient and angry if my pen couldn’t keep up with your lips.”

“Try it some day,” he laughed. “We’ll see how much of a monster I can be. But no talk of dictation now. I need you for another job. Do you see that farm-house over there, half hidden in trees?”

“Yes.”

“That’s where we’ll have breakfast. While the farmer’s wife makes café au lait on the hearth, you and five others I pick will spread butter over fifty fresh rolls.”

He marched us all down to the farm, which surrendered at once to our little cheerful army. Half a dozen of us worked under his direction to butter the huge basket of rolls the baker had dropped off that morning, while coffee and chocolate steamed on the fire, and bowls of cream and plates of new-laid eggs waited on the table. M. Paul, ever generous, was about to order whole legs of ham and jars of jam extra, when a group of us pretended to scold him for wasting food, calling him a spendthrift. He complained that we were a pack of miserly housewives, but we got our way, trimming the feast just enough to avoid waste. He stood by the kitchen hearth, grinning, happier than I’d ever seen him, just to watch all of us happy and laughing around him. We dragged the big heavy oak farmer’s chair to the head of the long table and sat him down there. For all his quick, fiery temper, all his storms of irritation, he was as soft and gentle as a lamb when someone met him halfway, understood him, and was kind to him. Even the youngest children felt perfectly safe with him.

Before we ate, he made the smallest little girl say a short grace, crossing himself as devoutly and simply as a child, a quiet act of piety I had never seen from him before. His eyes caught my soft, pleased smile, and he reached out his hand to me. “Give me your hand. I see we worship the same God, in the same spirit, even if we follow different rites.” Most of the other professors at the school were free-thinkers, cynics, men whose lives would not stand close scrutiny, but M. Paul was like an old knight of legend: passionate, impulsive, but honourable to his bones, with a pure, uncomplicated piety that tamed every wild, sharp part of him.

Breakfast was merry, no empty silly chatter, but the warm, easy kind of joy that only comes when everyone around the table is relaxed and at peace. When the meal ended, the girls ran off to play in the pastures. A few of us stayed behind to help the farmer’s wife wash up the earthenware, until M. Paul called me out to sit with him under a broad spreading tree, where he could watch the rest of the troop gambol across the fields. He sat on a rough wooden bench, and I sat on the root of the tree beside him, reading him from a tattered pocket copy of Corneille while he smoked his cigar. He listened so quiet, so soft, the look of calm on his face so rare for his usually impetuous, energetic nature, that my heart felt light and full, brimming with the perfect happiness of the warm May day, his company, his unexpected kindness.

After a while, he asked if I would not rather run off and play with my friends. I told him no, I was perfectly content right where I was, at his side. He asked me if, if I were his sister, I would always be happy to stay near a brother like him. I said I thought I would, and I meant it. Then his tone softened, turned a little mournful. He asked if I would be sorry if he left Villette, to go far away across the sea. I dropped the Corneille book, and could not speak.

“Little sister,” he said, “how long would you remember me, if we were separated?”

“Monsieur,” I whispered, “I cannot tell you that, because I do not know how long it will be before I stop remembering anything at all on this earth.”

“If I went overseas for two, three, five years, would you welcome me home when I came back?”

“Monsieur,” I said, my throat tight, “how would I live through the time in between?”

“I have been very hard on you, you know,” he said. “Very demanding.”

I hid my face behind the Corneille volume, tears spilling over the pages. I asked him why he was talking so sadly, and he promised he would not say another word, cheering me up with soft little jokes and gentle words for the rest of the afternoon. But his unusual tenderness made my heart ache. It felt too soft, too mournful. I almost wished he would go back to being his usual sharp, teasing, unpredictable self.

By noon the day had turned as hot and bright as mid-June, and he herded his “flock” of girls back towards home. The farm was a full league away from Rue Fossette, and the little girls were tired from running and playing, dragging their feet at the thought of walking over the hot, dusty, flinty roads in the midday sun. But he had thought ahead. Just past the farm gate, two large hired school carriages were waiting for us, and we all piled in, reaching the Rue Fossette safe and sound in under an hour. It had been a nearly perfect day—only that faint, wispy note of melancholy had dimmed its sunshine for a moment.

That same evening, just after sunset, that faint sadness bloomed again. I looked out the window of the first classe to see M. Paul walking slowly down the central garden alley, arm in arm with Madame Beck. They talked earnestly for almost an hour: he grave, restless, frowning, she looking surprised, expostulating, trying to talk him out of something. When Madame Beck finally went back inside as the dark fell, leaving M. Paul leaning against a tree in the half light, arms crossed, brow bowed, my heart ached. He had called me little sister that morning. If he really were my brother, I would run out to him right now, and ask what was weighing on his mind. Madame Beck would never comfort him. She would only remonstrate. He needed someone to listen.

Then he started walking, fast and straight, towards the house. I thought he was going to water the potted orange trees, the little chore he sometimes did of an evening—but he turned abruptly, heading straight for the berceau trellis and the glass door of the first classe, where I was standing, watching him. My cowardly, panicky heart took over. I did not wait for him. I ran, as if a bullet was flying at my head, slipping down the corridor to hide in the empty oratory. I leaned against the door, breath coming fast, and I heard him storm through every classroom, clashing the doors open impatiently, bursting into the refectory where the evening pious lecture was in session. I heard him call out, loud and clear: “Where is Mademoiselle Lucie?”

Just as I had summoned my courage to walk out, to go meet him, the sharp, false voice of Mademoiselle St. Pierre answered glibly: “She’s in bed.”

He stamped his foot in frustration, and turned away. Madame Beck met him in the corridor, scolded him, and marched him straight to the front street door, shutting it firmly behind him.

The second that door clicked shut, I was hit by a blow of utter, stunned misery. I knew he had come to find me. I knew he had something to tell me, something soft and important he had been leading up to all day. I wanted to hear it more than I wanted anything else in the world—and I had run away from it like a coward. I could have had that confidence, that solace, two minutes of standing my ground, and I threw it all away. There was nothing now but dark, empty suspense, no answers, no chance to know what he had been about to say.

I carried that bitter, foolish weight to my bed, and lay awake all night, counting every second of the silly, self-inflicted loss.

Madame Beck called for me on a drowsy Thursday afternoon, the pensionnat’s workroom humming with the soft snick of embroidery scissors and the girls’ quiet chatter, to ask if I was free to run small shopping errands for her across town.

I had no tasks to hold me, so I placed myself at her disposal at once. She pressed a list into my hand, scrawled in her neat, firm script: wools matched to the girls’ half-finished slippers, silks for new bell-pulls, skeins of glinting embroidery thread for the fancy purses they were stitching for fête day. I kitted myself out for the heavy, brooding clouds hanging low over the city, and had just flipped the spring latch on the front door to step out when Madame’s voice called me back to the dining room.

“Pardon, Miss Lucy!” she cried, feigning the bright surprise of a thought that had just popped unbidden into her head. “I have just remembered one more small errand for you, I hope you won’t think it too much of a favour to grant?”

Of course I assured her I was happy to help, and she darted into the little parlour to fetch a wicker basket lined with glossy dark green hothouse leaves, piled high with blushing, perfect summer fruit that looked far too fine to be carried through the muddy streets.

“There,” she said, pressing it into my hands. “It’s light, and won’t look like a servant’s chore that might rumple your neat dress. Do me the kindness of leaving this at the house of Madame Walravens, with my warm felicitations on her fête day. She lives in the old Basse-Ville, Number 3 Rue des Mages. The walk is a fair distance, but you have the whole afternoon ahead of you—no rush. If you miss dinner, I’ll have a plate saved, or Goton, who’s always so fond of you, will whip up a little something special just for you. You won’t be forgotten, my good girl. Oh, and one more thing!” she called again as I turned to go. “Make sure you insist on seeing Madame Walravens in person, hand the basket straight to her. She’s very particular about such things, I wouldn’t want any mishap. Goodbye, see you soon!”

I finally set off. The shopping errands ate up a good hour, picking through spools of silk and hanks of wool to match the exact hues Madame Beck wanted, selecting tassels and slides for the girls’ purse projects, until the whole tedious list was done, nothing left but the fruit basket and my fête day greeting.

I found myself looking forward to the long walk deep into the old, shadowed lower town. The evening sky over Villette was curdling into a thick, black-blue mass of storm cloud, its edges glowing with smouldering, heavy red. I never minded a storm’s rain, not the way I minded the sharp, effortful bluster of high wind: rain only asks for quiet resignation, to let your coat and your hair grow damp, and it sweeps the streets clean of all the noisy, crowded bustle, turns the grand boulevards empty and still as an enchanted dead city. Let the rain fall, I thought, so long as I deliver this basket first.

A distant tower clock tolled the quarter past five when I reached the address Madame Beck had given me. It was no proper street at all, but a quiet, forgotten square, grass pushing up between the wide grey flagstones, the tall old houses leaning back behind high walls where you could see the dark tops of old garden trees peeking over the roofline. Antiquity hung thick over the whole place, no shops, no carriages, no sign of the modern bustling Villette I knew. Rich families had lived here once, grandeur had made its home here, but wealth had long since flown, leaving these old hollow nests to fade. The dark, half-ruined turrets of the old Church of the Magi loomed over the square.

Large, fat raindrops, each the size of a five-franc piece, were beginning to spot the pavement when I crossed the empty square, and the only living soul I saw was a frail old priest, hobbling past with a staff, bent double with age. He had just stepped out of the very house I was looking for. I paused at the door he’d closed behind him and rang the bell, and he turned to stare at me, his gaze lingering—no doubt a young woman with a basket of fancy fruit looked very out of place in this quiet, decaying corner.

A very ancient woman in a tattered antique peasant dress answered the door, her cap draped with long ragged lace, her wooden sabots shaped like tiny little boats. Her face was sour, cantankerous, the grumpiest expression I had ever seen on a human being, and she nearly snatched the fruit basket straight out of my hand before the old priest stepped forward to stop her. He seemed half-deaf at first, so it took some effort to make him understand I had strict orders to hand the basket directly to Madame Walravens herself, no proxy. At last he convinced the grumpy old servant to let me cross the threshold, and escorted me up the creaking stairs to a dim, drafty old salon, where he left me to wait.

The room was huge, with a beautiful carved old ceiling and stained glass windows that looked more fit for a church than a private home, and in the gathering storm gloom it felt almost menacing. A second smaller room opened off it, its single casement blind pulled all the way down, so I could barely make out the shapes of the furniture. I amused myself squinting to pick out details, and my eye caught the outline of a large portrait on the far wall.

Then, to my utter shock, the portrait moved. It shook, sank, rolled back, vanishing completely to reveal an arched opening leading to a cold stone passage, a winding dungeon stair running down into the dark. I heard a tap, tap of a stick on the stone steps, a shadow crept up, and then the figure itself appeared.

She was barely three feet tall, no proper shape to her hunched little body, her skinny hands folded over the gilded knob of an ivory walking stick. Her great broad face sat almost on top of her chest, no neck to speak of, and her features looked a hundred years old, her eyes deep, malign, heavy under thick grey brows and livid swollen lids. She was dressed in a full bright blue brocade gown patterned with satin leaves, the colour as rich as gentian flowers, a massive costly shawl draped over her small frame, its fringed hem trailing on the floor. She dripped with jewels: great long drop earrings blazing with real, unmistakeable fire, rings on every skeletal finger set with thick gold hoops and stones of purple, emerald green, and deep blood red.

“Que me voulez-vous?” she croaked, her voice rough and deep as an old man’s, a thin silver beard bristling on her chin.

I held out the basket, recited Madame Beck’s greeting.

“Is that all?” she snapped.

“That is all,” I said.

“Truly, it was hardly worth the walk,” she sniffed. “Go back to Madame Beck and tell her I can buy my own fruit when I want it, and her little felicitations? I don’t care a fig for them.” She turned her back on me, sharp and rude.

The very second she turned, a great peal of thunder cracked overhead, a flash of lightning blazed so bright it lit the whole salon like midday. The old tales of enchantment felt suddenly very real: I was the wanderer lured into the evil fairy’s castle, the storm outside waking at the spell’s call. What strange sort of friend did Madame Beck keep, I wondered, offering gifts and greetings to this uncanny, ill-tempered little creature everyone called Malevola, the evil fairy?

The rain was pouring down in torrents now, the sky so dark the clouds looked pale with terror, streaked by forked lightning so bright it seared your eyes, thunder crashing so close the walls shook. For all my earlier bravado about not minding rain, I had no desire to step out into that downpour. I sat down on the cold stone seat on the landing to wait, and the old priest glided down the gallery above me.

“Mademoiselle, you cannot sit there,” he said gently. “Our benefactor would be most displeased to know a guest in his house was treated so poorly.” He insisted so earnestly that I come back inside out of the draft that I could not refuse to be rude. He led me into the smaller, more private room, pulled the blind up half way, and I saw it was less a parlour than a little oratory, solemn, full of relics and keepsakes.

The good father sat down beside me, pulled out a prayer book, and began whispering his litanies, his bald head gilded every few seconds by the yellow lightning flashes, his body deep in purple shadow, still as a statue. I realized then who he was: Père Silas, the same priest I had knelt to confess to in the Béguinage, the voice, the faint profile I remembered from that dim church service, all matching.

As I stared at him, I noticed the portrait that had earlier vanished to make the secret door: it was no Madonna, as I’d first thought, but a portrait of a young woman in a nun’s habit. Her face was soft, pale, sad, the shadow of deep grief hanging over her, and I found I could not look away.

Without lifting his eyes from his book, the old priest spoke in a slow, clear voice:
“She was much beloved.
“She gave herself to God.
“She died young.
“She is still remembered, still wept.”

“By Madame Walravens?” I asked, thinking that endless decades of grief might explain the old woman’s terrible temper.

He shook his head, a faint sad smile on his face. “No, no. A grandmother’s grief for her grandchild is great, but only a betrothed lover, whom fate, faith, and death have all three robbed of their happiness, can mourn as deeply as Justine Marie is mourned.”

He went on to tell me her story, as the storm died down outside. The man who loved her had once been a wealthy young banker’s son, engaged to the beautiful, gentle daughter of a rich jeweller. Then his father’s business had collapsed, he had been left penniless, and Justine Marie’s family, led by her vicious hunchback grandmother Madame Walravens, had forbidden the match. Too soft to run away with him, too good to marry another man for money, Justine Marie had entered a convent, and died during her noviciate.

Years later, Justine Marie’s own father had been ruined in a financial scandal, died of shame, leaving his wife and his terrible old mother Madame Walravens completely destitute. The man she had once cruelly forbidden her granddaughter to marry, the young man whose whole chance at domestic happiness she had destroyed, had come to their rescue. He housed them, cared for them, spent three quarters of his tiny income on supporting them, the old priest his former tutor, and a superannuated old family servant, keeping only enough for himself to buy bread and the most modest two-room lodging. He had never married, given his whole life over to piety and caring for the people who had ruined him.

When he named that man as Monsieur Paul Emanuel, my literature professor, my breath caught. All the little moments I had thought him petty, ungenerous, short-tempered, melted away at once, revealing the great, quiet, unshakable heart hidden under his gruff exterior. Père Silas, it turned out, had never lost track of me after that confession in the Béguinage, and this whole errand, Madame Beck’s sudden little request, my meeting Madame Walravens, this whole meeting, had been gently orchestrated by his careful Jesuit hand.

When the storm cleared, the sun breaking out pale and waterish in the west, I thanked the priest for his hospitality and stepped out to walk home, pondering all I had learned. The gruff, sharp little professor I had clashed with so many times was far greater, far kinder, than I had ever dreamed. And I knew now there was far more to this strange little errand than Madame Beck had ever told me.

“Forget the professors.” So said Madame Beck, smooth and assured as she was in all things. She was a shrewd, capable woman—but she should never have spoken those words. It was a fatal misstep. That night she ought to have left me calm, uninvested, sealed off in my own quiet, separate world, not stirred to even a flicker of connection to the very man they claimed I ought to banish entirely from my mind.

Forget him? The wiseacres around me chose the most absurd possible path to that end. They showed me just how good he was. They painted my sharp-tongued, quick-tempered little Paul Emanuel as a stainless, unassailable hero. Then they prattled on, unasked, about the depth and constancy of his capacity to love. Before that day, what certainty had I ever had that he could love deeply at all?

I had seen him jealous, suspicious; I’d caught glimpses of softness that rolled in like a warm summer breeze, and flickers of pity that vanished as quickly as morning dew, dried away in the sudden heat of his irritability. That was all I’d known of him. But Père Silas and Modeste Maria Beck—there was no doubt the two had acted in perfect concert—opened the hidden chamber of his heart to me. They showed me a grand, all-consuming love, born in the fiery ardor of his southern youth, so strong and perfect that it had laughed in Death’s very face, scorned his petty theft of mortal flesh, clung fast to the undying spirit, and stood faithful vigil beside a tomb for twenty long years.

This was no idle, indulgent daydream. He had proven that fidelity by pouring all his best energies into unselfish purpose, sacrificing every worldly comfort without limit for those who had once been dear to the woman he loved. For her sake, he had set aside all thoughts of vengeance, and taken up a cross no other man would have chosen to bear.

As for Justine Marie, I felt I knew her as clearly as if we had met face to face. She was the quiet, phlegmatic sort I had seen a dozen times in Madame Beck’s school: pale, slow, placid, kind-natured, innocent of evil but never remarkable for any great good.
If others pictured her wearing angel’s wings, I knew whose poetic fancy had fastened them to her shoulders. If they claimed her forehead glowed with the reflected light of a holy halo, I knew in whose dark, fiery eyes that ring of sacred flame had first been kindled.

Was I meant to be terrified by the ghost of a pale dead nun, to see her rise as an eternal barrier between Paul and the rest of the world? What of all the charities that had eaten away at his entire fortune? What of the vow that bound his heart to a life of consecrated singleness?
Madame Beck, Père Silas—you ought never to have planted those questions in my mind. They were the deepest puzzle, the sturdiest obstacle, and the sharpest, most thrilling stimulus I had ever known. For a whole week, sleeping and waking, I turned those two questions over and over in my head. There was no answer to be found anywhere in the wide world, except in the place where that short, dark little man stood, sat, walked, lectured, wearing his faded bandit-style bonnet-grec and his shabby old paletôt, spattered with ink and stained with age and smoke.

After that strange visit to the Rue des Mages, I ached to see him again. It felt as if, now that I knew the truth of him, his face would read like a clearer, more fascinating book than it ever had before. I longed to trace in his features the imprint of that early, unshakable devotion, the marks of that half-knightly, half-saintly chivalry the priest’s story had revealed beneath his gruff exterior. He had become my Christian hero, and I burned to see him under that new light.

Opportunity did not take long to arrive. My new impressions were put to the test the very next day. I was granted an audience with my supposed Christian hero—an interview that was far from heroic, sentimental, or biblical, but lively enough in its own chaotic way.

At three o’clock that afternoon, the peaceful hum of the first classe, overseen by Madame Beck herself as she gave one of her calm, clear, useful lessons, was shattered entirely by the wild, unannounced burst of a shabby old paletôt through the door.
I had never been quieter at that moment. Madame Beck’s presence had lifted all responsibility from my shoulders, her even tones soothed me, and I was happily bent over my desk, copying an elaborate line engraving, painstakingly working my copy up to match the perfect finish of the original. I took absurd pleasure in the tedious labour back then, producing fussy, almost Chinese facsimiles of steel engravings—works as trivial as fancy needlework, but which I thought very fine in those days.

In an instant, my drawing, my pencils, my precious half-finished copy were crushed into a crumpled heap and swept from my sight. I felt shaken right out of my chair, like a lone withered nutmeg tipped out of a spice box by an overexcited cook. That chair, and my desk, were seized by the man in the flying paletôt, one tucked under each arm, and borne away across the room. A second later I was following my furniture, and in two minutes the three of us were set down firmly in the very centre of the grand salle—the vast, seldom-used hall reserved for dancing and choral lessons—plunked down with enough emphasis to make me fear we would never be allowed to leave.

When I’d gathered my scattered wits, I saw two men before me, both flourishing full sets of moustaches, sidewhiskers, and imperial beards. One was dark, stiff and half-military in bearing, wearing a braided frock coat; the other had the careless, slouching air of a student or artist. M. Emanuel stood a little apart from them, his face dark with suppressed rage, one hand outstretched in the dramatic, speaking gesture he used at his lecture tribune.
“Mademoiselle,” he said, “you must prove to these gentlemen that I am no liar. You will answer their questions to the best of your ability. You will also write on whatever theme they choose. It seems they believe I am an unprincipled imposter: that I write essays myself, deliberately forge my pupils’ names to them, and boast that the work is theirs. You will disprove this lie.”

Great heavens. The show trial I had spent so long dodging had crashed down on me like a thunderbolt. These two sneering, foppish, braided, mustachioed figures were none other than Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte, dandy professors from the college—cold-blooded pedants, sceptics, and cruel scoffers. M. Paul had rashly shown them an essay I had written once, a piece he had never praised or even mentioned to me, which I had long thought forgotten. It was not remarkable by any standard, only looked exceptional compared to the average work of foreign schoolgirls; in an English school it would have passed almost unnoticed. The two men had chosen to claim it was a forgery, and now I was to be dragged through their cruel examination to clear M. Emanuel’s name.

What followed was a scene I will never forget.
They began with classics. I drew a total blank. They moved on to French history. I could scarcely tell Mérovée apart from Pharamond. They quizzed me on every branch of learning they could name, and all they got was a shake of my head, and the same unchanging reply: “I do not know.”
After a significant, annoyed pause, they moved on to general information, broaching subjects I knew well, and had thought deeply about. M. Emanuel, who had stood watching dark as the winter solstice, brightened a little. He thought I would finally prove I was no fool.

He was wrong. Answers surged up in my mind fast, filling me like a rising well—all the ideas were there, but no words would come out. I could not, or would not speak; partly I think my nerves had completely tangled, and partly my pride was up in arms. I heard the man in the braided coat whisper to his friend: “Is she actually an idiot?”
Yes, I thought. An idiot I am, and always will be, for the likes of you.

I suffered, cruelly. I saw damp beads of sweat gather on M. Paul’s brow, and his eyes held a bitter, sad reproach. He refused to believe I was naturally slow to speak; he thought I could be quick and sharp if I only tried.
At last, to release him, the professors, and myself from the torture, I stammered out:
“Gentlemen, you had best let me go. You will get nothing useful from me. As you say, I am an idiot.”
I wished I could have spoken with calm dignity, or that I had the sense to hold my tongue. My traitorous tongue tripped and faltered. I saw the two judges cast a triumphant, mocking look at M. Emanuel, heard the shaky distress in my own voice, and burst into choking tears. The emotion was far more anger than grief. If I had been a strong man, I would have challenged both of them to duels on the spot—but it was raw, ungoverned feeling, and I would far rather have been publicly whipped than have let them see it.

The fools! Could they not spot the unpolished work of a novice in that composition they called a forgery? I had started with nothing but a subject M. Paul had dictated to me, entirely new to me. I had hunted through books for facts, painstakingly built a skeleton out of dry, bare history, then clothed those bones in words and tried to breathe life into them. It had taken weeks of anxious work to arrange all the facts properly, to scrub out every flaw or falsehood, but that knowledge was not ripe, ready-stored in my head. I had to glean every bit of it fresh, like wild herbs, and shred them green into the pot. Boissec and Rochemorte could not see that. They mistook my rough, laboured work for that of a full-grown, experienced scholar.

They would not let me go. I had to sit down and write before them. My hand shook as I dipped my pen in ink, and I blinked at the blank page through tear-blurred eyes, as one of the professors began to mince out an apology for causing me pain.
“We act in the interests of truth. We do not wish to hurt you,” he said.
Scorn gave me nerve. I only answered: “Dictate, Monsieur.”
Rochemorte named the theme: “Human Justice.”

Human Justice? It was a blank, cold abstraction, not a single inspiring idea came to me. M. Emanuel stood sad as Saul, stern as Joab, and his accusers gloated.
I looked straight at them. I was gathering my courage to tell them I would not speak or write another word for their amusement, that their theme was hateful and their presence uninspiring, and that anyone who cast a shadow of doubt on M. Emanuel’s honour was the one truly defying the truth they claimed to champion—when a sudden flash of memory hit me.
Those two faces, half-hidden behind a thicket of long hair and whiskers—cold, bold, distrustful, arrogant—were the very same faces I had seen projected in gaslight from behind a portico pillar, the night I arrived desolate and friendless in Villette, the night they had chased me breathless across a whole quarter of the town and half-frightened me to death.
“Pious mentors,” I thought. “Pure guides for the young! If Human Justice were what she ought to be, you two would never hold your posts, or enjoy your fine reputations.”

The idea took root, and I set to work. “Human Justice” rushed before me as a ragged, red-faced old harridan, arms akimbo, holding court in a den of confusion. Servants screamed for her help and orders, and she never answered. Beggars starved on her doorstep, ignored. A swarm of sick, quarrelsome children crawled around her feet, yelling for sympathy and redress, and she cared nothing for any of it. She kept a warm seat by the fire, a short black pipe, and a bottle of soothing syrup for her own comfort. She smoked and sipped, lost in her own small paradise. Whenever a cry of suffering pierced her ears too sharply, she seized the poker or the hearth brush: if the sufferer was weak, wronged, and sickly, she beat them into silence; if they were strong and loud and troublesome, she only threatened, then reached deep into her pocket and tossed a shower of sugarplums to bribe them into quiet.

That was the rough, scathing sketch of “Human Justice” I scratched hastily onto paper, and handed over to Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte. M. Emanuel read it over my shoulder. I did not wait for their comments. I curtsied to all three, and left.

After school that day, M. Paul and I met again. The meeting was not smooth at first. There was a score to settle between us. That wild, forced examination could not be digested in an instant. Our cross, snappish dialogue ended with him calling me “a little mocker, heartless and cold,” and storming off.
I did not want him to leave entirely, only to feel that the kind of unruly outburst he had indulged in that day could not go unchallenged. I was glad, then, to spot him soon after, digging in the berceau, the garden bower. He approached the glass door, and I drew near too. We spoke first of the flowers growing beside the frame. He laid down his spade. Slowly, he turned the conversation to other subjects, until at last he touched on the thing we had both been avoiding.
He knew his behaviour that day had been wildly extravagant. He half-apologised, half-regretted his own fitful moods, and hinted that I ought to make allowances for him. “But,” he said, “I can hardly expect that from you, Miss Lucy. You know nothing of me, or my position, or my history.”

His history. I seized on the word at once.
“No, Monsieur,” I replied. “Of course, as you say, I know neither your history, nor your position, nor your sacrifices, nor any of your sorrows, trials, affections, or loyalties. Oh no, I know nothing at all about you. You are a complete stranger to me.”
“Hein?” he murmured, arching his brows in surprise.
“You know, Monsieur, I only ever see you in the classroom: stern, dogmatic, hasty, imperious. In town, I hear of you as active and wilful, quick to start new schemes, hasty to lead others, but slow to persuade, almost impossible to bend. A man like you, with no ties, can have no attachments. With no dependants, no duties. All of us you come into contact with are just machines you push around, careless of our feelings. You seek your amusements in public, under the chandelier light of evening. This school and the college are your workshops, where you turn out the goods you call pupils. I don’t even know where you live. It is only natural to assume you have no home, and need none.”

“I see how you judge me,” he said. “Your opinion of me is exactly what I feared it was. To you I am neither a man nor a Christian. You see me empty of affection and religion, with no friends or family to bind me, no faith or principle to guide me. Very well, Mademoiselle. That is the reward we get in this life.”
“You are a philosopher, Monsieur,” I said, glancing pointedly at his shabby paletôt, which he immediately brushed at self-consciously. “A cynical philosopher, who despises humanity’s foibles, rises above its luxuries, and does not need its petty comforts.”
“And you, Mademoiselle? You are neat, soft, cosy, and frightfully unfeeling, on top of everything else.”
“But honestly, Monsieur, you must live somewhere, do you not? Tell me where. What household of servants do you keep?”

He thrust his lower lip out in a scowl of dramatic scorn. “I live in a hole! I inhabit a den, Miss—a cavern you would not dare poke your dainty little nose into. Once, stupidly ashamed to speak the full truth, I called my room at the college my ‘study.’ Now you know that ‘study’ is my entire home. My bedroom is there, my sitting room too. As for my ‘servants’” —he mimicked my prim voice— “there are ten of them. Here they are.”
He spread all ten grimy fingers out under my eyes.
“I black my own boots,” he went on savagely. “I brush my own paletôt.”
“No, Monsieur, it is far too shabby, you never do that,” I put in.
“I make my own bed, do all my own housework. I eat my dinner at a restaurant, my supper is whatever I can scrounge. I pass long days of unremitting work, with no one to love, and long nights of total loneliness. I am ferocious, bearded, almost monkish. Nothing alive in this world loves me, except a few old hearts as worn out as my own, and a handful of impoverished, suffering people, poor in purse and spirit, whom the world does not want, but whom the gospel has promised the kingdom of heaven.”

“Ah, Monsieur—but I know all that.”
“What do you know? A great many things, I don’t doubt—but you do not know me, Lucy!”
“I know you have a pleasant old house in a quiet old square in Basse-Ville. Why do you not go and live there?”
“Hein?” he muttered again, surprised.
“I liked it very much, Monsieur. The steps leading up to the door, the grey flagstones out front, the tall old trees behind—real trees, not shrubs, dark and mature, growing for decades. And the little oratory boudoir—you should turn that into your study. It is so quiet and solemn.”

He stared at me closely, half-smiling, a dark flush rising to his cheeks. “Where did you hear all that? Who told you?”
“Nobody told me. Do you think I dreamed it, Monsieur?”
“How am I to guess a woman’s waking thoughts, much less her sleeping fantasies?”
“If I did dream it, I saw people in the dream, as well as the house. I

Before the quiet covenant of friendship we had so lately struck could feel truly sealed, there was another power to contend with besides Fifine Beck’s sharp-witted mother: Rome herself, keeping sleepless, jealous watch over her son through the slatted lattice I had once knelt before, the sliding wooden panel of the confessional M. Paul drew closer to month by month.

You might well wonder why I clung so fiercely to that new promise of his steady friendship, when he had shown me proof after proof of his partiality for years. It was the earnest, unreserved way he spoke it, I think: the soft, modest doubt in his own worth as a friend, the tender deference that trusted me enough to rest his weariness in my company. He had called me “sister”, and it felt perfect. Let him call me whatever he pleased, so long as he kept nothing back from me. I would gladly be his sister, so long as he never asked me to fill that role for some future wife of his — and given his long-held tacit vow of celibacy, that danger felt impossibly far off.

I turned that evening’s interview over in my head almost the entire night, too restless to sleep, counting the minutes till the morning bell rang. Prayers dragged, breakfast moved at a snail’s pace, every hour stretching too thin before it was finally time for our literature lesson. I ached to see how he would carry himself as my brother, to test how much of a sister’s gentle courage lived in me, how much of a brother’s unguarded frankness he would show.

When he arrived, the world proved it never could match the soft shape of my expectations. He never once spoke to me that whole day. His lessons were quieter, milder, and far graver than usual; he was kind, fatherly even, to all his other pupils, but not a single brotherly glance or word came my way. Before he left the classroom, I waited for a smile, if nothing else — but all I got was one hurried, shy little nod, gone before I could even react.

I told myself the distance was accidental, involuntary, that patience would make it melt. It did not melt. It stretched on for days, and grew wider. I bit back my surprise, swallowed the hot, stinging feelings that rose unbidden in my throat, and waited.

I had every right to ask him, when he offered me fraternity, “Dare I rely on you?” And he, knowing his own heart’s vulnerabilities better than I, had every right to withhold that pledge. He had bade me tease him, test him, make my own experiments with his good nature — but that privilege was never mine to take. I was the sort of woman who withdrew when repulsed, who stayed passive when left alone, whose lips would never utter a reminder, whose eyes would never dart a pleading glance. Somewhere in my neat calculations, a line had slipped out of place, and I needed time to find where the error lay.

At last the customary evening for our weekly private lesson rolled around, the one he had generously set aside seven days out of seven for months, to go over the week’s work and map out lessons for the days ahead. On those nights, we usually met wherever the other pupils and teachers happened to be, in the big second-floor classroom that emptied out once the day students left, leaving only a handful of boarders clustered around the surveillante’s desk. When the hour struck, I gathered my books, my pen and ink, and made my way there.

The classroom itself was empty, wrapped in cool, soft shadow, but through the open double doors I could see the sunlit common room, full of girls laughing and working, the westering sun blushing crimson over every wall, every dress, every face, fusing all the colours of the room into one warm, honeyed glow. M. Emanuel stood in the middle of their circle, chatting good-humouredly with the other teacher, his dark coat and jet black hair streaked with the sun’s red light, his sharp Spanish face lighting up with a smile when he turned, as if kissing the sun right back. I slipped into an empty desk.

The orange trees, the geraniums, the bright blooming cactuses all basked in that sunset glow, thirsty after a full day of heat. M. Paul had a soft spot for gardening; tending plants with his watering pot always soothed his frayed nerves, and I watched him move from pot to pot, watering each one carefully, his favourite cigar clamped between his lips, its soft blue smoke curling pretty through the blooms in the golden light. He soon stopped talking to the pupils and teachers, and started cooing soft endearments to a tiny silky spaniel that nominally belonged to the school, but loved him far more than any other living soul in the house. She trotted at his heels, her big soft eyes fixed on his face, and whenever he playfully dropped his bonnet or handkerchief, she would crouch over it like a tiny lion guarding a royal flag.

He fetched all the water from the courtyard well himself, so the work stretched on longer than I expected. The big school clock ticked past the hour we usually set for our lesson, and then another hour struck. The sunset glow faded, twilight creeping in fast. My lesson would be very short tonight, I thought — but then the last of the potted plants were watered. Surely it was my turn now?

Alas, he still had his favourite rose bushes and a handful of rare blooms to tend down the garden alley, and the little spaniel yapped gleefully after him, her small paws pattering over the paving stones. I stacked half my books away, no longer needing them, and sat waiting, watching the shadows creep slowly across the floor.

Soon enough the little dog came bounding back, announcing his return. He set the empty watering pot down by the well, washed his hands in the stone basin by the door, and stood slowly adjusting his cuffs, staring up at the thin pale horn of the new moon hanging in the opal sky. Sylvie, the spaniel, grew restless with his quiet contemplation, whined and jumped at his knees till he laughed, and lifted her up in his arms.

“Little greedy thing,” he said to her, “I can’t forget you for a single moment, can I?”

He wandered slowly across the courtyard, no more than a yard from the line of windows where I sat, fondling Sylvie against his chest, calling her all sorts of tender little pet names.
