On the front steps he paused, looked out over the grey cathedral spires fading into the blue mist of coming night, breathed in the sweet cool dusk air — and then suddenly his sharp gaze swept the length of the classroom windows. I think he bowed; if he did, I had no time to return the courtesy before the door clicked shut behind him, and he was gone.

I gathered up all my things, and carried the unused stack of books back to my own desk in the third classroom. The prayer bell rang, and I went through the motions of the service, my chest heavy.

He would not return to the Rue Fossette the next day, his entire schedule devoted to duties at his college. I dragged through my own teaching, got through the long empty hours, and prepared myself for the heavy loneliness of the evening. When I sat alone at my desk and flipped open its lid, reaching for a book to comfort me, a small lilac-covered pamphlet I had never seen before fell out. I had rearranged my desk only that afternoon, so it must have been slipped there while we were all at dinner.

I opened it, curious. It was no story, no poem, no history: it was a thin, soft-spoken theological tract, full of honeyed, gentle language, meant to persuade its reader to convert to Roman Catholicism. No thundering threats of hellfire, no harsh condemnation — only the soft, coaxing tone of a loving mother, promising the gentle comfort of Holy Church, the great solace of being able to pray your dead loved ones out of purgatory. It was written for tender hearts, not sharp intellects, milk for babes rather than strong meat, and it amused me more than it angered me. On the flyleaf, in his familiar small clear pencil handwriting, it read: From P.C.D.E. to L——y.

I laughed, and all at once the fog of my confusion cleared completely. The riddle was solved. Père Silas, his confessor, had drawn every last detail of our new fraternal covenant out of him, told him that friendship with a Protestant heretic was unholy, dangerous, a threat to his soul. That was the source of that cold, distant reserve, not any change of heart on M. Paul’s part. It was far easier to bear than the fear that he had grown tired of me all on his own.

That night, the sky was completely overcast, a clammy grey fog creeping in from the marshes around Villette, a fine soft rain drizzling steadily down. I heard Sylvie’s sharp happy bark out in the garden, the kind she only ever saved for M. Paul, and looked out to see him digging furiously in the wet dark soil, his coat soaked through, working as if he was digging for his daily bread by the sweat of his brow. I knew that habit of old: he always dug like that for hours, brows knit, teeth set, when his heart was torn apart by painful conflicting feelings, either nervous excitement or sharp stabs of self-reproach.

Sylvie spotted me at the window, and flew barking at the panes, as if to urge me out to join him. He whistled for her to stop, but she only barked louder, till he finally pushed the glass door open and let her burst inside. She jumped straight into my lap, her paws on my neck, her little pink tongue licking my face all over, scattering all my papers and books across the desk.

M. Paul stepped inside to quiet the chaos, gathered up my books, tucked Sylvie gently under his coat where she settled quiet as a mouse, her little peeking over his lapel. His gaze fell straight on the little lilac tract.

“You haven’t read that pamphlet yet, I suppose?” he said, tentative. “It’s not very inviting, is it?”

I told him I had read it. He waited, expecting me to give an opinion unprompted — but I was not going to make the first move, not after two days of cold silence. I deliberately picked up my dull penknife and a handful of quills, pretending to mend them, nicking my finger half on purpose, to make him scold me like he always used to.

“Clumsy thing!” he cried at last, snatching the knife and pens out of my hand. “You’ll cut your whole fingers to ribbons.”

He proceeded to mend the pens with the neat, swift accuracy of a professional, and asked if I had liked the little book. I suppressed a yawn, and said it had made me a little sleepy. He laughed, then chided me gently, insisting that I was far too sensitive to be untouched by its soft, loving appeal. I pulled out a perfectly dry, completely clean handkerchief from my pocket to prove he was wrong, and he launched into a stream of his old familiar sharp, playful criticisms that was sweeter than any music after two days of silence. I shared my chocolates with Sylvie, and he sat there watching, softening, till he leaned over the desk, his voice gentle again.

“Tell me honestly, little sister,” he said, “what did you think of me these past two days?”

I pretended not to hear, my eyes suddenly stinging, and stroked Sylvie’s fur to hide it.

“I called myself your brother,” he went on, his voice low. “I hardly know what I am to you — brother, friend, I can’t tell. I think of you constantly, I wish you every good thing, but I have to check myself. My best friends keep warning me you are dangerous. It’s your religion, that strange, self-reliant Protestant faith of yours. Père Silas says you are good, that he even loves you — but that proud, earnest creed of yours, it’s a threat. Sometimes I see it in your eyes, and my flesh creeps.”

I spoke to him then, soft and steady, no sharp arguments, no heated debate. I told him I believed in the same God, the same Christ, the same Bible that he did, that I would never hurt his faith, that his Church’s slanders about Protestants being irreverent pagans were untrue. He argued with me, pleaded his case for hours, but he could not shake my quiet, gentle certainty. When he left that night, he was not fully satisfied, not fully appeased — but he could no longer doubt that my faith was no threat to him. The rift his confessor had carved between us was not unbreakable, after all.

Paulina might have sworn off any further correspondence with Graham until her father gave his full blessing to their bond, but Dr. Bretton could not possibly live within a league of the Hôtel Crécy without finding every excuse he could to call. For a little while, the pair of them meant to keep a polite distance, to hold back from any grand, overt show of courtship as they’d promised themselves—but their hearts drew closer all the same, soft and unforced as the lengthening golden light of a June afternoon.

Every noble, best part of Graham woke and leaned toward Paulina; even the dazed, superficial fancy he’d once harbored for silly Ginevra Fanshawe faded entirely, for that old crush had never once engaged his sharp, hungry intellect. Now every part of him, every keen taste and curious faculty, lit up in her company, starved no longer for conversation that fed his soul. Paulina never once set out to reform him, or to school his mind—she thought him perfect already, far too wonderful to be improved by anyone. It was Graham himself who first stumbled into talking of a book he’d been reading, and when her response hummed with a harmony of sympathy he’d never known before, he spoke longer and better than he ever had in his life. She listened bright-eyed, answered with wit that sparkled, and every exchange uncovered some new hidden corner of his own mind he’d never noticed, some unacknowledged goodness in his heart he’d never had cause to nurture. Their thoughts fit each other like carefully strung pearls, matching line for line, and where Paulina was usually quiet and pensive left to her own devices, now she was as merry as a lark, glowing soft as diffused sunlight. All that careful, shy reserve she’d wrapped herself in for years thawed completely under Graham’s generous, warm influence, as if it had never been there at all.

They talked over the old days at the Bretton house, first with shy, smiling diffidence, then with open, unguarded candor. Graham never needed Lucy to prompt his memories of “little Polly”—every last recollection was his to tell, far sweeter coming straight from his own kind lips. Paulina would later recount to me, wonder in her voice, how vivid his memory was: he could remind her how she’d once grabbed his face in her small hands, stroked his thick dark curls and cried out “Graham, I *do* like you!” He could recall the weight of her little footstool dragged up beside his chair, the soft press of her small fingers smoothing his cheek, the way her tiny forefinger prodded the cleft in his chin and lisped that it was “a pretty dimple,” then looked up to tell him he had a “nice, strange face, far nicer than your mamma’s or Lucy’s.”

“To think I was so bold then!” she said to me once, cheeks pink. “He seems almost sacred to me now—his curls feel too lofty to touch, and when I look at his firm, marble chin, his straight, perfect Greek features, I almost feel afraid. People call women beautiful, Lucy, but he is nothing like a woman. Do other people see him as I do? Do *you* admire him?”

“I’ll tell you what I do,” I answered her, sharp and quick to stop the flood of tender confidences that would otherwise turn molten lead in my ear. “I never see him. I looked at him twice or thrice a year ago, before he recognized me, and then I shut my eyes. If he passed across them twelve times between sunset and sunrise, I would never be able to tell you what shape had gone by, apart from old memory.”

“Lucy, what do you mean?” she breathed.

“I mean I value my sight too much to risk being struck stone blind.”

It silenced her. She did not gush over his beauty to me again, though she still spoke of him sometimes, shy and soft, her voice strung with a tenderness that chafed at my lonely heart until I turned away with a sharp look. She only laughed, calling me her spartan, proud Lucy, and said Graham thought I was the quirkiest, most excellent little woman he had ever met, that the two of them wanted me to share their beautiful, bright life together. I told her I had no use for the kind of shared life they imagined. Solitude was sad, I admitted—but heartbreak was far sadder.

Lovers are selfish in their happiness, determined to have a witness no matter what it costs that onlooker. Paulina had forbidden letters, yet Graham wrote; she had sworn not to reply, yet she answered, if only to chide him for breaking their promise. She made me read his notes, and I could not blame her for being proud: they were fine, manly letters, tender and modest, gallant without being foolish. Her own letters were even lovelier—she had tried so hard to hide her love, to bridle his ardor, but every line glowed with the invisible magnet pull he had over her, warm from greeting to farewell.

“I wish papa knew,” she would murmur, anxious, over and over. “I so wish he did, but I fear it too. Graham is ready to go and tell him tomorrow, but I know he will be angry at first. It will be a shock, I know. I can hardly bear to think what he will say.”

M. de Bassompierre, who had walked through the last years blind to the growing attachment under his roof, was finally stirring awake. He said nothing to Paulina, but when she was not looking, I saw him stare at her, quiet and thoughtful, as if noticing for the first time that his little girl was no longer a child.

One evening, Paulina had gone up to her dressing room to write that very letter to Graham, leaving me in the library reading, when he walked in and asked me not to leave. He pulled out an old pocket memorandum book from his desk, and studied an entry for a long minute before turning to me.

“Miss Snowe,” he said, “do you know how old my little girl is?”

“About eighteen, sir, is it not?”

“So the book tells me. She was born the fifth of May, eighteen years ago. It is strange—I had forgotten to count the years right. I always thought of her as twelve, fourteen, some indefinite little age, a child forever.”

He sat quiet, heavy with thought. “My little jewel. She is the only pearl I have. Now others are starting to see how pure and precious she is. They will want to take her from me.”

I said nothing. Graham had dined with us that day, glowing, all his manners bright with the high hope he could no longer hide, and I knew Mr. Home had at last put the pieces together, working his slow, logical way through the whole story. When I told him Paulina was writing letters, not just to me or to Graham’s mother, he grumbled that the young man had no right to steal his only treasure—why could he not have set his sights on Ginevra Fanshawe, whom he would have handed over to Graham gladly, instead of his Polly? He could not fathom that any man could be good enough for her, that any title or fortune could ever measure up to what she was. I pressed him gently, asking if any of the stuck-up Villette barons or vicomtes would ever please him more than Graham, and he admitted he could not stand a single one of them. I reminded him that Graham had known them both his whole life, that he did not care a fig for her heiress fortune, that he had seen her wit and grace that night all the learned French scholars crowded around her at dinner, calling her *pétrie d’esprit et de graces*—all wit and grace.

He remembered then, how when he was deathly ill, little Polly had nursed him through, growing stronger and tenderer as he worsened, his only sunbeam in the sickroom as he recovered. He sighed, and admitted he had known Graham’s mother Louisa since they were children, that she had been a tall, blooming girl fit for a princess, that he had always thought well of the boy. It still felt like robbery, though.

The door opened then, and Paulina stepped in, all dressed for the June evening, her cheeks flushed summer pink, her curls falling loose and long over her lily-white neck, the unsealed half-written letter still in her hand to show me. She froze when she saw her father, the color rising all over her face. He teased her for blushing, and she stammered that she had not expected him to be there, that the letter was just a short note to a friend. When he pressed her to tell the truth, her courage suddenly broke through.

“Oh papa, I will tell you everything!” she cried, trembling all over. “I hate hiding things from you. I love you more than anything but God.” She laid the letter in his lap. He read it, his own hand shaking, his eyes glistening, and when he finished, he looked at her as if he could not believe that the little child who once stood at his knee could write such words, feel such things. He was gruff at first, pretending to call Graham a scamp, a robber who had dived deep into waters he had no business wading, teasing that he would send the lad off to Siberia. Paulina scolded him for being prejudiced, called him a naughty old Scotsman, before all her dignity melted away, and she flung herself sobbing around his neck, swearing she would never leave him, never make him unhappy. He choked out two words, rough and tender: “My lamb! My treasure!”

The room was dimming now, so I slipped quietly out to the antechamber to stop any servant bringing candles from interrupting. No servant waited there. Graham stood by the table, setting down his hat, pulling off his gloves slowly, lingering, waiting. He did not call to me, but his eyes said Lucy, come here, and I went. A soft, bright smile played across his face, the same brave charm he had always carried, even with his breath caught tight in suspense. He asked if he was about to be called to judgment, if Mr. Home had understood his intentions at last. I told him his star must be fortunate. He called me his old faithful little god-sister, asked me to say amen to his great venture. I did, and he turned to walk back into the library, me following at his heels.

“Sir,” he said, stepping up to Mr. Home, “what is my sentence?”

The father looked at him, Paulina’s face still hidden against his shoulder.

“Well, Bretton,” he said, gruff but softening already. “You have given me the usual reward of hospitality. I welcomed you into my house, and you have taken my best thing. I will not call it robbery—but I am bereaved, and what I have lost, you, it seems, have won.”

“Sir,” Graham said, steady, “I cannot repent.”

“Repent? Not you! You triumph, no doubt. You get that old Highlander Celt blood from your ancestors, don’t you—all that cunning, that charm, the clever tongue and the wily brain, passed straight down your line.”

It is not granted to all to walk the easy, sunlit path, and when our own way grows dark, what use is it to rage? His will shall be done, no matter if we bow to resignation or kick against the pricks. The very impulse of creation carries it forward; powers seen and unseen hold its fulfilment in their charge. That proof of a life to come must be written in fire, in blood if need be—we trace its mark across every wild corner of nature, across every sharp, unyielding stretch of our own experience. Sufferer, do not faint at the touch of that burning evidence. Tired wayfarer, gird up your loins, lift your eyes, and march onward. All of us who walk this wilderness together, equal in our weariness, steady in our tread, let us bear our cross as our banner. We have His promise for a staff, His providence for present hope, His open bosom for our final rest, and a glory beyond all imagining that waits for us at the end. So let us run that we may win, endure hardship as good soldiers, finish our course and keep the faith, and come off more than conquerors at the last: Art thou not from everlasting, my Holy One? We shall not die.

One soft July Thursday morning, we were all gathered in the first classe, waiting for our literature lesson. The hour was due, and we expected him any moment.

The pupils sat perfectly still, their neat, ribbon-tied compositions laid out on their desks, ready for Professor Emanuel’s quick, impatient rounds. The air was warm, the glass door to the garden propped ajar, and fresh breeze drifted in, making the jasmine plants at the lintel nod and sway, as if they were carrying secret, unspoken tidings.

M. Paul was not always perfectly punctual, so no one wondered much at the small delay—until the door opened, and instead of his swift, fiery step, cautious, unhurried Madame Beck stepped quietly into the room.

She walked straight to M. Emanuel’s desk, stood before it, pulled her light summer shawl tighter around her shoulders, and spoke in low, firm, unwavering tones. “There will be no literature lesson this morning.”

A full two minutes of dead silence followed before she spoke again.
“Lessons will be suspended for at least a week. I shall need that long to find a suitable replacement for M. Emanuel. In the meantime, we will fill the gap usefully.
“Your Professor, ladies,” she continued, “intends, if possible, to come and take formal leave of you all. He has no time for that ceremony right now. He is preparing for a long voyage: a very sudden, urgent duty summons calls him to a great distance. He has decided to leave Europe for an indefinite stretch of time. Perhaps he will tell you more of it himself. This morning, instead of your usual lesson with M. Emanuel, you will read English with Mademoiselle Lucy.”

She gave a small courteous nod, drew her shawl closer, and left the classroom.

A great, heavy silence fell, then a low, murmurous wave of sound rolled across the room. I saw more than one girl press her handkerchief to her face, her shoulders shaking.

The whispering and soft sobs only grew louder with time. Discipline frayed; it felt as if the school’s usual vigilance had vanished entirely. Habit and long practice let me rally fast, though. I stood, spoke in my usual firm, steady tone, and slowly, steadily restored order. I kept their English reading long and rigorous, occupying them for the whole rest of the morning. I was sharp, unsparing, even half-mocking of the girls who wept so loudly. Their emotion was only hysteric agitation, hollow as froth—and the truth was, I could not bear their gasping, noisy tears, not when every thread of my own self-control was stretched almost to snapping. When one weak, low-spirited pupil still sobbed after all the others had calmed, I spoke to her so sternly that she was forced to conquer the convulsion.

That girl might well have hated me—until the end of the school day, when I ordered her to stay back after the others left, and pressed her to my chest, kissing her cheek, a kindness I had never shown any student before. The second I did it, I sent her away, for the small burst of tenderness made her weep all the harder.

I stuffed every minute of that day full of work, and would have sat up all night by candlelight if I could, but the dark hours that followed were cruel, leaving me raw and unfit for the next day’s ordeal of endless, unthinking gossip. The news spread like wildfire. Reserve wore off fast; every mouth, from teachers to pupils to the lowest kitchen servant, chattered M. Emanuel’s name. The man who had been at the school’s side from its very opening, gone all at once! Everyone thought it strange.

They talked so much, so long, that bits of real information began to surface. On the third day, I heard he was sailing in a week, bound for the West Indies. I searched Madame Beck’s face, her eyes, her every small gesture, for confirmation or denial—but there was nothing there, only her usual unperturbed, commonplace calm. She declared his departure an immense loss to her, that he was her right hand, that she had fought the decision but he had convinced her it was his unavoidable duty. She said this loudly, publicly, in the classroom and at the dinner table, directly to Zélie St. Pierre, never once glancing at me, never once seeming to suspect I might have any care in the matter.

The week dragged on. No one spoke of M. Paul coming to say goodbye. No one asked if he would show up. No one betrayed the quiet, gnawing terror that he might slip away silent and unseen. They chattered endlessly, and never once touched that single vital point. Of course Madame could see him whenever she liked. Why would she care if he came back to the classroom?

At last the news was confirmed: his ship sailed for Basseterre, in Guadaloupe, and the business abroad was for a friend, not his own. I hardly slept those days. Whenever I didze off, I would jolt awake instantly, the words *Basseterre* and *Guadaloupe* ringing in my ears, scrawled in zigzag lines of red or violet light across the dark of my room.

There was no help for what I felt. How could there be? M. Paul had grown so kind to me lately, softer, warmer, hour by hour. It had been a month since we settled that sharp theological quarrel, and there had been no fight between us since. Our peace was no cold, distant standoff: he came to see me more often, talked to me far more freely, spent hours at my side with his temper smoothed, his eyes soft, his manner as home-like and gentle as I could have ever hoped. He asked after my plans to open my own small school, made me tell him the details again and again, teasing that it was nothing but a silly Alnaschar dream. The old jarring was gone. We understood each other. Feelings of union, of quiet hope, had settled deep in my heart: affection, deep esteem, a dawning trust that had woven itself into an unbreakable bond.

Our lessons in those last weeks were so quiet, so sweet. No more taunts about my “intellect”, no more threats to drag me up in front of the whole school to perform. In place of the jealous gibe and the half-passionate eulogy, there was only silent, indulgent help, fond guidance, a tender forbearance that forgave all my faults but never stooped to empty praise. Sometimes he would sit for minutes without speaking at all, and when dusk or duty pulled him away, he would murmur, *“It is soft, this rest. This quiet happiness is so precious.”*

Not ten days before that final announcement, he had joined me while I walked in my private alley after dark. He took my hand, and I looked up at his face, certain he was about to say something that would change everything.

*“Bonne petite amie!”* he said, soft as the twilight. *“Sweet little comforter!”* His touch, his words, stirred a strange new hope in me. Could it be that he cared for me as more than a friend, more than a brother? Did his look hold a kindness far beyond fraternity?

His eyes had more to say, his hand drew me closer, his lips were about to form the words—when the alley’s quiet was broken. Two ominous figures stepped out of the dusk: Madame Beck, and Père Silas.

I will never forget the priest’s face. At first it softened with that overdone sentimental sensibility, as if he was shocked to stumble on such a tender moment, then it hardened fast with the jaundiced bite of ecclesiastical jealousy. He spoke to me with cloying unction, and turned to M. Paul with sharp, unyielding sternness. Madame, of course, pretended to see nothing at all—even as M. Paul kept my hand clasped tight in his, not letting me pull it away.

After that, the news of his sudden departure had seemed impossible to believe at first. Only the constant repetition, the hundred and fifty voices all around me speaking of it as fact, forced me to accept it. That week of suspense, those blank, burning days with no word, no explanation from him, I remember, but I can find no words to describe how it felt to live through them.

The last day broke. That was the day he would either come and speak his farewell, or vanish silent, and we would never see him again.

It seemed no one else in the whole school shared that sharp, waiting dread. Everyone rose at the usual hour, ate their breakfast as usual, went about their usual duties with their usual phlegm, not a single one of them seeming to spare a thought for their old Professor. No one would band together to beg Madame Beck for one last interview with the man who had, at least in their own way, been loved by so many. What was the love of the crowd, after all, but a shallow, fleeting thing?

I knew where he lived. I knew exactly where to find him. The distance was barely a stone’s throw away. But I could not go. For all my quiet longing, I had no faculty to seek him out, to run after him, to remind him I was there. Even if he had walked within arm’s reach of me, silent and unnoticing, I would have stood still, and let him pass.

The morning wasted away. Afternoon came, and I thought it was all over. My heart trembled in my chest. My blood ran thick and troubled. I was so sick I could barely stay at my post, barely do my work, while every other person in that little world plodded on, indifferent, jocund, free of all care. The very girls who had wept hysterically a week prior had completely forgotten the news, what it meant, what they had felt.

A little before five, the hour of school dismissal, Madame Beck sent for me to her chamber, to translate an English letter she had received and write out a reply. Before we started, I noticed her softly close both doors to the room, then shut and fastened the casement even though the day was hot, and free air was usually non-negotiable for her. Why that precaution? A sharp, fierce suspicion flared in me. She was keeping sound out. What sound?

I listened harder than I ever had in my life, like the winter wolf scenting prey on the snow, hearing the distant tramp of a traveller miles off. I could write and listen at the same time. Halfway through the letter, I heard it: a tread in the vestibule. No bell had rung; Rosine had been sent ahead to open the door for him, no doubt on direct orders. Madame saw my pen halt. She coughed, bustled around, spoke louder. The tread moved on, towards the classrooms.

“Proceed,” she said. But my hand was fettered, my ear was caught, all my thoughts were held captive.

The classrooms were in a separate wing, the hall dividing them from the dwelling house, but through all the partitions and distance, I heard a sudden stir of dozens of people, a whole division rising to their feet at once.

“They are putting away their work,” Madame said.

It was the hour to put away work, yes—but why that sudden hush, that instant, quiet lull across the whole room?

“Wait, Madame. I will go see what it is.”

I set down my pen and left her. She could not detain me, so she rose and followed, close as my shadow. I turned on the last step of the stairs.

“Are you coming too?” I asked.

“Yes,” she said, her face clouded, but resolute.

We walked forward, not together, but she stepped exactly in my tracks.

He was there. When I stepped into the first classe, I saw him, that familiar face, that form I had longed for so badly. They had tried to keep him away, but he had come anyway.

The girls stood in a semicircle. He was making his way around them, saying his farewells, pressing each hand, touching each girl’s cheek in a parting kiss, the custom of the land for such a long, solemn goodbye.

It felt unbearable that Madame Beck was dogging me so close, watching my every move. My neck and shoulder burned under the warmth of her breath at my back, and a sharp, wild anger goaded me forward.

He was almost done making his way around the semicircle. He reached the last pupil, and turned. But Madame stepped out in front of me, suddenly, her whole frame seeming to swell, her shawl spreading wide to eclipse me, to hide me from view. She knew my weakness, that moral paralysis that could fall on me in a crisis, that I would never shout or push or draw attention to myself. She hurried straight to her kinsman, talking volubly, seizing all his attention, rushing him towards the glass garden door. I think he looked over his shoulder. If I could have caught his eye, courage would have rushed in to aid my feeling, I think I would have run to him, and snatched that meeting for myself—but the room was all confusion, the semicircle broken into chattering groups, and my plain, small figure was lost among thirty far more conspicuous girls. Madame had her will. She got him out the door, and he never saw me. He thought I was absent. Five o’clock struck. The dismissal bell blared. The school emptied.

For some minutes I stood alone in that empty room, swallowed up in total darkness and distraction, a grief so inexpressible it felt as if all my life’s hope had been torn up by the roots right out of my chest. What was I to do? When a small child, the tiniest pupil in the whole school, trotted in, all innocence, straight into the middle of my silent, raging grief.

“Mademoiselle,” she lisped in her treble voice, “I have to give you this. M. Paul said I was to look for you all over the house, from the attic to the cellar, and when I found you, give this to you.”

She handed me a note, that small dove bringing back the olive leaf. There was no address, no name, only these words:
> It was not my intention to say farewell to you when I greeted the rest, for I hoped to find you in the classroom. I was disappointed. Our interview is deferred. Be ready for me. Before I sail, I must see you at leisure, and speak with you at length. Be ready. My moments are numbered, and at present they are all monopolized. I have a private business I will share with no one, not even you. — PAUL.

Be ready? That meant tonight. His ship sailed tomorrow, I was certain: I had seen the departure date advertised. Oh, I would be ready. But could that longed-for meeting ever actually happen? The time was so short, the schemers watching me so active, so hostile. The path to him was narrow as a gully, deep as a chasm, and Apollyon himself straddled it, breathing flames. Could my Paul, my Greatheart, reach me through all that?

Who could tell? But some small courage, some small comfort crept into me. I could feel the pulse of his heart, still beating true, matching every throb of mine.

I waited. The whole evening I waited, clinging to the promise of that little note, even as a heavy, cold dread settled over me, the kind of presentiment that is never wrong. The first hours dragged on slow as honey. They passed like drift cloud, like wrack scudding fast before a storm.

The long, hot summer day burned away like a Yule log. The crimson of the sunset faded. I was left in the cool blue shades of the classroom, bent over the pale ashen glow of the night outside.

Prayers were over. It was bedtime. All the other boarders had retired to their rooms. I still remained in the dark, empty first classe, disregarding rules I had never once broken before.

I paced the length of the room for hours, mechanically shoving benches and desks aside to make a clear path for myself. There, when I was sure the whole household was asleep, out of earshot, I finally let myself weep. I had kept my tears sealed, my sobs chained, for too long. They heaved my heart, tore their way out. What grief in this house could ever be sacred?

Just after eleven o’clock—an unheard of late hour in the Rue Fossette—the door opened, quietly but not stealthily. A lamp’s flame cut through the moonlight. Madame Beck walked in, her air as composed as if she were visiting at an ordinary, reasonable hour. Instead of speaking to me at once, she went to her desk, pulled out her keys, and pretended to rummage for something, lingering far too long over the feigned search. She was too calm. My frayed nerves could not stand the pretence. I had long since left all my usual old respects and fears behind me. I would bear no yoke, no curb, not now.

“It is far past time for bed,” she said. “You have broken the house rules far too long.”

I said nothing, did not stop my pacing. When she stepped in my way, I moved her gently but firmly aside.

“Let me persuade you to calm, Meess. Let me lead you to your chamber,” she said, trying to speak soft and soothing.

“No,” I said. “Neither you nor anyone else will persuade or lead me.”

“Your bed will be warmed. Goton is still up. She will make you comfortable, give you a sedative.”

“Madame,”

Spellbound, I could not tear myself away from the little huddled group of people gathered a few paces off; the grass beneath my boots might as well have been rooted to my ankles. The interlaced boughs of the old lime trees draped me in deep, soft shadow, the warm night air hummed low with the distant echo of fête music, promising no one would stumble on my hidden nook, and a single guttering streetlamp flickered just long enough to illuminate a tucked-away seat half-buried in ivy before its flame died, leaving me perfectly concealed.

For a fortnight of dark, lonely nights I had gathered scraps of rumour, piecing together the full story of why M. Emanuel had been persuaded to leave Villette, and the motives behind his sudden, unannounced departure. The tale was common, cruel, and simple: its beginning was greed, its end nothing but cold self-interest.

Madame Walravens, misshapen and sharp-tongued as any carved idol in a far-off shrine, was treated by her little circle of hangers-on as if she held the power of a goddess. She had once been extraordinarily wealthy, and though she had no ready money to hand now, everyone knew she would one day inherit a vast fortune once more. At Basseterre, in Guadaloupe, stood a sprawling sugar estate that had come to her as a wedding dowry sixty years prior, seized by creditors after her husband’s bankruptcy, now finally cleared of all outstanding claims. If a trustworthy, hardworking steward of unshakable integrity travelled out to manage it, the property would turn a huge, steady profit within a handful of years.

Père Silas pushed for the scheme for the sake of his church, of which Madame Walravens was a fiercely devout patron. Madame Beck, distantly related to the old hunchback and well aware she had no surviving family of her own, had spent years quietly plotting over the fortune, ever courting Madame Walravens even when the old woman snapped and insulted her to her face. Both priest and pensionnat mistress had equal, greedy stakes in seeing the West Indian estate flourish.

But the journey across the sea was long, the climate deadly for Europeans unused to it. The perfect, devoted steward they needed was right before their eyes: M. Paul Emanuel. Madame Walravens had held him in her thrall for twenty years, draining his small savings, wearing down every last moment of his youth for her own comfort, like fungus sapping the life from a strong tree. Père Silas had raised him, trained him, bound him to his will with threads of gratitude and religious duty. Madame Beck knew well how easily he could be led to sacrifice himself for others, and she whispered to the others that if he stayed in Europe, he might stray from his faith, grow close to some heretic (meaning me, I thought, with a cold twist in my chest) and slip out of their control entirely. The three self-serving schemers closed in around the one unselfish man among them, pleading, cajoling, laying their troubles at his feet, begging him to give just two or three years of his life to their cause, promising he could live for himself afterwards. One of them, I had no doubt, hoped he would sicken and die in that tropical heat long before he could ever come back to claim his share.

M. Emanuel never could turn away a plea for help laid at his feet with trusting, open hands. No one asked what private heartbreak he hid at the thought of leaving Villette, no one cared what plans he had made for his own quiet future. Then one day he was gone, and he had left no word, no note, no sign for me at all. That was the sum of all I knew, until now, as I huddled in the ivy.

For a long while I paid no mind to the chatter of the group a few paces off; they gossiped of gowns, of the band playing at the main concert, of the lantern-lit fête illuminations, of the mild, fair weather that made ocean voyages smooth. I waited, half-hoping they would mention his ship, the *Antigua*, but no one spoke of it at all.

Old Madame Walravens grew restless, craning her neck this way and that, peering through the trees and out over the throng of fête-goers, muttering over and over, “Where are they? Why have they not come?” At last she spoke loud enough for all of us to hear, a question that sent a sharp shock right through my bones: “Messieurs et mesdames, où donc est Justine Marie?”

Justine Marie! The name of the dead nun whose portrait hung in the garret, the ghost I had seen gliding through the alley after dark! I half expected the group to fall silent, to cross themselves in fright, but no one seemed surprised at all. “Justine Marie is on her way,” one man said casually. “She’s in the kiosk. She will be here in a moment.”

The chatter shifted, full of half-hints and allusions I had to strain to catch. There was some scheme afoot, tied to this long-dead nun’s name, talk of a marriage, a great fortune, though I could not make out for whom it was intended. Once I thought the jokes were aimed at a fair-haired young German man in their party called Heinrich Mühler, but then Madame Walravens cackled sharply, and waved her stick to threaten Désirée, keeping the girl from straying an inch out of her sight.

“Here she comes!” one of the men cried suddenly. “There is Justine Marie arriving now.”

My breath caught. I called to mind the pale portrait of the nun on the garret panel, the old sad love story I had pieced together, the apparition I had seen in the dark alley, the strange moment I had found a tiny cradle tucked away in the attic. I felt a sharp, heavy premonition that a great secret was about to break open before my eyes.

The circle parted to welcome their new guest. At that exact instant, a park keeper carrying a flaming torch walked past, its golden glare mixing with the pale silver light of the moon to illuminate the figure stepping into their midst. My whole life seemed to stand still, I leaned forward so far I nearly lost my balance, waiting to see the ghost.

The torchlight fell full on her face. She was no spectre. She was a bright, buxom young girl, fresh from her finishing school, fair-skinned and plump with rosy round cheeks, rich brown hair falling in soft curls beneath her bonnet, dressed in a fine silk party gown, laughing as she greeted two older members of the party with a kiss, calling them “Uncle” and “Aunt”. She was familiar to me – I had seen her at Madame Beck’s Sunday evening parties, a relation of the family, the orphaned heiress Justine Marie Sauveur, named for the nun who would have been her aunt if she had lived.

Then my gaze fell to the third person in her little escort, and my hands clenched so tight my nails dug into my palms. It was him. Paul Emanuel. The *Antigua* had sailed days ago, and he had never been on it at all. Madame Beck had lied, or let the lie spread when it had stopped being true. A huge, crushing weight lifted off my chest, so heavy I nearly cried out with relief. But then a new, sharper dread coiled in its place. Who was this pretty young heiress to him?

I soon gathered he had delayed his voyage entirely of his own will, against the protests of all three of his scheming friends, booked a passage on the *Paul et Virginie* that would not sail for another fortnight, saying only he had a small piece of business he was determined to finish before he left. The hints flew fast and thick around the group: the family had agreed that when he came back from Guadeloupe with the fortune they would all share, he would marry his pretty young ward, the heiress Justine Marie. He laughed at their teasing, his face glowing with joy, as he lifted her hand to his lips, and the young German Heinrich Mühler shifted restlessly, looking angry. Paul drew her closer to him, wrapping his coat around her shoulders to shield her from the cool night air, watching her every move as if she was the most precious thing in the whole world.

It was no ghost story, no old tragedy. The secret was plain at last. The love I had dared to hope for, the quiet little bond I thought we had built between us over shared late lessons and stolen conversations, had never been anything but a foolish daydream. He had always belonged to her.

I did not flinch from the truth. I grabbed the whole bitter, painful reality and held it tight to my chest, as a wounded soldier wraps his regimental colours around his breast before he falls. No more soft, torturing lies, no more fragile hopes that his glances held a secret meaning for me. I was free.

I did not stay to watch any longer. I turned my back on the laughing group beneath the trees, slipped away from the park as the last of the fête crowds dispersed, and walked through the quiet streets of the lower town, where the moon shone bright and unrivalled, lighting my path like a calm, unblinking witness. I slipped through the quiet Rue Fossette, pushed open the pensionnat’s front door that I had left ajar, made my way up the stairs without a sound, shoeless, so no one would hear me.

I stepped into the dark dormitory. All nineteen other girls lay fast asleep in their beds, the room wrapped in the stillness of midnight. On my own bed, which I had left empty, lay a long, dark, still shape: the nun, the ghost I had seen haunting the pensionnat grounds three times before.

I did not scream, I did not faint. All the sharp grief and rage I carried in my chest surged to the surface, and I lunged at the shape, grabbing it by the shoulders and yanking it high off the bed. It was no ghost. It was nothing but a long bolster, dressed in a real nun’s black habit and crisp white wimple, arranged perfectly to look like a tall, slender figure lying still. It tore apart in my hands, falling to the floor in shreds of cloth. Pinned to the wimple was a scrap of paper, written in mocking pencil:
“The nun of the attic bequeaths to Lucy Snowe her wardrobe. She will be seen in the Rue Fossette no more.”

I stood there, the tattered fabric at my feet, left staring at the note, wondering who had played this cruel trick – who the tall, unidentifiable figure I had seen wandering the halls after dark could possibly have been.

The dawn after that extraordinary Midsummer night broke soft and bright, no common, ordinary sort of day—no fiery portents streaked the sky, no storms tore across the earth, no whirlwinds or floods tore through the streets of Villette. The sun rose laughing, warm and golden as a perfect July day should be, while morning draped herself in rubies at the horizon and spilled whole armfuls of roses across her path, petals drifting down so thick they stained the cobblestones pink. The Hours woke dewy as nymphs, emptied their vials of mist over the distant hills, and stepped out free of fog: shadowless, blue, and glorious, they led the sun’s horses on a cloudless, burning course across the sky.

It was the finest summer day any soul could wish for, and I doubt I was the only one in the Rue Fossette who noticed it—but everyone else’s thoughts were far too occupied to stop and admire the light. The same preoccupation hummed under my skin too, of course, though it held none of the shock, none of the overwhelming, unravelling mystery it bore for the rest of the pensionnat, leaving me half free to notice the roses and the sun while I turned the same secret over in my head. Still, as I wandered the garden, feeling the warmth sink into my shoulders and watching the blooms unfurl, I pondered the news every other tongue in the house was chattering about.

What news? Only this: when the bell rang for matins, there was an empty spot in the first row of boarders. When breakfast was laid, one coffee cup sat unclaimed, cold, untouched. When the housemaid stripped the beds, she found Ginevra Fanshawe’s bolster propped lengthwise under a cap and nightgown, meant to look like a sleeping girl. And when her music mistress arrived for their usual early morning lesson, the bright, flighty young pupil she expected was nowhere to be found.

They searched high and low, turned every room upside down, ransacked the entire house from attic to cellar, but it was useless. No trace, no note, no scrap of a hint rewarded their efforts: the nymph had vanished, swallowed whole by the dark of the previous night, like a shooting star burning out mid-flight. The supervising teachers were horrified, but Madame Beck’s terror was far deeper. I had never seen her so pale, so shaken: this was a blow struck straight at her most vulnerable spot, her reputation, her carefully guarded business. None of them could work out how it had happened—every casement was latched, every pane unbroken, every door bolted fast. To this day, no one but Lucy Snowe has ever known the truth of it: I had not forgotten how, the night of the park fête, I had stepped softly to the grand front door and drawn it closed to its lintel, shut, but never bolted, never locked. I remembered too, the thunder of the carriage and pair we had encountered on the dark lane, the flash of a white handkerchief waving from its window.

From those clues, and a handful of small, private observations no one else had made, I could draw only one conclusion: it was an elopement. Certain of that, and seeing Madame Beck so distraught, I finally told her what I knew. When I mentioned M. de Hamal’s long courtship of Ginevra, I found she had long been aware of the affair—she had discussed it at length with Mrs. Cholmondeley before, and washed her hands of all responsibility for the girl, laying it at the other woman’s feet. Now she sent at once for Mrs. Cholmondeley and M. de Bassompierre.

We soon learned the Hôtel Crécy had already heard the news. Ginevra had sent a vague note to her cousin Paulina, hinting at marriage, and the de Hamal family had already reached out with their own inquiries. M. de Bassompierre set off at once to hunt the fugitives down, but he arrived too late to stop the ceremony.

A week later, the post brought me a letter. I transcribe it here, for it explains every last mystery of those strange ghostly months:
> DEAR OLD TIM (short for Timon, you sour old thing),
> I’m off, as you see—gone like a shot! Alfred and I planned to elope almost from the very start. We never meant to get spliced the boring, humdrum way everyone else does: Alfred has too much spirit for that, and so do I, thank God!
> You’d never guess, that man who used to call you “the Dragon” has spent so much time around you these past few months he’s actually become quite fond of you. He begs you not to miss him now he’s gone, and sends his apologies for all the little trouble he put you to. He’s terribly sorry he startled you that day he snuck up on you in the grenier when you were reading that very important letter of yours—he couldn’t resist making you jump, you looked so completely wrapped up in it. To make up for it, he says you once gave him quite the fright when you burst into the room looking for a shawl or a bit of ribbon just as he’d struck a light to smoke a quiet cigar while waiting for me.
> Do you get it now? The Comte de Hamal was your so-called Nun of the Attic, sneaking in to visit little old me! I’ll tell you how he pulled it off. You know he has free run of the Athénée, where his three nephews go to school. The Athénée’s courtyard sits right on the other side of the high wall that borders the forbidden allée in our garden. Alfred climbs as well as he dances or fences: he got over that outer wall first, then pulled himself up the big old tree that spreads its boughs right over the grand arbour, branches resting on the roof of the pensionnat’s lower wings, until he could climb up to the first floor and the grand salle. One night he fell right out of that tree, tore half its branches off, nearly broke his neck, and ran off so spooked he almost got caught by Madame Beck and M. Paul Emanuel walking in the alley below.
> From the grand salle it was no trouble to climb the last flight of stairs up to the top garret. The skylight there is always left half open for air, day and night, right? He slipped right in through it. Nearly a year ago I happened to tell him our old legend of the ghost nun, and that’s when he came up with his brilliant idea for the spectral disguise. You have to admit, he carried it off perfectly.
> If it hadn’t been for that black gown and white veil, you and that “tiger Jesuit” M. Paul would have caught him a dozen times over. He thinks you two are very brave ghost-seers, but what surprises him most is how quiet you kept it all. How could you stand having that long spectre visit you over and over without screaming, rousing the whole house, waking the whole neighbourhood?
> Oh, and how did you like the nun as a bedfellow? *I* dressed her up, didn’t I do a perfect job? I’d have screamed myself hoarse if I’d woken up to that, but you—you have nerves of iron, real leather-hard! I swear you feel nothing at all, no pain, no fear, no grief. You’re a proper old Diogenes.
> Are you terribly cross at my moonlight flit and my runaway wedding? It’s been excellent fun, and I did it partly just to spite that minx Paulina and that bear Dr. John—show them that with all their lofty airs, I could get married just as well as they could. M. de Bassompierre was in a terrible temper at first, threatening to prosecute Alfred for kidnapping, all sorts of nonsense. He was so dead serious I had to put on a little melodrama: go down on my knees, sob, drench three whole handkerchiefs. Of course my uncle caved right away! What’s the use of making a fuss? I’m married, that’s all there is to it. He still huffs that the wedding isn’t legal because I’m not of age, as if that makes any difference! I’m just as married as if I were a hundred years old. But we’re going to have a second, proper wedding now, I’m getting a full trousseau that Mrs. Cholmondeley is overseeing, and uncle has promised to give me a decent dowry. That’s very convenient, because dear Alfred has nothing but his noble birth and his army pay. I just wish uncle would be generous about it, no strings attached. He’s being so annoying, making the dowry conditional on Alfred signing a promise he’ll never touch cards or dice again. They say my angel has a weakness for gambling? I don’t know anything about that—all I know is he’s the dearest, most adorable creature alive.
> Alfred planned our elopement like a genius, didn’t he? He picked the night of the park fête on purpose, because he knows Madame Beck’s habits, knew she’d be out at the concert all hours. I assume you went with her? I watched you leave the dormitory at eleven that night. I can’t imagine how you ended up walking home alone on foot. That surely was you we passed in the narrow old Rue St. Jean, wasn’t it? Did you see me wave my handkerchief out the carriage window?
> Goodbye! Rejoice in my good luck, congratulate me on my perfect happiness, and believe me, dear cynical misanthrope,
> Yours, in the highest of spirits,
> P.S. — Don’t forget I’m a Countess now! My parents and all the girls back home are going to lose their minds when they hear it. “My daughter the Countess!” “My sister the Countess!” Doesn’t that sound so much better than plain old Mrs. John Bretton?

Most readers would assume a wild, heedless elopement like Ginevra’s would end in bitter, years-long payback for her frivolity, that a great deal of suffering lay in store for her future. But all I can tell you of the years that followed is this: I saw her right at the end of her honeymoon, when she stopped by the pensionnat with her new husband to call on Madame Beck, and sent for me to join them in the salon. She flew straight into my arms laughing, blooming and beautiful, her curls longer than ever, her cheeks flushed brighter than any rose in the garden. Her white bonnet, fine Flanders veil, and wedding dress stitched with orange blossom suited her perfectly.
“I got my dowry!” she cried the second she saw me—Ginevra had always had a good head for practical things, no matter how much she scorned the boring bourgeoisie. “Uncle de Bassompierre is fully reconciled now. I don’t care if he calls Alfred a nincompoop, that’s just his stuffy Scotch upbringing! I swear Paulina envies me, and Dr. John is so wild with jealousy he’s fit to blow his own brains out. I’m so happy I hardly have anything left to wish for—except maybe a private carriage and a fancy hotel, and oh! I have to introduce you to my husband. Alfred, come here!”
The Comte de Hamal stepped out from the inner salon where he had been talking to Madame Beck, listening to her half felicitations, half scolding lectures. He was every bit as polite and charming as she had described him, and apologized for all the ghostly pranks with neat, well-turned words, ending by saying the best excuse for every one of his misdeeds was standing right beside him, his bride.
Then Ginevra sent him back to Madame Beck’s side, dragged me off to a corner, and nearly suffocated me with her giddy, girlish, nonsensical high spirits. She held up her wedding ring to show me a dozen times, crowing over the sound of “Madame la Comtesse de Hamal.” I said very little, gave her only the dry, quiet, reserved part of myself—but she expected no more of me. She knew me too well to look for flowery compliments, and my dry little jibes only made her laugh harder.
Not long after their marriage, M. de Hamal left the army, all to wean him away from his gambling companions and bad habits, and he was given a post as a diplomatic attaché that took the couple abroad. I thought Ginevra would forget me then, but she never did. For decades, she sent me a capricious, uneven stream of letters. The first couple of years, every line was all about herself and Alfred. Then Alfred faded slowly into the background, replaced by a new little person: Alfred Fanshawe de Bassompierre de Hamal, their son. She wrote pages and pages of boastful tales about his impossible, extraordinary precocity, and scolded me relentlessly for being so phlegmatic and unimpressed with his supposed genius. I didn’t know what it was to be a mother, she huffed, the whole depth of maternal feeling was lost on my hard, unfeeling heart.
In time, the little boy went through teething, measles, whooping cough: that stretch of months was a perfect whirlwind of dramatic distress in her letters. No woman on earth had ever endured so much hardship, no human being ever needed sympathy so badly. At first I was alarmed, and wrote back soft, comforting notes—but I soon learned there was far more noise than actual trouble in all her lamentations, and I lapsed back into my usual dry, unflappable self. The little boy weathered every illness like a hero, too: five times she swore he was on his deathbed, and five times he made a miraculous full recovery.
As the years passed, the letters began to carry quieter, more ominous notes about Alfred the First. M. de Bassompierre had to be called in over and over again to pay off his debts, many of them those dark, shameful “debts of honour” tied to cards. Small, plaintive troubles popped up over and over. No matter what storm blew up, though, Ginevra cried loudly for help and never once faced a problem alone. One way or another, from some generous relative or indulgent friend, she always got exactly what she wanted. She fought the whole battle of life by proxy, and in all the years I knew her, she suffered less than almost any other human soul I have ever met.

Must I, before this tale draws near its close, tell you what became of that bright taste of freedom and new life I thought I’d won on the park fête-night? Must I recount how the two grand, bold strangers named Freedom and Renovation, whom I’d brought home with me that starry evening, fared when the giddy glow of the festival faded?

I tested them the very next morning. They’d shouted loud boasts of their power to tear me free from the heavy chains of unspoken longing, but when I demanded proof—proof of lighter days, of a heart unshackled—Freedom stammered excuses that he was too poor, too weak to help me right now. Renovation never spoke a single word at all. He’d died quiet in the dark of night, before the sun was high.

There was nothing left for me then but to cling to the faint, secret hope that I had hurried myself to despair too fast, that the old gnawing pain of jealousy had warped my vision. After a short, useless struggle to keep my spirits light, I found myself dragged right back to the old rack of suspense, tied tight and stretched thinner than ever.

Would I see him before his ship sailed? Would he remember me? Did he mean to say goodbye? Would this day, would the next hour, bring him to my door? Or would I have to endure that searing pain of long, empty waiting, that brutal final agony of parting, that silent, mortal wrench that rips up hope and doubt both at once and shakes your whole life to its core—all while the hand that hurts you cannot be soothed into pity, because miles of absence have already fallen between you?

It was the Feast of the Assumption, so no classes were held. The boarders and all the other teachers had attended morning mass, then set off on a long country walk to take their afternoon snack of bread and jam at a friend’s farm. I did not go with them. Only two days remained before the *Paul et Virginie* was due to sail, and I clung to that last, thin chance to see him the way a half-drowned wreck survivor clings to his last splinter of raft, his last frayed cable.

There were carpenters scheduled to do repairs on a broken bench in the first classe that day, the kind of work you can only do when the pupils are all gone. I sat alone, half meaning to drift out to the garden to leave the workmen space, but too listless to even lift myself to my feet, when I heard their footsteps coming down the hall.

Labassecourien artisans always go about their work in pairs—you’d swear it takes two of them just to drive a single nail. As I fumbled to tie the ribbons of my bonnet, which I’d been letting hang idle from my hand, I was vaguely surprised to hear only one pair of footsteps. I noticed too—the sort of trivial, sharp detail you notice when you’re as cooped up and lonely as a prisoner in a dark dungeon—that this man wore soft leather shoes, not the clunky wooden sabots every local workman wore. I assumed it was the master carpenter, coming to inspect the space before sending his journeymen in, so I slung my light scarf around my shoulders. He stepped closer, pushed open the door, my back was turned to him—and a tiny, sharp thrill zipped up my spine, fast and fleeting, before I could even name the feeling. I turned, and there in the doorway, filling the whole frame, was M. Paul Emanuel.

Hundreds of the prayers we mumble heavenward wear out the air and bring us nothing at all. But once, just once in a lifetime, one perfect golden gift drops straight into your lap—bright, whole, unflawed, straight from the mint of fruition.

He wore the dark travelling coat trimmed with velvet he must have planned to wear for his voyage, and for a second I thought he was about to leave that very instant, though I’d been told the ship would not sail for two more days. He looked well, bright, cheerful, kind, his face soft and benign. He stepped toward me eagerly, across the room in a single second, all open warmth and amity. It might have been the bright giddiness of a bridegroom lighting up his face, but whatever it was, I could not meet his sunshine with cold distance. If this was our last moment together, I would not waste it on stiff, pointless formality. I loved him far too much to shove even the demon Jealousy out of my path, not when all she would do was block a kind final farewell. One warm word from his lips, one soft look from his eyes, would sustain me for every remaining day of my life; it would be my comfort through the loneliest, hardest years ahead. I would take that gift. I would drink that sweet elixir, and no silly pride would tip the cup over and spill it.

I thought the interview would be short, of course. He would say the same polite goodbye to me that he had said to every assembled pupil, hold my hand for two minutes, brush a soft kiss to my cheek for the first and only time—and then it would be over. Then the final parting, the wide uncrossable gulf between us, across which he would never glance back to remember me.

He took my hand in one of his, and with the other he pushed my bonnet back off my face, staring deep into my eyes. His bright, luminous smile faded, and his lips took on that soft, wordless expression a mother wears when she finds a child she thought well has wasted away, broken by illness or grief. A shadow fell over his face.

“Paul, Paul!” a woman’s hurried voice called from the hall behind us. “Paul, come to the salon at once! I have so much to say to you—we could talk all day, and Victor is waiting for you, and Josef is here. Come at once, Paul, your friends are waiting.”

Madame Beck, her vigilance or some uncanny sixth sense having led her straight to the door, pressed so close she nearly thrust herself right between M. Paul and me.

“Come, Paul!” she repeated, her sharp, hard gaze grazing me like the point of a steel stiletto. She pushed at his arm. I thought he would recede. I thought he would go with her. The pain pierced me deeper than I could bear, and I could not suppress the cry that tore out of my throat:

“My heart will break!”

It felt like literal, actual heartbreak—but then M. Paul breathed one single word against my ear, a soft whisper: “Trust me.” The weight of it lifted. I wept, great shaking sobs, cold shivers running through me, strong trembling wracking my frame, but all the while I felt a strange, light relief.

“Leave her with me,” the calm, cool Madame Beck said. “This is just a fit of nerves. I will give her a cordial, and it will pass.”

To be left alone to her and her “cordial” felt no different than being left to a poisoner and her bowl. But then M. Paul answered, his voice deep, harsh, brief, ringing with a sound that was strange and strong and life-giving all at once:

“Laissez-moi.” Let me be.

“Laissez-moi!” he repeated, his nostrils flaring, every muscle in his face quivering with restrained fire.

“But this is ridiculous,” Madame Beck snapped, stern. More sternly still, he replied:

“Sortez d’ici.” Get out of this room.

“I will send for Père Silas right this instant,” she threatened, stubborn. “I will call him to intervene.”

“Woman!” he cried, not in his low deep tones now, but his highest, most excited key. “Woman! Sortez à l’instant! Leave this room this very second.”

He was fully roused, and I loved him in that righteous wrath more fiercely than I had ever loved him before.

“What you are doing is wrong,” Madame Beck insisted. “This is exactly the sort of impulsive, unwise, inconsistent nonsense you unreliable, imaginative men always pull—vexatious, unworthy of anyone steady and resolute.”

“You have no idea how steady and resolute I can be,” he told her, his tone softening a little, “but you will see. The outcome will teach you. Modeste, be gentle. Be kind. Be a woman. Look at this poor face, and relent. You know I am your friend, and the friend of all your family. You know deep down you can trust me. I have never hesitated to sacrifice myself, but my heart aches at what I see here. It must give comfort, and it must receive it. *Leave me.*”

That time, the “leave me” was so sharp, so imperative, so full of bitter frustration, I was shocked that even Madame Beck dared to hesitate. But she stood her ground, stared him down dauntless, met his fixed, forbidding gaze as unyielding as stone. She opened her lips to retort, and I saw a quick, bright fire flare across M. Paul’s whole face. He moved gently, no violence, all courtesy, held out his hand toward her—and she spun, whirled out of the room, and the door clicked shut behind her in a single heartbeat.

That flash of temper faded as quickly as it had risen. He smiled at me, told me to wipe my eyes, and waited quietly until my sobs died down, dropping soft, soothing words every now and then until I was myself again, reassured, no longer desperate, no longer desolate, no longer friendless, no longer hopeless, no longer sick of life and ready to welcome death.

“It made you that sad, then, to lose your friend?” he asked.

“It kills me to be forgotten, Monsieur,” I told him truthfully. “All those long, weary days I didn’t hear a single word from you, and I was crushed by the growing certainty that you would sail away without saying goodbye at all.”

“Must I tell you the same thing I just told Modeste Beck? That you do not know me at all? Must I prove to you what kind of friend I can be? You will not rest easy, you will not let your hand stay soft in mine, you will not trust my shoulder as a safe place to lean, until you have seen clear proof? Good. The proof is already ready. I came here to justify myself to you.”

“Say anything. Teach anything. Prove anything, Monsieur. I can listen now.”

“Then first, you must come out walking with me, far out into the town. I came here on purpose to fetch you.”

I did not question his meaning, did not push to know his plan, did not offer a single objection. I re-tied my bonnet ribbons immediately, and was ready.

We walked along the wide tree-lined boulevards, and several times he guided me to sit on the stone benches under the blooming linden trees. He never asked if I was tired. He just looked, and drew his own conclusions.

“‘All these weary days’,” he repeated my own words back to me, mimicking my soft foreign accent with a gentle, playful tease that had never once wounded me, even when he liked to joke that no matter how well I wrote French, I would always speak it hesitantly. “I have not forgotten you for a single hour. Faithful women make the mistake of thinking they are the only faithful souls on God’s green earth. I myself, until very lately, hardly dared to hope such a living, fervent love could ever be returned. But—look at me.”

I lifted my eyes, and they were happy now, bright enough to show him exactly what was in my heart.

“Well,” he said, after staring at my face for a few quiet seconds, “there is no denying the mark written all over you. Constancy wrote it, with a pen of iron. Was it very painful, to bear that writing?”

“Severely painful,” I said, truthfully. “Take her hand away, Monsieur. I cannot bear that sharp, scratching pressure a moment longer.”

“Elle est toute pâle,” he muttered to himself. “That pale face hurts my heart.”

“Ah, I am not pleasant to look at, am I?” The words slipped out unbidden. I had carried a quiet, haunting dread of my own plainness for as long as I could remember, and that fear weighed heavier on me that day than it ever had before.

A great softness spread across his face, his deep violet eyes glistened under their thick dark lashes, and he stood up quickly. “Let us walk on.”

“Do I displease your eyes very much?” I dared to ask, for that question felt like the most vital thing in the world to me in that moment.

He stopped, and gave me a short, firm, unshakable answer that silenced every last one of my old insecurities, subdued all my silly fears, and satisfied me more deeply than I could ever put into words. After that day, I knew exactly what I was to him—and I stopped caring what the rest of the world thought of my looks, one way or another. Was it weak to care that much about one man’s opinion of my face? I suppose it was. But if that is weakness, I own it fully. I had a great fear of displeasing him, and a great, burning wish to please him, and no shame in that at all.

I hardly noticed where we rambled. Our walk was long, but it felt like five minutes. The path was soft, the day was fair and warm. M. Paul talked of his voyage—he thought he would be gone for three full years, and when he returned from Guadeloupe, he would be free of all his old debts and obligations, with a clear path laid out ahead of him. He asked what I planned to do in the long years while he was gone. He reminded me I had once talked of striking out on my own, of opening a tiny little school of my own. Had I dropped that idea?

“Not at all,” I told him. “I’ve been saving every penny I can, to make it happen.”

He said he hated the thought of leaving me behind in the Rue Fossette, that he feared I would miss him far too much there, that I would grow desolate and sad all alone. That was certainly true, but I promised I would do my very best to bear it.

“Still,” he said, his voice soft and low, “there is another problem with you staying there. I want to write to you sometimes. I can’t have any uncertainty that my letters will reach you safe. In the Rue Fossette, well—their strict Catholic rules about private correspondence, while well-meant, could easily be misused, or abused, when you least expect it.”

“If you write me letters,” I declared, “I *will* have them. Ten principals, twenty directresses, will not keep them from me. I am a Protestant. I will not abide that sort of surveillance. Monsieur, I will not.”

“Easy, easy,” he laughed, patting my hand. “We will work out a plan. We have our little tricks, our little resources. Don’t worry. Everything is arranged.”

As he spoke, we came to a stop. We were halfway through a neat, quiet suburb, where the houses were small but bright and welcoming, and he paused right on the white doorstep of a very tidy little cottage.

“I’m paying a call here,” he said.

He did not knock. He pulled a key out of his coat pocket, turned it in the lock, and stepped right inside, ushering me in behind him and shutting the door. No servant came to greet us. The tiny vestibule was freshly painted in soft, pale colors, and at the end of it a French window was draped with climbing vines, green leaves and tender tendrils pressing soft against the glass. The whole house was wrapped in deep, gentle silence.

He opened an inner door to show me the parlor: it was tiny, but the prettiest room I had ever seen. The delicate walls were the soft pink of a blush, the floors were waxed to a shine, a bright square carpet sat dead in the center, and the little round table gleamed as bright as the mirror hung above the fireplace. There was a tiny couch, a small chiffonnière, its crimson silk door half open to show shelves of pretty porcelain, a French clock, a lamp, little biscuit china ornaments, and the recess of the one large window held a green plant stand heavy with three blooming flower pots. In one corner, a small marble-top side table held a workbox and a glass full of fresh violets. The lattice window was open, letting in soft fresh air, and the violets filled the whole room with their sweet scent.

“It’s lovely! So lovely!” I breathed, and M. Paul smiled to see me so delighted.

“Must we sit here and wait for someone?” I whispered, half awed by that deep, still hush.

“First we can peek into all the little nooks of this tiny nutshell house,” he replied.

“Am I allowed to wander all through it?” I asked.

“Yes,” he said, quiet and sure.

He led the way. I saw the tiny kitchen, with its little stove and oven, bright clean brass cookware, two chairs and a table, and a small cupboard holding a neat, matching set of earthenware. “There’s a full china coffee service in the parlor,” he told me, as I stared at the six green-and-white dinner plates, four serving dishes, and the matching cups and jugs.

Up a narrow spotless staircase, he showed me two tiny, cozy bedrooms. Then he led me back downstairs, and paused with a little ceremony before a door larger than all the rest.

He pulled out a second key, turned it in the lock, opened the door, and pushed me gently in before him.

“Voici!” he cried.

I was standing in a much larger room, spotlessly clean, no carpet on the well-scoured wooden floors, lined with two rows of little green benches and desks, a wide aisle down the middle leading up to a raised platform with a teacher’s chair and table, and a blackboard behind it. Two maps hung on the walls, a few hardy flowers bloomed on the window sills. In short, it was a perfect, miniature schoolroom, neat, bright, welcoming.

“So this is a school?” I said, bewildered. “Who runs it? I never heard of any little girl’s school in this suburb.”

“Would you be so kind as to accept a stack of prospectuses to hand out on behalf of a friend of mine?” he asked, pulling a thick stack of printed papers from the pocket of his travelling coat and pressing them into my hand. I looked down, and read the neat, clear printed words:
*Externat de demoiselles. Numéro 7

No mortal can see the future, after all. Love is no crystal-ball oracle, and fear so often conjures phantoms that never come to pass. I sickened for months at the very thought of those three years of separation ahead: the grief they would bring felt as certain and unavoidable as death itself. I could picture the heavy chariot of that fate rolling toward me, Juggernaut atop it a grim, towering mass, its broad wheels sinking deep into soft, crushed soil—me, the prostrate, waiting devotee, bracing already for the annihilating crunch I was sure would tear me to nothing.

Strange as it sounds, stranger still as it proved true (and life holds so many such quiet, surprising mercies), that anticipatory agony was nearly all the torture I would ever bear. The great chariot rumbled past me, lofty and loud and sullen as a thunderhead, but it swept over like a shadow gliding across the noon sky, leaving nothing but a faint, chilling dimness in its wake. I lifted my head, and the demon charioteer and his heavy load were gone. I still breathed. I still was whole.

M. Emanuel was gone for three years. Reader, those were the three happiest years of my life. Do you balk at that paradox? Listen, and you will understand. I opened my little school, and I worked—worked long, hard, happy hours. I saw myself as the steward of the gift he had laid at my feet, and I swore, with God as my witness, that I would tend it well and return him a rich accounting when he came home. Pupils came first from the burgher families of the town, and before long, families of far higher standing sought places for their daughters too. Halfway through the second year, a lucky, unforeseen chance dropped an extra hundred pounds right into my hands: one day, a letter arrived from England holding that exact sum, sent by Mr. Marchmont, the cousin and heir of my dear, long-dead old mistress. He had only just recovered from a near-fatal illness, and the money was a peace offering for his pricked conscience, nagging at him over some papers and notes found after his kinswoman’s death that had named and recommended Lucy Snowe. Mrs. Barrett had passed him my address. I never pressed for the full details of what he felt he owed. I asked no questions, just put that money to the best possible use I could imagine.

With those hundred pounds, I dared rent and renovate the house right next door to my own. I would never leave the rooms M. Paul had chosen for me, the rooms where he had left his things, and where he expected to find me waiting for him when he returned. My small day school expanded into a full, thriving boarding school. And its success was never down to my own wit or skill, not truly. It grew out of a life unshackled, a heart no longer heavy with old loneliness. The spring that fed all my energy lay far across the sea, on a sun-warmed Indian isle. The day he left, he had left me a legacy: a bright, steady thought to carry me through each day, a glowing hope for the future, a reason to be persistent, hardworking, bold, patient, and brave. I could not flag. Almost nothing could shake me now. Almost no petty slight or small mishap had the power to vex me, frighten me, or drag me back into old dark moods. Even trivial little things felt like magic, full of charm.

Do not imagine that that warm, bright flame in my heart fed only on a parting promise. My generous, faraway provider sent me more than enough fuel to keep it burning steady. I was never left cold, never left wanting, never left to fear poverty, never left twisting in the wind of uncertainty. Every ship that docked from his route carried a letter from him. He wrote as he gave, as he loved: full-handed, full-hearted, no skimping, no holding back. He wrote because he wanted to, because he missed me. He never cut pages short, because he had no reason to hold back anything he wanted to say. He would sit down at his desk, pen in hand, simply because he loved Lucy and had a thousand small things to tell her—because he was faithful, thoughtful, tender, true. There was no falseness, no hollow pretense in him. He never coated his words in slippery, apologetic evasions, never sent cowardly excuses or empty nothings instead of news. He would never give me a stone, an excuse, a disappointment. His letters were real, nourishing food for my spirit, living, cool water that refreshed me after every long tiring day.

Was I grateful? God knows I was. I think no living soul who had ever been so well remembered, so steadily sustained, treated with such constant honor and kindness, could ever feel anything else, not until their last breath.

Devout to his own faith (he was never the sort to flip carelessly from one creed to another), he never once tried to pull me from my own quiet beliefs. He never teased me about it, never tempted me to convert. He said, plain and soft: “Stay a Protestant, my little English Puritan. I love that faith in you. I see its severe, gentle charm. There are parts of its ritual I could never embrace for myself, but it is the only creed that fits my Lucy.” All the bigotry of Rome could never take root in him, not even the Propaganda itself could turn him into a cunning, cold Jesuit. He was born honest, not false; artless, not manipulative; a free man, not a slave. His soft tenderness made him pliable in the hands of his priest, sometimes—his affection, his devotion, his sincere, earnest piety blinded his kind eyes sometimes, made him set aside his own good to do the work of crafty people, serve the ends of selfish men—but those are such rare, costly faults, we may well count them one day as precious jewels, not flaws.

And now the three years are done. M. Emanuel’s return date is fixed. It is autumn, and he will be here long before November’s thick mists roll over the town. My school thrives. The house is all ready for him. I have built him a small private library, lined the shelves with all the books he left in my care. I was never much of a gardener by nature, but I tended all his favorite plants out of love, and many of them are still blooming even now. I thought I loved him the day he sailed away—but that love has deepened, grown new roots. He is more mine now than he ever was before.

The sun slips past the equinox. The days draw short, the leaves turn sere and gold. But he is coming.

Night frosts creep over the windowpanes. November sends its fogs ahead to haunt the streets. The wind keens the old autumn moan around the gables. But he is coming.

The skies hang heavy, dark and full. A gale front sails in from the west. The clouds twist into strange, grand shapes: grand arches, broad glowing streaks of light. There are resplendent dawns, glorious and royal, deep purple as a king in his state robes, the whole sky blazed with flame. They are so wild they rival the heat of a thick battle, so bright and crimson they outshine Victory herself, perched on her war chariot. I know the signs of the sky; I have watched and noted them since I was a small child. God, watch over that ship. Oh, guard it, keep it safe.

The wind shifts to the west. Peace, peace, you banshee keening at every windowpane! The gale rises, swells, shrieks out long and loud. I wander from room to room all through that night, sleepless, and I cannot lull that raging blast. The hours slip by, and the storm only grows stronger. By midnight, every wakeful soul in the town hears it, fears it: a wild, screaming south-west tempest.

That storm raged frenzied, unstopping, for seven full days. It did not die until the Atlantic was strewn with wreckage. It did not lull until the deep darks of the ocean had gorged themselves full of what they wanted. Not until the destroying angel of the tempest had finished every last bit of his terrible work did he fold his thunderous wings, the sweep of them carrying storm and roar across the waves.

Peace, be still. Oh, a thousand weeping people, praying in agony on the shores waiting for their loved ones to sail home, strained to hear that quiet fall—but for many, the hush did not come soon enough. When the sun returned at last, its light was no better than dark night for the families of some who never came back.

Pause here. Pause at once. I have said enough. I will trouble no soft, kind heart with more of the dark story. Leave the sunny, tender imaginations to their hope. Let them picture the joy that bursts fresh and bright out of terrible fear, the rapture of being pulled back from the jaws of peril, the wondrous reprieve that erases every last dread, the long-awaited moment he steps off the ship and into your arms at last. Let them paint the picture of our long union, the quiet happy life that stretches ahead for us, full of small daily joys. Madame Beck prospered all the days of her life. So did Père Silas. Madame Walravens lived to see her ninetieth year before she passed away.

Farewell.
